Show HN: Hacker Times, a New York Times-Inspired Version Hacker News
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-03
01:53:34 UTC
10:53:34 KST

An MIT CSAIL team reports early experiments in which GPT-6 Astra interpreted and modified neural-network weights without writing a training loop. In one Qwen test, the authors say a small weight change sharply improved math and instruction-following scores; the findings are preliminary.
HackerTube gathers 6,454 YouTube videos mentioned by Hacker News readers across nearly two decades. Karpathy describes using AI coding tools to build an alternative browsing experience, with its own recommendations and no user accounts.
Robert Haas attributes the delayed release to a growing backlog of AI-assisted bug reports. He describes late feature reversions and the human review still required to turn suggested fixes into reliable code.
After a longtime fan site vanished, a web archivist makes the case for personal backups. The post walks through capture and replay tools, with a disclosure that the author works for the company behind them.
Matt Scherer argues in Tech Policy Press that Anthropic’s spending commitments and uncertain AI demand pose risks to investors and retirement savings. His essay urges regulators to stop the planned offering.
In an October 31 article, freenode news reported that Ubuntu’s 22.04, 24.04 and 26.04 LTS releases still lacked kernels patched for CVE-2026-98432. This is the report’s account, not a fresh verification of Canonical’s current patch status.
Syscall.sh lists system-call numbers, arguments, registers and calling conventions across Linux architectures and kernel releases. The site says its tables are generated from kernel source, with version histories and JSON downloads available for each architecture.
In a guest essay on Terence Tao’s blog, Karthik C. S., Amit Sahai and Jie Xue argue that mathematical work should be valued for the understanding it brings, not for outperforming other people or machines. They urge a less ego-centered response to AI’s role in research.
The project presents a three-dimensional model of central Warsaw assembled from OpenStreetMap, LiDAR and the city’s tree database, with a voxel resolution of one meter.
A Slate essay examines how abrupt layoffs and deteriorating trust shape decisions to quit without notice. Drawing on personal experience and workplace interviews, it questions whether the customary two-week departure still makes sense.
In a Microsoft blog recollection, the author describes slipping into an early rehearsal and learning that Patrick Stewart would host the Windows 2000 launch. A headset-wearing crew member stood in for the actor during the run-through.

Apple may try to increase revenue by doing…something with third-party AI apps
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-03
01:49:52 UTC
10:49:52 KST

Apple’s services business remains at record levels, but it’s facing growing pressure. The central part of that business, Apple’s App Store, has faced regulatory pressure and a slump in mobile gaming, which have hurt services growth.
However, on Monday’s earnings call, Apple signaled that it may look to boost services revenue through third-party apps. What does that mean, exactly? Unfortunately, it’s not entirely clear.
When discussing how AI could potentially “contribute to revenue growth over time,” Kevan Parekh, Apple’s chief financial officer, said: “On the product side, you know, we feel AI offers a compelling reason for customers to upgrade and switch in order to get the latest capabilities, and we also feel there’s opportunities across third-party apps and partnerships as well as first-party opportunities.”
Parekh did not elaborate on what sorts of opportunities might be available via third-party apps. However, Reuters reported Monday that investors are wondering whether Apple could profit by distributing third-party AI agents through the App Store and taking a cut of the revenue they generate.
Regulatory and legal changes have cut into App Store commission revenue in some markets, even as Apple’s services business as a whole continues to grow. Third-party estimates in August, for example, suggested the company had seen an 18% drop in U.S. commission revenue, with declines also in Brazil and Japan after those countries introduced new regulations. Apple has traditionally controlled payments within its app marketplace tightly, taking a cut of all App Store transactions. But a lawsuit by Epic Games ultimately forced the company to allow apps to redirect users to outside payment systems. Other legal challenges throughout the world have further hampered the business.
During the earnings call, Parekh referenced Apple’s App Store woes and returned to the idea that the company’s service business could expand into new territory.
“We’ve also seen some factors that impacted the performance of the App Store, and during the September quarter, the performance on the App Store was similar to the prior year, and we had a record number of paid subscriptions with double-digit growth for third-party apps on the App Store,” Parekh said. “At the same time, we did see some headwinds in mobile gaming that we’ve talked about before, and expect to see those continue into the summer quarter.”
Parekh added: “As we move ahead, we expect services growth to to be between 8 to 9% [in the December quarter]. This outlook contemplates our best estimates on sequential headwinds, primarily from third-party licensing and the additional half percentage point of foreign exchange I just mentioned.”
TechCrunch has reached out to Apple for more information and will update this story if it responds.

DiscImageStudio: burn visible artwork onto CDs, DVDs and Blu-ray discs
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-03
01:33:43 UTC
10:33:43 KST

Windows 桌面版支持设计与刻录，Linux 和 macOS 的实验性命令行版可离线生成 CD、DVD 和蓝光镜像。
Disc Image Studio 可将一张图片转换成符合 CD、DVD 与单层 BD-R 光盘物理层约束的数据，刻录后将使光盘盘面呈现肉眼可见的图案。
Windows 下载 win-x64.exe 后可直接运行。Linux 或 macOS 请下载与 CPU 匹配的实验性 CLI 压缩包，解压后运行 ./disc-image-studio --help。这些发布包均自带 .NET 运行时。 GitHub Releases
在主页选择 CD、DVD 或蓝光，指定输出位置后创建工程。
在设计页导入图片，用图层完成排版，并在右栏「影调」模块调整抖动、伽马、亮度与灰阶档位。
在预览页核对盘面投影，在导出页生成 ISO / RAW / WAV+CUE，或使用即时刻录功能直接写入空白盘片。
即时刻录为实验性功能，请只使用可报废的测试介质。
- CD 支持：支持任何 CD 。您可导出带格式头的 WAV 音轨及配套 CUE，兼容 cdrecord, ImgBurn等刻录软件；也允许导出 RAW 音轨。详见 CD 刻录兼容说明。
- DVD 支持：支持任何 DVD。您可导出 ISO 镜像。
- 蓝光支持：支持任何单层 BD-R 25 GB，支持纯绘图与 UDF 2.01 文件。详见 ADDING_BLURAY.md。
- 随盘文件：CD／DVD／蓝光可在设计页直接选择要保存的文件夹及内圈／外圈位置，也可在导出页调整；设置随工程保存。
- 文件区域预览：混合盘的设计投影和成品读回用黄色标出文件区（含文件系统）。
- 介质灰度方案：CD 与 DVD 均使用黑白二阶，CD 使用 0D / AA，DVD 扰码域使用 A5 / 92。详见 PHYSICAL_MODEL_AUDIT.md。
- 灰度抖动工具：独立程序也可将原图处理为 0/255 黑白二阶 PNG；支持打开或拖入原图、并排预览及无损 PNG 导出。详见使用与构建说明。
- 盘片预设：内置数个品牌的光盘参数，覆盖 CD／DVD／BD。您也可将自己标定好的数据贡献给我们，详见预设维护说明。
Disc Image Studio 提供了一套智能且精准的辅助标定流程，相较人工肉眼识别标定，辅助标定算法将可确定的盘面物理数据精确度从 10-6 提升到了 10-14。校准前采用恒定轨距，十字线的径向线条出现明显弯曲；使用辅助标定得到的三次轨距参数重新生成并刻录后，线条更接近径向直线。
流程与参数说明见辅助标定说明。
- 镜像文件输出：您可以导出完整 ISO / RAW 文件并使用合适的刻录软件手动刻录。
- 即时流式刻录（实验性）：CD、DVD 与 BD-R 边生成边刻录，不保存完整镜像；BD-R 走 MMC 顺序写入。
- 不要使用非空白介质！
- 多语言界面：点击右上角「设置」按钮，在「通用 → 语言」选择简体中文、繁體中文、English、日本語或跟随系统，保存并重启应用后生效。语言和主题默认均跟随系统，已保存的明确选择继续保留；跟随系统时区分简体和繁体中文，日文系统使用日文，其他系统语言使用英文。
- 双入口：不带参数将打开 GUI；带参数则进入统一命令行。
- 模块化：CD、DVD 与蓝光通过 IOpticalDiscModule 接入，统一命令目录与任务契约，详见架构说明。
- 桌面版需要 Windows 10 2004（2004/20H1，19041）及以上，或 Windows 11。
- 构建：.NET 10 SDK（global.json 锁定 10.0.x）；自行构建的非自包含 GUI 需要 .NET 10 Windows 桌面运行时。Release EXE 已包含运行时，无需另行安装 .NET。
- “即时刻录”功能需要一台 Windows 可识别的 CD / DVD / BD 刻录机与空白盘片；BD-R 需要支持顺序写入的单层空白盘。
dotnet build DiscImageStudio.slnx --configuration Release
dotnet run --project src/DiscImageStudio.App --configuration Release
构建回归检查：
dotnet run --project src/DvdImageSolver --configuration Release -- selftest
dotnet run --project tests/DiscImageStudio.ArchitectureTests --configuration Release
版本规则、CI 生成 EXE、Release 草稿检查、MSIX 打包与签名流程见发布指南。
Linux（glibc）可选 linux-x64 或 linux-arm64。Apple Silicon Mac 选 osx-arm64，Intel Mac 选 osx-x64。CLI 在本地生成镜像，实际写盘可交给支持相应镜像或音轨的刻录软件。
使用 .NET 10 SDK 从源码运行 CLI
dotnet run --project src/DiscImageStudio.Cli -c Release -- --help
CLI 可读取 PNG、JPEG、BMP 和 GIF，使用动画图片的第一帧。下面的例子用两扇区音轨验证安装，同时生成配套 CUE。
./disc-image-studio cd-generate --input picture.png --output sample.wav --sectors 2
各命令的完整参数见对应文档。
src/DiscImageStudio.Cli/     Linux 和 macOS 离线命令行入口
src/DiscImageStudio.ImageIO/ WIC 和跨平台图像编解码
src/DiscImageStudio.Core/    介质无关的模块契约、工程/预设模型、任务与命令目录
src/DiscImageStudio.Imaging/ CD/DVD/BD 共用的图片预处理与抖动辅助
src/DiscImageStudio.Burning/ Windows IMAPI2 设备枚举、流缓冲与安全刻录层
src/DiscImageStudio.Cd/      CD-DA 生成核心与 CD 模块
src/DiscImageStudio.Dvd/     DVD 引擎适配模块
src/DiscImageStudio.BluRay/  BD-R 几何、17PP/ECC、3T/6T 映射、流式生成与 ISO
src/DiscImageStudio.Iso/     UDF 2.01 / ISO9660 写入器
src/DiscImageStudio.App/     WPF GUI、统一命令行与商店资源
src/DvdImageSolver/          保持隔离的 DVD 编码/求解引擎
tests/                       无第三方测试框架的架构回归测试
packaging/                   MSIX 清单与打包脚本
tools/                       独立辅助工具与资产生成脚本
docs/                        架构、引擎、预设、发布等文档
Disc Image Studio 的实现与物理模型主要参考以下规范：
CD 公共编码部分还参考了 ECMA-130，第 2 版（CD-ROM，1996）。
- Disc Image Studio 不安装驱动或服务；“即时刻录”功能使用了 Windows 自带的 IMAPI2，拒绝非空白介质。
- “即时刻录”功能已有部分实盘反馈，对不同设备、介质与刻录入口的验证覆盖仍有限，属于实验性功能，请只使用可报废的测试介质。刻录中断、断电或生成速度不足仍可能使盘片报废！
- 实际可见效果取决于盘片、刻录机、固件与写入策略。
- 混合文件夹会在开始刻录前扫描目录并固定布局；期间源文件大小改变会安全终止任务，已开始写入的盘片仍可能报废。
- BD-R 顺序写入不提供备用区重映射；中断后同一盘片通常不能继续写入。
欢迎报告问题与提交代码。开发环境搭建、模块边界规则、测试要求与 Pull Request 流程见贡献指南。
项目采用 GNU GPL v3.0 or later（GPL-3.0-or-later）。CD 交织实现包含从 CDImage 移植并修改的代码，整体分发沿用兼容的 GPL 授权；分发二进制或修改版时须遵守对应源代码等要求。第三方字体与组件遵循各自许可证，详见 授权说明 和 第三方声明；应用"关于"页也可离线查看完整文本。
感谢 arduinocelentano 的 CDImage 项目，它展示了通过生成 CD 音轨在盘面形成可见图像的方法。本项目的 CD 交织实现由其相关代码移植并继续修改，CD 盘面绘图思路也受其启发。CDImage 原作者及贡献者的工作为本项目提供了重要基础。

Ukraine’s new pocket-sized gadget turns Russian drones’ cameras against them
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-03
01:12:55 UTC
10:12:55 KST

Ukrainian drone teams have a new way to spy on the drones that spy on them. A Kyiv equipment startup called CODE380 has been gaining attention for a pocket-sized receiver, the D·Vision SDR, that picks up live camera video from many Russian drones and shows it on a screen.
Those drones send their video over the air without any scrambling, so anyone with the right receiver can watch along. A Ukrainian unit can see where a drone is heading and what its pilot is aiming at before it hits.
According to Defense News, the device is light, at about 10 ounces, which is less than half the weight of competing Ukrainian models, one of which weighs a couple of pounds.
It also listens on the radio channels Russia’s newer Molniya drones started using for video in June. Serhii “Flash” Beskrestnov, a radio specialist who advises Ukraine’s president, said in a June Facebook post cited by Defense News that “most detectors don’t see these frequencies, or see them poorly.” He warned that putting too much trust in a detector could prove fatal.
Beskrestnov has also warned that digital video could one day replace analog, which would blind receivers like this one. The New York Times reported in August that some Molniyas are AI-guided, using Nvidia chips and emitting no signals.

Y Combinator’s Garry Tan uses AI agents so much, he spends up to $200K a year on tokens
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-03
00:35:52 UTC
09:35:52 KST

Y Combinator CEO Garry Tan has, in the past, jokingly admitted that he’s suffered from “cyber psychosis” and sometimes barely sleeps because he’s so excited to be working with his AI agents. On a new episode of Walter Isaacson’s podcast The Builders, set to publish on Tuesday, Tan put a price tag on his passion.
“I spend about maybe $100[,000] to $200,000 a year on tokens,” he told Isaacson. He uses agents for countless work projects and coding tasks and has freely shared his set of 23 Claude Code skills, called gstack, on GitHub — an effort to help teach others what agents can do, he said.
On the one hand, he uses his agent the way many people use AI: to help him research Isaacson’s podcast and prep for the kinds of questions he might be asked. On the other, his agent also helps him run his life.
“It’s read all of my email and all of my Dropbox. It’s read, like, all of my Apple Notes, even my therapy notes going back 20 years,” he said.
He describes using AI agents as getting help with “almost anything that you do in your day-to-day life, and for it to do it somewhat more autonomously. Think of it as a Jiminy Cricket on your shoulder at all times. Like, almost anything you want to do, it knows you, and it’s there to help. And it will actually sort of run ahead and say, ‘What about this?’ Or it’ll help you see around corners.”
As an early OpenClaw adopter who is now a power user, Tan says one thing he’s learned is not to treat AI agents like traditional software. Some people are so worried about “repeatability and safety that they actually trade off all of the beautiful parts of working with an AI agent, which basically can be so similar to a human,” he said.
For instance, Y Combinator is also using an agent that is “rebuilding the way we operate at YC,” he said. “We now have an internal AI agent, for instance, named Stray Light that kind of has a personality, and then it has a company brain. Like it actually has the total context of every founder we’ve ever backed, anyone who’s ever emailed us, like all the applications.”
Tan, who is, naturally, an avid lover of technology (as anyone running Silicon Valley’s famed startup accelerator would be), believes personal AI agents will eventually become mainstream, if one major problem is solved: Costs have got to come down.
“It’s too expensive right now,” he said, adding that he knows the amount of money he spends on AI agents is “a crazy privilege that not everyone can do.”
But freebie agents controlled by Big Tech maybe aren’t the answer, either. When discussing the future he’d like tech to build, and how Meta as a social media company has impacted us, he said, “This is a huge company that controls the algorithm, and we all wish that they didn’t at some level,” he said. “So you could imagine a personal AI that you control, that you program.”

Hinge tests a cheaper plan as users become more fed up paying for dating apps
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-03
00:14:51 UTC
09:14:51 KST

Hinge is testing a cheaper subscription plan as its parent company Match Group looks for new ways to get users to pay for dating apps at a time when people are increasingly tired of subscriptions and, in some cases, dating apps altogether.
During its third-quarter 2026 earnings call on Monday, Match Group said the new Hinge tier will include features such as additional filters and custom location boundaries, giving users more control over who they see and where they meet people.
“It’s designed for women in particular, but also for price-sensitive users,” said Steve Bailey, chief financial officer of Match Group. “Remember that Hinge is one of the highest-priced products we have. The revenue per payer, for example, at Hinge is almost twice that of Tinder, around $30. It also has one of the lower payer penetrations. Its payer penetration is only slightly more than half that of Tinder.”
Match Group didn’t share how much the new subscription will cost. TechCrunch has reached out to the company for more details.
The move comes as dating apps continue to struggle to convince people to pay for services they’re growing tired of using. Between subscription fatigue, endless swiping, and frustration with the overall dating experience, users are increasingly questioning whether premium dating features are worth the money.
Interestingly, Hinge is making this move despite being one of Match Group’s strongest performers. The app had a much better quarter than Tinder, with global monthly active users growing 12% year over year, direct revenue increasing 19%, and paying users rising 23% to 2.3 million. The app generated $221 million in direct revenue and had 2.3 million paid subscribers as of the end of the quarter. Direct revenue is money earned from subscriptions and in-app purchases.
By comparison, Tinder brought in $480 million in direct revenue but experienced a slight 2% decline year-over-year, although it has 8.9 million paid users, a 4% decline from a year earlier.
The lower-priced subscription could help Hinge build on that momentum by converting more users into paying customers without requiring them to spend as much upfront.
The move also marks a shift in Hinge’s subscription strategy. In 2023, the company experimented with a more expensive tier aimed at users willing to pay extra for features designed to help them get more out of the app. Now, it’s testing whether a more affordable option could appeal to a broader audience.
Still, cheaper subscriptions alone are unlikely to solve the bigger problem facing dating apps: user fatigue. Match Group says it’s seeing some signs of improvement, partly thanks to efforts to make its platforms feel less like traditional dating apps.
Recent initiatives include Group Hangouts and Events, which offer users alternative ways to connect beyond the usual one-on-one matching experience. Match Group plans to ramp up those efforts in the fourth quarter.
Another initiative on the horizon is real-time adaptive recommendations, which the company plans to begin testing in the fourth quarter. Announced during its CEO Connection presentation in September, the feature will allow Tinder to respond to user actions within seconds rather than hours, with the goal of making recommendations more relevant and personalized.

Intel stock collapse worsens as $3.5T chip rout spreads
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-03
00:08:53 UTC
09:08:53 KST

Wall Street mostly swept up last week's tech wreckage Monday. Chip stocks kept tracking mud back through the house.
About 7 in 10 S&P 500 stocks rose Monday, yet nearly every chip stock fell, and the group shed more than $400 billion in market value in a single session, by Yahoo Finance calculations.
Intel (INTC) led the way down again.
The chipmaker slid about 9% after its worst day since 1974 on Oct. 30, when a disappointing forecast knocked nearly 29% off the stock. Monday's close left Intel worth less than half of what it was when September ended.
And the damage didn't stick to Intel.
Micron (MU), Sandisk (SNDK), Seagate (STX), and Western Digital (WDC) — the memory and storage names that led the chip boom this year — all got clobbered, falling roughly 6% to 8%. Marvell (MRVL) fell even harder, a drop that came as it agreed to buy Lumentum (LITE) in a $115 billion deal. Nvidia (NVDA) held up better than most, but it's still down about 16% since Oct. 6.
Zoom out and the mess gets bigger. Chip and memory stocks have lost about $3.7 trillion in market value since Oct. 6, by Yahoo Finance calculations. Nvidia lost the most in dollars, but Intel's drop of nearly half is the steepest among the giants.
Not everything tied to AI bled. SpaceX (SPCX) rose, though it's still well below where it started October. Software was mixed, apart from cybersecurity stocks like Fortinet (FTNT) and CrowdStrike (CRWD), which fell with chips.
Adam Johnson, founder of the Bullseye Brief and portfolio manager of the American Ingenuity Fund, thinks last week's sell-off already priced in the worst.
"The market kitchen-sinked it," Johnson told Yahoo Finance on Monday. "They discounted everything that could go wrong. And when you do that, typically that's when you see a bottom."
John Chambers, founder and CEO of JC2 Ventures and Cisco's former CEO, expects the gap between winners and losers to keep widening.
"There will be major train wrecks," Chambers told Yahoo Finance, also on Monday. "And rising tide does not raise all boats. I would not bet against Nvidia."
Stock Trader's Almanac editor Jeff Hirsch, who coined the term "Octoberphobia," bought the sell-off on Oct. 30. History is on his side. Chips have climbed in all eight midterm cycles since 1994, measured from the last October close through the following June, by Yahoo Finance calculations. But in 2002 and 2018, they first fell about another 11%.
Next up is AMD (AMD), which reports third quarter results Tuesday after the bell, the same day as the midterm vote.

====================================================================================================

GUI64 – GUI for the Commodore 64 running on top of the built-in ROM
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-02
23:32:22 UTC
08:32:22 KST

GUI64
Description[edit | edit source]
GUI64 is a graphical user interface (GUI) for the C64, first released in version 1.0 in November 2024. Although GUI64 may appear to be a standalone operating system, it is just a graphical layer running on top of the C64’s built-in ROM-based operating system, the Kernal. GUI64 presents files and directories in freely movable windows, allowing for easy file management on floppy disks as well as on disk images stored on SD2IEC devices. When GUI64 is running on an Ultimate device, all settings are saved, and disk images can be mounted directly from within the GUI. The current version of GUI64 is 2.0.The current version of GUI64 is 2.1.
Operation[edit | edit source]
GUI64 can be operated using a mouse (such as the 1351 or compatible models) connected to Control Port #1, or with a joystick in Control Port #2 (right click with CBM key + fire). To enable support for the mouse wheel—e.g., for scrolling or in up/down controls (see below)—the Micromys adapter must be used. This functionality is also available when using the VICE emulator.
Look and Feel[edit | edit source]
Starting with version 1.9, GUI64 is available in two variants:
Windows-like interface
In this variant, GUI64 visually resembles Windows-style interfaces. The GUI is window-based, with a taskbar at the bottom of the screen, featuring a "Commodore (CBM) button" embedded on the left and a recessed clock button on the right. In between are the task buttons — similar to those in Windows 95.
Mac-like interface
This variant visually imitates the interface of the first Macintosh computers from Apple. The menus are not integrated into the windows but are displayed in a menu bar at the top edge of the screen. The system menu in the upper-left corner includes, in addition to the entries from the corresponding CBM menu in the Windows variant, an “About” entry, which in the Windows variant is part of the file browser’s menu.
In both versions, the desktop itself is empty except for two drive icons in the upper-left corner (in the Mac design: upper-right corner), and—unlike other interfaces such as C64OS[1] or GEOS—does not support placing file icons directly on it. Instead, the desktop serves as a background for windows, which can be freely moved and positioned. Unlike GEOS, for example, GUI64 does not use the VIC-II’s bitmap graphics mode, but operates in text mode. GUI64 is therefore character set–based. For instance, windows cannot be moved pixel-perfectly, but are instead aligned to the 8×8-pixel grid of the character screen. The desktop consists of 22 rows (in the Mac design: 24 rows) and 40 columns of characters. Strictly speaking, GUI64 should therefore be considered not a GUI, but a TUI (Text User Interface).
Elements on the Desktop and in the Taskbar[edit | edit source]
Drive Icons[edit | edit source]
By default, the two drive icons are assigned to device numbers 8 and 9, but this can be changed via right-click on the icons or, alternatively, in the menu of the associated browser window. A double-click on a drive icon opens a file browser window, see below.
Ultimate Icon[edit | edit source]
If an Ultimate cartridge is connected to the C64, or if the computer itself is an Ultimate device, and if the Ultimate Command Interface is enabled, a third desktop icon in the shape of a USB flash drive has been available since version 1.98: the Ultimate icon. Double-clicking this icon opens a browser window that provides access to the file system of a USB flash drive (or SD card) connected to the Ultimate device, allowing disk images to be mounted directly from within GUI64.
[edit | edit source]
Clicking the Commodore button in the lower-left corner of the taskbar opens a menu with three entries: "Settings", "About", and "BASIC". Starting with version 1.98, Ultimate owners will also see an additional "Reboot" entry, which reboots the C64. Selecting "BASIC" (after confirming the prompt) launches the familiar C64 BASIC editor. Clicking "Settings" opens a configuration window where the appearance of GUI64 can be customized. If GUI64 detects an Ultimate system with the Command Interface enabled, the settings are stored in the Ultimate's flash folder as of version 1.97. These settings include various color options as well as an option to display the desktop with a dotted background. Clicking "About" opens a dialog containing information about GUI64.
Time[edit | edit source]
Since version 1.96, the clock automatically displays the Ultimate's time (provided an Ultimate cartridge is connected or the C64 is FPGA-based on an Ultimate 64 board and the Ultimate Command Interface is enabled). Since version 2.0, the clock also displays the correct time when a WiC64 module is connected to the user port. Double-clicking the clock opens a dialog box for adjusting the time. If no Ultimate system or WiC64 is detected when GUI64 starts, the time is set to “00:00” by default.
Tooltips[edit | edit source]
Because the GUI64 screen has limited horizontal space, taskbar button titles are truncated to fit. Starting with GUI64 v1.8.1, users can see the full title in a tooltip by hovering over a button for at least one second. Tooltips are also displayed when hovering over desktop drive icons, showing the full path for drives such as SD2IEC or the Ultimate.
Graphical Elements on the Desktop[edit | edit source]
Windows[edit | edit source]
The primary graphical elements that convey information in GUI64 are windows. Each window has a title bar at the top, with the window title on the left (Mac design: centered). In the Windows design, there are various control buttons on the right. One of these buttons is always an X-icon (X) to close the window; if the window supports minimizing or maximizing, corresponding buttons for those actions are also present. In the Mac version, there is only a button for closing the window on the left-hand side of the title bar. Windows-version: A minimized window disappears from the desktop but remains accessible as a button in the taskbar. Clicking this button restores the window to the desktop. This helps keep the desktop organized and clutter-free.
A special type of window is the dialog. Dialogs cannot be minimized and always retain full focus. This means that user input is restricted to within the dialog window only.
Controls[edit | edit source]
The graphical elements within windows are called controls. Each of these controls serves a specific function—even if that function is simply displaying text, as is the case with labels (see below). The controls available in the current version of GUI64 are, in order:
Functionality[edit | edit source]
GUI64 allows users to work with drives and files. The following drives are detected and supported: Commodore 1541 floppy drive, Commodore 1571 floppy drive, Commodore 1581 floppy drive, and SD2IEC devices. GUI64 may also work with hard drives and other storage devices; however, due to a lack of testing, reliable functionality for these cannot be guaranteed at this time.
Double-clicking one of the drive icons on the desktop opens a file browser window, which—after loading—displays the contents of the medium (floppy disk, SD2IEC directory, or disk image within an SD2IEC directory). GUI64 can also handle dnp images. Files and directories are listed vertically within the window. Each entry shows the file type (as an icon), the file name, and, optionally, the file size (see the "View Menu" below). Entries can be selected by clicking on them. The list can be scrolled by either using the scrollbar on the right edge or the mouse wheel.
Running Programs[edit | edit source]
To run programs (equivalent to LOAD"...",x and RUN), a simple double-click on the corresponding entry in the drive window is required. This will display a progress bar in a seperate window, showing the loading progress. Once the file is loaded into the C64's memory, the file will be executed. To load a machine code file (without a BASIC stub, such as 10 SYS 2063), see "Boot" in the File menu. Note: GUI64 will not remain in the background after running a file and has to be loaded back into memory afterwards, e.g. by pressing the Reset button if GUI64 was loaded from cartridge.
Opening Directories and Disk Images on SD2IEC Drives[edit | edit source]
When the file browser window displays the contents of a directory on an SD2IEC drive, directories and disk images (if available) will also be shown. Directories are represented by a folder-like icon. A double-click on a directory or disk image initiates a loading process, after which the contents of the selected directory or disk image will be displayed in the browser window.
Menus[edit | edit source]
Each file browser window in GUI64 has a menu consisting of the entries "Disk," "File," and "View." These menus provide the user with various options for manipulating drives, files, and directories.
The Disk Menu[edit | edit source]
The 'Disk' menu in GUI64 allows users to manipulate floppy disks (or disk images) and view information about the drive at the corresponding device number.
The File Menu[edit | edit source]
The 'File' menu of GUI64 allows for copying or 'cutting' files, as well as deleting, renaming, viewing, booting files, and creating disk images.
*Note: After creating a new image, the corresponding virtual disk needs to be formatted. For this, double click the image, click Format in the Disk menu and choose Full Format.
The View Menu[edit | edit source]
With the entries in the 'View' menu, the display in the file browser window can be changed.
Applications[edit | edit source]
Currently, there are no applications for GUI64. However, after the release of version 2.0, an interface for developers is planned, which will allow for the easy creation of applications for GUI64.
Development[edit | edit source]
GUI64 was developed by Friedrich (Fritz) Philipp, also known as WebFritzi. Especially during the implementation of the drive functions, he received valuable support from numerous members of Forum64, who assisted him with advice and guidance. Some code snippets originate from Codebase64.

Apple says it’s ‘hard to predict’ when iPhone Duo constraints will improve

08:25:32 KST

Apple Chief Operating Officer Sabih Khan acknowledged the shipping delays plaguing the new iPhone Duo during the company’s fiscal fourth-quarter earnings call on Monday, but offered little clarity on when customers can expect relief.
The delays were just one point of concern during an earnings call filled with questions about the rising costs of memory chips, driven by soaring demand from the AI industry.
“iPhone Duo is constrained as of today. The ramp is going well, and we’re working hard to get this revolutionary new product into the hands of our customers as quickly as possible. But at this time, it’s really hard to predict when supply and demand will be in balance,” Khan said.
Customers who didn’t wait in line at an Apple Store or immediately jump on preorders are now seeing ship times stretch into next year. Meanwhile, Verizon has been apologizing to its customers for the delays by offering gift cards.
In an October report from Financial Times, Apple described demand for the Duo as “nothing short of extraordinary,” after the device sold out on the company’s website within hours of going on sale, pushing delivery dates into next year.
The prolonged shipping delays are notable given Apple’s reputation for its highly efficient supply chain. But the company is hardly alone in facing supply pressures. Soaring demand for memory chips from the AI industry has driven up component costs for hardware makers across the board, threatening to push device prices even higher.
Apple, too, has raised prices on a number of products, Khan noted on the call, adding that those decisions were made “reluctantly.”
In another sign of the company’s efforts to meet demand for the Duo, Apple said it’s “deepening” its partnership with Perryman, a titanium producer for supplying materials for the new foldable device.
“We’ve been thrilled with the early response from reviewers and customers, and we are working hard to get iPhone Duo into customers’ hands as quickly as we can,” Apple CEO John Ternus said during his opening remarks.

Trajectory raises $45M to make AI model training accessible to nontechnical experts
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-02
23:13:53 UTC
08:13:53 KST

Whether developed by OpenAI or Anthropic or built on open source alternatives, current AI models share a critical limitation: They don’t learn from their mistakes without further training.
Businesses adapting these models for specialized fields, such as law and finance, typically get around this by fine-tuning general models. However, that often requires hiring internal research teams or relying on expensive consultants, commonly known as forward-deployed engineers (FDEs).
Trajectory, a startup founded about a year ago by three Stanford classmates with experience at Google DeepMind and Apple, wants to automate that process, allowing AI models to learn from feedback and continuously improve at specialized tasks.
The 18-person company just announced that it raised $45 million in a Series A round at a $300 million valuation, led by Sequoia, with participation from Nvidia, Bessemer Venture Partners, and Bain Capital Ventures.
Trajectory co-founder Ronak Malde compared foundational models to a very smart PhD student who’s always starting a new job. “But what the economy values is someone who worked on the job for 30 years,” he told TechCrunch.
The startup aims to solve this problem through an approach known as continual learning. Businesses using Trajectory can start with an open source AI model and connect the platform to their applications, allowing it to collect feedback on how the model performs. Trajectory then uses that information to retrain the model, helping it improve at specialized tasks over time.
With Trajectory, lawyers, doctors, and other specialists can improve the models near automatically themselves, Malde said. That capability could make deploying specialized AI faster and cheaper.
Companies like Harvey and Rogo are already using Trajectory’s platform to post-train open source models, helping them perform more like experienced lawyers or financial analysts by learning from previous tasks, such as reviewing a particular type of legal contract or valuing a company using a specific methodology. Trajectory’s other customers include Decagon, Mercor, and Nvidia. The startup is now making its post-training platform publicly available.
The people using Trajectory decide “what good looks like for their organization and can turn that into improvements for their model,” Malde said.
Trajectory’s approach to continual learning shares some similarities with recursive self-improvement (RSI), the idea that AI systems can improve their own capabilities. But co-founder Michael Elabd argues that keeping humans involved in the training process makes Trajectory’s approach safer, since autonomous self-improvement could lead to unintended or even dangerous outcomes.
Trajectory isn’t the only startup trying to automate post-training. It considers Thinking Machines Lab, founded by former OpenAI CTO Mira Murati and the company behind the fine-tuning platform Tinker, a direct competitor, along with Applied Compute, which was valued at $3.25 billion last month, and Prime Intellect, which helps companies build and train their own AI agents.
Malde believes the startup’s biggest advantage is its team. He was among the handful of researchers Google DeepMind acqui-hired from Windsurf. Elabd also worked at DeepMind, while Trajectory’s third co-founder, Arjun Karanam, previously worked on generative foundation models at Apple. The startup has also recruited researchers and product designers from OpenAI, Stripe, and Netflix.
Why does design matter so much to a company focused on AI training? Karanam says the startup has developed an interface that makes post-training more accessible to its nontechnical customers.

Apple’s inventory surges 154% as memory chip costs climb
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-02
23:04:57 UTC
08:04:57 KST

Apple’s stockpiling of inventory, particularly computer memory, continued in its fiscal fourth quarter, according to earnings reported Monday. The company reported that its inventory increased significantly in the quarter, growing from $11.09 billion in Q3 to $14.54 billion in Q4, a jump of 31.1%. Year-over-year, inventory grew 154.3% from $5.72 billion in the fourth quarter of 2025.
Apple’s forecast of a 45% to 46% gross margin in the December quarter was largely attributed to the impact of higher memory costs. The company reported a gross margin of 47.9% for the September quarter.
“What we’re seeing with memory pricing right now is unprecedented,” said Apple CEO John Ternus, speaking to analysts on the call. “As we said earlier in the September quarter, we did see higher memory costs as compared to the June quarter, which were partly offset by the benefit of carrying inventory and lower costs on certain non-memory components.”
He said that the company expected “even higher memory costs” in the December quarter, which would be partially offset by price increases implemented earlier this year on some devices, including Mac and iPad at the end of June and iPhones in September.
Ternus also said that beyond December, Apple expects memory costs “continuing to increase.”
“As we said before, we do believe we’ll see a decreasing benefit from the carry-in inventory on memory over time,” he noted, adding that the company was increasing its purchases of this key component. “As we work to improve supply, our manufacturing purchase obligations have increased by 33 billion dollars from June to the September quarter, which you’ll see in our annual filing when it comes out tomorrow.”
Apple also brought COO Sabih Khan, who oversees the company’s supply chain and manufacturing, onto the call to address questions about memory. He reiterated the increased purchase obligations but did not rule out future price increases on Apple devices.
The company also blamed supply constraints for weak iPad sales in the quarter. The iPad was one of the only product segments that didn’t grow, even as iPhone and Mac sales beat estimates. iPad revenue was $6.2 billion, below analysts’ expectations of $6.7 billion. The figure was also down 10% from the prior year.

Study Claims Vizio Simply Ignores 'Smart' TV Spying Opt Out Requests
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-02
23:00:00 UTC
08:00:00 KST

Study Claims Vizio Simply Ignores 'Smart' TV Spying Opt Out Requests (techdirt.com) 31
			
		 	
				An anonymous reader quotes a report from Techdirt: Recently, reports emerged that LG TVs are sniffing around your local network, and even listening to your local environment, even when the TV is purportedly "off." Some of those voice commands were then stored in plain text and uploaded to the internet. LG's response? To deny everything. [...] That latter point is central to a new study out of Northeastern that conducted 5,943 experiments across twelve commercial "smart" TV devices. The researchers found that most smart TVs utilize automatic content recognition (ACR), a technology that captures audio or video snapshots of viewed content and then monetizes that data to help build personalized profiles of viewers.
TV makers insist that users are supposed to be able to opt out, but the choice simply doesn't work across a large segment of TVs. Companies like Vizio simply ignored the consumer requests entirely more often than not: "We find that the advertised ACR opt-out fully suppresses ACR-attributable traffic on only four operating systems; on Samsung Tizen traffic consistent with ACR activity persists in more
than 60% of evaluated scenarios, and on Vizio SmartCast a content correlated endpoint hosted on Amazon Kinesis continues to receive traffic in 100% of opted-out scenarios."
[...] The researchers say since they can't see the precise scope of what's being collected due to encryption, they're unsure whether Vizio is technically violating its FTC agreement (not that we have a current FTC that would care if they were): "The opt-out setting has no observable effect on traffic sent to this endpoint; however, we cannot inspect the payload due to encryption so we cannot confirm whether this violates the FTC settlement." Results of whether opting out of ACR tracking actually stopped ACR tracking were all over the map. Roku, for example, ignores opt-out requests when users are watching Roku's own channels. None of the smart TV vendors track Netflix viewing for fear of violating agreements with the streaming giant. Samsung's opt out function was more likely to actually work when a user was watching antenna broadcasts.
On two Smart TV platforms, personal viewing data collected by ACR remains unencrypted, despite years of bad press and scandal on the subject. Many appear to be violating the law, but because U.S. regulatory oversight has been gutted, they see no meaningful accountability. That includes smart TV OS' like VIDAA, which is most prominently found on Hisense and Toshiba smart TVs: "VIDAA OS sends ACR data to servers run by Unruly over MQTT without encryption, so anyone on the network path between the TV and those servers can read it. Each message carries a Client ID, a permanent identifier for the TV that can't be reset, along with the time zone, the app name, the input (antenna, HDMI), the antenna channel and an audio fingerprint."

Anthropic's growth just flattened
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-02
22:33:20 UTC
07:33:20 KST

Anthropic shared high-level Q3 financials with investors yesterday, report the NYT’s Andrew Ross Sorkin, Lauren Hirsch, and Sri Muppidi.
Revenue for Q3 was reportedly $16.8 billion.
At first glance, this number seems astonishing. And, in one sense, it is. It’s another jaw-dropping sequential gain for what is likely the fastest-growing company in history.
But, break the trend down into implied monthly growth, and the picture looks different.
Specifically, it seems Anthropic barely grew from July to September.
Anthropic’s sequential revenue growth in Q3, moreover, was smaller in absolute dollars than its (gobsmacking) growth in Q2.
So, for several possible reasons, Anthropic’s growth has meaningfully slowed.
This is likely another reason why the stocks of “neocloud” providers like CoreWeave are collapsing. CoreWeave, et al, have borrowed tens of billions of dollars on the assumption that demand for AI compute will continue to grow at rates way beyond consensus expectations for years.
Now, not only are interest rates soaring — raising the carrying costs of this debt — the near-term revenue growth for AI services may be flattening.
Analyzing Anthropic’s growth…
Anthropic’s reported revenue growth this year has been unprecedented:
Q1: $4.8 billion
Q2: $11.6 billion (+$6.8B and +141%, sequentially)
Q3: $16.8 billion (+$5.2B and +45%, sequentially)
Again, these numbers are astonishing.
But so are AI valuations and expectations.
Note that Anthropic added more revenue in Q2 than it did in Q3.
Note, too, that what matters most for Anthropic’s IPO valuation — and, likely, for the future trajectory of the AI bubble — is not what has happened but what will happen.
The key question is, “What do these numbers suggest about how fast Anthropic will grow from here?”
These figures suggest that the market should temper its expectations, at least temporarily.
If we break down the quarterly figures above into monthly estimates and add one more reported data point — that Anthropic had a $65 billion annualized revenue run-rate in July — we see that Anthropic’s growth has flattened even more than the quarterly figures suggest.
Below are back-of-the-envelope estimates for Anthropic’s monthly revenue in 2026. They are based on the reported quarterly revenue figures as well as the $65 billion annualized revenue in July (which equates to ~$5.4 billion of July revenue).
Even the quarterly numbers suggest that Anthropic’s average monthly growth slowed from ~$760 million in Q2 to (“only”) $580 million a month in Q3.
But the “$65 billion annualized revenue in July” data point suggests the slowdown is even sharper.
If Anthropic did $5.4 billion in revenue in July and $16.8 billion in Q3, it likely only grew modestly in August and September.
According to DealBook, at its meeting with investors yesterday, Anthropic did not share an annualized revenue number based on September’s revenue. This suggests — to this analyst, anyway — that Anthropic did not think the September annualized revenue figure would show impressive-enough growth versus July.
Indeed, when we extrapolate the monthly figures, it seems Anthropic saw a sharp change in its growth trajectory in August and September.
What does this mean?
It means that the growth of the leading company in the sector appears to have slowed sharply in the past two months.
The slowdown might at least in part be the result of seasonality. August, at least, tends to be a slow month. But it’s early in a product cycle to see such sharp seasonality. And September’s growth should have sped back up.
It might also mean that Anthropic is simply not able to keep up with the growth of demand. If this is the reason for the slowdown, it’s a high-quality problem, and it won’t have a long-term impact on the overall AI spending growth trajectory (or bubble).
But it may also mean that the near-term growth rate of AI spending is moderating — either because demand growth is moderating or because companies are using cheaper models from China for some of their AI needs. (Usage data suggests the latter is in fact happening).
If a slowdown in spending growth is the explanation, we should expect the wild investor enthusiasm for all things AI — and the colossal infrastructure spending growth supporting it — to also begin to moderate.
The end of all bubbles comes when supply begins to catch up with demand.
Anthropic’s apparent slowdown suggests we may be closer to reaching that moment.
See Also: When will the AI Bubble burst? And why?

====================================================================================================

Anthropic's growth just flattened
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-02
22:33:20 UTC
07:33:20 KST

Anthropic shared high-level Q3 financials with investors yesterday, report the NYT’s Andrew Ross Sorkin, Lauren Hirsch, and Sri Muppidi.
Revenue for Q3 was reportedly $16.8 billion.
At first glance, this number seems astonishing. And, in one sense, it is. It’s another jaw-dropping sequential gain for what is likely the fastest-growing company in history.
But, break the trend down into implied monthly growth, and the picture looks different.
Specifically, it seems Anthropic barely grew from July to September.
Anthropic’s sequential revenue growth in Q3, moreover, was smaller in absolute dollars than its (gobsmacking) growth in Q2.
So, for several possible reasons, Anthropic’s growth has meaningfully slowed.
This is likely another reason why the stocks of “neocloud” providers like CoreWeave are collapsing. CoreWeave, et al, have borrowed tens of billions of dollars on the assumption that demand for AI compute will continue to grow at rates way beyond consensus expectations for years.
Now, not only are interest rates soaring — raising the carrying costs of this debt — the near-term revenue growth for AI services may be flattening.
Analyzing Anthropic’s growth…
Anthropic’s reported revenue growth this year has been unprecedented:
Q1: $4.8 billion
Q2: $11.6 billion (+$6.8B and +141%, sequentially)
Q3: $16.8 billion (+$5.2B and +45%, sequentially)
Again, these numbers are astonishing.
But so are AI valuations and expectations.
Note that Anthropic added more revenue in Q2 than it did in Q3.
Note, too, that what matters most for Anthropic’s IPO valuation — and, likely, for the future trajectory of the AI bubble — is not what has happened but what will happen.
The key question is, “What do these numbers suggest about how fast Anthropic will grow from here?”
These figures suggest that the market should temper its expectations, at least temporarily.
If we break down the quarterly figures above into monthly estimates and add one more reported data point — that Anthropic had a $65 billion annualized revenue run-rate in July — we see that Anthropic’s growth has flattened even more than the quarterly figures suggest.
Below are back-of-the-envelope estimates for Anthropic’s monthly revenue in 2026. They are based on the reported quarterly revenue figures as well as the $65 billion annualized revenue in July (which equates to ~$5.4 billion of July revenue).
Even the quarterly numbers suggest that Anthropic’s average monthly growth slowed from ~$760 million in Q2 to (“only”) $580 million a month in Q3.
But the “$65 billion annualized revenue in July” data point suggests the slowdown is even sharper.
If Anthropic did $5.4 billion in revenue in July and $16.8 billion in Q3, it likely only grew modestly in August and September.
According to DealBook, at its meeting with investors yesterday, Anthropic did not share an annualized revenue number based on September’s revenue. This suggests — to this analyst, anyway — that Anthropic did not think the September annualized revenue figure would show impressive-enough growth versus July.
Indeed, when we extrapolate the monthly figures, it seems Anthropic saw a sharp change in its growth trajectory in August and September.
What does this mean?
It means that the growth of the leading company in the sector appears to have slowed sharply in the past two months.
The slowdown might at least in part be the result of seasonality. August, at least, tends to be a slow month. But it’s early in a product cycle to see such sharp seasonality. And September’s growth should have sped back up.
It might also mean that Anthropic is simply not able to keep up with the growth of demand. If this is the reason for the slowdown, it’s a high-quality problem, and it won’t have a long-term impact on the overall AI spending growth trajectory (or bubble).

Anthropic’s apparent slowdown suggests we may be closer to reaching that moment.

====================================================================================================

The SEC Should Block Anthropic's IPO to Protect the Public
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-02
22:30:47 UTC
07:30:47 KST

The SEC Should Block Anthropic’s IPO to Protect the Public
Matt Scherer / Nov 2, 2026
Matt Scherer is co-author of the recent report Brace for Impact: Protecting people and the economy when the AI bubble bursts.
Things are seldom what they seem when it comes to the finances of the AI industry. The AI ecosystem’s circular business dealings, extensive use of financial engineering, and otherwise murky finances make it hard for investors, regulators, or workers saving for retirement to get an accurate financial picture of AI companies.
That is particularly true of Anthropic, which filed the registration statement for its initial public offering (IPO) last week ahead of a planned November stock market debut.
The company’s topline financial figures look quite impressive for a startup, with revenues that appear to have grown quickly over the course of 2026 and a fairly healthy balance sheet — on the surface, that is.
Dig a little deeper, however, and a far bleaker picture emerges. Footnotes to the company’s most recent financial disclosures revealed nearly $900 billion in spending commitments, most of them stretching into the 2030s. The company’s financial results for 2026 have not been subjected to formal auditing, which makes them less-than-reliable, but the third-quarter results suggest that the company’s revenue growth slowed to a crawl over the summer.
Anthropic will have to reverse that trend and sustain extraordinary revenue growth to meet its vast obligations. But the financial pressures that are squeezing Anthropic — and, indeed, the AI industry as a whole — will make it exceptionally hard for the leading model developer to do so. If the company goes public, workers, retail investors, and retirees will increasingly be left holding the bag when Anthropic’s bills come due.
The financial mountain that Anthropic must climb is unprecedented for a company making its stock market debut. While Anthropic has an impressive $96 billion in cash reserves, its amended registration statement reveals at least $582 billion in non-cancelable spending commitments to just five companies (Amazon, AMD, Broadcom, Google, and Microsoft) to gain access to the vast computational power required to train and run Anthropic’s generative AI models.
Even though they do not appear on Anthropic’s balance sheet, those obligations are effectively debts in a legal and practical sense: they are payments that Anthropic is contractually required to make in the future, regardless of whether the company can afford to do so.
Moreover, it is not clear how much of the remaining $300 billion or so of the $895 billion in contractual commitments will ultimately land on Anthropic’s balance sheet. With the company continuing to negotiate further massive infrastructure and compute contracts, Anthropic may well end the year with a trillion dollars or more in contractual commitments.
Generating the revenues necessary to meet those obligations would be, to say the least, quite difficult for any company. But the margins of AI model developers are being squeezed by several forces that will make that task extraordinarily challenging for Anthropic.
Key forces squeezing the AI industry’s margins
First, it isn’t clear that there is nearly enough demand for generative AI products and services to justify Anthropic’s spending. Multiple studies, including from organizations notable for their bullishness on AI, suggest LLMs are not accelerating productivity or otherwise generating positive returns on investment for most businesses. Worryingly for enterprise-focused Anthropic, one study found that the number of Americans using AI at work fell significantly between the summers of 2025 (46%) and 2026 (33%).
Anthropic’s spending commitments suggest that the company believes it will be able to convince businesses to pay for ever-larger and more expensive models. But there are growing signs that most businesses are not interested in paying for more powerful and expensive models. When Anthropic released its much-hyped Fable 5 frontier model this summer, its commercial reception was tepid.
Even if a future Anthropic model captivates businesses, the company would run into a second obstacle: the AI industry’s frenzied competition. Anthropic is part of a crowded Silicon Valley ecosystem where numerous well-heeled corporations (including Google, Meta, Microsoft, OpenAI, and SpaceX, along with Anthropic) offer generative AI models that are largely indistinguishable in their quality and capabilities. As a result, AI companies can quickly offer competing products whenever a rival identifies a use case that shows even a hint of commercial potential.
In such an environment, companies can rarely afford to raise prices because doing so will prompt customers simply to switch to a rival. The rush to grab customers ahead of their IPOs led Anthropic and OpenAI to launch a price war this summer that remains ongoing. Competition from Chinese companies offering open-weights models puts further downward pressure on prices, as does the increasing availability of models that can run locally on phones and laptops — no expensive data centers required.
That leads to the third problem: the extraordinarily high costs of the chips and data centers needed to train and run AI models, which put powerful and continuous upward pressure on Anthropic’s costs. Between the chips, servers, networking equipment, electricity and cooling infrastructure, construction costs, and other expenses, a 1 gigawatt (GW) data center costs an estimated $31 billion, and the prices for some components are soaring.
That is for 1 GW. Anthropic has committed to 15 GW of data center capacity in the coming years and the company estimates it will ultimately need 20-25 GW, which explains the company’s half-trillion-plus dollars in spending commitments.
And that is just the cost of the physical infrastructure. Once a data center is up and running, training AI models entails using tens of thousands of chips running in parallel that require massive amounts of energy to power, run hot, and need to be kept cool. Moreover, each time someone prompts a model to answer a question, create an image, or perform a task, additional computational power, energy, and cooling are needed to process the query and generate a response or complete the task.
Finally, there are external factors that are delaying data center construction and piling on additional costs. The fierce and still-rising backlash to AI in general and data centers in particular across the country has scuttled a number of data center projects and delayed many others. Every delay limits the computational capacity Anthropic is counting on to generate the revenue it will desperately need, and rising costs due to the Iran War and supply-chain challenges could drive Anthropic’s operational costs still higher.
Anthropic will further pad its cash reserves if its IPO is successful, but the brutal economics of its business are not likely to improve anytime soon. Even if demand picks up significantly, intense competition will apply unrelenting downward pressure on prices even as high costs and resource constraints squeeze it from the opposite direction. That is a deadly mix for Anthropic in light of its slowing revenue growth and massive off-balance-sheet debts, a vast and immovable fixed cost.
If Anthropic were to go public now, it would pile additional risk onto a stock market that is already historically overvalued and disproportionately concentrated in a handful of tightly interconnected mega-cap companies from the tech sector. That has already created a risk of a historically damaging stock market crash; never before has such a huge amount of wealth depended on the performance of a small number of companies whose fortunes are inextricably linked. Adding another huge, unprofitable tech giant to public exchanges would exacerbate both of those factors and create a higher risk of contagion if (or, rather, when) Anthropic reaches a financial precipice.
It also would mean that an ever-larger share of the fallout from a potentially devastating AI crash would fall on ordinary people. As a private company, Anthropic’s shares are overwhelmingly owned by corporate insiders, tech giants, and venture capitalists. Once the company goes public and shares start getting funneled into 401(k)s, retirement accounts, and index funds, it will be workers, retirees, and retail investors that are increasingly left holding the bag when the forces squeezing Anthropic’s margins start to bite.
The Securities and Exchange Commission (SEC) has the power to stop that from happening. The agency can delay or stop companies from going public if it believes a company’s registration statement is “on its face incomplete or inaccurate in any material respect.” That legislative language might as well have been written with Anthropic in mind, considering that the company’s prospectus includes only unaudited financial results for the period where it claims the greatest revenue growth, relegates its most significant future obligations to the footnotes of its registration statement, and has continued to announce new 11-digit financial commitments as its IPO approaches.
The SEC should block Anthropic’s stock market debut until the company provides two consecutive quarters of audited financial statements, with all material information about the company’s spending commitments disclosed. Failing to do so could have dire consequences for workers, investors, and ultimately the financial system and economy.

====================================================================================================

V100 SXM2 pinout and NVLink reference
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-02
22:30:13 UTC
07:30:13 KST

This project aims to become an open-source carrier-board design for NVIDIA V100 SXM2 modules, including NVLink connections for two- and four-GPU configurations.
While that board is being developed, this reference kit shares the pin mappings, connection plans and connector-only KiCad resources so other people can develop their own designs.
My contribution so far is primarily curation: gathering public information, comparing sources, reconciling connector orientation and signal names, and making the results easier to follow. The original reverse-engineering work belongs to its authors, credited in Sources and attribution.
- Download the complete reference ZIP from v0.1.0-reference, or use Code > Download ZIP for the current development files.
- Extract the archive and keep the directory structure. Choose a single-, two- or four-module KiCad starter, then open its .kicad_pro file in KiCad 10.0.7 or a compatible newer version. Keep the whole kicad/ folder so the shared symbol library is available.
- For reading without KiCad, use the complete schematic PDFs or standalone workbook.
The download guide includes print editions and library-path help. Change notes identify reference editions. This is a public reference pre-release, not a hardware-qualified carrier board.
- 800 canonical contacts: 400 on connector A and 400 on connector B, with signal names, evidence and electrical-treatment notes.
- Two- and four-GPU connection tables: exact Connector B pads, directions, lanes and polarity.
- Three editable KiCad starters: one module’s A/B connectors, plus two- and four-module versions with NVLink data connected. Every contact stays labelled, including unconnected clocks, power and control pins. No regulator, power-control or clock circuits are included.
- Catppuccin Mocha visuals: contact maps, topology diagrams, schematic previews and the standalone dark reference workbook. An optional KiCad colour theme is included.
External clock required: the NVLink starter schematics show data wiring, not a complete working NVLink implementation. Each V100 needs a 156.25 MHz differential NVHS clock on B/E20 (+) and B/F20 (−). The separate PCIe clock is 100 MHz on A/E7 (+) and A/F7 (−). Clock requirements and scope.
These are the actual connector-only KiCad schematics: 400 labelled contacts on A and 400 on B. Click a preview to open the full-size vector drawing. The two- and four-GPU NVLink arrangements are shown separately below and in the connection guides.
Open the KiCad project · Schematic overview · KiCad guide
The full six-group two-GPU arrangement is derived from the documented pairing families; the four-GPU arrangement follows the curated topology. Each guide explains its source evidence and remaining uncertainties. These are logical connection plans, not routed bridge schematics.
Open the two-V100 KiCad project · Native schematic previews
Open the four-V100 KiCad project · Native schematic previews
Two-GPU connections · Four-GPU connections
The actual local reference footprint, exported directly by KiCad: 800 contact pads, housing outlines and mounting features. A raw PCB footprint is not included in this edition; see footprint availability and open questions and visual provenance.
This is a render of an existing project geometry preview, using the Amphenol connector model. It illustrates connector placement; it is not a completed carrier board or proof of connector A/B identity, mating-part selection or viewing direction. See render provenance.
The longer-term goal is a reusable open-source board design. This reference kit currently covers the pin mappings, NVLink connection plans and connector starter. Routed boards, manufacturing outputs and hardware qualification are future work. See the roadmap.
Corrections are particularly useful when they include an oriented drawing, exact source reference, continuity result or measured behaviour. Please distinguish a confirmed observation from an interpretation.
Use the correction and file-help forms, following the contribution guide. To reference this edition in your own work, use GitHub's Cite this repository option or CITATION.cff, and retain the relevant upstream source credits.
Our original hardware design contributions use CERN-OHL-W-2.0; our original documentation, diagrams and workbook presentation use CC BY-SA 4.0. Both allow commercial reuse, with attribution/notices and the applicable reciprocal sharing requirements. See licence scope and full texts and third-party notices. The licences do not claim ownership of pinout facts or replace upstream terms.
Follow the project and join the discussion on the Level1Techs forums: designing an open-source budget 4× V100 SXM2 NVLink backplane.
Thank you to everyone on the Level1Techs forums for your help, ideas and contributions to this project.

====================================================================================================

Anthropic warns that it’s very close to RSI capability, aka self-building AI
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-02
22:14:50 UTC
07:14:50 KST

While much attention has focused on the updated financials Anthropic revealed in its revised S-1A on Monday, (revenue is soaring, but so are expenses), the AI lab dropped another little bombshell in the fine print.
The filing seems to warn that the company is on the cusp of creating a model capable of recursive self-improvement (RSI).
RSI is the point at which AI grows so good at training itself that it spawns a dramatic acceleration of AI capabilities. To the AI doomers, RSI represents the cataclysm from which there is no return, in which AI builds itself into something powerful enough that it slips beyond human control and may be indifferent to the damage it causes while achieving its goals.
To AI enthusiasts, RSI, or some version of AI reliably using itself to improve, represents the technology’s promise, where it becomes a tool of unimaginable productivity, scientific discovery, and more.
Anthropic seems to believe that RSI is both a promise and a peril. It says in the S-1 that it uses AI in its R&D, but it cautions: “Using AI to help build more capable AI could also pose a considerable safety risk, particularly if it sharply accelerates the pace of progress. This is one of the areas covered by our Responsible Scaling Policy, which sets out our plans to monitor and mitigate the risks as we approach what has been termed ‘recursive self-improvement.’”
When the term RSI became popular earlier this year, many believed it was a buzzword or, at best, a goal too hard to achieve, define, or prove.
But on Monday, Anthropic added new statements to its Risk Factors section, saying 1) it has already used AI to develop new models whose leaps in capability it didn’t recognize at the time, and 2) it may soon meet its own internal definition of RSI (bold emphasis ours, below):
In addition, our increasing use of AI systems to accelerate our own research and development may cause the capabilities of our models to advance more rapidly than our ability to develop, validate, and implement corresponding safety, alignment, and security measures. As a result, we have in the past not identified, and may in the future not identify, a significant acceleration in our rate of development until after it has occurred.
For example, our internal estimates and our expectations regarding future models, rather than our current models, indicate that we may imminently meet our Responsible Scaling Policy’s definition of “dramatically accelerated progress attributable to AI.” We have not designated any of our current models as exceeding this threshold, and we continue to assess the underlying data.
However, Anthropic also said in the filing that none of its models have crossed this RSI threshold yet and that it is promising to keep humans in the R&D process for the foreseeable future. “We do not anticipate being able to safely use Claude to fully autonomously design and build successive generations of frontier models without meaningful human supervision or understanding, if and when that becomes technically possible. Accordingly, we are considering updates to our Responsible Scaling Policy to keep humans in control of frontier model development at Anthropic.”
Skeptics might point out that an S-1 functions as both disclosure and marketing, and that claims of being close to a model so powerful that it can build itself could fall into the latter category.
Nevertheless, there’s no question that Claude’s capabilities, especially in terms of coding, math, and cybersecurity, have grow in leaps and bounds this year. If Anthropic thinks the possibility of RSI is close, maybe we should believe it and act — perhaps govern — accordingly.

====================================================================================================

Apple reports fourth quarter results
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-02
22:13:42 UTC
07:13:42 KST

PRESS RELEASE
                    
                    
                        November 2, 2026
                    
                    
                
Apple reports fourth quarter results
                
                
                    September quarter records for total company revenue, iPhone revenue, and EPS 
Services revenue reaches new all-time high
                
            
CUPERTINO, CALIFORNIA Apple today announced financial results for its fiscal 2026 fourth quarter ended September 26, 2026. The Company posted quarterly revenue of $114.5 billion, up 12 percent year over year. Company gross margin was 47.9 percent, including a favorable impact of approximately 1 percentage point from tariff refunds. Diluted earnings per share was $2.10, up 14 percent year over year, and included a favorable impact of $0.05 from tariff refunds.
“Today, Apple is proud to report a September quarter revenue record of $114.5 billion, up 12 percent from a year ago, with iPhone reaching a September quarter record and Services reaching an all-time high, capping off our best fiscal year ever,” said John Ternus, Apple’s CEO. “We’re closing the year with our most ambitious lineup yet. That includes the iPhone Duo, the most transformational iPhone since the original, alongside iPhone 18 Pro and iPhone 18 Pro Max, our most powerful iPhones yet. We also just introduced a whole new generation of hardware for the home and brought Touch to Mac for the first time. Together with the all-new Siri AI, it’s an incredible lineup to carry us into the holiday season.”
“Apple delivered September quarter records for net income and EPS. Our record September quarter capped off a strong fiscal 2026, with all-time record revenue of $479 billion and double-digit EPS growth,” said Kevan Parekh, Apple’s CFO. “We generated $28 billion in operating cash flow during the quarter, allowing us to return $28 billion to shareholders. Thanks to our very high levels of customer satisfaction and loyalty, our installed base of active devices reached a new all-time high across all major product categories and geographic segments.”
Apple’s board of directors has declared a cash dividend of $0.27 per share of the Company’s common stock. The dividend is payable on November 16, 2026, to shareholders of record as of the close of business on November 12, 2026.
Apple will provide live streaming of its Q4 2026 financial results conference call beginning at 2:00 p.m. PT on November 2, 2026, at apple.com/investor/earnings-call. The webcast will be available for replay for approximately two weeks thereafter.

Apple set an iPhone sales record even without a new standard iPhone
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-02
22:07:09 UTC
07:07:09 KST

Apple had a record-breaking quarter for iPhone sales — despite not releasing a standard model during its latest refresh. In its fourth-quarter earnings report released on Monday, Apple says it earned $57.58 billion in iPhone revenue, a 17 percent jump from the same time last year.
Apple set an iPhone sales record even without a new standard iPhone
iPhone revenue jumped 17 percent with Pro-only models during this year’s refresh.
The increase in revenue follows the launch of Apple’s iPhone 18 lineup in September, which included a pricier iPhone 18 Pro and Pro Max model. The company raised prices across its older models at the same time. Apple also took the wraps off its first folding phone, the iPhone Duo, but its preorder sales aren’t included in this earnings report.
Like other device-makers, Apple has been dealing with supply chain constraints and rising memory costs, leading it to raise prices across its products. Meanwhile, Apple only had a “limited” number of iPhone Duos available in store at launch, with some estimated shipment dates extending into 2027. “We saw higher memory costs as compared to the June quarter, and that was partly offset by the benefit of carrying inventory and lower costs on non-memory components,” Apple CEO John Ternus said during an interview with CNBC.
Apple reported a 12 percent increase in revenue overall, ballooning to $114.5 billion. Its Mac business grew 24 percent to $10.79 billion, while Apple’s services division, which includes App Store commissions along with subscriptions to services like Apple TV and Apple Music, grew to $31.57 billion. iPad revenue declined 10 percent year over year, with $6.23 billion in sales.
Apple shook up other areas of its product lineup with some other major launches over the past several weeks. It made a big push into the smart home space with its first smart home display and a series of video doorbells, security cameras, and door locks, made in collaboration with LG and Schneider Electric. Last week, Apple also revealed a MacBook Pro with a touchscreen — a design choice that its executives famously resisted.
