SLAC researchers find proof of altermagnetism, a new magnetic phase
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-02
21:42:25 UTC
06:42:25 KST

SLAC researchers find proof of altermagnetism, a new magnetic phase
The team directly observed a unique property in an altermagnetic candidate material, delivering key evidence of a new phase of magnetism first postulated in 2022.
Key takeaways:
- The scientific case for a third type of magnetism – termed “altermagnetism” – has been building over recent years.
- Now, a SLAC-led team has experimentally proven that a material exhibits characteristics that are theoretically impossible to explain without altermagnetism.
- Altermagnetic materials could be used to design advanced electronics, with applications including quantum technologies, memory devices and data centers.
For decades, physicists recognized only two types of magnetism that occur in materials without external influence: ferromagnetism and antiferromagnetism. Today, in findings published in Science, a team led by the U.S. Department of Energy's SLAC National Accelerator Laboratory has delivered conclusive experimental proof of a third magnetic phase called altermagnetism.
“In the past few years, scientists have observed phenomena that don't fit neatly into the ferromagnetism-antiferromagnetism dichotomy,” explained lead author Daniel Jost, associate scientist at SLAC and Stanford Institute for Materials and Energy Sciences. “Researchers have been compiling evidence of a third type of magnetism that bridges the qualities of these two classes. We believe our results provide robust experimental confirmation that this third phase – altermagnetism – is real.”
Building on previous studies, this research confirmed that manganese telluride (MnTe) is an altermagnet – a new class of magnet that exhibits properties of both ferromagnetism and antiferromagnetism. Their results showed direct experimental evidence of chirality – an asymmetric magnetic structure, akin to a spiral staircase or a screw – in the spins of an altermagnetic candidate material.
Identifying these unique materials could allow researchers to manipulate their properties in ways that make them ideal candidates for advanced electronics, with applications including quantum technologies, memory devices and data centers.
A primer on magnetism
To most of us, magnetism is a tangible force: It’s what holds a child's artwork to a refrigerator door and points a compass needle north. But to a physicist, magnetism is more complex. It’s a quantum property of a material, and not all magnetic materials have a directly observable magnetic effect.
In physics, a material is considered magnetic if its electrons’ spins – their internal angular momentum, which can be either “up” or “down” – are polarized, or ordered in a specific pattern.
In ferromagnets, all electronic spins point the same direction (up-up-up or down-down-down), creating an observable magnetic field. Humans have known about and made use of this class of magnets since ancient times. In 1933, scientists discovered another type of material with polarized spins. Antiferromagnets have alternating electronic spins (up-down-up-down) that cancel each other out, producing no net magnetic effect.
A new type of magnetism?
For nearly 90 years, this two-category system dominated our understanding of magnetism.
But in recent years, researchers have noticed that some materials don’t fit neatly into either category. Certain materials seemed to exhibit features of both antiferromagnetism, such as alternating spin configuration with no net magnetization, and ferromagnetism, such as the ability to use spin currents. These behaviors were once thought to be mutually exclusive.
In 2022, researchers proposed the term “altermagnets” to label this wily class of materials.
    
    
We believe our results provide robust experimental confirmation that this third phase – altermagnetism – is real.
Associate Scientist at SLAC and Stanford Institute for Materials and Energy Sciences
To explain this behavior, researchers postulated that altermagnets exhibit an alternating spin polarization (up-down-up-down), much like conventional antiferromagnets, but with a crucial twist: the crystal lattice structure surrounding the “up” spins is rotated relative to the lattice surrounding the “down” spins. This geometric twist is a break in symmetry that could give rise to their unusual behavior.
Chirality: the final piece of the puzzle
“By 2024, there were some factors that spoke strongly in favor of altermagnetism being real,” Jost said, “but there was still a missing piece to the puzzle.”
That missing piece was experimental evidence of chirality in an altermagnetic candidate material. Chirality, often referred to as “handedness,” means a material cannot be superimposed on a mirror image of itself, just like a left hand cannot fit into a right-handed glove.
Chirality offers a clever way to distinguish subtle, quantum-level differences between antiferromagnets and suspected altermagnets: While an altermagnetic material would have alternating spins (up-down-up-down) like an antiferromagnet, the geometric twists in its lattice would give the material’s electronic and magnetic structure a distinct handedness that conventional antiferromagnets lack.
Jost’s team chose to investigate chirality in MnTe – a material considered antiferromagnetic for decades until a few years ago when it was suspected to be altermagnetic. The team brought it to the European Synchrotron Radiation Facility (ESRF) in France, to a beamline known for its ability to generate highly controlled polarized X-ray beams.
“Chiral materials scatter left-handed polarized X-ray beams differently than they do right-handed beams,” explained Jost. “Non-chiral materials would scatter each beam of light the same way.”
By scattering circularly polarized X-rays from the material and comparing how it responded to left- versus right-handed light, they teased out a clear chiral signal that shouldn’t exist in a conventional antiferromagnet.
“This chiral signature gave us the solid experimental ground to say altermagnetism stands in a class of its own,” Jost said. The initial experiment was completed in 2024; the team repeated their experiment in early 2025, confirming their earlier results.
“This technique helped us confirm that manganese telluride was really exhibiting altermagnetic properties,” said Rachel Nickel, postdoctoral scientist with ESRF, a co-author who helped design and conduct the experiment on the ID32 beamline. “Altermagnetism was only proposed a few years ago, and the rapid progress in confirming these models is incredible.”
Altermagnetism in the real world
This discovery has big implications for the field of spintronics, where researchers are leveraging the polarized spin of magnetic materials to design next-generation electronics.
Where traditional computing technologies use only an electron’s charge to store and move information, spintronics takes advantage of both an electron’s charge and its spin, giving researchers an additional control knob. Spintronic devices have the potential to be faster, be more robust and significantly decrease power consumption in computing and data centers.
Researchers have had some success with spintronic devices, but that success has been limited by the nature of ferro- and antiferromagnetism.
“Ferromagnets are too easily influenced by magnetic fields,” Jost explained. “If you have a permanent magnet in your pocket and walk by a spintronic device built with ferromagnets, you could start flipping the bits, scrambling the information stored there. Antiferromagnets fix that problem, but they are practically invisible – so you can’t easily read the data stored in them or use them to drive efficient spin currents.”
“Altermagnets combine the best of both,” Jost said. “They have zero net magnetization, so they're stable, but they still have the capability to generate spin-polarized currents needed to easily extract information. That gives you an additional degree of freedom to work with.”
Future research will explore how altermagnetic states can be influenced and controlled using light and electricity to enhance their usefulness in spintronic solutions for quantum technologies, memory devices and data centers.
This research is supported in part by the DOE Office of Science.
Citation: D. Jost et al., Science, 29 October 2026, 10.1126/science.adv3196.
For media inquiries, please contact media@slac.stanford.edu. For other questions or comments, contact SLAC Strategic Communications & External Affairs at communications@slac.stanford.edu.
About SLAC
SLAC National Accelerator Laboratory explores how the universe works at the biggest, smallest and fastest scales and invents powerful tools used by researchers around the globe. As world leaders in ultrafast science and bold explorers of the physics of the universe, we forge new ground in understanding our origins and building a healthier and more sustainable future. Our discovery and innovation help develop new materials and chemical processes and open unprecedented views of the cosmos and life’s most delicate machinery. Building on more than 60 years of visionary research, we help shape the future by advancing areas such as quantum technology, scientific computing and the development of next-generation accelerators.
SLAC is operated by Stanford University for the U.S. Department of Energy’s Office of Science. The Office of Science is the single largest supporter of basic research in the physical sciences in the United States and is working to address some of the most pressing challenges of our time.

Can’t fish Ethernet? Use “invisible” fiber instead
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-02
21:42:18 UTC
06:42:18 KST

My family owns a NYC townhouse, and with that I had Cat6 cables put in on each floor.
However, more recently, one of the Cat6 cables has gone “bad,” meaning it only does 100 Mbps. While we are nowhere close to Gigabit speeds, very less multi-Gig (despite me having had symmetric Gigabit in the past), it’s still not something I’m happy about.
What do you mean only 100 Mbps? My switch and AP support 2.5 Gbps.
The most common approaches in my scenario are:
- Fish a new cable
- Abandon the cable and use wireless mesh
However, there are a few issues:
- The cables were fished in a funky way, and considering how clumsy I am I don’t want to cause more damage
- Our walls are thick, meaning wireless mesh would suck
When I did use mesh on HPE AP25s (the APs I use), the performance was 120 Mbps at two prior homes, that with thin walls and fiber. I also want VLAN segmentation so a typical consumer Eero or Orbi won’t work.
So what did I do? “Invisible” fiber between two floors.
In case you didn’t know, there are special types of fiber cables originally designed for apartment retrofits. This is to avoid landlord/HOA complaints. Our prior place had Verizon Fios via these cables in the hallway.
But these cables are also sold on Amazon and AliExpress for people who’re in rentals or don’t want to fish cables.
So how did I do it?
Galore, the endpoints of my 20m/60ft ‘fiber in the home’ loop.
Switch
While there are SFP+ switches, I already had an unused MikroTik CRS304-4XG-IN, so I just used it here. It’s technically copper 10G, but I’d lose more by trading it for a SFP+ switch so I’ll just keep it. Not to mention copper SFP+s get hot.
Media Converters and Transceivers
I used two media converters and a pair of bidirectional SC SFP+s.
The SFP+s use the same SC connector used in PON networks, but otherwise use Active Ethernet. I’m not sure if they’re using IEEE standards or are proprietary on the glass, but I’ll assume the latter.
While there’s copper on the media converters, the other option would be to replace our switch which I’ve mentioned is not the best idea.
The Fiber
While the fiber can (and is designed to) be superglued, I chose to use the included adhesive instead. Why? In case I ever have to remove it, which has happened before.
That’s the same reason why I did the basement AP25 using command strips when everyone else just drills the mount.
While our fiber is UPC, our transceivers support both SC fiber types.
Conclusion
If I look at the Instant On portal, I’m posting this from my MacBook connected to the new ‘fiber in the home’ loop (via an HPE AP25).
While we currently lack fiber-to-the-home (Mamdani, please fix Fios rights of way!), I’m still happy I could DIY a fix for a Cat6 cable with bad pairs.
In fact, while I don’t have multi-Gig I could still get a respectable ~300 Mbps:
So yes, even if you don’t have fiber to the home, fiber is still useful in a home network. As it saved me from having to hire a contractor and possibly tear our walls. Not to mention a $1500 Samsung TV is on the wall the “defective” Cat6 cable is behind.

BYD sold more EVs last month than the US did all quarter, topping 300,000
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-02
21:38:49 UTC
06:38:49 KST

BYD sold over 300,000 electric vehicles in October, a new record and more than the US managed in the entire third quarter.
BYD October EV sales reach a record 300,000+
The world’s leading EV maker is showing no signs of slowing down. BYD sold 490,168 new energy vehicles (NEVs) in October, up 11% from the prior year and the company’s highest monthly total in 2026.
BYD’s battery electric vehicles drove the growth, with a record 305,743 units sold, up 37% year over year and marking the first time its EV sales have crossed the 300,000 mark.
To put that into perspective, just over 262,500 EVs were sold in the US in the entire third quarter combined, according to Kelley Blue Book estimates.
October marked BYD’s sixth straight month of higher sales growth and its third straight month of higher EV sales. Through October, BYD sold 1,953,700 electric vehicles, up 6% from the same period last year.
BYD’s all-electric vehicles (EVs) accounted for about 63% of total sales last month. Plug-in hybrid vehicles, meanwhile, fell 17% in October to 177,382 units. Through October, BYD’s PHEV sales fell 11% to 1,625,969 units.
According to BYD’s filing, the company exported 170,194 vehicles in October as it expands overseas to drive growth in markets like Europe, Southeast Asia, and Central and South America.
Through October, BYD exported over 1.5 million electric and plug-in hybrid vehicles, nearly double compared to last year.
By the end of 2026, BYD expects overseas shipments to reach about 2 million vehicles. In 2027, that number is expected to rise to more than 2.5 million vehicles.
Top comment by KingFisher
For Musk to back the Largest Criminal Government in US History is mystifying.
This Pay to Play, the US has never seen the scope of this criminality.
What crime did Musk commit?
The in your face disrespect for the law, and constitution are shocking.
With several new and updated vehicles launching with its new Blade Battery 2.0 and Flash Charging technology, BYD aims to continue attracting new buyers with longer range, faster charging, and more tech-oriented models.
Electrek’s Take
While the US is coming off its best quarter of the year in EV sales, BYD’s performance shows how far ahead China is in the transition to electric vehicles.
China continues to lead the global EV market, accounting for over half of all electric cars sold worldwide.
With a wave of low-cost domestic brands flooding China’s market, BYD and other automakers are driving growth overseas in major markets like Europe, South Korea, Southeast Asia, and Central and South America, where legacy OEMs are already falling behind quickly.

The Ford Privacy Promise: You're in Control of Your Data
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-02
21:37:00 UTC
06:37:00 KST

Even for a tech-savvy consumer, it can be hard to understand the relationship between your vehicle, the data it generates, and your privacy and rights. With our connected vehicle and app user communities growing every day, Ford takes its responsibility in safeguarding customer data seriously. That’s why, today, Ford is announcing the Privacy Promise.
Ford is launching the Privacy Promise to make an explicit commitment to customers that their vehicle data is safe with us and they’re in control of what they share. Our newly launched Privacy Promise website provides education on our privacy practices and how to customize your vehicle data and privacy settings anytime in-vehicle or via the Ford App.
Our Privacy Promise
We know that trust is earned. Protecting your personal information is one way we intend to earn it every day. Learn about our privacy practices by reading our Privacy Notices.
- Your Privacy Is Our Priority. We design and build our products with privacy in mind — from collection of data to its use and storage and even disposal. This can include privacy-enhancing technologies like anonymizing or aggregating data to further protect your privacy.
- Straight Talk About Your Data. We strive to provide clear and easy-to-understand information about how we use your personal information. This empowers you to navigate your privacy choices with confidence. Once you make those choices, we respect your decision.
- You Drive Your Privacy Choices. We understand the trust you place in us every time you drive our vehicles and are dedicated to protecting your privacy. We believe using vehicle data to raise the bar on safety and quality is the right thing to do. We offer vehicle privacy settings that allow you to control what data you want to share, subject to the features and services you wish to enjoy. We also never sell data about how you drive your vehicle to data brokers.
Research Insights
As we created the Privacy Promise, Ford wanted to learn more about what customers understood about vehicle and data privacy.
We surveyed just over 1,000 U.S. consumers — both Ford and non-Ford owners. Our customers tell us that vehicle privacy is important to them.
As much as they value privacy, we also learned that customers understand the value of data sharing. A value our survey respondents kept returning to was control: the ability to adjust their settings on demand and in detail.
Ford created the Privacy Promise to help drive awareness of a customer’s responsibility and choices when it comes to their data.
Survey respondents listed automakers as more trusted stewards of privacy than many other large organizations that handle data.
Ford customers, in particular, showed more comfort than non-Ford customers with tech-forward, connected solutions like smart home devices.
How Can I See What Data I Share with Ford?
To learn more about the Privacy Promise, how to customize your vehicle settings, and connected vehicle data, visit our new website.
Kristin Jones is Ford's Chief Privacy Officer and Deputy General Counsel, Data & Privacy.

Meta will end Quest 2 OS updates in 2027
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-02
21:04:20 UTC
06:04:20 KST

Meta is planning to release the last OS update for its Quest 2 and Quest Pro VR headsets by March 2027, the company announced today. The last OS update for those devices is planned to be v213, and after that, Meta will continue to ship security patches and critical bug fixes through the end of 2027. Apps that support the Quest 2 or Quest Pro can also be submitted through December 2027, Meta says.
Meta will end Quest 2 OS updates in 2027
The Quest 2 and Pro were discontinued in 2024, and next year, Meta will stop releasing OS updates for the VR headsets.
Meta is calling the Quest 3 and 3S “now the baseline for Meta VR gamers.” The company is announcing this shift ahead of the planned release of its new VR Glasses, which are set to launch sometime in spring 2027.
“Quest 2 and Quest Pro laid the foundation for this platform, and millions of people still play on them,” Meta says. “Quest 3 and Quest 3S give your apps more performance headroom and mixed reality built in, and apps built for them carry forward to Meta VR Glasses, which run on the same OS and SDKs.”
Meta launched the Quest 2 in 2020 and the Quest Pro in 2022. It discontinued both products in 2024.

Jeffrey Gibson’s ‘A Real Love Thing’ Surveys a Decade of the Artist’s Multidisciplinary Practice
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-02
20:54:53 UTC
05:54:53 KST

Jeffrey Gibson’s ‘A Real Love Thing’ Surveys a Decade of the Artist’s Multidisciplinary Practice
Through abstract, prismatic acrylic paintings to garment-like sculptures and textile collages, Jeffrey Gibson explores questions of identity, lineage, and cultural translation. Working across media, the artist intricately beads monumental works and incorporates a wide range of fabrics and materials.
Jeffrey Gibson: A Real Love Thing at Johns Hopkins University Bloomberg Center delves into the cross-disciplinary breadth of the artist’s practice throughout the past decade. Fifteen works amplify his interest in pattern, queer iconography, and his Indigenous Mississippi Choctaw and Cherokee heritage. Drawing on the early and continuing influence of music, the artist often integrates song lyrics into tapestry-like wall hangings and three-dimensional works.
Music also inspired the title of the exhibition, which Gibson drew from a 1971 interview with soul artist Marvin Gaye, who described his seminal album What’s Going On by saying, “I think I’ve got a real love thing going. I love people, I love life, and I love nature, and I can’t see why other people can’t be like that.”
Reflecting this sentiment, Gibson’s approach is a luminous celebration of Indigenous symbolism and traditional materials, such as jingle cones used in ceremonial garments, tipi poles, and glass beads that have coated bags, moccasins, and other apparel hand-crafted by Native Americans throughout the centuries. The artist also posits a future full of hope, joy, acceptance, and, like Gaye, a love-above-all ethos.
A Real Love Thing continues through March 13 at the Bloomberg Center in Washington, D.C., where Gibson will present a talk as part of the esteemed Sam Gilliam Lecture series on November 12. And if you’re near Eugene, Oregon, the artist’s traveling solo exhibition They Teach Love is also on view through through March 7 at the University of Oregon.

Valve "played capitalism so well they broke it" and now Steam can't be replaced
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-02
20:42:36 UTC
05:42:36 KST

For the quickest way to join, simply enter your email below and get access. We will send a confirmation and sign you up to our newsletter to keep you updated on all your gaming news.
Join the GamesRadar community for quick access. Enter your email below and we'll send confirmation, and sign you up to our newsletter.
By submitting your information, you confirm you are aged 16 or over,
have read our
Privacy Policy
and agree to the
Terms & Conditions. Geographical rules apply.
A former Epic Games developer is calling out Steam users, saying that they wouldn't move on from Valve's store even if a better alternative emerged.
Kiaran Ritchie, former principal animation engineer at Epic Games kicked off discussions surrounding Steam's 30% sales cut last week (with the assertion that "you could vibe code a Steam alternative in a week"). Which in-turn led noted Steam hater Epic CEO Tim Sweeney to assert that "Valve has held gaming back for years" – because the person whose company proudly asserted it had"vibe coded the Epic Games Launcher" knows what's holding gaming back!
Ritchie elaborated on their opinions on the 30% cut in a separate post, with the post saying that while developers are paying 30% in order to access Steam infrastructure, "in reality it is purely to get access to the massive Steam userbase." He adds "PC developers have no option but to distribute through Steam or lose the vast majority of sales."
As for that 30%, he says that it's "siphoned out of the PC games industry into a black hole," arguing that "Valve could easily lower the distribution fee while maintaining healthy margins." He later gives "Kudos" to Valve, saying "They played Capitalism so well they broke it."
And while a point can certainly be made that Steam is generally a better platform than the alternatives, and that it has the most to offer players in its library, Ritchie argues that it doesn't really matter, claiming "no avid Steam user wants other stores to succeed."
"Even if an alternative were objectively better, by whatever metric you care about, it would still be of no interest to Steam users," Ritchie says, adding, "Competition in this space is not only uninteresting to most Steam users, it's actively denigrated!" He then says that the Steam users "the very ones maintaining this unbreakable stalemate, would benefit the most from a better financed game development ecosystem." He concludes, "I don't think this is fixable. It's just sad."
Personally, I don't think Steam needs to charge as much either, but Ritchie hits the nail on the head, that it's "to get access to the massive Steam userbase." Because unfortunately, losing 30% to Valve is probably better than joining the list of games that flopped on PC because they were Epic Games Store exclusives.
Sign up to the GamesRadar+ Newsletter
Weekly digests, tales from the communities you love, and more
Emmanuel Rosier, director of market intelligence at research and analyst firm Newzoo told GamesRadar+ that "As long as Valve is independent and not a publicly traded company," it looks impossible for Steam to be overthrown as the de-facto PC gaming marketplace.
Scott has been freelancing for over four years across a number of different gaming publications, first appearing on GamesRadar+ in 2024. He has also written for the likes of PC Gamer, Eurogamer, VG247, Play, TechRadar, and others. He's typically rambling about Metal Gear Solid, God Hand, or any other PS2-era titles that rarely (if ever) get sequels.

One of Google’s Android partners turned 1.3 million set-top boxes into a botnet
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-02
20:37:49 UTC
05:37:49 KST

Google says one of its Android manufacturing partners secretly built a backdoor in 1.3 million devices and turned them into a botnet. The company says it has now dismantled the backdoor and “cut ties” with the manufacturer, the Shenzhen, China-based Videostrong Technology Co., which has been banned from building or selling certified Android devices or supplying software for them.
One of Google’s Android partners turned 1.3 million set-top boxes into a botnet
Google says a manufacturer ‘intentionally’ hijacked its devices with malware.
The infected devices were used without owners’ knowledge for “malicious activity,” like ad fraud, data exfiltration, and routing unauthorized network traffic. Google claims the “backdoor and botnet were intentionally developed, deployed, and operated for financial gain,” saying:
Videostrong monetized the backdoor by selling access to criminal affiliates and loading those affiliates’ malware modules onto infected devices. These secondary modules were conducting ad-fraud; silently enrolling infected devices into residential proxy networks; and loading additional malware onto XCPE-compromised devices. We also took action to disrupt the command and control (C2) domains that operate these affiliate modules. Because these affiliates each have diversified distribution channels for their modules beyond XCPE, we assess that taking down their C2 domains will sever access to at least 10 million additional compromised devices.
It wasn’t just one device that was impacted, either — the malware was found “built directly into many different device models and brands over multiple years.” Most of those were Android TV set-top boxes, but the botnet also infected other “Android-based IoT devices, including telehealth devices, karaoke machines, and digital signage devices.”
Google says Videostrong used “obfuscation techniques” to load the malware onto users’ devices after they booted up the hardware for the first time. Google also says it made changes to its review process for device manufacturers after this incident.

BMW is taking on Tesla’s FSD with urban driver assist starting in 2027
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-02
20:04:22 UTC
05:04:22 KST

BMW says that it will expand its navigation-guided, partially automated driving capabilities into urban environments starting in 2027, launching first in Germany with other markets to follow.
BMW is taking on Tesla’s FSD with urban driver assist starting in 2027
BMW will bring navigation-guided city driving to select models via software updates in 2027, taking on Tesla’s FSD while facing growing scrutiny over Level 2 driver safety.
The new system can help steer, speed up, and slow down the vehicle on both highways and city streets using its built-in GPS navigation. While driving in town, it can assist with changing lanes, making turns, yielding to other cars, and driving through roundabouts. But as a Level 2 driver assist system, it still requires drivers to keep their eyes on the road and stay ready to take over at any moment. On the highway, BMW will allow drivers to remove their hands from the steering wheel — but on local roads, they’ll need to keep their hands on the wheel.
BMW is the latest automaker to commit to releasing a partially autonomous city driving feature to its customers, as the industry races to keep up with Tesla and its Full Self-Driving (FSD) system. BMW, in particular, recently introduced its next-generation Neue Klasse platform, which includes a driver assist system partly powered by Qualcomm’s technology. And with hands-free highway driving now widely available in many mass-market vehicles, the next frontier is a safe and capable city driving feature that rivals FSD.
BMW says its system works cooperatively with drivers rather than fighting their control, which means they can steer, brake, or speed up at any point to adjust their driving without shutting the system off. At the same time, the car shows what it sees and what the assistance system is doing right in your line of sight through the heads-up display and in a clear 3D map on the main dashboard screen.
The necessary cameras and sensors already come built into models like BMW’s 3 Series, 7 Series, X5, iX3, and iX4. BMW can activate the feature through an over-the-air software update. The system follows new European safety rules that let cars suggest and make automated driving moves on city streets for the first time. BMW says that outside experts have already road-tested the setup and confirmed it meets the technical standards, with final official government approval expected in early 2027.
With more cars equipped with partial automation hitting the road, safety researchers are growing increasingly worried about driver attention and the potential for these systems to cause crashes. There is compelling evidence that, regardless of the company, most driver-assist systems are less safe than normal human driving. Drivers tend to develop an overreliance on these systems to drive the car for them — even after a short period of use. And when it’s time to take back control of the vehicle, their reaction times are slower than what’s considered safe.
BMW is entering risky territory with the adoption of a Level 2 driving feature. Much like Tesla, the company is emphasizing that drivers remain responsible for the vehicle even while using the partially autonomous system in urban environments. Tesla does the same for its FSD feature, but some customers have successfully argued in court that the company bears some responsibility for crashes when they occur.

OpenAI Might Rebrand Its Data Centers As 'AI Compute Campuses' (techcrunch.com) 38

The Big Idea: Nancy Kress
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-02
19:47:20 UTC
04:47:20 KST

The Big Idea: Nancy Kress
Posted on November 2, 2026 Posted by Athena Scalzi Leave a Comment
Spooky season may be over, but you can still be in the spirit with author Nancy Kress’s newest novel, The Queen’s Witch. Take a peek through the veil of magic and into the Big Idea to see some historical truths about these “witches.”
NANCY KRESS:
Forget Halloween witches, pock-marked and flying on broomsticks. Forget Shakespeare’s weird sisters, boiling and toiling and bubbling. Forget even modern Wicca. Think pagan worshippers who throughout medieval and Renaissance eras performed both healing with plants and minor magic—or so everyone believed. In England, they were called “cunning women,” and they predated Christianity. They also co-existed with it, especially in East Anglia and the North. Until Henry VIII decided otherwise, England was Catholic, and Catholicism was already comfortable with mysteries, miracles, and special people set aside from the ordinary. Even today in England you can see old churches whose beams are still carved with both angels and pagan symbols like the Green Man or the dagaz rune.
Cunning women persisted for centuries. 1863, The Prince of Wales, who would become Edward VII, bought Sandringham in Norfolk and evicted all its tenants—except one. The prince’s agent believed her to be a witch and was too frightened to evict her. When the prince fell ill, she was one of the healers summoned to cure him. And as late as 1968, a Norfolk newspaper reported that the local weekly livestock sale sold more than cows; people came to consult “white witches.”
Anne Boleyn, Henry VIII’s second queen (of six; he was hard on wives) has always fascinated me—a woman who gambled everything and lost. Many in Tudor England believed Queen Anne to be a witch. After her beheading, a strange legend grew up around her: that her body was taken from its hasty grave in unhallowed ground inside the Tower of London and was moved to the Church of St. Peter and St. Paul at Salle, in Norfolk. Anne’s ancestors were buried there, and she had been baptized in that church. We know that her cousin Thomas Wyatt, the poet, was in love with Anne, and he later wrote she had received “sacred burial…in consecrated ground.” Not in the Tower of London.
Anne was not a cunning woman. To tell her story, and that of the Sisterhood of witches, I invented my protagonist Alysoun Rowe. Alysoun’s paths and Anne’s become inextricably tangled together, an uneasy but necessary alliance. They need each other to achieve their very different goals. That alliance ultimately affects not only the two women but also the other huge forces sweeping through England: Lutherism, arriving from the continent. Catholicism as Henry VIII tries to redefine it. Papism, of old-style Catholics still loyal to the pope. Also, unknown to those other three groups, a secret Sisterhood of witches who manipulate events behind the scenes to try to stave off the mass persecutions they fear are coming.
Witches are also symbols of something else: female power. There existed in Tudor England some cunning men, but not many and with lesser abilities than the women. The Physicians and Surgeons Act of 1512 was passed to restrict the practice of medicine to only those licensed by the government. The licensees were all men. The Act was in part an effort to keep healing arts away from cunning women, who “boldly and consistently take upon them great cures and things of great difficulty in which they partly use sorcery.” Women are dangerous.
In addition to Alysoun Rowe and Anne Boleyn, this novel let me write about a vivid ruler who is egotistical, vengeful, allows no challenge to his power, and seeks to remake the foundations of the nation he rules. Witches are not the only type of people whose existence persists.
The Queen’s Witch: Amazon

Germany says lawmakers’ iPhones may have been hacked with spyware
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-02
19:45:54 UTC
04:45:54 KST

The German government is warning politicians and public figures that they were targeted by government-backed spyware, days after Apple began sending out notifications warning victims that their iPhones may have been hacked.
Germany’s federal cybersecurity office, known as the BSI, said in a note over the weekend that it had become aware that members of the Bundestag, Germany’s parliament, and other unnamed notable individuals had received warnings from Apple that they may have been targeted by cyberattacks.
Per the Financial Times, German government spokesperson Stefan Kornelius said the government in Berlin was “taking the incident and the warning very seriously,” but he did not say who in the federal government had been targeted, including whether German chancellor Friedrich Merz was among them.
Apple periodically sends out threat notifications to people it thinks may have been targeted by spyware, which can steal messages, location data, and other sensitive information from their phones. This spyware often relies on zero-click exploits, which can hack individuals almost invisibly as they don’t require a victim to click on a link or open a file.
Apple does not attribute attacks to specific governments or say whether someone’s phone has actually been compromised. For that reason, the BSI said that anyone who receives an Apple threat notification “should therefore assume that their device may have been compromised.”
Others outside of Germany also received the recent threat alert from Apple. Radwan Mortada, a Beirut-based filmmaker, said in a post on X that he received a new threat notification warning that Apple had detected “another attack against your device,” after an earlier spyware attack targeted him in 2021.
It’s unclear who is behind this latest round of spyware attacks, or which government may have orchestrated the targeting. Per the notice received by Mortada, Apple said its latest batch of threat notifications went out to users in 99 countries.
The episode highlights again the murky business behind a growing number of attacks against members of civil society. Governments rarely build these tools themselves. Many buy them from private vendors, often called “mercenary spyware” makers, the best known being Israel’s NSO Group, which sells the controversial Pegasus spyware. Germany’s own central federal law agency, the BKA, reportedly bought access to Pegasus in 2019.

====================================================================================================

Oracle files to sell $20B in new stock, bonds to finance AI buildout
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-02
19:27:37 UTC
04:27:37 KST

Oracle filed plans on Monday to issue new stock and more debt to finance its artificial intelligence buildout.
The hyperscaler will sell $10 billion in common stock and $10 billion in senior notes, according to filings with the Securities and Exchange Commission. While the fundraising was already outlined at an investor day last week, shares tumbled 8.8% Monday.
"This seems to be exactly what they previewed," said Rob Oliver, senior research analyst at Baird. "So I'm not sure why the market is freaking about it."
Oracle said it plans to use the proceeds for general corporate purposes, which may include capital expenditures, repayments of debt and future investments.
The hyperscaler's stock initially bounced after last Wednesday's investor day, when Oracle also issued a revenue forecast of at least $225 billion for fiscal 2030. At the time Oracle also said it was limiting its additional capital market funding for the fiscal 2027 to 2030 investment cycle to that $20 billion.
"This is a major relief given concerns about the state of debt markets and the cost of capital (although UBS has argued that these debt market concerns have been over-stated)," UBS analyst Karl Keirstead said in a note on Thursday.
Oracle plans to turn to vendor financing, customer-provided hardware and customer prepayments to help it transition to positive free cash flow, which it expects to reach in the second half of 2028.
Stock and bonds in Oracle, co-founded by former CEO Larry Ellison, have been under pressure amid growing investor concerns about the massive amount of debt being taken on to fund AI infrastructure.
Oracle shares are down 33% in 2026 and 50% over the past 12 months. The yield on its investment-grade 4.95% bond due in 2031 is currently 7.276%.
The sentiment shift is also seen in Oracle's five-year credit default swaps, which are insurance against default. The spread sits at 292, up 265% in one year. That's more in line with a high-end junk bond rating, according to S&P Global Market Intelligence's Gavan Nolan.
While still an investment-grade credit, Oracle was downgraded by S&P to a BBB- in July, one rating level above high yield. It has $125.34 billion in debt, as of Aug. 31, with the bulk in senior notes.
What ultimately matters for Oracle is execution, Pat Nerney, head of investment solutions at Dynasty Financial Partners, said in an email to CNBC Monday.
"Can they do what they say they are going to do? We view this as the narrative across the AI space," he said. "If this gets executed according to plans laid out, they will be rewarded. If they have to come back to market in the back half of '27 after telling everyone this was the last of the capital needed ... look out."
— CNBC's Seema Mody and Ari Levy contributed reporting.

Web host Squarespace lays off 19% of its employees
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-02
19:25:59 UTC
04:25:59 KST

Squarespace, the web company that helps customers build and host their own websites, is laying off about one-fifth of its workforce, in a move that will affect hundreds of jobs.
Jeff Seedman, a public relations specialist representing Squarespace, confirmed the layoffs to TechCrunch. The Verge initially reported the layoffs.
“Squarespace is restructuring its organization and reducing its global workforce by approximately 19%, representing around 300 employees,” said Seedman. “The changes are intended to simplify how we operate, sharpen our priorities and enable the company to move faster as we position Squarespace for its next phase of growth.”
Website building has changed immensely over the last few years as a result of the generative AI boom. “Vibe-coding” website builders, which only require simple prompts from users to spin up functional front-end designs, have largely upended the industry, making it possible for users to create sites with significantly less effort.
These kinds of sites have become an increasingly central part of the website-building business. Last year, web host rival Wix bought Base44 for $80 million. Similar offerings, like Lovable, have generated significant investor interest; Lovable raised a $400 million Series C round in August that valued the company at $13.3 billion. Google also recently launched Stitch, a feature that allows users to swiftly generate user-interface designs.
Squarespace has also begun a big push into AI. In 2024, the company launched an AI tool called Blueprint. The company also recently announced Forma, an upcoming AI native platform, whose waitlist users can join.
Squarespace was founded in 2004 and went public in 2021 at a $10 billion valuation. However, the company struggled in the ensuing years to generate enthusiastic public market interest. Squarespace ultimately decided to go private again by way of a $7 billion private equity deal in 2024.
An earlier version of this report incorrectly stated that Squarespace had not yet launched AI tools; the story has been updated.

The Apple Watch Ultra 4 is $100 off, matching its best price to date

The Apple Watch Ultra 4 is the company’s most capable smartwatch. Whether you intend to put its many tracking features to the test, or you simply want the longest battery life of any Apple Watch, it’s currently on sale for $699.99 ($100 off) in multiple colors at Amazon, matching its all-time low price.
The Apple Watch Ultra 4 is $100 off, matching its best price to date
We also found a good deal on Ugreen’s soccer ball-shaped Bluetooth tracker with seven years of battery life.
Powered by Apple’s new S11 chip (also in the Series 12), the Apple Watch Ultra 4 delivers faster performance than the last-gen version, plus a handful of new Apple Intelligence features. Of note, those include Sound Recognition, which alerts you to noises like alarms and doorbells, and the Live Rewind feature that lets you revisit the last 15 seconds of an in-person conversation. An upcoming Siri Recap feature will be able to create summaries of conversations for later reference.
This model has broader health and fitness capabilities than previous models, too. Its sensors promise more accurate EKG measurements, while the watch can track your heart rate as often as every five seconds. The new Readiness feature (not available on the Ultra 3 and earlier models) also analyzes your recent activity, sleep quality, and other health metrics to generate a score from zero to 10, helping you determine whether to push yourself or take it easy.
On the hardware front, battery life is perhaps the biggest highlight, with Apple promising up to 50 hours on a charge — twice as long as the Series 12. Like its predecessor, this model features Apple’s brightest watch display and a rugged design that’s water-resistant up to 100 meters and IP6X-rated for dust resistance. It also retains conveniences like the second-generation Ultra Wideband chip for Precision Finding, and helpful gesture controls so you can you can perform actions with a double tap or flick of your wrist.
Three more ways to save
- Ugreen’s FineTrack2 Bluetooth tracker is on sale for $13.99 at Amazon, just a few cents shy of its best price to date. The soccer ball-shaped tracker stands out with its playful design, but it also offers some useful features including a non-rechargeable battery can last up to seven years. It works with Apple’s expansive Find My network to help you locate misplaced belongings, while its lanyard makes it easy to attach to your keys, bag, or other items.
- If you’re looking to spruce up your Apple Watch Ultra or Series smartwatch, a variety of Apple’s official bands are on sale for up to 69 percent off at Woot. The silicone Sport Band is down to $14.99 ($35 off), while the soft, breathable Sport Loop is available for $19.99 ($29 off). You can also grab the rugged Trail Loop for $34.99 ($64 off).
- Hoto’s new Pulse 4-in-1 Cordless Staple Gun is on sale for $69.99 ($10 off) at Amazon. The versatile tool can help with repairing stuff, or putting together decorations. It works with four types of staples and nails, letting you switch between them depending on the task, while offering two firing modes. It also features a built-in display that shows battery life and alerts you when you can safely fire the stapler and when it’s time to reload it. The stapler comes with 1,200 fasteners.

New York Times and other outlets sue web browser Brave over alleged content scraping
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-02
19:19:17 UTC
04:19:17 KST

The New York Times sued Brave, the 11-year-old, privacy-focused browser company, on Monday, alleging it scraped the outlet’s copyrighted content and unlawfully distributed it to customers the suit names as Anthropic, Perplexity, Mistral, xAI, and Cohere.
Separately on Monday, the Daily News, the Chicago Tribune, and the Orlando Sentinel filed a similar lawsuit. Both were brought in federal court in Manhattan.
The New York Times, which has also sued OpenAI and Perplexity over similar claims, likens Brave to a “supplier, dealer, and distributor of stolen copyrighted content” for AI companies.
“In this way, Brave acts both like a burglar that misappropriates the content of publishers, and an AI ‘fence’ that sells stolen content to third parties,” the suit reads. It says Brave built an index of more than 30 billion web pages using an unidentified crawler, leaving publishers unable to block it.
The suit also targets a Brave browser feature that bypasses paywalls and the company’s practice of blocking publishers’ ads while selling its own.
Founded in 2015, Brave has raised roughly $250 million to date, according to private-market trackers. The Times seeks an injunction and damages.
Brave did not immediately respond to a request for comment.

Snap settles lawsuit accusing platform of fueling teen depression and eating disorders
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-02
19:11:49 UTC
04:11:49 KST

Snap has reached a settlement in a lawsuit brought by two teenagers who accused the company of designing its platform to be addictive and contributing to their depression, anxiety, and eating disorders, as first reported by Reuters. The plaintiffs had previously reached settlements with Meta, TikTok, and YouTube.
The trial was set to be Snap’s first in a broader wave of litigation over social media’s impact on young users. The company had already settled its other cases scheduled for trial. Snap CEO Evan Spiegel was expected to take the stand.
The plaintiffs said they started using YouTube as young as age 3 and other social media platforms at around age 9. They said they developed compulsive usage habits, which they attributed to features such as autoplay and infinite scrolling.
“The Parties are pleased to have been able to resolve this matter in an amicable manner,” a Snap spokesperson said in a statement to TechCrunch. “We’ve built safeguards, launched safety tutorials, and partnered with experts and because safety is never finished, we remain committed to continually strengthening the safety, privacy, and well-being of our community.”
Social media giants are facing thousands of lawsuits in federal and state courts over claims that they knowingly designed their platforms to hook young users and fuel a youth mental health crisis involving anxiety, depression, and suicide.

Ford promises not to sell your driving habits to data brokers
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-02
19:05:46 UTC
04:05:46 KST

Ford vowed to never sell customer driving data to third-party data brokers as part of a new privacy promise aimed at setting the automaker apart from competitors that have faced legal and regulatory blowback for sharing telematics with data brokers without authorization.
Ford promises not to sell your driving habits to data brokers
Following GM’s $220 million settlement, Ford promises to never sell customer driving data to data brokers, attempting to set itself apart in an industry rife with tracking.
As part of its new promise, Ford says it will strive to protect customer data by using aggregating and anonymizing techniques. It will also “provide clear and easy-to-understand information about how we use your personal information.” And while Ford says it will still use data to improve safety, it will allow customers to adjust how much or how little data they would prefer to share.
In making an explicit pledge not to sell customer data to outside firms, Ford is trying to position itself as one of the better actors in an industry rife with shady practices. “We know that trust is earned,” Kristin Jones, chief privacy officer at Ford, writes in a blog post. “Protecting your personal information is one way we intend to earn it every day.”
“We know that trust is earned.”
— Kristin Jones, Ford’s chief privacy officer
The promise comes in the wake of General Motors agreeing to pay $220 million to settle claims that it illegally sold driver and location information to data brokers. The agreement requires GM to limit the driving data it collects and obtain “express consent” from drivers before collecting their information.
Other automakers have been caught with their hands in the customer data cookie jar. A recent examination of 21 vehicle brands found that every single one transmitted data to at least one third-party domain over Wi-Fi. Over half contacted domains that specialize in advertising, tracking, or analytics (ATA), like Adobe, LexisNexis, and Amplitude, which gather details about your vehicle or driving behavior to sell to insurance companies or target you with ads.
In a recent report, Mozilla crowned modern connected cars “the worst category of products for privacy” ever reviewed. The global nonprofit found that 92 percent of the reviewed automakers provide drivers with little (if any) control over their personal data, with 84 percent sharing user data with outside parties.
Ford said it surveyed over 1,000 US customers, both Ford and non-Ford owners, who agreed that privacy is a top concern while over half of the respondents said they understand that some data sharing is okay. Forty-one percent said they were extremely or very concerned about data privacy in the auto industry, which was less than other groups like social media companies, the government, and financial institutions.
To be sure, Ford’s promise isn’t just words on a screen. In the US, the Federal Trade Commission stops companies from misleading the public through deceptive claims. If Ford publicly promises something like, “We never sell your driving data to brokers” but breaks that promise, the FTC and state attorneys general can step in. They can sue Ford, force the company to sign legally binding agreements to correct the issue, and impose substantial financial fines.
It should be noted that a company’s blog post or public promise is not a legal contract. The actual rules that apply to you are found in Ford’s official privacy policy and connected vehicle terms. Those legal agreements contain standard exceptions that allow Ford to share your data, such as complying with police requests or working with outside service providers. Also, opting out of data sharing does come with tradeoffs. While Ford states that data collection depends on the features you want to use, turning off vehicle tracking or location sharing often disables key features like built-in navigation, remote start, and wireless software updates.

The New York Times Sues Brave Web Browser For AI Scraping, Bypassing Paywalls
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-02
19:00:00 UTC
04:00:00 KST

The New York Times Sues Brave Web Browser For AI Scraping, Bypassing Paywalls 61
			
		 	
				The New York Times and several other publishers have sued (PDF) Brave Software, accusing the browser maker of scraping their websites without permission and helping users bypass paywalls. The Verge reports:  According to a complaint (PDF) filed by the companies that run New York Daily News, the Chicago Tribune, and the Orlando Sentinel, Brave Software has monetized its index of scraped websites by, in part, providing AI companies with paid access to "real-time online content from the Brave Index." The AI companies then use that index to train their models, access content to "respond to specific user queries," or both. Brave has allegedly provided "stolen copyrighted content" to "many" AI companies including Anthropic, Perplexity, Mistral, xAI, and Cohere. Brave also promotes "hundreds of top publishers" as being accessible through its "Paywall blocker" feature in its browser.
"Brave has never asked for the Publishers' permission to access their websites, much less to copy the material and provide it to users or other AI companies," the complaint says. "On the contrary, as noted above, Brave allows its users to bypass the Publishers' paywalls without their consent, and has taken other steps that prevent the Publishers from blocking Brave's access to the Publishers' websites. Brave has never compensated the Publishers for the unlawful use, copying, and distribution of the Publishers' copyrighted content."  "While we believe in the ethical and responsible use and development of AI, Brave has flouted established copyright law and ignored repeated requests by The Times to stop its infringing conduct," Graham James, an NYT spokesperson, says in a statement. "We filed this lawsuit to hold Brave accountable for stealing our content to power its own products and to provide it to some of the most prominent and well resourced generative AI companies in the world."

Anthropic clamps down on Claude’s AI video game clones
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-02
18:56:02 UTC
03:56:02 KST

Anthropic has updated Claude Code’s system prompt to prevent the AI tool from being used to re-create existing video games, as reported by Tom’s Hardware. Retro gaming is currently being overrun with AI-made decompilations, recompilations, and ports, but while Anthropic will still let people use Claude Code to make games, it doesn’t want people to use it for direct clones.
Anthropic clamps down on Claude’s AI video game clones
Anthropic is steering Claude away from re-creating specific titles, but users can still use it to make their own games.
Last week, a user on X posted a screenshot allegedly showing two new sentences added to Claude Code’s system prompt: “Do build original games, games in a genre, and games from the user’s own designs and assets. Don’t build a recreation of a specific existing game, even if it is named or called original.”
Anthropic’s Addy Osmani, in a reply, didn’t confirm that specific language but did say that the company had updated the prompt in Claude Code on Opus 5.5:
We added two sentences to Claude Code’s prompt on Opus 5.5 to steer it away from recreating someone else’s specific game because using Claude to infringe IP is against our usage policy.
Original games, genre games, and games built from your own designs and assets are meant to keep working.
We’ll continue to iterate to ensure we’re striking the right balance. In the meantime, if Claude turns down something that’s yours, send it my way.
We’ve asked Anthropic if it has or will be updating other Claude system prompts as well.

Egoless Mathematics in the AI Era
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-02
18:54:44 UTC
03:54:44 KST

[This is a guest post by Karthik C. S., Amit Sahai, and Jie Xue. This blog post was initially written in a different file format and converted using AI. — T.]
This is a good time for mathematicians to think carefully about the purpose of their work. The development of artificial intelligence brings that question before us with unusual force. Much of the discussion concerns which tasks machines will perform and which abilities will remain distinctively human. But our response also depends on a prior question: what do we understand mathematical work to be, and what gives it its worth? If our answer has become inseparable from the production of results and the demonstration of individual ability, the present uncertainty may reveal a difficulty in our conception of the work itself.
There are broadly two perspectives on mathematics. According to one, we invent it as we construct it: we introduce concepts, choose axioms, and develop theories. According to the other, mathematics exists independently of these activities, and our task is to discover it. These perspectives need not disagree about every aspect of mathematical practice. We plainly develop formalism and arguments. The deeper question is whether the truths we come to understand owe their existence to these acts, or whether our constructions enable us to recognize something that does not depend on us.
While discovery of mathematical truth can sound passive, a paleontologist searching for fossils must decide where to excavate, develop techniques, and recognize the significance of incomplete remains. The fossils existed long before the investigation, but finding them may require extraordinary imagination. Their prior existence gives direction to the investigator’s activity. Similarly, a mathematical discovery may require the invention of an entire language and framework through which a previously unnoticed relationship becomes intelligible. The discovery may require great creativity, but that does not make the truth discovered a creation of the individual. We encounter this distinction whenever our constructions surprise us. A definition introduced for one purpose reveals an unexpected connection elsewhere. A conjecture that seemed to organize our examples admits a counterexample. Two theories developed through different questions turn out to express a common structure. In each case, our thought encounters something it did not freely determine. We can revise our definitions or abandon our conjecture, but we cannot make the original argument valid merely by wishing it so.
The Platonic perspective gives a natural account of this independence. On this view, there were infinitely many primes before anyone proved that fact. Mathematical truth neither waits for our recognition nor receives its authority from it. We may choose how to describe a structure, but we do not choose its properties in the same way. This perspective also changes the place of the mathematician. Our intelligence becomes a means through which an order independent of us can be understood. The significance of the work lies in its relation to that order.
The distinction between a correct derivation and an understanding of its significance is equally important. An axiomatic system permits indefinitely many deductions, but the ability to produce them does not by itself supply a worthy direction of inquiry. Mathematical statements may be formally correct without being enlightening. We can introduce countless definitions and prove countless statements about them. What makes one definition illuminating? Why does one theorem disclose something fundamental while another merely adds to the available material? These questions concern the relation of a particular investigation to the principles it brings into view. Mathematical taste has a place here, provided we understand it as more than individual preference. We value a theory when it reveals a unity among things previously understood separately, or enables us to recognize why a phenomenon occurs. Although mathematical truths may exist independently of us, their discovery can transform our understanding and enrich our experience of the world. The ability to carry out longer chains of reasoning does not by itself explain such understanding. Reasoning serves understanding by articulating relationships and making their justification explicit. However, the success of reasoning does not mean that all knowledge can be reduced to it. Nor does our ability to reason more extensively determine what is worth knowing or why we seek knowledge.
Mathematics is only one field of knowledge. Not everything can be understood mathematically, nor is mathematical achievement the ultimate purpose of intellectual life. Yet we increasingly measure intellectual life through publications, citations, awards, and other indicators of performance. While useful, these measures become problematic when they define the worth of the work itself. We begin to see mathematicians as collections of productive abilities, and mathematics as merely their output. From this perspective, anxiety about being replaced by AI is natural: a machine that performs these tasks better appears to threaten our worth. But the problem began before AI arrived. We had already come to understand ourselves in terms suited to machines. AI merely exposes the consequences of this view.
The response cannot simply be to seek refuge in distinctively human abilities such as creativity, intuition, or taste. If we value these abilities primarily because they give us an advantage over machines, we remain trapped in the same conception of intellectual worth. Why should the value of our pursuit depend on being better at it than someone or something else?
Mathematics offers an opportunity to reconsider this whole orientation. Its truths do not become more true through the distinction of the person who expresses them. In understanding a proof, we bring our thought into conformity with a relationship whose validity does not belong to us. The particular path by which we arrive may bear the marks of our individuality. What is understood is available beyond that individuality. We can allow this experience to loosen the belief that knowledge has its highest value as an achievement attributable to ourselves.
Egoless mathematics begins with taking that possibility seriously. Humility then reaches beyond modesty about our accomplishments or generosity in sharing credit. It concerns the place from which we pursue knowledge. We may seek truth to serve our existing purposes, or allow what we discover to change those purposes themselves. If we approach truth on the condition that its discovery must preserve our importance, we have already made it subordinate to ourselves. Accomplishing this reordering can be demanding, as intellectual sophistication can furnish elaborate defenses of the very attachments that prevent us from understanding. What matters is whether our abilities remain answerable to truth, including when it requires us to reconsider what we have been using those abilities for.
From this perspective, the possibility of mathematical discoveries through AI need not be a source of sadness about our purpose. A truth has become accessible; its worth is not diminished by the absence of our authorship. We need not immediately turn the event into a judgment about our relative importance. The search can continue without requiring every advance to vindicate the seeker. Indeed, the demand for such vindication has always burdened the pursuit, whether the competing claim came from another mathematician or from a machine.
The opportunity before us is therefore more substantial than a rearrangement of mathematical labor. It invites us to reconsider what gives an intellectual life its worth, and to let the pursuit of truth shape not only our work but also how we approach life.
This may sound like asking us all to become saints. Insofar as it asks us to grow in virtue and relinquish our attachment to our own importance, that impression is not far from the truth. But virtue is not something to cultivate for the admiration of others. It is necessary for genuine joy, even in our work. The satisfaction of solving a difficult problem or winning a prize can be fleeting when it rests on our standing relative to others. The joy of understanding is different. Something previously obscure becomes clear, and we recognize a beauty that does not diminish when someone else discovers it first. We can share this joy without depriving anyone of it. Growing in virtue allows us to experience it more fully, free from the need to establish our own importance.
This perspective may also help reconcile two positions within the mathematical community regarding AI. Some are comfortable with or even enthusiastic about using AI, but wish to develop appropriate processes and principles for its use. Others prefer not to use AI at all. Both positions have their merits. Egoless mathematics offers a way of reconciling them by introducing a higher consideration: both approaches can reflect the same underlying commitment to mathematical truth, regardless of how we choose to pursue it. Our aim here is not to settle particular questions of policy, but to develop a higher-level perspective that can guide our deliberations and ultimately our actions.
Seen in this light, we need not be afraid of what AI may achieve in mathematics. Mathematics was beautiful before any of us were born, and it will remain beautiful long after we are gone. Its worth does not depend on our accomplishments, nor does our joy in understanding it have to depend on being the ones who make the discoveries. Our purpose rests on our willingness to let truth govern what we seek and what we become.
We, the authors of this essay, are still far from having attained such egolessness. Nevertheless, we recognize it as an ideal toward which we wish to strive.
We conclude with a thought experiment. Ramanujan is reported to have said, “An equation has no meaning for me unless it expresses a thought of God” (Robert Kanigel, The Man Who Knew Infinity, 1992). If he were alive today, witnessing the advances of AI in mathematics, would he be perturbed by what they might mean for his purpose as a mathematician?
Statement of AI Usage: While the ideas expressed here are entirely our own, we gratefully acknowledge the assistance of GPT Astra 6.0 and GPT 6.1 Sol in drafting this post.
