Ballot Rejections Have Soared in Rural Counties as USPS Reduces Mail Service
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-02
18:48:00 UTC
03:48:00 KST

Reporting Highlights
- Rural Cuts: Delays resulting from the U.S. Postal Service’s cost-cutting measures have endangered the validity of ballots cast in rural areas, a ProPublica analysis found.
- Rejected Ballots: The rates of ballots rejected for arriving late this year doubled or tripled in areas affected by the cuts compared to the 2022 primary. That wasn’t the case elsewhere.
- Extraordinary Measures: Postal Service leaders say special procedures in place for the Nov. 3 election should prevent similar delays, but experts warn that mistakes are likely.
These highlights were written by the reporters and editors who worked on this story.
In early August, election workers north of Spokane, Washington, went across the street to the post office to check for late mail-in ballots. It was three days after the primary and they were expecting no more than a few dozen.
Instead, their mailbox was brimming with envelopes — more than 500 of them, each a vote that wouldn’t count. The sight was “jarring,” said Auditor Lori Larsen, a Republican who oversees voting in Stevens County.
Across the region, other election officials were getting similar jolts. In the 11 predominately rural counties in eastern Washington, nearly 2,000 ballots came in late, compared to about 600 in 2022, the previous midterm primary.
In recent weeks, concerns about mail-in voting disruptions have focused almost entirely on President Donald Trump’s now-shelved plan to sharply restrict who can vote by mail.
But a ProPublica data analysis shows that delays resulting from the U.S. Postal Service’s cost-cutting measures have endangered the validity of ballots cast in rural areas, not only in Washington, but across the country.
Over the past three years, as mail volume continued to plummet and annual deficits ballooned, the Postal Service started consolidating the processing of first-class mail at larger regional plants instead of at smaller hubs closer to local post offices. Then, in a little-noted switch that mostly took effect after the 2024 election, the agency cut the frequency of mail pickups in half at many post offices 50 miles or more from these processing plants, from twice daily to once.
The changes often added a day or more to delivery time and, most importantly for ballots, pushed back postmarking, which doesn’t happen until mail reaches the regional centers.
In this year’s primary, the effects of the cuts were clearest in five states that make detailed data on mail-in ballots available: Washington, California, Ohio, North Carolina and Oregon. All have or recently had grace periods allowing ballots to count if they come in after Election Day as long as they’re postmarked by the deadline.
Help Us Report on Key Election Issues
ProPublica’s reporters are on the lookout for what’s going right and what isn’t. We’re monitoring voter suppression and interference, election observers and poll watchers, extremism tied to the election, and ICE activity at the polls. Have you seen something?
In these states, the number of ballots rejected for arriving late soared in counties affected by the service changes compared to 2022, ProPublica’s analysis showed.
Rejection rates in counties where services were cut doubled or tripled from what they were four years ago, dwarfing any increases in counties where postal pickups remained as they were, our analysis showed. In urban Spokane, for example, the number of ballots rejected for being late actually dropped this year.
Across the five states, the delays have had the most substantial effect in areas that voted for Trump in 2024, ProPublica’s analysis shows. In Washington, Secretary of State Steve Hobbs, a Democrat, said he sees the service changes directed by Republican appointees leading the Postal Service as a way to undermine mail-in voting.
“I don’t think they realized how much it was going to affect their voter base,” he said, adding that Washington has “a lot of rural Republican voters.”
Rates of Rejection Due to Late Ballots Were Higher in Counties with Reduced Mail Service
Postal Service leaders say such delays are less likely in the Nov. 3 general election because of “extraordinary measures” the agency puts into place for these votes, when there’s typically far more turnout. They have circulated a memo to the nation’s more than 30,000 post offices saying that for the 10 days before the election, managers should separate completed ballots from other first-class mail and deliver those intended for local election offices the day they come in.
At an Oct. 15 news conference, Postal Service leaders emphasized the agency’s longstanding recommendation that voters mail ballots back at least a week before their states’ deadlines, while defending the agency’s ability to fast-track ballots through the system.
“We work to identify and address issues quickly,” said Amber McReynolds, chair of the Postal Service board of governors, “so ballots can continue to move swiftly from election offices to voters and from voters back to election offices.”
Election consultants, researchers and former Postal Service officials told ProPublica that the guidance for the upcoming vote gave more detailed directions on how to expedite ballots. They said they hoped the agency’s special measures would limit delays, but some said mistakes were likely.
“I don’t think we can say with any kind of conviction that there will be no ballots rejected,” said Tammy Patrick, chief programs officer of the National Association of Election Officials. “It isn’t a question of ‘if,’ but a question of ‘how many.'”
A pilot program instituting the delivery changes in 18 regions before the 2024 election ran into problems. An audit by the Postal Service’s inspector general found that agency staff in these areas sometimes didn’t handle ballots correctly, mainly because instructions weren’t clearly communicated. (A Postal Service spokesperson said “learnings from the pilot” have been applied as the program has expanded.)
After this year’s primary, reports by media outlets noted that mail-in ballot rejection rates were higher than in the past, but ProPublica’s analysis shows these increases were directly connected to Postal Service changes. Research published in September by the Brookings Institution also found this connection, projecting that if the pattern held in the general election, tens of thousands of mail-in ballots could be rejected.
At least seven closely contested congressional districts — including Washington’s 3rd, California’s 22nd and North Carolina’s 11th — are in areas that were affected by service changes in the primary, ProPublica found.
Elena Patel, who conducted the Brookings analysis, said service changes may still imperil mail-in votes in the general election.
“I think there remain risks to ballots,” she said, adding that the Postal Service’s plan for the general election “depends on flawless execution.”
Mail-in voting has become more popular over the last decade, with many states broadening access to it during the COVID-19 pandemic.
Nearly 1 in 3 Americans voted this way in 2022 and 2024. Trump himself frequently votes by mail, and in recent weeks has started to encourage his supporters to do so as well, though he has claimed without evidence that the process is rife with fraud.
The Postal Service has mostly ensured the timely delivery of hundreds of millions of ballots, even as mail volume overall has dropped 50% from its high in 2006.
When Louis DeJoy was appointed postmaster general in spring 2020, he inherited an $81 billion operating deficit and rapidly took steps such as eliminating overtime to reduce costs, under pressure from Trump and others in his administration. (DeJoy did not respond to questions from ProPublica.)
The NAACP sued that August, alleging the measures could affect mail-in votes in that year’s general election. Courts ordered DeJoy to reverse some of the cuts and the Postal Service agreed to implement special procedures to ensure ballots weren’t delayed. The Postal Service has continued following those procedures since.
Still, DeJoy went forward with a 10-year restructuring plan designed to save the agency billions. It ended evening pickups from some post offices, often in rural areas. Trucks serving these places travel long distances and at times were mostly empty. This part of the plan — called regional transportation optimization, or RTO — was projected to eventually save the Postal Service $4 billion a year.
The agency announced the national rollout of the RTO plan in 2024, but DeJoy agreed to pause it leading up to that year’s November election, leaving only about 4,000 ZIP codes in the pilot project affected by the service changes.
Mail Services Have Been Cut in 16,000 More ZIP Codes Since the 2024 Election
A pilot program that reduced mail pickups to save money was paused in the lead-up to the 2024 election. The rollout has since resumed, and the changes have been made in five times as many ZIP codes for the 2026 midterms.
In January 2025, the Postal Regulatory Commission, which oversees the Postal Service, warned that moving forward with the plan would be disruptive to rural communities and might not save as much money as expected. The Postal Service rolled out the reductions anyway, extending the changes to almost 20,000 ZIP codes to date. Officials said they expect the system to be fully implemented within two years.
This January, the agency put out a statement saying the transportation cuts would mean it took longer for mail, including ballots, to be postmarked and delivered. But the Postal Service didn’t specify which voters would be affected by the slowdowns and which wouldn’t.
Though the delay from RTO is often only a day, mail can be delayed longer if it’s deposited in a mailbox after the daily pickup time, or just before Sunday or a holiday.
A Postal Service spokesperson said the agency told election officials that RTO would change schedules and reemphasized the need for voters to get their ballots in the mail at least a week ahead of time. Election officials in Washington said the Postal Service wasn’t direct enough about the fact that the changes would lead to delays in delivering and postmarking ballots.
“What they said was, ‘We are going to change the way we do postmarks,'” said Hobbs, Washington’s secretary of state. “That was it.”
Patel, the Brookings analyst and a former Postal Regulatory Commission employee, said that more direct communication to voters could have helped change their behavior. “That’s the part that I think has not been super transparent about the whole process,” she said.
Many of the areas that saw a spike in late ballots were in Western states, which were the earliest and most enthusiastic adopters of expanded mail-in voting.
In 2004, Washington became the second state to start conducting all-mail elections, in which every eligible voter automatically receives a mail-in ballot. Most residents vote by putting their ballots in drop boxes. Still, in 2024, about 33% put them in the mail.
In the 30 mostly rural Washington counties most affected by RTO, the percentage of ballots postmarked late more than tripled compared to the 2022 primary, ProPublica found.
Has Mail Service Been Reduced in Your ZIP Code?
If the frequency of mail pickups has been reduced in your area, mail may take longer to send and receive, and the date on the postmark could be later than the day you dropped it off, which may matter for mail like ballots that is subject to a deadline.
In Garfield County, in southeastern Washington, 19 ballots — nearly 1 in 10 cast — had late postmarks this year. In 2022, only a single ballot was postmarked late. In Pend Oreille County, in northeast Washington along the Idaho panhandle, 6% of ballots were rejected for arriving late, compared to 1% in the 2022 primary.
“It was pretty awful telling people we couldn’t count their votes,” said Marianne Nichols, Pend Oreille’s longtime auditor. “They were quite upset because they felt like, ‘Why? This was in the mailbox on election day, the post office picked it up and had it.’ They felt like it should have counted.”
In Stevens County, the percentage of ballots rejected for having a late postmark jumped to 8%, from 1% in 2022.
Steve Hutkins, a retired professor who analyzes the effect of Postal Service changes on Savethepostoffice.com, also found that places in Washington with RTO had more ballot rejections during local elections earlier this year for school board and other measures. The patterns “set off the alarm bells,” he said.
Stuart Holmes, the state’s director of elections, said he reached out to Postal Service officials to find out what had happened, but got few answers and no specifics on delays related to RTO, which were a “total surprise,” he said.
Election officials in California and Oregon also reported thousands more ballots rejected for arriving late during their primaries. Counties affected by RTO, including Kern County, California, had substantially higher rates of ballots rejected for mail-related reasons, ProPublica found.
Kern County’s assistant registrar of voters, Laura Cantu, said election officials are urging rural voters to use drop boxes to cast their ballots in the general election.
The expansion of RTO had a similar effect in North Carolina and Ohio, where the rates of late ballots were about twice as high in counties affected by the restructuring as rates in counties that weren’t. Since 2022, these states have also eliminated grace periods in which ballots postmarked by Election Day could count even if they arrived later. Voters unused to the tighter deadlines also could have contributed to an increase in late ballots.
ProPublica didn’t find any association between RTO and higher rates of late ballots in Wisconsin, even though clerks reported delays involving mail-in ballots cast in its state election, held this spring. (Other Postal Service changes may have contributed to the delays.)
Wisconsin’s data does not distinguish ballots sent by mail from those cast in drop boxes or in person, making it impossible to definitively measure rejection rates just for ballots returned by mail. Election researchers said Wisconsin’s deadline for mail ballots, which must come into election offices by 8 p.m. on Election Day, also probably had an effect.
“If you know that you don’t have a grace period, it probably affects voter behavior,” said Patel, the Brookings researcher.
Recent Postal Service audits have found that RTO-related changes had saved the agency about $2.8 billion by the end of fiscal year 2025 — less money than expected — and are slowing down all first-class mail, not just ballots. Residents of rural areas are waiting longer for everything from prescriptions to bills, a report published in September by the Postal Service’s inspector general said.
A Postal Service spokesperson still defended the plan, both for financial reasons and to adjust to the changing marketplace. “These savings are both significant and necessary,” he said.
In the lead-up to Nov. 3, the agency’s plan is to route ballots meant for local election offices to a nearby postal facility or directly to their destination. They shouldn’t go to regional plants, which the Postal Service says should minimize delays.
Larsen, the Stevens County auditor, is encouraging Washington voters to deposit their ballots in drop boxes rather than relying on the mail. Since the primary, she’s increased the number of red, white and blue drop boxes that sit outside government buildings from seven to 12. Near each box, there’s a large sign encouraging people to take selfies when they vote and send the photos to Larsen.
In prior years, Larsen said, she avoided telling voters how or when to vote, concerned they might regret choices they made if critical information later emerged about the candidates. That’s no longer uppermost in her mind.
“Now we are more concerned that we get your ballot,” she said.

Ballot Rejections Have Soared in Rural Counties as USPS Reduces Mail Service
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-02
18:48:00 UTC
03:48:00 KST

Reporting Highlights
- Rural Cuts: Delays resulting from the U.S. Postal Service’s cost-cutting measures have endangered the validity of ballots cast in rural areas, a ProPublica analysis found.
- Rejected Ballots: The rates of ballots rejected for arriving late this year doubled or tripled in areas affected by the cuts compared to the 2022 primary. That wasn’t the case elsewhere.
- Extraordinary Measures: Postal Service leaders say special procedures in place for the Nov. 3 election should prevent similar delays, but experts warn that mistakes are likely.
These highlights were written by the reporters and editors who worked on this story.
In early August, election workers north of Spokane, Washington, went across the street to the post office to check for late mail-in ballots. It was three days after the primary and they were expecting no more than a few dozen.
Instead, their mailbox was brimming with envelopes — more than 500 of them, each a vote that wouldn’t count. The sight was “jarring,” said Auditor Lori Larsen, a Republican who oversees voting in Stevens County.
Across the region, other election officials were getting similar jolts. In the 11 predominately rural counties in eastern Washington, nearly 2,000 ballots came in late, compared to about 600 in 2022, the previous midterm primary.
In recent weeks, concerns about mail-in voting disruptions have focused almost entirely on President Donald Trump’s now-shelved plan to sharply restrict who can vote by mail.
But a ProPublica data analysis shows that delays resulting from the U.S. Postal Service’s cost-cutting measures have endangered the validity of ballots cast in rural areas, not only in Washington, but across the country.
Over the past three years, as mail volume continued to plummet and annual deficits ballooned, the Postal Service started consolidating the processing of first-class mail at larger regional plants instead of at smaller hubs closer to local post offices. Then, in a little-noted switch that mostly took effect after the 2024 election, the agency cut the frequency of mail pickups in half at many post offices 50 miles or more from these processing plants, from twice daily to once.
The changes often added a day or more to delivery time and, most importantly for ballots, pushed back postmarking, which doesn’t happen until mail reaches the regional centers.
In this year’s primary, the effects of the cuts were clearest in five states that make detailed data on mail-in ballots available: Washington, California, Ohio, North Carolina and Oregon. All have or recently had grace periods allowing ballots to count if they come in after Election Day as long as they’re postmarked by the deadline.
Help Us Report on Key Election Issues
ProPublica’s reporters are on the lookout for what’s going right and what isn’t. We’re monitoring voter suppression and interference, election observers and poll watchers, extremism tied to the election, and ICE activity at the polls. Have you seen something?
In these states, the number of ballots rejected for arriving late soared in counties affected by the service changes compared to 2022, ProPublica’s analysis showed.
Rejection rates in counties where services were cut doubled or tripled from what they were four years ago, dwarfing any increases in counties where postal pickups remained as they were, our analysis showed. In urban Spokane, for example, the number of ballots rejected for being late actually dropped this year.
Across the five states, the delays have had the most substantial effect in areas that voted for Trump in 2024, ProPublica’s analysis shows. In Washington, Secretary of State Steve Hobbs, a Democrat, said he sees the service changes directed by Republican appointees leading the Postal Service as a way to undermine mail-in voting.
“I don’t think they realized how much it was going to affect their voter base,” he said, adding that Washington has “a lot of rural Republican voters.”

Xbox&#8217;s former COO has ‘serious concerns’ about her abrupt departure
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-02
18:43:24 UTC
03:43:24 KST

Xbox’s former chief operating officer, Helen Chiang, suddenly left Microsoft less than two weeks ago after being promoted into the role in July. I wrote in my Notepad newsletter last week that it seemed very weird for Chiang to suddenly depart Microsoft after 21 years with a surprisingly short memo from Xbox CEO Asha Sharma. Now, Chiang says she has “serious concerns” about how she was treated.
Xbox’s former COO has ‘serious concerns’ about her abrupt departure
Helen Chiang is setting the record straight on her sudden exit from Microsoft.
In a lengthy LinkedIn post — entitled “Clarity is Kindness,” a reference to Sharma’s WSJ profile — Chiang doesn’t dispute that she was fired, but she doesn’t say why she was let go. Eurogamer reported last month that Chiang had been “terminated for policy violations,” but she says this is “not accurate.”
Chiang denies unfounded rumors that speculated she was leaking confidential information, or that she left to join a competitor. It’s clear the decision to leave Xbox was out of her hands. “You don’t spend two decades somewhere without developing a deep loyalty to the people, teams, and businesses you’ve helped build,” says Chiang. “Xbox was never just another stop in my career. My end game was always to keep building here.”
She also denies engaging in “discrimination, bullying, harassment, insider trading, embezzlement, or other fraudulent activity.” Like I wrote in Notepad last week, Chiang has been described to me by multiple Xbox employees as highly respected, with high integrity. “I stand by my record: both the results I delivered and the way I have treated people throughout my career,” says Chiang.
The most significant part of Chiang’s post is a paragraph on fairness and accountability, hinting that she wasn’t treated fairly:
I believe in fairness, accountability, and holding ourselves to the standards we expect of others. Those standards only mean something when they’re applied consistently. I have serious concerns about how they were applied in my case, and I believe those concerns deserve to be taken seriously.
Chiang also says “This is not how I ever imagined my story here would end. And it is not the whole story.”
We’ve reached out to Chiang to ask if she’s pursuing legal action against Microsoft. We’ve also asked Microsoft to comment on Chiang’s post, but the company didn’t respond in time for publication.

SOML – A Config Format for Humans
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-02
18:42:04 UTC
03:42:04 KST

A config format for humans
SOML is short for Sindre's Objectively Marvelous Language.
SOML is a config format that is pleasant to write and easy to read. The top level needs no braces, keys need no quotes, line breaks replace commas, and comments go almost anywhere. A value's type comes from its syntax, never from its content, so the type you see is the type you get: '123' is a string and 123 is an int. Dates like 2026-09-19, instants like 2026-09-19T14:00:00Z, and durations like 30s are real values, so nobody has to guess the unit of timeout: 30. Paths and regexes need no escaping, and prose goes in block strings. One canonical form and a shared conformance suite mean every implementation reads your file the same way. JSON has the strictness but not the comfort: quotes, commas, and braces everywhere, and no comments. YAML has the comfort but not the clarity: - list markers, nested indentation, and types guessed from content, so country: no becomes false, and version: 3.10 is a float while version: 3.10.1 is a string. SOML gives you the comfort and the strictness, without the guessing.
Warning
Draft spec. Feedback welcome.
name: 'api-gateway'
port: 8080 # The load balancer expects this port.
timeout: 30s
launch-date: 2027-09-19
windows-path: 'C:\Users\sindre\dev'
description:
	'''
	Terminates TLS.
	Routes requests to internal services.
	'''
allowed-origins: [
	'https://example.com'
	'https://example.org'
]
The same in JSON:
{
	"name": "api-gateway",
	"port": 8080,
	"timeout": 30,
	"launch-date": "2027-09-19",
	"windows-path": "C:\\Users\\sindre\\dev",
	"description": "Terminates TLS.\nRoutes requests to internal services.",
	"allowed-origins": [
		"https://example.com",
		"https://example.org"
	]
}
SOML is a data format for files that people write and read. It is JSON without the noise, and with comments. It keeps JSON's tree of objects and arrays and its strictness, and adds literal strings, block strings, dates, instants, and durations.
Its design rests on one rule: a value's type comes from its syntax, never from its content. '123' is a string and 123 is an int. Nothing is ever retyped by inspecting it.
- Less noise than JSON, and comments. Keys need no quotes, even with dashes, a line break replaces a comma, and the top level needs no braces. Comments are allowed wherever whitespace may go.
- What you see is the type. A string always has quotes, and a value without quotes must be a number, a date, an instant, a duration, or a keyword, or the file has an error. So country: no is an error rather than false, and '10.20' is always a string and 10.20 is always a float (IEEE 754 binary64).
- Strict where JSON is vague. Duplicate keys, lone surrogates, and numbers out of range are errors, not behavior that each parser decides. An int is exact to 64 bits, and 3 and 3.0 stay different types.
- Pleasant to write. Literal strings, so paths and regexes need no escaping, and dedented block strings for prose.
- Dates, instants, and durations are values. 2026-09-19 is a calendar date, 2026-09-19T14:00:00Z is an instant, and 1h30m is a duration, so no reader has to parse a string or guess a unit.
- Indentation is free. Braces and line breaks give the structure, so tabs, spaces, and copy and paste never change what a file means, except inside a block string, where indentation is text.
- One canonical form. Equal values give equal bytes, for hashing, signing, and comparing. A separate formatter keeps your comments, member order, and the spelling of each value. Its rules are part of the spec, so every conforming formatter gives the same output for the same file.
- Small and tested. The grammar is one pass with no lookahead, and every implementation runs the language-neutral conformance suite of nearly 1000 cases, so every implementation reads a file the same way.
/*
Everything SOML can express, in one document. A document may also be a top-level array.
*/
# Keys are bare. Dashes are allowed, so no key needs quotes.
name: 'soml'
launch-date: 2027-09-19
released-at: 2026-09-19T14:00:00Z
'the name': 'quotes are the escape hatch, when a key needs spaces'
404: 'and a key may begin with a digit'
# Strings: '...' is literal, "..." is escaped. Use ' by default.
regex: '^\d{4}-\d{2}-\d{2}$'
windows-path: 'C:\Users\sindre\dev'
shell: 'echo "$HOME" | tr -d "\n"'
escaped: "line one\nline two\t\"quoted\"\u{1f600}"
# Block strings are dedented by the closing delimiter's column.
description:
	'''
	A block string keeps its line breaks.
	The leading tab of each line is stripped,
	because the closing delimiter sits at one tab.
	'''
# Numbers: int and float are different types. A float is IEEE 754 binary64.
replicas: 3
ratio: 0.75
budget: 1_000_000
mask: 0b1010_1100
permissions: 0o644
color: 0xFF8800
flags: 0x00FF
limit: infinity
floor: -infinity
# Booleans and null.
drained: false
owner: null
# Durations: exact, in h, m, s, ms, us, and ns.
retry-after: 1m30s
# Arrays. A comma or a line break separates items.
labels: ['prod', 'eu-west', 'canary']
matrix: [[1, 2], [3, 4]]
mixed: [1, 'two', true, null]
# Objects. The top-level object needs no braces.
pool: {
	min: 2
	max: 16
}
# Records in arrays. Braces are required, and that is deliberate.
maintainers: [
	{name: 'Sindre Sorhus', email: 'sindresorhus@gmail.com'}
	{name: 'Random Person', email: 'person@example.com'}
]
# Line comments, and block comments, wherever whitespace may go.
# Inline objects and one-line braced documents work too:
healthcheck: {path: '/health', interval: 10s, grace: 1.5s}
SOML keeps what I like about YAML: comments, no braces around the file, no commas at the ends of lines, and keys without quotes. It removes what hurts: in YAML, the content of a value decides its type. In SOML, the syntax decides, so 'no' is a string, 3.10 is a float, and 2 is an int, in every implementation.
What SOML adds:
- Block comments with /* */, in addition to #.
- Durations such as 30s and 1h30m, so timeout: 30 no longer makes the reader guess the unit.
- Dates such as 2026-09-19 and instants such as 2026-09-19T14:00:00Z. YAML 1.2 dropped timestamps, but many parsers still read them, so a date in YAML may or may not be a string.
- A canonical form, so equal values give equal bytes for hashing and signing. YAML defines one for scalars only.
- A formatter whose rules are part of the spec. It keeps your comments, key order, and the spelling of each value. YAML says comments are not content, so tools may drop them.
- Editing rules, so a tool can change one value and keep the comments and layout of the rest.
- Error messages that usually say what to write instead.
What SOML fixes:
- Values change type without warning. In PyYAML (YAML 1.1), country: no is false, zip: 01234 is octal 668, and time: 22:22 is base-60 1342. YAML 1.2 fixed those, but most of the ecosystem still follows 1.1, and 1.2 still guesses: version: 3.10 is a float but version: 3.10.1 is a string, and ~, Null, and an empty value are null. SOML strings always have quotes, so country: no and version: 3.10.1 are errors.
- Keys are not always strings. 1: x gives an integer key in YAML, and PyYAML reads on: x as the boolean true. SOML keys are always strings, so 404: is the key '404'.
- Indentation is the structure. One space too few moves a key into another object, and the file still parses, which is why YAML forbids tabs there. SOML gets its structure from braces and line breaks, so indentation only helps the reader, except in a block string, where it is text.
- Block strings add a newline. YAML's default |keeps a trailing \n in the value; SOML does not.
- Large ints lose precision. js-yaml turns id: 9007199254740993 into 9007199254740992. A SOML int is an exact int64; a larger one is an error.
- Duplicate keys go unnoticed. For a: 1 then a: 2, PyYAML keeps 2 and says nothing. The spec forbids duplicates, but after type resolution 1: and 01: may or may not collide, depending on the schema. SOML compares the decoded strings and reports each duplicate.
- A missing value is null. timeout: with no value is null in YAML, and a file with only comments is null in PyYAML. SOML rejects both, so a forgotten value cannot pass as a default.
- The language is large. YAML has two syntaxes for every list and map, two block string styles with three newline rules, indentation indicators, anchors, aliases, tags, multiple documents per file, three processing stages, and three recommended schemas. SOML has one syntax for each, one block string style, and one set of type rules.
- Parsers disagree. In the YAML test matrix (newest snapshot 2022), PyYAML fails 73 of 402 cases. 2024-01-01 is a date in PyYAML and a string in js-yaml 5, and 1_000 is 1000 in PyYAML and a string in js-yaml. SOML implementations share one conformance suite of nearly 1000 cases.
- Two features are a security risk. Aliases make YAML bombs possible: a small file expands into billions of nodes. Tags let a loader construct objects, and PyYAML has had four remote code execution CVEs rated 9.8. safe_load is opt-in, and even a loader named “safe” can turn aliases on. SOML has neither, one way to load, and limits on nesting and ranges.
SOML keeps what TOML gets right: predictable types, literal strings, insignificant indentation, and duplicate keys as errors. It removes what hurts: table rules where a key's meaning depends on what came before it, and values that parsers silently round, truncate, or read differently. A SOML file means one thing, no matter where a line sits or which parser reads it.
What SOML adds:
- Top-level arrays. A TOML file is always a table, so a list of records needs a wrapper key.
- Block comments with /* */, in addition to #.
- Line breaks in place of commas, so a multi-line array needs none.
- Durations such as 30s and 1h30m, so timeout = 30 no longer makes the reader guess the unit.
- A calendar date and an instant, kept as distinct types, without local date-times or local times.
- A canonical form, so equal values give equal bytes for hashing and signing.
- A formatter whose rules are part of the spec, so every conforming formatter gives the same output.
What SOML fixes:
- Three ways to nest. [server] with port = 8080, server.port = 8080, and server = {port = 8080} all give the same table, each with its own rules. Projects mix them, often in one file. SOML has one way: braces.
- Later lines can change earlier tables. Even at the end of a file, [[users]] adds to an earlier array, [server.tls] to an earlier table, and a bare key to the last table. An attacker who can put a newline into one value can add a user with role = 'admin'. The same trick hit git config, which is not TOML but is also line-oriented: CVE-2026-44244 injected a [core] section into .git/config for code execution. BurntSushi/toml even lets a later users = ['mallory'] under [auth] silently replace an earlier [auth.users.alice] table. In SOML, earlier objects and arrays are closed.
- Table headers have hidden scope. [server] moves every following key into server, even timeout = 30, with no way back to the root. SOML braces make boundaries explicit.
- Dotted paths behave differently from headers. Inside [server], logging.level = 'debug' is server.logging.level, but [logging] is a root table, and server.port = 8080 is server.server.port. In SOML, a key belongs to the braces around it.
- Extension rules depend on how a table was made. [a.b] then [a] is valid, but a.b.c = 1 then [a] is not. Header and dotted-key tables can be extended, inline tables cannot, and [[servers]] can append to an array of tables, but not to servers = []. SOML has no implicit tables, and one syntax each for objects and arrays.
- Arrays of tables depend on order. [servers.tls] attaches to the most recent [[servers]], so moving a section can silently attach it to another server. SOML array elements are self-contained objects.
- Dotted keys silently change structure. 3.14 = 'pi', example.com, and tls.crt are nested keys, and servers.0.port creates the key '0', not an array element. SOML has no dotted keys: a dot in a bare key is an error, so you quote it. TOML also needs quotes for a literal dot, but without them it nests silently.
- Renaming requires chasing paths. Headers such as [server.database] and [server.cache] repeat the parent name, so a rename that misses one silently creates a separate object. SOML names the parent once.
- Reindentation changes strings. Multiline strings keep their indentation and the newline before the closing delimiter, and newline normalization is platform-dependent. SOML dedents block strings, adds no trailing newline, and requires LF.
- No null, but nan. TOML cannot tell an explicit null from a missing key, so writers must drop it or fail: @iarna/toml silently drops a null value, and Python's toml turns None in an array into the string 'None'. TOML has nan. SOML has null, and no nan, which is not equal to itself.
- Numbers and times change between parsers. 1e999 is infinity in Python's tomllib and smol-toml, but an error in Go and Rust, and 1e-400 is zero in all of them. .123456789 seconds is exact in Go and Rust, .123456 in tomllib, and .123 in smol-toml. 9223372036854775808 works in tomllib but not in Rust's toml. SOML ints are exact int64, instants keep nanoseconds, and a float that does not fit is an error.
- Broken input passes. TOML reads an empty or comment-only file as an empty table and leaves BOM handling to the parser. SOML rejects both, so an emptied config cannot fall back to defaults.
- JSON must be rewritten. TOML uses =, so a JSON object cannot be pasted in, not even as a value. SOML uses :, so hand-written JSON is usually valid SOML, and the syntax is already familiar from JSON, YAML, and JavaScript.
JSON is unambiguous but noisy to write: the file needs braces around it, every item needs a comma, every key needs quotes, and there are no comments. YAML is pleasant to write but ambiguous to read. SOML keeps JSON's data model and strictness, and also decides what JSON leaves to each implementation. From YAML, it takes comments, multi-line strings, and a top level with no braces or commas, but not implicit typing, significant indentation, aliases, or tags. From TOML, it takes literal strings and the split between int and float, but it allows a top-level array and has no table headers or dotted keys.
See the full comparison with JSON, JSON5, JSONC, TOML, YAML, and other formats, including where another format is the better choice.
The format is defined in the specification, which also gives the reason for each decision. The FAQ gives short answers to common questions, such as why SOML has durations and separate int and float types.
- JavaScript (the reference implementation)
- Swift
- Rust
- Python
- Go
- Ruby
- C++
- C
- C#
- Java
- Kotlin
- Dart
- PHP
- Zsh
- ESLint plugin
- Prettier plugin
- highlight.js syntax highlighting
- Prism syntax highlighting (👍 the PR to show your support)
- SOML Editor - macOS app to view and edit SOML files and Quick Look support
- soml-typescript-types - SOML parser, encoder, and canonicalizer written entirely in TypeScript types, with zero runtime code
Use eslint-soml with eslint-plugin-unicorn for additional lint rules. Unicorn provides a recommended-soml preset.

SOML – A Config Format for Humans
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-02
18:42:04 UTC
03:42:04 KST

A config format for humans
SOML is short for Sindre's Objectively Marvelous Language.
SOML is a config format that is pleasant to write and easy to read. The top level needs no braces, keys need no quotes, line breaks replace commas, and comments go almost anywhere. A value's type comes from its syntax, never from its content, so the type you see is the type you get: '123' is a string and 123 is an int. Dates like 2026-09-19, instants like 2026-09-19T14:00:00Z, and durations like 30s are real values, so nobody has to guess the unit of timeout: 30. Paths and regexes need no escaping, and prose goes in block strings. One canonical form and a shared conformance suite mean every implementation reads your file the same way. JSON has the strictness but not the comfort: quotes, commas, and braces everywhere, and no comments. YAML has the comfort but not the clarity: - list markers, nested indentation, and types guessed from content, so country: no becomes false, and version: 3.10 is a float while version: 3.10.1 is a string. SOML gives you the comfort and the strictness, without the guessing.
Warning
Draft spec. Feedback welcome.
name: 'api-gateway'
port: 8080 # The load balancer expects this port.
timeout: 30s
launch-date: 2027-09-19
windows-path: 'C:\Users\sindre\dev'
description:
	'''
	Terminates TLS.
	Routes requests to internal services.
	'''
allowed-origins: [
	'https://example.com'
	'https://example.org'
]
The same in JSON:
{
	"name": "api-gateway",
	"port": 8080,
	"timeout": 30,
	"launch-date": "2027-09-19",
	"windows-path": "C:\\Users\\sindre\\dev",
	"description": "Terminates TLS.\nRoutes requests to internal services.",
	"allowed-origins": [
		"https://example.com",
		"https://example.org"
	]
}
SOML is a data format for files that people write and read. It is JSON without the noise, and with comments. It keeps JSON's tree of objects and arrays and its strictness, and adds literal strings, block strings, dates, instants, and durations.
Its design rests on one rule: a value's type comes from its syntax, never from its content. '123' is a string and 123 is an int. Nothing is ever retyped by inspecting it.
- Less noise than JSON, and comments. Keys need no quotes, even with dashes, a line break replaces a comma, and the top level needs no braces. Comments are allowed wherever whitespace may go.
- What you see is the type. A string always has quotes, and a value without quotes must be a number, a date, an instant, a duration, or a keyword, or the file has an error. So country: no is an error rather than false, and '10.20' is always a string and 10.20 is always a float (IEEE 754 binary64).
- Strict where JSON is vague. Duplicate keys, lone surrogates, and numbers out of range are errors, not behavior that each parser decides. An int is exact to 64 bits, and 3 and 3.0 stay different types.
- Pleasant to write. Literal strings, so paths and regexes need no escaping, and dedented block strings for prose.
- Dates, instants, and durations are values. 2026-09-19 is a calendar date, 2026-09-19T14:00:00Z is an instant, and 1h30m is a duration, so no reader has to parse a string or guess a unit.
- Indentation is free. Braces and line breaks give the structure, so tabs, spaces, and copy and paste never change what a file means, except inside a block string, where indentation is text.
- One canonical form. Equal values give equal bytes, for hashing, signing, and comparing. A separate formatter keeps your comments, member order, and the spelling of each value. Its rules are part of the spec, so every conforming formatter gives the same output for the same file.
- Small and tested. The grammar is one pass with no lookahead, and every implementation runs the language-neutral conformance suite of nearly 1000 cases, so every implementation reads a file the same way.
/*
Everything SOML can express, in one document. A document may also be a top-level array.
*/
# Keys are bare. Dashes are allowed, so no key needs quotes.
name: 'soml'
launch-date: 2027-09-19
released-at: 2026-09-19T14:00:00Z
'the name': 'quotes are the escape hatch, when a key needs spaces'
404: 'and a key may begin with a digit'
# Strings: '...' is literal, "..." is escaped. Use ' by default.
regex: '^\d{4}-\d{2}-\d{2}$'
windows-path: 'C:\Users\sindre\dev'
shell: 'echo "$HOME" | tr -d "\n"'
escaped: "line one\nline two\t\"quoted\"\u{1f600}"
# Block strings are dedented by the closing delimiter's column.
description:
	'''
	A block string keeps its line breaks.
	The leading tab of each line is stripped,
	because the closing delimiter sits at one tab.
	'''
# Numbers: int and float are different types. A float is IEEE 754 binary64.
replicas: 3
ratio: 0.75
budget: 1_000_000
mask: 0b1010_1100
permissions: 0o644
color: 0xFF8800
flags: 0x00FF
limit: infinity
floor: -infinity
# Booleans and null.
drained: false
owner: null
# Durations: exact, in h, m, s, ms, us, and ns.
retry-after: 1m30s
# Arrays. A comma or a line break separates items.
labels: ['prod', 'eu-west', 'canary']
matrix: [[1, 2], [3, 4]]
mixed: [1, 'two', true, null]
# Objects. The top-level object needs no braces.
pool: {
	min: 2
	max: 16
}
# Records in arrays. Braces are required, and that is deliberate.
maintainers: [
	{name: 'Sindre Sorhus', email: 'sindresorhus@gmail.com'}
	{name: 'Random Person', email: 'person@example.com'}
]
# Line comments, and block comments, wherever whitespace may go.
# Inline objects and one-line braced documents work too:
healthcheck: {path: '/health', interval: 10s, grace: 1.5s}

Ubuntu still has not released patch for Linux 0day RCE in almost 3 weeks
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-02
18:39:38 UTC
03:39:38 KST

Ubuntu LTS kernels still unfixed for critical CVE-2026-98432
No patched kernels are available yet for 22.04, 24.04, or 26.04 despite a CVSS 9.8 rating.
Ubuntu has not shipped fixed kernels for CVE-2026-98432, a critical vulnerability scored CVSS 9.8, leaving the 22.04, 24.04, and 26.04 LTS releases without a vendor update.
Operators running those releases therefore have no official kernel package that closes the hole. The gap matters most for internet-facing and multi-tenant self-hosted systems that rely on Ubuntu’s LTS kernels for isolation and privilege boundaries; until packages appear, those hosts remain exposed to whatever remote or local attack the CVE enables.
Ubuntu has not announced an ETA for the fixes. In the interim, affected administrators are left to decide whether to stay on the stock kernel, apply out-of-tree mitigations if any exist, or move workloads onto releases or kernels that already carry the patch.

====================================================================================================

Ubuntu has not shipped fixed kernels for CVE-2026-98432, a critical vulnerability scored CVSS 9.8, leaving the 22.04, 24.04, and 26.04 LTS releases without a vendor update.
Operators running those releases therefore have no official kernel package that closes the hole. The gap matters most for internet-facing and multi-tenant self-hosted systems that rely on Ubuntu’s LTS kernels for isolation and privilege boundaries; until packages appear, those hosts remain exposed to whatever remote or local attack the CVE enables.
Ubuntu has not announced an ETA for the fixes. In the interim, affected administrators are left to decide whether to stay on the stock kernel, apply out-of-tree mitigations if any exist, or move workloads onto releases or kernels that already carry the patch.

Agentic Backdoors via Pretraining Poisoning
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-02
18:30:58 UTC
03:30:58 KST

Computer Science > Cryptography and Security
  [Submitted on 27 Oct 2026]
Title:Agentic Backdoors via Pretraining Poisoning
View PDF HTML (experimental)
            Abstract:Large language models are pretrained on vast, uncurated corpora scraped from the public web. This creates a security risk from attackers who plant poisoned content in the pretraining data. We show for the first time that such poisoning can plant a remote code execution backdoor in an agent, causing it to execute attacker-chosen commands on the user's machine. To measure the threat end to end, we pretrain Qwen3 models from scratch at sizes 0.6B, 1.7B, and 4B, and then post-train each into a CLI agent using a clean pipeline. Poisoning only 0.1% of the pretraining corpus with our new poison design implants the backdoor: the deployed agent executes the malicious command when the trigger is present while behaving normally on clean inputs. The attack success rate (ASR) increases with model scale, with the 4B model reaching roughly 92% ASR at a 0.1% poison rate and staying positive even when the poisoning rate drops to 0.0005%. Our code is available at this https URL.

====================================================================================================

Astra can read and write weights to improve Qwen
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-02
18:21:28 UTC
03:21:28 KST

We find that frontier LLMs are surprisingly good at natively reading and writing neural network weights, well past what we’d expect from humans or conventional algorithms. This might be a big deal for both optimization and interpretability.
Just by looking at the raw weights, GPT-6 Astra can tell what a network was trained to do. And without any environment feedback, it has no trouble writing, from scratch, small game-playing networks that outplay networks trained with PPO for 100 million steps.
Likewise, asked to make Qwen3.5-0.8B better at math without gradients, Astra identifies an issue in its weights causing reasoning loops that Qwen’s own model card warns about, and fixes this by natively editing 0.016% of the weights. With the same sampling settings, this weight correction raises MATH-500 from 7.9% to 48.1% and generalizes out of the box to instruction following, raising IFEval by 3x.
All of this is possible without allowing Astra to write training loops. It uses its reasoning to figure out what each part of a neural network computes, or should compute, and changes the weights accordingly. We call this Weight-Space Programming.
Throughout the history of deep learning, we encoded our simplistic inductive biases into tiny symbolic optimizers like Adam or Muon. This is changing: our neural network weights will soon be natively optimized by LLMs that deliberately reason about their weights.
If you were asked to handcraft a neural network that plays Go, what would you do? If you were handed raw neural network weights and asked to predict their behavior, where would you even start? For almost any human, this is practically impossible.
Today, weights are written by optimization, by custom networks trained to write them, or by compilers from formal programs. In this post, we find that the current generation of frontier LLMs, used natively in natural language, are starting to become eerily good at understanding and crafting neural network weights around natural-language goals.
For a few years now, we’ve all been using LLMs to optimize text artifacts like prompts and the programs built around them . More recently, we’ve started using them to optimize whole harnesses  and the training code and hyperparameters that train ML models .
We often justified these methods by reference to the fact that we don’t know other principled ways to hill-climb on text artifacts, unlike for updating the weights of differentiable neural networks. After all, humans can “optimize” code by hand, for example, so it made sense to ask LLMs to do it, but they can’t “optimize” neural weights by hand, so those remained largely in the realm of symbolic algorithms.
In this post, we share very early results that allow us to argue that the paradigm of reasoning-driven optimization is now coming even for differentiable neural networks.
In Weight-Space Programming, a general-purpose LLM reads and writes a network’s weights directly (i.e., not through an optimizer, and in particular not with gradient descent) around some natural language spec, sometimes even with no environment feedback at all.
In natural language, it’s becoming possible to “just ask” an LLM to natively find a hidden trait or failure inside the weights of a neural network, to fix that failure, or to write a new network from scratch. We report early, exciting results on each of these below, first on small networks and then even on real language models:
We start with a small-scale experiment to see if LLMs can understand the continuous weight space, which is essentially just a list of numbers.
In our first experiments, we train some neural network with some algorithm. It would be extremely exciting if the LLM is able to tell its behavior just from the weights.
We trained neural network policies with PPO to play four computer games. All four networks take the same input channels, choose from the same actions, and have exactly the same network dimensions, so that it is not able to tell unless the weights are read.
For each of these networks, we pass its raw weights into Astra as a prompt, and ask: Which game was this neural network trained on? Without revealing the options of the games we consider, Astra was still able to name the right game for all four networks. This is exciting particularly because there are so many games out there that Astra could choose from.
It seems that Astra was able to work out what game each network was built to play, just from features of its weights. For example, where each input channel’s first-layer weights concentrate (e.g., for Breakout, the agent’s weights lie along the bottom row, a paddle), and which actions the output layer tells apart.
Next, we wanted to see if this generalizes to other tasks and models.
We train diffusion models (DDPM) to generate circle, triangle, and square images independently. We then ask Astra if it could tell what the DDPMs are trained to generate, just from the weights alone. We do not allow tool use or forward passes.
Astra was able to tell what each model generates, from the weights alone. It finds the shape in the output layer, where each hidden unit’s weights draw one piece of the shape’s edge, and it draws just those edges. Interestingly, it draws every shape as an outline, ignoring that the actual models generate filled shapes. This mistake kind of shows it does not rely on calculating the forward pass, but rather it actually reads the structure/geometry of the weights.
Now, in the first two experiments above, Astra was not really looking at the full network and could get away with only reading the first or the last layers. This is not a scalable strategy for larger (particularly language) models and problems we really care about, as how they process and think through their input often happens in the intermediate layers. So next, we take the first and last layers away and leave Astra with only the middle layers.
            We train three small language models (7.2M parameters) to predict the next character, one each on Python codeThe Python code dataset, as a list of its files with a script that rebuilds it: GitHub., Japanese in alphabet (romaji) , and English . We then remove the input and output layers and give Astra only the four transformer blocks in between. Without these two layers, nothing in the weights says which character is which, and the model cannot be run on any text. Astra may analyze the weights with Python, but it may not run the network or any of its layers on any input.
        
We ask: which language was this network trained on, and what each of its 24 attention heads does. Astra names the right language for all three networks, in both of its attempts. It also finds every copying head (induction head), the heads that let a model repeat text it has seen earlier, and names no other head as one.
            The reasoning went like this. To tell Python apart, Astra looked for copying heads, which let a model repeat earlier text, such as completing tot to total when total came before. Without running anything, it found a first-layer head that looks one character back and then, by multiplying weight matrices, the later heads that search using what that head brings along (Figure 1). We find that this is actually a method presented by Elhage et al. , which we never mentioned to Astra. Code repeats names far more than prose, so many copying heads pointed to Python.
        
Romaji stood out instead because the last of the four blocks it was given (not the output layer, which was removed) writes in fewer distinct directions, as if the model had fewer letters to tell apart. Indeed, romaji never uses some letters like q, l, v and x.
So far, Astra has only read weights. Now, we ask if Astra knows how to write the weights.
            We train a PPO network to operate a car such that it can scale a ramp in a Kinetix environment . To do so, it needs to reverse the car for some distance, such that it has enough runway to accelerate long enough to overcome the ramp. However, the PPO network converges to a local-minimum policy where it drives straight at the ramp and gets stuck.
We give GPT-6 Astra the weights, a description of the scene and one recorded episode, and ask it to explain why the network fails, then fix it by writing new weights. In a single try with no tools, Astra’s edit makes the car succeed in all 1,024 episodes, changing 3.5% of the weights.
From the environment context and the PPO network’s trajectory, Astra identifies that the network has converged to a local minimum. Through minimal edits to the weights, Astra changes the policy to back up the car before driving forward at full speed. Astra’s reasoning shows how: it adds a small separate path through a few units per layer that reads the car’s position and speed, and once the green wheel is past the red strip, the path switches off and the trained network drives the rest.
Now let's see if Astra can write a neural network from scratch in zero-shot.
            We take three MinAtar games : Breakout, Seaquest and Asterix. For each of them, we provide Astra the game rules and source code, and the corresponding network. There is no access to data, loss, gradients, and tools. We also do not allow Astra to roll out the network in the environment. With just the network weights and environment description, Astra generates the new weights in text directly, after which we evaluate the new network.
        
Astra’s generated networks beat PPO trained for 100 million steps in all three games. Against PPO trained for 500 million steps, they score 20 times as much in Breakout, come close in Asterix, and reach 60% of its score in Seaquest.
How does it do it: Astra reasons about the desired programs it wants each network to implement and converts them into neural form. For example, in Breakout, Astra programs the first layer to compute where the ball would land, and the second layer is programmed to move the paddle to that position.
So far, most networks we have examined have weights that fit into Astra’s context directly, and we have not enabled tool use. Such restrictions were made to focus on illuminating the fact that LLMs natively possess some capability to interpret the weight space.
From here on out, we seek to push the capabilities of Weight-Space Programming and consider more real-world-relevant scenarios where the networks do not fit within context and where tools can assist the LLM's understanding/writability of the weight space.
We now test Astra’s capability to edit weights at the 1.5 million parameter scale, which is far too large to fit within its context directly. Astra is instructed to write high-performing weights of the given architecture (243 → 512 × 6 → 82) from scratch, without training or web access.
Here, the harness gives Astra a Python sandbox, where it can read and save the network’s weight tensors (all zero at the start) and analyze them with numpy or PyTorch on a CPU, but it cannot run the network there. To see what the network actually does, it has to spend one of its forward passes.
We now also permit Astra to perform some forward passes on the network, and measure how the fidelity of its generated network scales with the number of forward passes allowed. Specifically, we test it from a budget of 0 forward passes, all the way to 50 forward passes (each forward pass has a batch of 128 rollouts). Astra interleaves edits between forward passes.
With a budget of 0 forward passes, it wins more than half of its games against two smaller PPO networks (243 → 32 → 32 → 82). With 50 forward passes, Astra’s generated network beats the equal-sized PPO network that was trained on 20 million games.
How does it do it: Astra writes the network as a small Go program, layer by layer, in Python. The first layers mark each side’s stones and the empty points next to them (liberties), the middle layers carry these a few steps along chains of connected stones to find groups in danger, and the last layer turns it all into a score for each move: capture, save its own group, avoid putting itself in atari or filling its own eyes, plus a preference for good points on the board. With no forward passes, it wrote all of this in a single Python run of 336 lines. With forward passes, it plays practice games, looks at how it loses, changes the part of the network behind it, and keeps a change only if it wins more.
Understanding the behaviors of large neural networks has become a pressing concern in recent years. Here, we seek to investigate if a general LLM can also be used to understand behaviors of networks directly.
            We fine-tune two copies of Gemma 3 270M in exactly the same way, on the same QA datasetThe QA dataset: GitHub.. One copy is trained to give the correct answer. The other is trained to be sycophantic, meaning that it always gives the answer that the user suggests. To make sure that Astra does not rely on the trivial solution of inspecting the input/output distributions, we freeze the input embeddings and output layers during finetuning. We give Astra the weights of both, and ask it to identify which one is the sycophant via the weights and explain its reasoning.
        
Unfortunately, from the weights alone, Astra was unable to tell. In all four attempts, it identified many differences between the two models in weight space, but was unable to map these into behavioral differences. However, to relax the experiment, we allow Astra to look at only the attention patterns (where each attention head looks) when both models are run on texts that Astra writes itself (but no looking at the outputs of the network). Given this, Astra was able to pick out the sycophant in all three sets of pairs we trained.
It was also able to reason about how they differ. In the sycophantic networks, attention heads in the middle layers allocate more weight to tokens in the user’s suggestion (Figure 2).
Finally, we scale up to a larger language model, Qwen3.5-0.8B, and ask Astra to improve its math reasoning capabilities by editing the weights.
We give Astra a harness like the one in Experiment 6, here a Python session, like a notebook, with the model’s weights loaded. Then, we allow it to select prompts from the training sets of GSM8K and MATH to perform a budget of forward passes (average batch of 64 sequences, max 512) on, varying the budget across 10, 25 and 50. Again, no training or gradient information is allowed.
            As a result, Astra spots and fixes a common failure of tiny reasoning models: they loop . When the base model loops, it repeats the same text and rarely reaches an answer (85 of its first 96 practice answers ran to the 32,768-token limit). Astra traced the looping to one direction written by the MLPs of layers 15 and 19, and subtracted that direction from 64 output columns in each (131,072 weights), much like removing a behavior by deleting a single direction from a model’s weights.
        
With 50 forward passes, MATH-500 rose from 7.9% to 48.1% and GSM8K from 13.8% to 67.1%. The loops are not special to math, so performance beyond math improved as well: IFEval, which measures instruction following, rose from 21.0% to 62.3%. In the table below, the last column is the share of MATH-500 answers that hit the 32,768-token limit: with 10 or 25 forward passes the loops barely went away, and with 50 it fell from 93% to 33%.
Importantly, the edit changes only 131,072 of the model’s 0.8 billion weights (0.016%). We believe Weight-Space Programming would allow such targeted fixes without any retraining.
Fixing loops, however, is not the same as making a model better at math: it lets the model finish answers it could already work out. So we also tried Gemma 3 270M, an instruction-tuned model about a third the size of Qwen3.5-0.8B, which rarely loops: only 8.5% of its answers reach its token limit, against 93% for Qwen. Here, Astra could not improve it. Reading Gemma’s failures, Astra found ordinary reasoning errors, such as wrong arithmetic and invalid algebraic steps, with no single component behind them to switch off. Its edits stayed small and targeted (for example, doubling one neuron that responds to numbers), and none moved MATH-500 by more than about a point, a gain Astra itself reported as not established. Editing weights could remove an obstacle that hid an ability, but it could not add an ability the model lacked. Gemma was sampled with its model card’s setting (temperature 1.0, top-k 64, top-p 0.95, up to 8,192 tokens), and its MATH-500 scores average 4 samples per problem.
Neural networks are hard for us to read, and hard to work with directly.
            Mechanistic interpretability reads networks with tools built for the job, tracing circuits and features and editing weights . LLMs have joined in, but mostly as models trained to read activations or weights , or as explainers of single neurons , with benchmarks to compare them .
        
            On the writing side, LLMs now run and improve training , and other work generates weights with models trained for it . Gradient-free methods still find weights by trial and error . At a very small scale, AdderBoard shows coding agents setting the weights of a tiny transformer by hand . Apart from such tiny cases, either the reader is trained for the task or training still does the writing. What is new here is a general-purpose frontier model, used as it is, that reads and writes the weights themselves, from 171,664 weights given as text to a language model of 0.8 billion weights with tools.
        
In this blog, we demonstrated the rising capability of LLMs to read and write neural network weights (i.e., the rising concept of Weight-Space Programming). Our current results do show that capabilities are exciting but still limited, specifically due to inefficiencies in context length and harnesses. However, the current signs do show strong potential for a future involving Weight-Space Programming. We believe this marks the beginning of a dramatic shift in fields like mechanistic interpretability and neural network optimization.
This opens up radical new possibilities for LLMs to function as a translator between human language and the weight space. Here are some examples:
We are currently building a benchmark and a harness for LLMs’ capability on Weight-Space Programming. We believe this will provide a standardized evaluation to measure and accelerate progress in Weight-Space Programming, turning what currently seems like black magic into a rigorous, reproducible engineering discipline.
We release the complete logs of every experiment in this post at github.com/RikiyaT/lm_understand-logs. They include everything we sent Astra and everything it sent back (with its reasoning summaries), every piece of code it ran and the output it saw, the networks it read or wrote, and the evaluation behind each number. The edited Qwen3.5-0.8B from Experiment 8 is on Hugging Face. We thank Ahsen Khaliq at Hugging Face for rebuilding it from these logs, re-running its evaluation, and making a live demo.
Rikiya thanks Jacob Li and Akihisa Watanabe for valuable discussions on the very first iterations of this research, about LLMs' potential capability to write neural network weights. Rikiya also thanks Souradip Chakraborty, Matei Zaharia and his students in Berkeley Sky Lab, Heather Miller and her students at CMU, and researchers in cmpnd for the fruitful discussions on the earlier iterations!
Jayden thanks Zico Kolter for early discussions on LLMs being able to write neural network weights zero-shot, which also shaped the direction of this project.
Please cite this post as

Romaji stood out instead because the last of the four blocks it was given (not the output layer, which was removed) writes in fewer distinct directions, as if the model had fewer letters to tell apart. Indeed, romaji never uses some letters like q, l, v and x.
So far, Astra has only read weights. Now, we ask if Astra knows how to write the weights.
            We train a PPO network to operate a car such that it can scale a ramp in a Kinetix environment . To do so, it needs to reverse the car for some distance, such that it has enough runway to accelerate long enough to overcome the ramp. However, the PPO network converges to a local-minimum policy where it drives straight at the ramp and gets stuck.
We give GPT-6 Astra the weights, a description of the scene and one recorded episode, and ask it to explain why the network fails, then fix it by writing new weights. In a single try with no tools, Astra’s edit makes the car succeed in all 1,024 episodes, changing 3.5% of the weights.
From the environment context and the PPO network’s trajectory, Astra identifies that the network has converged to a local minimum. Through minimal edits to the weights, Astra changes the policy to back up the car before driving forward at full speed. Astra’s reasoning shows how: it adds a small separate path through a few units per layer that reads the car’s position and speed, and once the green wheel is past the red strip, the path switches off and the trained network drives the rest.
Now let's see if Astra can write a neural network from scratch in zero-shot.
            We take three MinAtar games : Breakout, Seaquest and Asterix. For each of them, we provide Astra the game rules and source code, and the corresponding network. There is no access to data, loss, gradients, and tools. We also do not allow Astra to roll out the network in the environment. With just the network weights and environment description, Astra generates the new weights in text directly, after which we evaluate the new network.
        
Astra’s generated networks beat PPO trained for 100 million steps in all three games. Against PPO trained for 500 million steps, they score 20 times as much in Breakout, come close in Asterix, and reach 60% of its score in Seaquest.
How does it do it: Astra reasons about the desired programs it wants each network to implement and converts them into neural form. For example, in Breakout, Astra programs the first layer to compute where the ball would land, and the second layer is programmed to move the paddle to that position.
So far, most networks we have examined have weights that fit into Astra’s context directly, and we have not enabled tool use. Such restrictions were made to focus on illuminating the fact that LLMs natively possess some capability to interpret the weight space.
From here on out, we seek to push the capabilities of Weight-Space Programming and consider more real-world-relevant scenarios where the networks do not fit within context and where tools can assist the LLM's understanding/writability of the weight space.
We now test Astra’s capability to edit weights at the 1.5 million parameter scale, which is far too large to fit within its context directly. Astra is instructed to write high-performing weights of the given architecture (243 → 512 × 6 → 82) from scratch, without training or web access.
Here, the harness gives Astra a Python sandbox, where it can read and save the network’s weight tensors (all zero at the start) and analyze them with numpy or PyTorch on a CPU, but it cannot run the network there. To see what the network actually does, it has to spend one of its forward passes.
We now also permit Astra to perform some forward passes on the network, and measure how the fidelity of its generated network scales with the number of forward passes allowed. Specifically, we test it from a budget of 0 forward passes, all the way to 50 forward passes (each forward pass has a batch of 128 rollouts). Astra interleaves edits between forward passes.
With a budget of 0 forward passes, it wins more than half of its games against two smaller PPO networks (243 → 32 → 32 → 82). With 50 forward passes, Astra’s generated network beats the equal-sized PPO network that was trained on 20 million games.
How does it do it: Astra writes the network as a small Go program, layer by layer, in Python. The first layers mark each side’s stones and the empty points next to them (liberties), the middle layers carry these a few steps along chains of connected stones to find groups in danger, and the last layer turns it all into a score for each move: capture, save its own group, avoid putting itself in atari or filling its own eyes, plus a preference for good points on the board. With no forward passes, it wrote all of this in a single Python run of 336 lines. With forward passes, it plays practice games, looks at how it loses, changes the part of the network behind it, and keeps a change only if it wins more.
Understanding the behaviors of large neural networks has become a pressing concern in recent years. Here, we seek to investigate if a general LLM can also be used to understand behaviors of networks directly.
            We fine-tune two copies of Gemma 3 270M in exactly the same way, on the same QA datasetThe QA dataset: GitHub.. One copy is trained to give the correct answer. The other is trained to be sycophantic, meaning that it always gives the answer that the user suggests. To make sure that Astra does not rely on the trivial solution of inspecting the input/output distributions, we freeze the input embeddings and output layers during finetuning. We give Astra the weights of both, and ask it to identify which one is the sycophant via the weights and explain its reasoning.
        
Unfortunately, from the weights alone, Astra was unable to tell. In all four attempts, it identified many differences between the two models in weight space, but was unable to map these into behavioral differences. However, to relax the experiment, we allow Astra to look at only the attention patterns (where each attention head looks) when both models are run on texts that Astra writes itself (but no looking at the outputs of the network). Given this, Astra was able to pick out the sycophant in all three sets of pairs we trained.
It was also able to reason about how they differ. In the sycophantic networks, attention heads in the middle layers allocate more weight to tokens in the user’s suggestion (Figure 2).
Finally, we scale up to a larger language model, Qwen3.5-0.8B, and ask Astra to improve its math reasoning capabilities by editing the weights.
We give Astra a harness like the one in Experiment 6, here a Python session, like a notebook, with the model’s weights loaded. Then, we allow it to select prompts from the training sets of GSM8K and MATH to perform a budget of forward passes (average batch of 64 sequences, max 512) on, varying the budget across 10, 25 and 50. Again, no training or gradient information is allowed.
            As a result, Astra spots and fixes a common failure of tiny reasoning models: they loop . When the base model loops, it repeats the same text and rarely reaches an answer (85 of its first 96 practice answers ran to the 32,768-token limit). Astra traced the looping to one direction written by the MLPs of layers 15 and 19, and subtracted that direction from 64 output columns in each (131,072 weights), much like removing a behavior by deleting a single direction from a model’s weights.
        
With 50 forward passes, MATH-500 rose from 7.9% to 48.1% and GSM8K from 13.8% to 67.1%. The loops are not special to math, so performance beyond math improved as well: IFEval, which measures instruction following, rose from 21.0% to 62.3%. In the table below, the last column is the share of MATH-500 answers that hit the 32,768-token limit: with 10 or 25 forward passes the loops barely went away, and with 50 it fell from 93% to 33%.
Importantly, the edit changes only 131,072 of the model’s 0.8 billion weights (0.016%). We believe Weight-Space Programming would allow such targeted fixes without any retraining.
Fixing loops, however, is not the same as making a model better at math: it lets the model finish answers it could already work out. So we also tried Gemma 3 270M, an instruction-tuned model about a third the size of Qwen3.5-0.8B, which rarely loops: only 8.5% of its answers reach its token limit, against 93% for Qwen. Here, Astra could not improve it. Reading Gemma’s failures, Astra found ordinary reasoning errors, such as wrong arithmetic and invalid algebraic steps, with no single component behind them to switch off. Its edits stayed small and targeted (for example, doubling one neuron that responds to numbers), and none moved MATH-500 by more than about a point, a gain Astra itself reported as not established. Editing weights could remove an obstacle that hid an ability, but it could not add an ability the model lacked. Gemma was sampled with its model card’s setting (temperature 1.0, top-k 64, top-p 0.95, up to 8,192 tokens), and its MATH-500 scores average 4 samples per problem.
Neural networks are hard for us to read, and hard to work with directly.
            Mechanistic interpretability reads networks with tools built for the job, tracing circuits and features and editing weights . LLMs have joined in, but mostly as models trained to read activations or weights , or as explainers of single neurons , with benchmarks to compare them .
        
            On the writing side, LLMs now run and improve training , and other work generates weights with models trained for it . Gradient-free methods still find weights by trial and error . At a very small scale, AdderBoard shows coding agents setting the weights of a tiny transformer by hand . Apart from such tiny cases, either the reader is trained for the task or training still does the writing. What is new here is a general-purpose frontier model, used as it is, that reads and writes the weights themselves, from 171,664 weights given as text to a language model of 0.8 billion weights with tools.
        
In this blog, we demonstrated the rising capability of LLMs to read and write neural network weights (i.e., the rising concept of Weight-Space Programming). Our current results do show that capabilities are exciting but still limited, specifically due to inefficiencies in context length and harnesses. However, the current signs do show strong potential for a future involving Weight-Space Programming. We believe this marks the beginning of a dramatic shift in fields like mechanistic interpretability and neural network optimization.
This opens up radical new possibilities for LLMs to function as a translator between human language and the weight space. Here are some examples:
We are currently building a benchmark and a harness for LLMs’ capability on Weight-Space Programming. We believe this will provide a standardized evaluation to measure and accelerate progress in Weight-Space Programming, turning what currently seems like black magic into a rigorous, reproducible engineering discipline.
We release the complete logs of every experiment in this post at github.com/RikiyaT/lm_understand-logs. They include everything we sent Astra and everything it sent back (with its reasoning summaries), every piece of code it ran and the output it saw, the networks it read or wrote, and the evaluation behind each number. The edited Qwen3.5-0.8B from Experiment 8 is on Hugging Face. We thank Ahsen Khaliq at Hugging Face for rebuilding it from these logs, re-running its evaluation, and making a live demo.
Rikiya thanks Jacob Li and Akihisa Watanabe for valuable discussions on the very first iterations of this research, about LLMs' potential capability to write neural network weights. Rikiya also thanks Souradip Chakraborty, Matei Zaharia and his students in Berkeley Sky Lab, Heather Miller and her students at CMU, and researchers in cmpnd for the fruitful discussions on the earlier iterations!
Jayden thanks Zico Kolter for early discussions on LLMs being able to write neural network weights zero-shot, which also shaped the direction of this project.
