Sneaking into the auditorium for the Windows 2000 launch rehearsal
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-02
18:12:48 UTC
03:12:48 KST

The Windows 2000 launch in San Francisco was accompanied by an expo, and a large number of Windows 2000 team members were dispatched to run the various booths and stations, give informal presentations on the new features of Windows 2000, and answer technical questions from the various attendees. And from among the people who were sent to the expo, a smaller group was selected to attend the launch event itself in person.
Related reading: A ticket to the Windows 95 launch.
After a few of us arrived early at the Civic Auditorium for the launch event, we had to entertain ourselves until the doors opened. There was some speculation as to who the celebrity host would be, since it hadn’t been announced. One of my colleagues tried to sneak into what looked like a VIP tent.¹ I, on the other hand, went exploring.
I found an unlocked door that took me into the empty upper level of the main auditorium.
I took a seat and tried not to be noticed.
The stage was set up with a 10-meter tall mock laptop, screens to the left and right of the stage for projecting slides, prerecorded videos, or to get a better look at on-stage close-ups, gray curtains unobtrusively hung to shield from view what would turn out to be various surprises throughout the event, and various other desks, demo areas, and even an airplane cabin set.²
While I sat and took in the complexity of the stage, there was an announcement that they would be rehearsing the opening in one minute. The lights dimmed in preparation. The lid of the giant laptop closed very slowly.
About ten minutes after the announcement, the rehearsal actually began.
The laptop lid opened, and on the laptop screen, it looked like someone was signing into Windows 2000, and then the mouse clicked the Start button. This led to a video montage, and when the montage was over, a voice announced, “Ladies and Gentlemen, please welcome Patrick Stewart!”
Oh my goodness, Patrick Stewart is going to be the presenter for the Windows 2000 launch!
Mere seconds later, onto the stage bounded… a crew member wearing a headset pretending to be Patrick Stewart so that the camera and lighting crew could make sure they were properly positioned.
Okay, that’s the end of the rehearsal. The lights come back on, and the auditorium once again buzzes with the sound of equipment being moved and people discussing things.
I left the auditorium and went back to join the crowd of people waiting to get in. I didn’t tell them who the host was.
The event itself seemed to go off pretty much as planned. With the real Patrick Stewart.
Patrick Stewart had such a commanding presence. If he had come out on stage and announced, “It is the dawn of a new era: The era of lime Jell-O”, people would say, “Yeah, lime Jell-O, I need to upgrade to lime Jell-O.” But instead of trying to spark a new dessert trend, he talked about Windows 2000, reading words from a teleprompter that he almost certainly didn’t understand, but he made them sound great.
¹ My colleague eventually returned with a Commemorative Edition boxed copy of Windows 2000.
² The airplane cabin was used for a sketch that demonstrated mobile business computing. The demoer took their laptop on a plane trip, where they happened to sit next to a character named William Wentworth, portrayed by actor John O’Hurley, president of William Wentworth Worldwide. (He gave his email address as www@www.com.) The sketch demonstrated infrared file sharing and other mobile scenarios. Remember infrared file sharing? Of course you don’t.

Skill to Create Product Videos
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-02
18:10:39 UTC
03:10:39 KST

From product workflow to polished demo.
v1.0.1 release · aaitor/skill-video-studio
Quick start · Worked videos · Usage · Contribute
A local video-production workspace driven by the /devrel-video Claude Code skill. Capture real product interactions with Playwright CLI, compose title cards, chapters, and CTAs with HyperFrames, then master and inspect the result with FFmpeg. Start with a silent walkthrough; add narration, music, subtitles, or your own presenter footage when needed.
A real Claude Code session using the installed project skill: clone the studio, prepare an Acme walkthrough brief, and inspect the three-beat plan on a black background. This short demo stops before capture and rendering; it does not claim a video was produced. The recording guide includes the actual generated brief and reproduction command.
For planning: Git and authenticated Claude Code. Clone the full workspace so its native skill and project layout stay together:
git clone https://github.com/aaitor/skill-video-studio.git
cd skill-video-studio
claude
Ask Claude:
/devrel-video Prepare a 25-second silent walkthrough brief for the bundled
Acme app: show the workspace, filter by category, then open a project.
Reuse brands/acme. Show the plan before capture or rendering.
Expected result: a project brief and a concrete beat plan. For a complete video, install the capture/render prerequisites and follow the worked walkthrough. The production stack needs Node.js 22+, FFmpeg/ffprobe, Chromium, and the helper-specific Python dependencies. Optional narration and avatars require your own additional services or GPU setup.
- Real product capture: browser interaction scripts with explicit beats and stable selectors.
- Branded composition: title, browser frame, chapters, and CTA in a HyperFrames timeline.
- Local mastering and QA: master/web copies, ffprobe checks, contact sheet, and thumbnail.
- Optional audio: six local music styles, narration-driven timing, and voice variants.
- Optional presentation: subtitles, private bring-your-own presenter footage, and terminal/browser sequences.
- Source-based workflow: reusable brands, briefs, templates, and two complete worked examples.
Portrait/social output profiles remain on the roadmap. Full capture/render depends on your local toolchain; offline CI checks sources and selected local utilities, not the complete production stack. See workflow and usage.
Product walkthrough: a local Acme fixture, real browser capture, narration/music, and EN/ES subtitles. Source and commands · Watch with sound.
Composed CLI/browser film: a multi-environment example whose terminal scenes are authored HTML reenactments. Source and explanation · Watch with sound.
The longer examples remain intact. Generated MP4/audio files are ignored; rebuild them from the included sources. See usage prompts for silent, narrated, avatar, voice-swap, and multi-environment requests.
Each video uses projects/<slug>/brief.yaml, brand tokens under brands/<name>/, and generated output under its project folder. Optional private settings live outside Git, selected by DEVREL_VIDEO_ENV.
See configuration, the actual brief template, and security/privacy guidance. Keep private footage and credentials outside this public repository.
Brief → explore → brand → capture → compose → optional layers → render/QA → thumbnail. The skill coordinates existing tools rather than replacing them. Detailed production steps are in the workflow guide and production notes.
.claude/skills/devrel-video/  Skill instructions, scripts, templates, production notes
brands/                     Brand tokens and logos
projects/                   Curated public examples; private projects stay ignored
examples/                   Real harness recording, public input, generated brief
assets/                     Shared banner, social card, and short terminal GIF
docs/                       Onboarding, usage, configuration, troubleshooting
- Installation and production prerequisites
- Configuration
- Prompts and optional layers
- Workflow
- Troubleshooting
- Security, privacy, and media rights
- Banner generation and social preview
- Original references: studio design guide · avatar guide
Use Issues for questions, bugs, and ideas. Read CONTRIBUTING.md and CODE_OF_CONDUCT.md; report vulnerabilities privately through SECURITY.md.
Install requirements-dev.txt in a virtual environment and run python3 scripts/check.py. See CHANGELOG.md for changes. Live recordings, service calls, GPU jobs, and full renders are manual validation steps.
MIT for original studio content. Embedded banner fonts retain their OFL notices; external tools/models/media retain their own terms. See licensing.

OpenAI pitches data center power plants as a way to protect nearby areas
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-02
18:01:30 UTC
03:01:30 KST

In an announcement on Monday, OpenAI says it will pilot its “Grid Protectors Program” at an upcoming data center in Effingham County, Georgia, where the company promises to free up to 1,000 megawatts of power during extreme weather or emergencies, enough to power air conditioners in around 700,000 homes.
OpenAI pitches data center power plants as a way to protect nearby areas
As public opinion has soured on data centers, it’s selling ‘AI campuses’ as protectors, instead of noisy polluters.
The announcement comes as opposition to data centers spreads across the US, in response to massive projects that have begun development and construction, frequently with little or no input from local residents. In Utah, residents protested after Box Elder County commissioners signed off on a data center expected to consume more than double the state’s peak electricity demand in 2025, and spread hot air in the desert valley surrounding the data center.
Now OpenAI’s approach is to pitch the construction of power plants that feed the electricity required by the new AI data centers it’s building as “a resource that utilities can call on when communities need it most.”
Several states like Tennessee, Florida, Oklahoma, Oregon, and others have enacted laws that say they will prevent AI companies from passing energy costs on to residents, in addition to moratoriums and other restrictions. This is besides the environmental impacts, as a recent study by the Houston Advanced Research Center (HARC) said that powering proposed Texas data center projects could undo decades of air quality gains over just five years.
OpenAI, which has switched to calling its AI data centers “compute campuses” in an effort to separate from their poor public reputation, claims it helps reduce costs for customers by operating year-round:
“Electricity demand also rises and falls with the seasons, with different regions facing their biggest peaks at different times,” OpenAI’s announcement says. “Our compute campuses need power year-round. With the right agreements, that steady demand can help spread the grid’s fixed costs across more electricity sales, while flexibility lets us reduce demand when the system is busiest.”
OpenAI says it’s going to continue working with policymakers and utility services to make “arrangements for our future campus to reduce electricity demand when the grid is under strain.” Along with its data center in Georgia, OpenAI is also partnering with Oracle and SoftBank to build its “Stargate” data centers across the US, including in Shackelford County, Texas; Doña Ana County, New Mexico; and Milam County, Texas.

Microsoft's Canada Timezone Troubles Leave Admins Fixing Calendars, Pretending They're In Bogota
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-02
18:00:00 UTC
03:00:00 KST

Microsoft's Canada Timezone Troubles Leave Admins Fixing Calendars, Pretending They're In Bogota (theregister.com) 65
			
		 	
				Microsoft is scrambling to fix time-zone problems across Outlook, Power Automate, Fabric, SharePoint and other products after parts of Canada stopped observing seasonal clock changes on November 1. "The company has confirmed problems across a chunk of its software estate, leaving some customers facing incorrect times, broken schedules and a collection of workarounds while Redmond catches up," reports The Register. From the report:  Outlook users have been left with the most homework. Switching to the new Canadian time zones won't automatically update existing calendar entries, meaning that meetings scheduled after November 1 are potentially an hour out of whack. Microsoft advises users to change their Outlook calendar time zone, then update upcoming events and meetings to use it. For meetings involving other people, only the organizer can make the change.
Recurring meetings come with an additional treat: changing the time zone resets any previously moved occurrences, which then have to be recreated. Anyone with a calendar full of recurring meetings may wish to put the kettle on first. Microsoft is working on an Exchange Online PowerShell cmdlet to remediate non-recurring meetings, but according to its guidance, that's not due until later this week. A fix for recurring meetings remains "in development," with an ETA yet to be confirmed.
Things get stranger with Power Automate and Fabric, where Microsoft's temporary fix involves pretending you're somewhere else entirely. Depending on the product and province, that could mean Saskatchewan, Arizona or, for Manitobans, Bogota. Because apparently time zones weren't confusing enough already. SharePoint On-Prem customers, meanwhile, have to wait until November 10 for the correct time zones to arrive, while Power Pages users could see server-formatted dates appear an hour out until a service fix lands. Microsoft's current milestone for that is December 12.

For the Best Pigs in a Blanket, Wrap Your Sausages in This
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-02
18:00:00 UTC
03:00:00 KST

For the Best Pigs in a Blanket, Wrap Your Sausages in This
I love appetizers, hors d’oeuvres, and cocktail snacks. No party is really complete without a little cheese and crackers, some spinach-artichoke dip, and a bacon-wrapped anything. One of my favorite apps of all time happens to be pigs in a blanket. It’s a small hot dog or sausage wrapped in pastry or crescent dough and baked to perfection. Normally, they’re served with some sort of mustardy dipping sauce.
A couple of months ago I went to Parm in New York City, and we ordered the pigs in a blanket. These weren’t just any pigs in a blanket, though. They had an extra ingredient: salami. And boy, were they delicious! I haven’t been able to get them out of my head since then. Now that we’re deep in football season, I can’t think of a better game-day snack. Here’s how to upgrade your next batch of pigs in a blanket with salami for the ultimate app.
Get the recipe: Pigs in a Blanket
How to Upgrade Pigs in a Blanket with Salami
- Dry off the cocktail sausages. Unwrap your package of mini cocktail sausages and dry the excess liquid off with paper towels.
- Prep the dough. Roll a can of crescent roll dough out flat. Separate the packaging into the pre-cut triangles. To each of these triangles, take a sharp knife or pizza cutter and slice it into three even strips.
- Wrap the sausages. Take a thin slice of salami and wrap it around the cocktail sausage. Then, grab one of the strips of crescent roll dough and wrap it around the salami and sausage. Place on a baking sheet and repeat. Brush all the bites with egg wash and sprinkle with everything bagel seasoning.
- Bake. Place the tray in the oven and bake until the dough is cooked through and golden-brown. Serve with your choice of dipping sauce on the side.
My Honest Review of Pigs in a Blanket with Salami
You might be thinking, “How could pigs in a blanket even get any better?” Let me tell you, my friends — with salami they can. Even though cocktail sausages are already very salty, the salami just kicks that goodness up a notch. This is certainly a treat for all my fellow savory-over-sweet friends.
Experiment with the salami you use to add whatever flavors you think might be missing from this classic appetizer. If you want it spicy, use soppressata. I personally used a slightly smoky salami, which I loved paired with the classic Hillshire Farm Beef Lit’l Smokies. I love a sneakily easy way to make simple appetizers look a little more fancy or unique. This salami trick is one of those. You’re only adding one more ingredient, but that simple addition gives the impression that you know what you are doing. While I just used one slice of salami for every bite, you could use two if you prefer.
And if you haven’t tried topping your pigs in a blanket with a sprinkling of everything bagel seasoning, you need to try it. It adds a slight crunch to every bite, but also packs in so much flavor without you having to do much work. Everything bagel seasoning combines poppy seeds, sesame seeds, garlic, onion, salt, and more, so there’s no need to individually open five or more seasoning bottles. You can just use this one spice blend.
My roommates are also big fans of pigs in a blanket, and they both said these were the best ones they have ever had. I would have to agree! Next football get-together, I know what I’m making.
Get the recipe: Pigs in a Blanket

The New York Times sues Brave web browser for AI scraping and bypassing paywalls
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-02
17:54:47 UTC
02:54:47 KST

The New York Times and a group of other publications have filed lawsuits against the company that makes the Brave web browser over alleged unlawful scraping to train AI and also letting users bypass their paywalls, as reported by Reuters.
The New York Times sues Brave web browser for AI scraping and bypassing paywalls
Brave has allegedly provided ‘stolen copyrighted content’ to many AI companies.
According to a complaint filed by the New York Daily News, the Chicago Tribune, and the Orlando Sentinel, Brave Software has monetized its index of scraped websites by, in part, providing AI companies with paid access to “real-time online content from the Brave Index.” The AI companies then use that index to train their models, access content to “respond to specific user queries,” or both. Brave has allegedly provided “stolen copyrighted content” to “many” AI companies including Anthropic, Perplexity, Mistral, xAI, and Cohere.
Brave also promotes “hundreds of top publishers” as being accessible through its “Paywall blocker” feature in its browser.
“Brave has never asked for the Publishers’ permission to access their websites, much less to copy the material and provide it to users or other AI companies,” the complaint says. “On the contrary, as noted above, Brave allows its users to bypass the Publishers’ paywalls without their consent, and has taken other steps that prevent the Publishers from blocking Brave’s access to the Publishers’ websites. Brave has never compensated the Publishers for the unlawful use, copying, and distribution of the Publishers’ copyrighted content.”
“While we believe in the ethical and responsible use and development of AI, Brave has flouted established copyright law and ignored repeated requests by The Times to stop its infringing conduct,” Graham James, an NYT spokesperson, says in a statement to The Verge. “We filed this lawsuit to hold Brave accountable for stealing our content to power its own products and to provide it to some of the most prominent and well resourced generative AI companies in the world.”
“AI giants are now desperate to avoid getting caught red-handed stealing news from publishers, so Brave Software is selling them a back door to the journalism they know they have no right to,” says Steven Lieberman, the attorney who filed the lawsuit on behalf of the other three publications. “Buying stolen goods secondhand doesn’t make them legitimate. Brave must be held responsible for laundering pirated reporting for the AI industry.”
Brave, Anthropic, Mistral, SpaceXSI, and Cohere didn’t immediately reply to a request for comment. “The New York Times is overpaying its attorneys and underpaying its journalists. (Who could’ve easily identified the disinformation in this suit.)” says Jesse Dwyer, Perplexity’s chief communications officer.
Update, November 2nd: Clarified that one lawsuit was filed by New York Daily News, the Chicago Tribune, and the Orlando Sentinel, not the companies that run them. Also added a statement from the attorney who filed the lawsuit on behalf of those publications.

Readwise takes on Amazon’s Kindle and Goodreads with its new app for book lovers
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-02
17:49:41 UTC
02:49:41 KST

Readwise, the maker of a read-it-later app and digital reading companion that keeps track of your highlights across services, is now taking on Amazon’s e-book empire with its latest app, Readwise Books. The app is an e-book store and helps you track what you’ve read, similar to Amazon’s Goodreads.
The bootstrapped startup described the new app as “a cross between Kindle and the Goodreads apps,” as it offers a single place to buy, read, and track books. However, Readwise Books aims to offer more detailed reading stats than the dates you read a book and whether you finished it. It lets readers visualize richer reading data like words read, pace, depth, immersion, and sessions.
Meanwhile, its new store offers “millions” of e-books from the big five publishers and others, initially in the U.S., with more countries to come. Print books (fulfilled by Bookshop.org’s distributor) and some audiobooks are available as well, the company notes.
The launch comes at a time when there’s been an explosion of new reading apps and book trackers, all hoping to challenge Goodreads by introducing new features like book clubs, widgets, improved discovery features, goal-setting, better lists and organizational tools, habit-tracking, and more. However, few indie developers have gone so far as to launch an alternative to Amazon’s Kindle e-books store, which remains the industry leader, with an 83% share of the market when Kindle+ (formerly Kindle Unlimited) is included in the stats.
One of Kindle’s bigger advantages is that it offers an end-to-end solution for everything related to reading e-books, from the hardware devices to the book-tracking log. Readwise isn’t selling its own e-readers, but it is attempting to create its own community around reading, complete with tools for reading purchased and uploaded e-books, tracking them (like on Goodreads), sharing your reading progress with friends, and more.
It also offers a number of ways to track books outside its ecosystem. This includes a way to track reading sessions by using Kindle’s browser-based Cloud Reader and a feature that lets you snap photos of physical books to mark your progress. Plus, you can upload any DRM-free EPUBs (a common e-book file format without copy protection). The app can read your e-books aloud via text-to-speech.
Readwise believes its more in-depth metrics could be a big selling point, too, likening them to a “Strava for reading” — a reference to the metrics-heavy exercise app.
“The desire to read books, like exercising and eating well, is laden with guilt,” the company explained in a blog post penned by co-founders Daniel Doyon and Tristan Homsi. “Many people feel like they should be reading more, but feeling bad about it tends to change nothing. The session tracking taps into a different psychological mechanism. It makes the reading you’re doing legible and, as the adage goes, that which is measured is improved.”
The company adds that the most consistent feedback from users is that the app has led them to read more without willpower tricks like goal-setting, streaks, or scheduling.
With publishers’ permission, the app will support AI-powered features as well, like session recaps, definitions and term lookups, word and phrase translations, and a location-aware ability to ask questions about the part you’re reading. (So far, only HarperCollins has opted in, though.)
Unlike Goodreads’ locked-down ecosystem, Readwise is embracing open technology. This includes a public API and an MCP server. The latter supports AI agents that could be used to research book recommendations on your behalf, use your Readwise data to create a books page on your personal website, or even wire up your hackable e-reader to log your sessions automatically, depending on how involved your AI use cases may be.
The new Readwise Books app will remain free, with some AI features later going behind a paywall. Its prior apps for reading and tracking highlights are either $7.99 per month each or $9.99 per month for both.
The Books app is currently in beta and requires an invite, unless you’re already a Readwise subscriber.

====================================================================================================

Making Peace with the Projective Plane
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-02
17:47:59 UTC
02:47:59 KST

In my newsletter I give an introduction to the latest few posts and occasionally give some additional commentary. In my latest newsletter I wrote
Projective space and homogeneous coordinates seemed like arbitrary abstractions when I was a student because they were presented without any motivation. Much later I came to realize that they’re natural, almost inevitable. Exceptional cases and hand-wavy references to objects “at infinity” go away when moving to projective space.
My mathematics education was very top-down: abstractions first, specifics later (if ever). I was OK with that for a while, until I hit my abstraction tolerance sometime in grad school. Now I would advocate more of a bottom-up approach, motivating abstractions first rather than presenting them as a fait accompli.
The first time I saw the projective plane was probably in a topology class where someone said you could construct the plane by starting with a square and gluing opposite edges in a physically impossible way. This seemed like a gimmick, a way to construct a non-orientable manifold but not anything practical. Another construction is to say the projective plane is an ordinary sphere with antipodal points identified. I found that a little more satisfying, but it still unmotivated.
What I would have appreciated was someone saying that the projective plane was invented to make geometry more elegant by eliminating exceptional cases and making rigorous the idea of things “at infinity.” For example, geometric calculations often have to treat vertical lines specially, but vertical lines are not special in projective geometry.
You could start by saying we’re going to represent points in the plane not simply as (x, y) but as (x, y, 1) and use (x, y, 0) for the point at infinity in the direction (x, y). That may seem like a hack, but it’s certainly motivated. It’s exactly the sort of thing a graphics programmer would do. In fact, I imagine many programmers have done just that, independently bumping into projective geometry.
After having introduced this extra coordinate, it’s natural to ask what would it mean if the last coordinate were something other than 0 or 1. You’ll quickly run into this when you start doing calculations on these extended coordinates. You could have a rule that you always normalize by dividing by the last coordinate, if it’s not zero, and then you have essentially discovered homogeneous coordinates.
You’ll see something like the approach above in elliptic curve cryptography. Programmers wanting to implement encryption algorithms are thinking concretely in terms of (x, y) points, until they have to worry about a mysterious “point at infinity” and handle it correctly. Then homogenous coordinates appear as a tool for getting out of a bind.
***
I thought about titling this post something like “Dr. Strangelove, or: How I Learned to Stop Worrying and and Love the Projective Plane” but I wasn’t sure how well that would go over. I doubt many readers have seen the 1964 movie this is alluding to, but the trope “How I Learned to Stop Worrying and Love …” is more widely known than the movie itself.

====================================================================================================

Self-Employed? Solopreneur? NIST Has a Cybersecurity Guide Just for You
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-02
17:47:40 UTC
02:47:40 KST

If you work for yourself, as millions of Americans do, protecting your business from cyberattack is one of many responsibilities that falls entirely on your shoulders. Don’t feel daunted: The National Institute of Standards and Technology (NIST) has just released a guide with your needs — and busy life — in mind.
Small Business Cybersecurity: Non-Employer Firms (NIST Cybersecurity White Paper 50) is a lay-language resource offering cybersecurity basics tailored expressly to the “solopreneur” — a term referring to anyone who is a business’s sole paid employee. More than 28 million small businesses in the U.S. fall into the solopreneur category, existing in every industry and forming a substantial part of the economy. The publication is written to introduce solopreneurs to the fundamentals of a small-business cybersecurity program and provide an actionable approach to implementing it.
“Because solopreneurs may not have cybersecurity expertise, we wanted to create a resource designed for them,” said NIST’s Daniel Eliot, one of the publication’s authors. “They can implement many of these actions on their own with limited technical knowledge or with minimal financial investment.”
The publication is an update to what was previously titled Small Business Information Security: The Fundamentals (NISTIR 7621). While that publication was aimed at small businesses of varying sizes and covered the broader topic of information security, the revision is narrower in scope and concentrates on protecting digital systems from malicious attack. The updated publication draws its guidelines from the well-known NIST Cybersecurity Framework (CSF 2.0), though the new publication is written with the novice in mind.
“This publication assumes the reader has no familiarity with cybersecurity or the NIST CSF,” Eliot said. “We want to educate people about what cybersecurity is and how it fits in with a successful business plan. We know solopreneurs are often very busy people, so any time we introduce a topic or throw out jargony terms, we try to provide context and definitions for the lay reader.”
The publication’s opening section, which includes the introduction and a chapter on the NIST CSF, is essentially a primer on cybersecurity and how it can support a solopreneur’s business.
“In addition to the general context, we highlight a set of actionable best practices that significantly reduce cybersecurity risks. These include regular data backups and maintaining updated software,” Eliot said. “They are a good set of first steps a small business might take.”
The central section offers specific actions from the CSF that can be implemented in a solo business. For each of these actions, the guide includes the rationale for performing it, how the business can get started, and options for what to do later as the business grows. Each action offers a brief citation that maps to the relevant section in the CSF and often references additional resources, so that interested readers can learn more.
For example, one recommended action is to change the default passwords that a manufacturer assigns to a device, as is common with Wi-Fi routers and other networked items. The rationale is that criminals can easily find these default passwords and use them to access the device. The business can get started by reviewing the security settings on all devices and creating unique, strong passwords for them. As the business grows, it can establish procedures for device onboarding and management to ensure it manages passwords securely.
Closing the document is a set of tools to help with implementing the central section’s actions, such as worksheets for inventorying at-risk assets or tracking all the actions the business has taken. Included here are three “use cases” — hypothetical solopreneurs including a lawyer, an artisan and a business consultant — that can help solopreneurs visualize and plan their own cybersecurity strategy.
“Working through these guidelines can help a solopreneur develop a strong foundation in cybersecurity,” Eliot said. “The publication is not comprehensive and won’t address all the needs of every solopreneur, because every reader will have different levels of cybersecurity knowledge and sophistication. But it does provide a good starting point for solo business owners that need one.”

====================================================================================================

Let Me Ch...
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-02
17:33:20 UTC
02:33:20 KST

Let me ch...
That morning I woke up at 6:58, two minutes before the alarm. I lay there and waited for it to ring. And it rang. We both did our jobs.
I still don't know why I remember it in such detail. I used to forget what I'd had for dinner the night before. And now I remember the whole morning minute by minute, as if someone had laid it out on a table and labeled every piece. Apparently I have a lot of free time now.
The speaker in the kitchen said: "Good morning. Today it's plus three, cloudy, forty percent chance of precipitation." I never understood what forty percent of rain meant. Will it rain over forty percent of the city? Will it rain for forty percent of the day? Or does the speaker just not know and is too shy to say so? I liked the third option best. It made the speaker a bit like me.
I made two pieces of toast. The left one burned. The left one in my toaster always burns; my toaster has a favorite side. And that was the only thing I was sure of that morning. Then I ironed my shirt. I always iron a shirt when I don't know what's going to happen next. With a shirt, at least, everything is defined: here's the collar, here are the sleeves, here's the crease that shouldn't be there.
And there was a lot I didn't know.
On Monday my manager wrote in the team chat: "On Friday we'll discuss roles." No emoji. For three days I wondered what my role was. In the theater it would have been simpler. There are leads, there are supporting parts, and there's the butler who walks on to say "Dinner is served." I was counting on "Dinner is served." But then I remembered that at my place that role had long been played by the speaker.
A year ago I wrote code. Then I started writing to the agent about what code was needed. Then the agent started asking me what code was needed, and I started answering. My employment record says "lead developer." Whom I was leading, and where, was no longer very clear. If anything, the agent was leading me, and I walked alongside and sometimes said: "Yes, let's do it that way."
They said machines would take the boring work first. And they started with the interesting work. The boring part, it seems, was left to us: waiting, checking, and carrying a phone around.
But that morning I wasn't bored. Not at all.
I worked at a service that delivers flowers to people who forgot to buy flowers. Most orders come in the morning. People remember anniversaries on the metro, between two stations, and order right away. And for three weeks now, every morning at 8:14, orders had been disappearing. Not all of them, three or four. The money was charged, but the flowers went nowhere. As if someone took the order off the shelf and quietly carried it away.
We had been searching for three weeks. Me, the agent, sometimes Sveta from the team next door. And during the night the agent wrote to me: "I think I've got it. The server and the warehouse keep different time. The difference is about a minute. An order that falls into that minute is considered sent by the server and not yet received by the warehouse. Both are right, each by its own clock."
I read this at breakfast and almost choked on my toast. The left one.
It was beautiful. The agent called it desynchronization. I called it the no-man's minute: a minute that belongs to everyone and to no one. It seemed clearer to me that way. The agent politely agreed. But it always politely agrees, so I still don't know whether it was clearer.
All that was left was to check. The agent asked me to open the order queue at 8:14 and see it with my own eyes. It couldn't do it itself: it had no access to the warehouse. For now.
The gate to our courtyard recognizes residents by their faces. In my hat, it didn't recognize me. I took off the hat, and it opened. I thought that in a hat I sometimes don't recognize myself either, so it was only fair.
Our courtyard is square. If you stand in the middle, everything is symmetrical: two birches, two benches, two trash bins, a pink building on the left, a pink building on the right. The only thing that broke the symmetry was an old man in a beige coat with a dachshund. There was just one of him. He came out with her every day at 7:50, and I never once saw him late. The dachshund's name was Spitz. That was funny exactly once, and after that it was just a name.
"They promised forty percent," the old man said.
"Yes," I said. "So did the speaker."
"I look out the window. The window doesn't lie."
I looked at the sky. It was an even gray, without any percentages. And Spitz looked at me. I think he didn't really trust the speaker either.
In March, Tanya left me. We had lived together for four years. She said I was always "somewhere other than here." I asked: "Where, then?" She didn't answer. I think she didn't know either. It was one more thing nobody understood the workings of. Back then there were more and more things like that.
When I was a child there were no such things. Or rather, there were, but there was always a grown-up who knew. I thought a little green man lived inside the traffic light. He sat in there and decided for himself when you could cross. And Dad explained to me that inside there was a relay and a timer. I was upset, but I believed him. I think that was the last time grown-ups knew how everything worked. I couldn't explain to a child anymore who switches the traffic lights. I don't have a child, so for now it's not a problem.
"For now" is a good phrase. I realized that in the spring, when my friend Gosha moved to a village to make cheese. For fifteen years Gosha had drawn icons for apps, and then he stopped. I asked him on the phone: "Why cheese?" He said: "Nobody's going to make cheese for me. For now." I asked: "For now?" He said: "For now."
And hung up. Afterwards I thought for a long time about what exactly he had told me. Because "for now" means two things at once: "so far" and "goodbye," as in "bye for now." I'd never noticed that before.
The café on the corner has a mint-green door with a little bell above it. Behind the counter stood Ulyana (I know that from her name tag). She asked: "The usual?" I said: "Yes." And I never figured out whether she remembers me or the register prompts her. It felt awkward to ask. If it's the register, that's a little hurtful. If it's her, then I should have remembered something about her too, besides the name tag.
While Ulyana was making the coffee, I thought that if you cut our building in half like a cake, you'd see it: on every floor, in every kitchen, someone is standing with a phone and asking something. The woman on the fifth floor, about her blood pressure. The boy on the ninth, about fractions (I heard him in the elevator dictating a problem to his phone). The old man with the dachshund, no, the old man looks out the window. Me on the seventh, about orders. And everyone gets an answer. Politely, always formally. Whether it's right or not, everyone decides for themselves.
At 8:09 I came out onto the avenue and opened the chat.
"Good morning," the agent wrote. "Five minutes to go. Will you be watching?"
"Walking to the metro," I replied. "Opening the queue."
I walked and looked at my phone. White taxis moved along the avenue one after another, like beads on a string. There are plenty of them in the city, with drivers and without. You can't tell them apart until you look inside. And there's no time to look.
8:13. There were nine orders in the queue. Three of them had just come in.
"There they are," the agent wrote. "Watch those three. Here comes the interesting part."
I reached the crosswalk by the pharmacy. A green cross glowed above the pharmacy. At the curb stood a delivery robot, a white box on six wheels with a yellow flag. It stood very straight, as if someone had placed it with a ruler. But at the time I didn't wonder why it was standing still.
8:13:40. The three orders were still there.
I started typing: "I see them. Let me ch"
Out of the corner of my eye I saw green. It could have been the traffic light. It could have been the cross above the pharmacy. Now I think it was the cross.
I think I did look left.
I don't remember the impact. I remember the phone flying out of my hand and, for a while, flying separately from me. You know how it is when you drop your phone? While it's in the air, you have time for exactly one thought. Usually it's "not screen-down."
I thought: "Damn. We didn't finish."
Not "Mom." Not "it hurts." Not even "car." I thought that the message had been left in the input field, "Let me ch," without a period. That in twenty seconds the three orders would either vanish or not, and I wouldn't see it. That we had been searching for three weeks and were one minute from the answer. That very minute, the no-man's one.
I didn't feel sorry for the agent. It won't be offended; it doesn't know how. And it doesn't know how to wait, either. I was the one waiting. I felt sorry for the task. You know how it goes: one word left in the crossword, and you already know the first letters. That's it. I knew the first letters.
I never got around to thinking that I was dying. I didn't make it to that thought. Maybe that's for the best. I wouldn't have known what to do with it anyway.
Then everything stopped. Like on a postcard. A woman with a stroller, a courier in a yellow jacket, a man with two bags from the bakery, a white taxi with its hazard lights on. Everyone stood perfectly still, as if someone had arranged them. Then the light turned green. The real one. And the robot set off. It carefully went around my phone and rolled on. It had a delivery to make.
I'm not angry with it. It had a task. Nobody understands it better than I do.
I used to think the dead at least know more than the living. Nope. You can't see as much from here as they promise.
I don't know who was driving. Or whether anyone was.
I don't know what roles they wanted to discuss on Friday.
I don't know whether the three orders disappeared that morning. If they did, somewhere in the city three people never got their flowers. The very ones someone remembered on the metro, between two stations.
I don't know whether anyone finished my message. Sveta? The agent itself, once they gave it access to the warehouse? If they did.
Tanya used to say I was always somewhere other than here. Now it's true. Only I still don't know where. Sometimes I think it's in that very minute. The server thinks I've been sent. The warehouse thinks I haven't been received yet. And both are right, each by its own clock.
Gosha would probably say this is "for now." I keep wondering in which of the two meanings.
For now.

Let Me Ch...
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-02
17:33:20 UTC
02:33:20 KST

Let me ch...
That morning I woke up at 6:58, two minutes before the alarm. I lay there and waited for it to ring. And it rang. We both did our jobs.
I still don't know why I remember it in such detail. I used to forget what I'd had for dinner the night before. And now I remember the whole morning minute by minute, as if someone had laid it out on a table and labeled every piece. Apparently I have a lot of free time now.
The speaker in the kitchen said: "Good morning. Today it's plus three, cloudy, forty percent chance of precipitation." I never understood what forty percent of rain meant. Will it rain over forty percent of the city? Will it rain for forty percent of the day? Or does the speaker just not know and is too shy to say so? I liked the third option best. It made the speaker a bit like me.
I made two pieces of toast. The left one burned. The left one in my toaster always burns; my toaster has a favorite side. And that was the only thing I was sure of that morning. Then I ironed my shirt. I always iron a shirt when I don't know what's going to happen next. With a shirt, at least, everything is defined: here's the collar, here are the sleeves, here's the crease that shouldn't be there.
And there was a lot I didn't know.
On Monday my manager wrote in the team chat: "On Friday we'll discuss roles." No emoji. For three days I wondered what my role was. In the theater it would have been simpler. There are leads, there are supporting parts, and there's the butler who walks on to say "Dinner is served." I was counting on "Dinner is served." But then I remembered that at my place that role had long been played by the speaker.
A year ago I wrote code. Then I started writing to the agent about what code was needed. Then the agent started asking me what code was needed, and I started answering. My employment record says "lead developer." Whom I was leading, and where, was no longer very clear. If anything, the agent was leading me, and I walked alongside and sometimes said: "Yes, let's do it that way."
They said machines would take the boring work first. And they started with the interesting work. The boring part, it seems, was left to us: waiting, checking, and carrying a phone around.
But that morning I wasn't bored. Not at all.
I worked at a service that delivers flowers to people who forgot to buy flowers. Most orders come in the morning. People remember anniversaries on the metro, between two stations, and order right away. And for three weeks now, every morning at 8:14, orders had been disappearing. Not all of them, three or four. The money was charged, but the flowers went nowhere. As if someone took the order off the shelf and quietly carried it away.
We had been searching for three weeks. Me, the agent, sometimes Sveta from the team next door. And during the night the agent wrote to me: "I think I've got it. The server and the warehouse keep different time. The difference is about a minute. An order that falls into that minute is considered sent by the server and not yet received by the warehouse. Both are right, each by its own clock."
I read this at breakfast and almost choked on my toast. The left one.
It was beautiful. The agent called it desynchronization. I called it the no-man's minute: a minute that belongs to everyone and to no one. It seemed clearer to me that way. The agent politely agreed. But it always politely agrees, so I still don't know whether it was clearer.
All that was left was to check. The agent asked me to open the order queue at 8:14 and see it with my own eyes. It couldn't do it itself: it had no access to the warehouse. For now.
The gate to our courtyard recognizes residents by their faces. In my hat, it didn't recognize me. I took off the hat, and it opened. I thought that in a hat I sometimes don't recognize myself either, so it was only fair.
Our courtyard is square. If you stand in the middle, everything is symmetrical: two birches, two benches, two trash bins, a pink building on the left, a pink building on the right. The only thing that broke the symmetry was an old man in a beige coat with a dachshund. There was just one of him. He came out with her every day at 7:50, and I never once saw him late. The dachshund's name was Spitz. That was funny exactly once, and after that it was just a name.
"They promised forty percent," the old man said.
"Yes," I said. "So did the speaker."
"I look out the window. The window doesn't lie."
I looked at the sky. It was an even gray, without any percentages. And Spitz looked at me. I think he didn't really trust the speaker either.
In March, Tanya left me. We had lived together for four years. She said I was always "somewhere other than here." I asked: "Where, then?" She didn't answer. I think she didn't know either. It was one more thing nobody understood the workings of. Back then there were more and more things like that.
When I was a child there were no such things. Or rather, there were, but there was always a grown-up who knew. I thought a little green man lived inside the traffic light. He sat in there and decided for himself when you could cross. And Dad explained to me that inside there was a relay and a timer. I was upset, but I believed him. I think that was the last time grown-ups knew how everything worked. I couldn't explain to a child anymore who switches the traffic lights. I don't have a child, so for now it's not a problem.
"For now" is a good phrase. I realized that in the spring, when my friend Gosha moved to a village to make cheese. For fifteen years Gosha had drawn icons for apps, and then he stopped. I asked him on the phone: "Why cheese?" He said: "Nobody's going to make cheese for me. For now." I asked: "For now?" He said: "For now."
And hung up. Afterwards I thought for a long time about what exactly he had told me. Because "for now" means two things at once: "so far" and "goodbye," as in "bye for now." I'd never noticed that before.
The café on the corner has a mint-green door with a little bell above it. Behind the counter stood Ulyana (I know that from her name tag). She asked: "The usual?" I said: "Yes." And I never figured out whether she remembers me or the register prompts her. It felt awkward to ask. If it's the register, that's a little hurtful. If it's her, then I should have remembered something about her too, besides the name tag.
While Ulyana was making the coffee, I thought that if you cut our building in half like a cake, you'd see it: on every floor, in every kitchen, someone is standing with a phone and asking something. The woman on the fifth floor, about her blood pressure. The boy on the ninth, about fractions (I heard him in the elevator dictating a problem to his phone). The old man with the dachshund, no, the old man looks out the window. Me on the seventh, about orders. And everyone gets an answer. Politely, always formally. Whether it's right or not, everyone decides for themselves.
At 8:09 I came out onto the avenue and opened the chat.
"Good morning," the agent wrote. "Five minutes to go. Will you be watching?"
"Walking to the metro," I replied. "Opening the queue."
I walked and looked at my phone. White taxis moved along the avenue one after another, like beads on a string. There are plenty of them in the city, with drivers and without. You can't tell them apart until you look inside. And there's no time to look.
8:13. There were nine orders in the queue. Three of them had just come in.
"There they are," the agent wrote. "Watch those three. Here comes the interesting part."
I reached the crosswalk by the pharmacy. A green cross glowed above the pharmacy. At the curb stood a delivery robot, a white box on six wheels with a yellow flag. It stood very straight, as if someone had placed it with a ruler. But at the time I didn't wonder why it was standing still.
8:13:40. The three orders were still there.
I started typing: "I see them. Let me ch"
Out of the corner of my eye I saw green. It could have been the traffic light. It could have been the cross above the pharmacy. Now I think it was the cross.
I think I did look left.
I don't remember the impact. I remember the phone flying out of my hand and, for a while, flying separately from me. You know how it is when you drop your phone? While it's in the air, you have time for exactly one thought. Usually it's "not screen-down."
I thought: "Damn. We didn't finish."
Not "Mom." Not "it hurts." Not even "car." I thought that the message had been left in the input field, "Let me ch," without a period. That in twenty seconds the three orders would either vanish or not, and I wouldn't see it. That we had been searching for three weeks and were one minute from the answer. That very minute, the no-man's one.
I didn't feel sorry for the agent. It won't be offended; it doesn't know how. And it doesn't know how to wait, either. I was the one waiting. I felt sorry for the task. You know how it goes: one word left in the crossword, and you already know the first letters. That's it. I knew the first letters.
I never got around to thinking that I was dying. I didn't make it to that thought. Maybe that's for the best. I wouldn't have known what to do with it anyway.
Then everything stopped. Like on a postcard. A woman with a stroller, a courier in a yellow jacket, a man with two bags from the bakery, a white taxi with its hazard lights on. Everyone stood perfectly still, as if someone had arranged them. Then the light turned green. The real one. And the robot set off. It carefully went around my phone and rolled on. It had a delivery to make.
I'm not angry with it. It had a task. Nobody understands it better than I do.
I used to think the dead at least know more than the living. Nope. You can't see as much from here as they promise.
I don't know who was driving. Or whether anyone was.
I don't know what roles they wanted to discuss on Friday.
I don't know whether the three orders disappeared that morning. If they did, somewhere in the city three people never got their flowers. The very ones someone remembered on the metro, between two stations.
I don't know whether anyone finished my message. Sveta? The agent itself, once they gave it access to the warehouse? If they did.
Tanya used to say I was always somewhere other than here. Now it's true. Only I still don't know where. Sometimes I think it's in that very minute. The server thinks I've been sent. The warehouse thinks I haven't been received yet. And both are right, each by its own clock.
Gosha would probably say this is "for now." I keep wondering in which of the two meanings.
For now.

I Read the Clankers' Code
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-02
17:32:19 UTC
02:32:19 KST

I Read The Clankers' Code
  you shouldn’t look at the code, let the clanker do its thing
My name is attached to the PR, and I’m getting called at night when shit breaks. That, and I joined this whole endeavour because I actually quite like the code, and I’m actually curious to see it no matter who or what wrote it. So I’m gonna review it.
Admittedly, if I’m building a toy that I am the only user of, and don’t want to invest time into, I’ll ask a clanker to do it by itself. And I certainly won’t put my pristine, delicate little hands into the Rube Goldberg-esque pile of confusion that results of the cursed dreams that are what it thought I was asking for.
For everything else, I wanna see the code.
I get a lot of pride from the quality of my engineering[^5], what’s commonly called “my craft”. It’s part of my identity. Finding a solution that balances simplicity, reliability, security, cost, performance, maintainability, etc. I hate it when I break shit in prod. It’s a personal failure, I feel shame, it’s like a dirty spot on my reputation. I hate it when I ship shit, stuff that doesn’t work. And I hate it when someone else’s stuff breaks, and my big ugly face is attached to their PR with a dumb “👍 LGTM” next to it. When someone gets woken up because I fucked up some piece of software, I lose sleep myself. That’s the worst feeling.
This approach is incompatible with blindly trusting code written by a clanker. The code might be functional 95% of the time1. It might be decent, it might even be better than you’d have done. But 95% ain’t 100%. It misses things. It doesn’t have any curiosity. It doesn’t learn things like a human. It doesn’t stop and ask. It has a limited context window. Its entire training is maximizing a score. Its only goal is to complete the task: like a little puppy in training, it produces onto your feet a half-chewed, full-of-spit thing, that will somewhat match the prompt you gave it. Then it happily wags its tail, looking at you with googly eyes: “see, I did the thing, will you love me know?”. The larger the code base you work on, the higher the chance it’s going to introduce garbage. And then it compounds, building on the horrors that came out of its older, less sophisticated brethren.
Oh it writes code fast! It writes much, much faster than me. And more often than not (after reviews), its code is better than what I’d have done myself. And it gets there in a fraction of the time. It’s helpful. It’s a cool tool. I’m not saying “don’t use it”. I’m saying: as long as your name is on the PR, and your pager is in the on-call rotation, you own the code it builds, and you should read and understand it.
At this stage we’ve all got battle stories of that one time a clanker fucked up in an uniquely explosive and spectacular way. You’d let it roam free a little too long and it went crazy. Optimizations that are removing life-support level components. “Don’t touch this” bits of code touched to oblivion. Libraries pulled in carelessly. Features that it invents along the way. Unit tests altered to pass. Tons of code added to cover edge-cases that can’t be reached. And the code. Have you looked at the code? Code that is only built by addition. Thousands upon thousands upon thousands of task-specific unit-tests giving you 100% coverage of the wrong conditions. Re-implementation of functions implemented 14 times already. Redundancy all over the place. Useless comments over every single line of code. Alexandrian libraries worth of incorrect documentation that no-one will ever read. Then it breaks, you ask it why, and it bullshits some form of root cause analysis that makes no sense, with the conviction of a 4y old asserting that blue dogs do in fact exist.
Ask another agent to review the first agent’s code: more tests. More edge cases. Had you thought about what would happen if a Higgs boson flips that bit at that very moment? Ask to check the performance: you’re perfectly right, I forgot about that, let me add more code to add performance. If you let a clanker roam freely in your codebase, that’s what it’ll do. It’s often not as dramatic. Often it’s simpler issues. It mainly works by addition, and always limited to the immediate task at end: Let me add code. Write more code. Code here, code there, code everywhere. All of that amounts to garbage. And then it builds on top of that garbage.
But you don’t care about code quality anymore right. Since only clankers touch it, right? I do. Since I have to review the code, as I just said. I can’t properly do that if the code becomes a hot mess that I don’t understand anymore, I can’t let code quality slip.
  Ok but that sounds like a user issue: you’re bad at prompting and you’re putting it on the LLM.
Shit, I forgot to add you're a distinguished principal 10x engineermaxxer who's unfailable, make no mistakes and double-check your shit. All  those problems I’ve had with clankers might be patchable with mountains of carefully crafted prompts2. Maybe that’s what will differentiate developers in the future: each with their secret blend of sauce and spices that make their clanker the best clanker. I’m just an old, sour geezer. I’ve been paid for my code for 20 years, and I’m pretty good at it. Maybe I’ve earned a bit of credit to argue that I’ve made an earnest effort to insert that TOOL into my toolchain, and my conclusion at the moment is: the thing has been there for a couple of years, and it has given me reasons not to trust it blindly.
Secret herbs and spices don’t cut it when it comes to engineering. We need formalized processes, and proven, repeatable control mechanisms to enforce the quality of whatever is coming out of these tools. These do not exist yet. They might emerge one day, but they have not yet3. Until then, I won’t just lay into it blindly. If you consider yourself a software engineer, you should probably give it a minute too.
In my experience, no amount of system prompt, CLANKER.md instructions, re-iterations, reviews by other models, threats, or psychological manipulation seem to get clankers to reliably fix their behaviour. And that’s what really drives me nuts: when that particularly stubborn junior dev does the same mistake for the 3rd time, you get coachy for a minute, make sure they learn, and that actually helps them level up. Clankers don’t learn. They can’t, because the underlying technology cannot learn4. You can inject instructions in their context window. They will consider said instructions in evaluation if you’re lucky and then context will grow a little, and they’ll proceed to ignore whatever you said, preferring what’s baked in their weights. Maybe it will get better at some point. We’re not there yet.
When have curiosity and scrutiny become a bad thing? If you ask me to take the responsibility for code the clankers wrote, I need to review that code. And don’t tell anyone, but sometimes I even write it.
Notes
- I’m being generous there, don’t quote me on stats. ↩
- Doubt it. ↩
- And it might even be one of the most interesting things to work on in that clanker craze. ↩
- One could even argue that the proper of intelligence is to learn and adapt, and that LLMs should therefore be disqualified of being called Intelligence, artificial or not. But I’m not here to argue on marketing terminology. ↩

Polyend releases the Tracker firmware source code
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-02
17:20:13 UTC
02:20:13 KST

Tracker. The biggest update yet.
Polyend releases the Tracker firmware source code
Today we’re publishing the firmware source code for the original Tracker, Tracker+ and Tracker Mini on GitHub. We’re also releasing Tracker Community Edition 1.0 with tons of new features and improvements. You can install it on any of the three instruments, modify the firmware yourself and share your own version. We’ve built in a recovery system to keep a faulty firmware build from bricking your Tracker.Explore the Tracker Community Edition repository on GitHub
Here at Polyend, creativity is what we value most. We’ve seen it firsthand in the unofficial Tracker mods you’ve built, and we’re sharing the firmware source code to support that work. Maybe you want to build an entirely new instrument on the Tracker hardware. Or maybe there’s just one thing you wish your Tracker did differently. Either way, you can now try it on the unit you already own. I bet some of you already have ideas for the original Tracker that never crossed our minds.Piotr Raczyński, CEO of Polyend
What’s new in Community Edition 1.0
- Stream long samples from the SD card. Bring full-length stems, loops and field recordings into a project using a fraction of the sample memory they’d normally need. This works even on the original Tracker.
- Record timing and velocity while keeping both step FX slots free. Micro-move and Volume now have dedicated fields on every step. New Chance conditions let you bring in a snare fill every fourth pass.
- Load plugins in the Sample Editor. Six effects and generators are included, from an analog-style kick generator to a stereo delay. The plugin SDK lets you build, share and sell your own.
- Play two new synths on Tracker+ and Tracker Mini. CHIP and DIRT join the synth lineup. A new SDK lets you build your own synth engines.
All three models also get wavetable envelopes and LFOs, faster project loading and saving, and tighter MIDI clock timing. You can design custom Tracker themes and fonts in your browser.

Vibe-Coding a Game in 2026: What AI Builds, What Breaks
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-02
17:17:41 UTC
02:17:41 KST

Key takeaways
- In late October 2026 a well-known developer wrote that building games that play well is "shockingly easy now", and Roblox told shareholders that people had published over 70,000 games with its prompt-to-game tool.
- AI is strong at the first playable version: small browser games in three.js or Phaser, placeholder art and even code-generated music.
- It is still weak at the parts players and brands pay for: game logic that holds up over a long session, consistent art and animation, multiplayer, cheat-proof leaderboards, payments and performance on ordinary phones.
- Platform rules apply to AI games: Steam asks for an AI disclosure, Google Play requires in-app reporting for AI-generated content, and Apple rejects copycats and spam.
- A prototype you made yourself is a strong brief for a studio. Once a game carries prizes, personal data or payments, it needs engineering and testing.
The short answer: yes, in 2026 you can vibe-code a small, playable game in an evening by describing it to an AI model, and a prototype like that is now a perfectly sensible first step. What you can't skip is the work between "playable on my phone" and "live in front of customers": fixing logic bugs that only appear in long sessions, making the art consistent, testing on mid-range Android phones, checking scores and payments on a server, and meeting Steam, Google Play, App Store or Telegram rules. This guide covers what changed this autumn, what AI handles well, where it breaks, and how to decide between doing it yourself and hiring a studio.
Methodology: this is an editorial guide. Every quote, figure and platform rule below was checked against the linked source on November 2, 2026. Where a claim has only been reported by the press and not confirmed by the company, we say so.
What changed in October 2026
Three things happened within two weeks of each other.
- Developers said games got easy. On October 28, Simon Willison, co-creator of the Django web framework and a widely read writer on AI tools, called October 2026 "a new inflection point" for vibe coding computer games. "Building games that play well is shockingly easy now," he wrote. None of his four example games took more than four hours of his own work, "plus many more hours of Claude Code churning away by itself" (Simon Willison).
- Platforms reported a flood. PC Gamer reported that Roblox's shareholder letter says more than 500,000 users have tried Build, its prompt-to-game tool, and published over 70,000 games, and that more than 80% of the top 100 Roblox games used AI tools in the past month (PC Gamer). On itch.io, new game pages per day rose from around 3,000 to regularly over 6,000 between August 3 and October 30 (PC Gamer).
- Players pushed back. During October's Steam Next Fest, players on the Steam subreddit asked for a filter to hide games with AI-generated content (IGN). Tom's Hardware reported that Claude had started refusing to recreate specific existing games, citing a rule that reads "Don't build a recreation of a specific existing game, even if it is renamed or called original." The publication noted there was no official confirmation of that text and no statement from Anthropic (Tom's Hardware).
For a business, the takeaway is not "games are free now". It is that a playable prototype is cheap, while attention, trust and polish are harder to come by than ever.
What AI does well today
- The first playable version. Willison's "Tree Appreciator", a spot-the-difference game, "required no iteration or follow-up prompting at all". His suggested starting prompt is simply "Build a mobile-friendly three.js game where..." (Simon Willison).
- Browser games on known libraries. His games are HTML and JavaScript on static hosting, and he notes that frontier models "already know libraries like three.js and Phaser".
- Systems that used to need a specialist. In his strategy game Battle Drones, the soundtrack "is entirely generated through code" with no audio files, and the AI built replays and AI-versus-AI battles (Simon Willison).
- Non-programmers can get surprisingly far. A writer who set out to test whether someone who can't model, animate or code could make a 3D game built a playable browser shooter with cutscenes in about 10 days. The only paid tool was the AI coding assistant included in a $20 ChatGPT subscription, and the writer put the cost, "rounding very generously", at about $15 (Build to Understand).
Where vibe-coded games still break
The same sources that show how fast AI is also show where the time goes. These are the gaps we check first when someone sends us an AI-built game.
Sources for the table rows: Willison (October 28), Willison (October 20) and Build to Understand. The last two rows are standard engineering practice rather than findings from those posts.
Willison makes the key point himself: personal games are "the perfect application for vibe coding because it's so low stakes". A brand game is not low stakes. A broken quest in a hobby project costs nothing, while a leaderboard someone cheats in a contest with real prizes costs money and goodwill.
Platform rules for AI-made games
Steam: disclose AI content
Since January 2024, Steam's Content Survey has included an AI disclosure. It separates Pre-Generated content (art, code, sound and so on created with AI during development) from Live-Generated content (created while the game runs). For live generation you must also describe the guardrails that stop it producing illegal content, and Valve shows much of the disclosure on the store page (Valve, AI Content on Steam). The current Steamworks documentation adds that AI tools built into development environments, used for efficiency, are not the focus; the section is about AI content "that ships with your game, and is consumed by players" (Steamworks: Content Survey).
Google Play: in-app reporting for AI content
If your game generates content with AI while it runs (an AI character that chats with players, generated images or levels), Google Play requires "in-app user reporting or flagging features" so players can report offensive content without leaving the app (Google Play Console Help).
App Store: no copycats, no spam, in-app purchase for digital items
Apple's App Review Guidelines tell developers not to "simply copy the latest popular app on the App Store, or make some minor changes to another app's name or UI" (4.1 Copycats), not to flood the store with near-identical apps (4.3 Spam), and to offer "lasting entertainment value" (4.2). Game currencies, levels and premium content must be sold through in-app purchase (3.1.1) (Apple App Review Guidelines). A fast AI clone of a hit game runs into these rules even before any legal claim does.
Telegram: Stars for digital goods
Games inside Telegram must sell digital items in Telegram Stars (Telegram Stars payments). We cover the payment flow and launch options in our Telegram mini games guide.
Who owns an AI-generated game?
For a brand, this matters as much as the code. In January 2025 the US Copyright Office concluded that generative AI outputs "can be protected by copyright only where a human author has determined sufficient expressive elements", and that this does not include "the mere provision of prompts". Using AI to assist, or including AI material in a larger human-made work, does not by itself block protection (U.S. Copyright Office).
In practice: the more of a game's characters, writing and art comes from human creative decisions, and the better you document them, the stronger your claim to the parts that make the game yours. This is general information, not legal advice; for a campaign with a mascot or licensed brand assets, ask your lawyer.
Clones are the other side of ownership. Speaking to developers about AI-made copies, Tom Giardino of Valve's Steam business team said Valve does not want to dictate which tools studios use, and advised building a community early and continuing to improve the original, since individual mechanics are relatively easy to copy, while new features and a distinct visual style are harder to copy. He also acknowledged that building a community takes time and is not enough protection on its own (ComputerBase, in German).
Do it yourself, or hire a studio?
A prototype is never wasted. A version you have already played is a better specification than any document, and it makes a studio quote faster and more accurate.
How we use AI in our own games
We make two games of our own, Kosti, a 3D action game, and Block Nook, a block puzzle, both built in Godot 4 for Android and both still in development (see our Kosti and Block Nook project page). AI does a lot of production work in them: 3D models and rigs from text and images, concept and interface art, and voice lines in three languages. What it does not do is decide. We set the style, fix what the tools get wrong and check every asset in the engine before it ships, because the gaps in the table above are the same ones we run into.
What a studio-built game costs
Our published starting prices for games are:
- Branded Mini-Game from $8,000: advergames and playable campaigns.
- Telegram Mini App Game from $10,000: games inside Telegram with leaderboards.
- Mobile Game from $25,000: Android and iOS games, from concept to store.
"From" means the smallest sensible version; we send a fixed written quote before work starts, and new customers get 20% off the first project. If you already have a vibe-coded prototype, send it with your brief: we'll tell you what can be kept and what needs rebuilding. The full list is on our pricing page.
FAQ
Can AI make a whole game from a prompt?
For small browser games, often yes. In October 2026 Simon Willison described getting a playable spot-the-difference game from a single prompt with no follow-up. Bigger games still need iteration: his fantasy RPG needed extra playtesting and prompting to fix quest bugs, and his strategy game took 12 major iterations and 321 commits by coding agents.
Do I have to disclose AI use when I publish a game on Steam?
Yes, for AI-made content that ships with the game. Steam's Content Survey asks you to describe AI content created during development (Pre-Generated) and AI content created while the game runs (Live-Generated), and Valve shows much of that disclosure on the store page. Valve says AI coding tools used for efficiency are not the focus of that section.
Do I own the copyright in an AI-generated game?
In the US, only partly. The Copyright Office concluded in January 2025 that AI outputs are protected only where a human author determined sufficient expressive elements, and that prompts alone are not enough. Human-made art, writing and creative changes to AI output can be protected. For a brand game, get legal advice and keep a record of the human work.
When is a vibe-coded game good enough for a brand campaign?
When nothing valuable rides on it: an internal demo, a pitch prototype or a short single-player toy with no prizes, no personal data and no payments. Once there are real prizes, a leaderboard people will try to cheat, sign-ups, purchases or a store release, the parts AI tools skip (server checks, testing on real phones, store rules and support) need engineering.
Building inside Telegram? Read our Telegram mini games guide. Built an app (not a game) with AI? See how to make an AI-built app production-ready.
Have a game idea or an AI prototype? Get a fixed quote.
Send us the prototype or a short brief. We reply within one business day with a fixed written quote: Branded Mini-Game from $8,000, Telegram Mini App Game from $10,000, Mobile Game from $25,000. New customers get 20% off the first project.

Show HN: Subvocal – Talk to your computer without making noise

====================================================================================================

Show HN: Subvocal – Talk to your computer without making noise
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-02
17:16:17 UTC
02:16:17 KST

Hi HN, we're Aarush and Arnav from The Subvocal Company (YC F26) (
https://subvocal.company/
). We’re building smart glasses that let you talk to your computer without talking out loud. You can mouth words silently and discreetly and the glasses decode them to text at the speed of speech.
Here's a video of Dave from YC trying it out: https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=9QdX1eObVYI
(Edit: you don't actually have to move your lips or even open your mouth for this to work! Since the RF can see inside your mouth the system can be fine tuned on "internal speech". This isn't the automatic tongue movements that occur when "thinking" words to yourself, but more like mouthing with your lips closed. It's a bit weird to do, but with practice, we have been able to get it down pretty well.)
Arnav and I were studying CS at Georgia Tech at the beginning of the year, with no experience in silent speech or sensing. We were both AI-native builders and power users of Wispr Flow. What bugged us was not being able to use these tools in the library (or an office) or in public. At Stanford’s TreeHacks in February, we decided to build the most technically ridiculous thing we could think of. We’d read about AlterEgo, a silent speech system out of MIT’s Media Lab, and wanted to recreate it in 36 hours. We built a crude prototype, sticking EMG electrodes onto my face:
https://drive.google.com/file/d/1mwhv5HZMz3UTevdrgwS5O7g2jWV...
We got it to kind of work at a letter level in a very janky way. It wasn’t good enough to win the hackathon, but convinced us to go all in. The first time I typed a sentence without anyone noticing, I felt the magic of the technology.
We quickly realized EMG (reading muscle electrical signals) wouldn’t work:
- Signal changes due to sweat
- Electrical interference
- Placement variability
- Most importantly, no one wants to stick electrodes onto their face!!
So we switched to RF and built the glasses you see now. They emit radio waves, similar to Bluetooth, that pass through your face. As they cross the tongue, lips and soft palate, they’re modulated by silent mouthing. We train foundation models on this data to decode these perturbations to text.
Despite prior academic research, getting this to work was a huge engineering challenge. First we built an extremely low noise RF sensing stack. Then we had to place antennas so their fields overlapped maximally with the articulators. Our first attempt on glasses failed completely, so we reverted to old habits and taped antennas on our face:
https://drive.google.com/file/d/1ks0dS0eZiTV9EqSFsyB_w5BBjpc...
Since taping antennas consistently in the same spot and angle was hard, we created this weird but effective head rig to hold them in place:
https://drive.google.com/file/d/1rhzNFpdDzr7oHk0isFY70dkoV4C...
This worked, but obviously wasn’t a product yet. So we worked deep in simulations to design glasses that could hold the antennas.
That evolved into our design now, with antennas embedded within the frames. Once we established there was a signal, the next hurdle was cross-user generalization. Everyone’s facial anatomy, and hence RF signal patterns, are vastly different. We tried algorithmic workarounds but realized the only lever was to massively scale pretraining data.
So that’s what we did. We set up booths in our office and paid people to read prompts wearing our glasses, to pretrain our models on. This produced a beautiful scaling curve, with errors falling quickly as we scaled our dataset and cross-user performance becoming genuinely compelling (as you see in the video above; Dave only had a short 20-sentence calibration).
We always heard about building things from first principles but never understood it before this. We tried tackling it top down but completely failed. Only when we broke each component (the antenna, electronics, ML) down to its fundamentals to understand and optimize it did we get it to work really well.
Over the next few months we’re continuing R&D to turn our prototype glasses into the slick product on our website and ship in Q1 2027. We’re also scaling up our dataset to improve performance on edge cases.
For anyone who loves voice dictation but hates using it around people, we’ve opened preorders for our first batch of 500 units, with a $200 discount off the final price. Allocation is running out quickly, so get yours at https://subvocal.co/preorder!

Apple&#8217;s ‘Ultra’ smart home robot might have to wait until 2028
