Apple has reportedly delayed the launch of its rumored smart home robot until next year at the earliest or possibly even 2028, according to The Information. It says the robot’s hardware “has been finished for around two years,” but that the software isn’t ready. The device’s software team has reportedly pushed to delay the launch and “attempted to strip back some proposed features and focus the product on a few capabilities related to FaceTime and automating home tasks, like switching on a light.”
Apple’s ‘Ultra’ smart home robot might have to wait until 2028
The HomeView-like tabletop robot could cost over $1,000, reports The Information.
According to The Information, the device is codenamed J595, but former Apple employees predict it will be called the “HomeView Ultra,” taking after the new HomeView smart display that launched last month. The robot might cost significantly more than the HomeView, though, at around $1,000, potentially more depending on price hikes for RAM and other components.
Bloomberg’s Mark Gurman said in 2024 that the robot could have a stationary, tabletop design resembling Pixar’s famous lamp. The Information says it will have a 9-inch display that swivels around on a robotic arm, with cameras and sensors for things like following you around during a FaceTime call.

Marvell to Buy Lumentum In $115 Billion Bet On AI Connectivity

Marvell to Buy Lumentum In $115 Billion Bet On AI Connectivity 8
			
		 	
				Marvell has agreed to acquire optical components maker Lumentum in an all-stock deal with an implied enterprise value of $115 billion. "AI infrastructure is undergoing a fundamental architectural shift toward optical connectivity, creating an opportunity that comes along once in a generation," said Marvell Chairman and CEO Matt Murphy. "The companies that move first and help define this transition will set the standard for decades to come." CNBC reports:  Through the acquisition, Marvell said it can combine electrical and optical technologies to meet accelerating and shifting AI connectivity demands across regions and systems. Companies are increasingly relying on optical connections to piece together chips used to run and train large language models. Lumentum shareholders will receive 4.5 Marvell shares for each Lumentum share under the agreement. The deal is expected to close during the first half of 2028. Marvell's takeover marks one of the most significant bets on AI in 2026 and comes despite recent concerns over the long-term sustainability of infrastructure spending.

Cottage Cheese Pasta alla Vodka
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-02
17:00:00 UTC
02:00:00 KST

Cottage Cheese Pasta alla Vodka
Everything you love about the original, with a little extra protein from creamy cottage cheese.
Serves4 to 6
Prep5 minutes to 10 minutes
Cook20 minutes to 25 minutes
Why You’ll Love Our Cottage Cheese Pasta alla Vodka
- A protein-packed twist on a beloved classic. You get the rich, incredibly creamy pasta sauce that makes pasta alla vodka so irresistible, but in this version cottage cheese replaces heavy cream for a high-protein, tangy twist in this creamy pasta recipe.
- Totally doable any night of the week. Dinner hits the table with minimal prep and just about 20 minutes of cooking.
Dear Dinner Therapist: I’ve been trying to incorporate more high-protein plant-based meals in my dinner rotation but struggle with cooking things that are actually interesting. I need plant-based dinner ideas that are actually delicious and include a protein that’s not beans or tofu, take less than an hour to make, and don’t require a ton of ingredients that I’ll never use again. Please help! —Christina
Christina: A bowl of pasta likely isn’t the first thing most of us think of when trying to pack more protein into dinner, but this spin on a beloved classic is plant-based and does just that.
Inspired by the silky tomato cream sauce I’ve been ordering at restaurants for decades, this spin leans on protein-rich cottage cheese for a tangy twist. Here, whole-milk cottage cheese and nutty Parmesan are blended with a rich tomato base of tomato paste, diced tomatoes, vodka, and aromatics to create a totally irresistible sauce reminiscent of the one we know and love. To up the protein even more, consider swapping regular pasta for high-protein pasta or stirring in frozen peas at the end of cooking.
It comes together with mostly pantry items and leaves barely any leftover ingredients, plus there’s minimal prep, and it hits the table in about 30 minutes from start to finish.
Straight from Our Recipe Tester
“Adding protein to one of my favorite pasta sauces without changing the flavor or texture? I was immediately sold, and you should be too! This sauce is rich, creamy, smooth, and so delicious. One heaping bowl of (protein-packed) pasta please. “ —Celia, October 2026
Why You Should Trust Me
I’m The Kitchn’s resident Dinner Therapist. I have three cookbooks under my belt, and over a decade of experience as a professional recipe developer and tester. But perhaps more importantly, I’m a busy mom who’s packing lunch boxes and, just like you, I’m in the kitchen almost every night scrambling to get dinner on the table to feed my family (which happens to include an elementary schooler with strong opinions). Similar to the recipes I share here, my bread and butter is low-effort, high-reward recipes that keep ingredients and prep to a minimum, require no more than 30 minutes, and taste totally delicious (if not a little fancy).
Key Ingredients in Cottage Cheese Pasta alla Vodka
- Pasta. Tube-shaped pasta is a great choice for the recipe. Penne is a classic, and I happen to love mezze rigatoni. And if you want to pack even more protein into dinner, opt for a protein-rich pasta, like Brami Protein Pasta, Rummo Maxima, or Barilla+.
- Cottage cheese. The key to a smooth sauce is allowing ample time for the cottage cheese to come to room temperature. I’ve learned from experience that when the cottage cheese is even a little bit cold it will separate. Use leftover cottage cheese to make 3-ingredient cottage cheese pancakes, cottage cheese brownies, or lemony cottage cheese pasta.
- Tomato paste. I recommend using double-concentrated tomato paste here (that’s the stuff in the tube) because it adds sweetness and gives the sauce a deeper tomato flavor.
- Vodka. You don’t necessarily need to use a top-shelf bottle, but go for a vodka that you’d also use for making a cocktail. A good rule of thumb is that if you wouldn’t drink it, don’t use it here. Cheap vodka has a harsh flavor that will come through in the sauce.
How to Make Cottage Cheese Pasta alla Vodka
- Boil the pasta. Cook the pasta in a pot of salted boiling water according to package instructions until al dente. Reserve pasta water and drain the pasta.
- Cook the tomato base for the sauce. Cook aromatics in a large high-sided skillet or Dutch oven until softened. Stir in a full tube of tomato paste and cook until it’s dark red and sticks to the bottom of the pan. Deglaze the pan with vodka, scraping up any browned bits. Stir in diced tomatoes and their juices and pasta sauce, then simmer until the sauce is slightly thickened and flavors meld.
- Blend the sauce. Using a blender (ideally a high-speed blender) makes the smoothest sauce, although a food processor also works. Blend the tomato sauce until smooth, then blend in the cottage cheese and Parmesan.
- Combine the pasta and sauce. Return the sauce and add the pasta to the skillet and cook over medium-low heat, tossing constantly, until the pasta is well-coated and heated through.
Helpful Swaps
- A couple of small to medium shallots make a nice substitute for yellow onion.
- A 6-ounce can of regular tomato paste works as a substitute for double-concentrated tomato paste. The sauce will still be great; the tomato flavor just won’t be quite as rich.
- Canned whole peeled tomatoes can be used as a substitute for diced tomatoes.
Dinner Therapist is my column dedicated to solving your dilemmas around the most important — but let’s be honest, sometimes most dreaded — meal of the day. Prepping dinner night after night can be so hard. Here, I deliver practical and hopefully fun advice to make cooking less complicated and more enjoyable. Got your own dinner struggles? We want to hear from you! Fill out this super-quick form for your chance to be featured in an upcoming column.
Cottage Cheese Pasta Alla Vodka Recipe
Everything you love about the original, with a little extra protein from creamy cottage cheese.
Prep time 5 minutes to 10 minutes
Cook time 20 minutes to 25 minutes
Serves 4 to 6
Nutritional Info
Ingredients
- 1 cup 4% milkfat cottage cheese, at room temperature
- 1 pound dried short pasta, such as mezze rigatoni or penne
- 3 tablespoons olive oil
- 1 medium yellow onion, diced (1 1/2 cups)
- 3 cloves garlic, thinly sliced
- 1/2 teaspoon kosher salt, plus more as needed
- 1/4 teaspoon red pepper flakes, plus more for garnish
- 1 (about 5-ounce) tube double-concentrated tomato paste
- 1/4 cup vodka
- 1 (14.5-ounce) can diced tomatoes
- 1 ounce Parmesan cheese, finely grated (1/4 cup) plus more for garnish
- Fresh basil leaves, for garnish (optional)
Instructions
- Bring a large pot of salted water to a boil. Add 1 pound dried short pasta and cook according to package directions until al dente. Reserve 1 1/2 cups of the pasta water, then drain.
- Meanwhile, heat 3 tablespoons olive oil in a Dutch oven or large 12-inch high-sided skillet over medium heat until shimmering. Add 1 diced medium yellow onion and cook, stirring occasionally, until softened, about 5 minutes. Add 3 thinly sliced garlic cloves, 1/2 teaspoon kosher salt, and 1/4 teaspoon red pepper flakes, and cook until fragrant, about 30 seconds.
- Add 1 (about 5-ounce) tube tomato paste. Stir to coat the onion mixture and cook, stirring often, until dark red and it sticks to the bottom of the pan, 2 to 3 minutes. Add 1/4 cup vodka and scrape up any browned bits from the bottom of the pot.
- Stir in 1 (14.5-ounce) can diced tomatoes and their juices and 1 cup of the pasta water, and bring to a simmer. Reduce the heat to maintain a gentle simmer and cook until the flavors meld and the sauce thickens slightly, about 10 minutes.
- Transfer to a blender or food processor. Process until smooth, 30 seconds to 1 minute. Add 1 cup room-temperature cottage cheese and 1 ounce finely grated Parmesan cheese, and process until very smooth and creamy, about 1 minute. Taste and season with more kosher salt as needed.
- Return the sauce to the skillet. Add the pasta and cook over medium-low heat, tossing constantly, until the pasta is evenly coated and heated through, about 2 minutes. Toss in more pasta water a little at a time as needed if the pasta is dry. Garnish with more Parmesan cheese, red pepper flakes, and fresh basil leaves if desired.
Recipe Notes
Storage: Refrigerate leftovers in an airtight container for up to 4 days. Reheat over low heat on the stovetop, adding water a couple spoonfuls at a time to reconstitute the sauce.

My Favourite Laptop
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-02
16:59:57 UTC
01:59:57 KST

I’m not a big owner of things, but the best laptop I’ve ever owned is a roughed-up 2015 Thinkpad X250.
For the flex, here it is running a GUI shell sat on top of the microkernel I’m currently working on (blog post about this incoming… eventually).
Here’s a list of things that I think make it brilliant. I don’t want to pretend that any of these things are uniquely exemplary: far from it. It is the confluence of them, and not any one in particular, that makes me love using it.
Size
It’s pretty tiny. Its form-factor fits snuggly into the footprint of an A4 piece of paper, so it sits alongside books and folders when placed in a small rucksack without risking damage.
It weighs almost nothing too: even with the extra capacity battery, it’s only about 1.45 Kg. Light enough that you almost don’t notice it when travelling.
Performance
Mine has an i5-5200U: 4 logical cores, maxing out at 2.7 GHz. I upgraded it to 8 GB of RAM a while back: more than enough for some light on-the-go programming.
Even at full pelt, I’ve never heard the fan kicking up much of a fuss. Now that I think about it, I’m not even certain that it works.
Battery
The X250 has both an internal battery and an external battery slot. I’ve opted for the largest after-market external battery I could find. When doing casual work the laptop will easily survive for north of 10 hours without a charge, stretching to 12 hours if I’m careful.
Repairability
The X250 was quite popular and replacement parts retail very cheaply on ebay. It’s not the easiest to disassemble Thinkpad by any means but I can tear it apart entirely with the pocket phillips I keep on my keyring. I have previously replaced:
- The internal battery
- The lid camera
- The keyboard
- The motherboard
Every time you open up a laptop you run the risk of damaging something important either via a slip of the hand or simply the accumulated toll of loosening and tightening dozens of tiny screw threads. Despite that, my X250 has proved remarkably resilient and has never sustained any major damage despite more than more than a dozen tear-downs.
Keyboard
As with most Thinkpads, the keyboard feels lovely to use. When you spend a lot of time writing code the reliability and sensitivity of a keyboard starts to matter a lot. Coupled with my text editor, the experience of writing code on the thing is beautifully snappy.
Operating System
I opted for Endeavour OS. In the usual Arch Linux tradition, it favours native package installs over sandboxed applications. As a result my install sits at around 30 GB. A 128 GB M.2 SSD raided from another second-hand machine is more than enough to cover that and everything I’m working on.
I’ve slimmed down my systemd setup as much as possible and the thing boots from cold in about 8 seconds.
Cost
Here’s where the X250 beats out most competition. I bought mine second-hand for about £100 in 2018. They’ve only got cheaper since then. I’m not afraid to take it anywhere because it’s barely worth stealing and would be cheap to replace even if that were to happen. Its value is very much not in its on-paper specs and instead in how I use it.
Conclusion
I get a certain glee in keeping older or second-hand hardware running as a daily driver. It sounds silly, but it feels like a quiet revolt against bigger-faster-costlier consumption culture. Come the end times, this is the machine I’ll be riding it out with.

Squarespace is laying off 19 percent of employees
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-02
16:52:36 UTC
01:52:36 KST

The website-building platform Squarespace is laying off 19 percent of its employees as it works to “simplify” its operations, Squarespace spokesperson Jeff Seedman says in a statement to The Verge. The job cuts will affect around 300 of Squarespace’s 1,800 employees.
Squarespace is laying off 19 percent of employees
The layoffs will impact about 300 workers.
The layoffs will impact about 300 workers.
“The changes are intended to simplify how we operate, sharpen our priorities and enable the company to move faster as we position Squarespace for its next phase of growth,” Seedman says. “We are grateful for the contributions of our departing colleagues and are providing comprehensive support to help them through the transition.” Seedman adds that impacted employees will receive 16 weeks of base pay and healthcare benefits into May 2027.
The layoffs come a little over two years after Squarespace’s big push into AI. In 2024, the company announced its Blueprint AI tool, which uses AI to build websites as well as generate website copy, blog posts, product descriptions, and more. The private equity firm Permira also closed its acquisition of Squarespace around that time.

SpaceX reportedly wants to buy Boost Mobile to compete with AT&T, T-Mobile, and Verizon
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-02
16:44:48 UTC
01:44:48 KST

SpaceX is looking to buy Boost Mobile from EchoStar, according to a report from The Washington Post. If the deal goes through, it would help the Elon Musk-owned company build a hybrid cellular-satellite network that could help catapult it into fourth place among the top U.S. wireless providers.
The space-launch company has been dabbling in cellular connectivity, rolling out satellite access to T-Mobile’s subscribers in 2024. Last year, SpaceX bought $17 billion worth of wireless spectrum from EchoStar for cellular services, which could help the company’s Starlink service reach inside buildings, for example, something it currently struggles with.
“This is the last critical piece of the spectrum puzzle needed for SpaceX to provide complete phone coverage in America,” Musk said on X at the time of the spectrum acquisition. Yet, if Musk is still looking to buy access to terrestrial networks, it apparently wasn’t the last piece.
Elon Musk, SpaceX’s CEO, reportedly spoke directly with EchoStar chairman Charlie Ergen in October about acquiring Boost Mobile, the mobile virtual network operator. EchoStar has about 7.5 million customers between Boost Mobile and Gen Mobile, its other mobile service offering.
Musk has also approached AT&T, T-Mobile, and Verizon about buying capacity on their networks. All three rejected Musk, and they may still have a say in a SpaceX acquisition of Boost Mobile. Boost doesn’t operate its own towers, instead leasing capacity from AT&T and T-Mobile, which might need to approve such a deal. Sources speaking to The Post said that network leases are usually nontransferable.
The big three providers each have over 100 million subscribers, dwarfing Boost Mobile. But while the acquisition would give SpaceX an immediate bump in subscribers, the true value of the deal would be giving the company access to terrestrial cellular networks.
Gaining access to towers leased to Boost Mobile would expand coverage significantly — Boost currently covers most of the 48 contiguous states in the U.S. — allowing Starlink to cover devices when they’re outdoors and relying on cell towers to handle devices when Starlink signals can’t reach them.

Anthropic's internal AI use, at retail prices, now exceeds its employee pay
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-02
16:40:29 UTC
01:40:29 KST

Here's a remarkable milestone in the AI revolution: Anthropic now spends more on internal AI usage than it does paying employees.
The company revealed this in its IPO filing. In April, the value of Claude AI used internally, calculated at prices Anthropic charges customers, exceeded its monthly employee compensation costs, excluding stock awards.
By September, that internal AI usage had surged more than 250-fold since January.
There's an important distinction here. Anthropic isn't actually spending more cash on AI than salaries. It's valuing its internal AI consumption at retail prices.
Still, this feels like a watershed moment for the tech industry.
In March, I wrote about a new trend: Tech companies are starting to treat AI tokens (the main way to measure usage) as a fourth component of employee compensation, alongside salary, bonuses, and stock awards.
Anthropic's numbers show why. More than 90% of the code added to its software in September was written by Claude. Meanwhile, revenue has grown vastly faster than headcount.
For decades, talented humans were the most valuable resource in Silicon Valley. Now AI is challenging that assumption.

42AI comes out of stealth with $62.5M and a world model that can ‘predict human behavior’
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-02
16:30:00 UTC
01:30:00 KST

Here’s a question straight out of sci-fi: What if AI could predict what you were going to do before you decided to, even in a completely new situation?
Israeli startup 42AI says it is developing a “behavioral world model” that can do just that, and it has just come out of stealth with $62.5 million in seed funding. The company is initially running its predictive system at a handful of financial brokerages, where it says the model can help predict how individual traders will respond to volatile conditions.
“Behavior is not deterministic; it’s constantly probabilistic,” 42AI’s CEO and co-founder Amit Gal-Or told TechCrunch when I asked him to justify his company’s tall claim. He argues that people have underlying psychological tendencies that influence their decisions even when their circumstances change, and that’s what the startup is trying to measure and convert into data that the model can understand.
“A week from now, you might be a different person, but your tendencies will always play out in certain areas to a probability or distribution,” his co-founder and CTO Ehud Ezra added. And that, the company argues, is what the model does: show the probability of a person making a certain decision.
42AI says the model tries to figure out people’s internal motivations instead of extrapolating patterns based on their previous actions. To do that, 42AI examines how people react to seemingly unrelated events. For an investor or trader, for example, such events might include completing KYC processes, onboarding, support calls, money transfers, or trading stocks.
“When the person is talking to support or sales in the initial process […] we’re looking at the sentiment, we’re looking at the voice, we’re looking at a lot of interactions that they’re doing, how many times do they call, and so forth,” added Ezra. “And all of those data points are going into the model. Later on, you have the interactions: how you buy and sell, and when, and we’re looking at the market at that stage.”
“So we’re trying to look at how they’re looking at the world. In a world model, every individual is the world,” he added.
The company then converts those observations into mathematical representations of traits, which are then fed into a transformer-based world model that lays out probabilities for possible decisions under different circumstances. (The company uses a separate language model to generate explanations for its predictions.)
Ezra said the model is trained to preserve a person’s underlying tendencies during simulations. So if someone is usually risk-averse, but the model suddenly treats them as a risk-taker, “the target is off,” he said, explaining how the model’s training rewards are structured.
42AI spent three years developing this approach, combining psychological research with neuroscience and genetics, as well as advice from experts in psychology and PTSD, and Dan Shechtman, who was awarded the 2011 Nobel Prize in Chemistry.
Gal-Or said the team first studied thousands of people via psych assessments and behavioral scenarios, before adding government and biobank datasets covering more than 2 million people. Its customer datasets have since expanded to cover almost 30 million people, he said.
In live deployments, the model analyzes customers’ existing records, including transactions and support calls. Gal-Or pointed out the system doesn’t need personally identifiable information, arguing that “the model never knows who the person is.” However, it seems the company still creates individual behavioral profiles from linked customer activity.
In predicting market trades, 42AI claims its model achieves 98% accuracy, but Gal-Or clarified that this is more a measure of whether trading flows fall within a predicted range, not if each trade was predicted correctly.
He also said the model consistently achieves an F1 score of 0.8 to 0.85 for predicting trading direction and volume. (F1 score is a metric in machine learning that measures how well a classification model can balance correct predictions against false alarms and missed events.)
And it’s not like the model can work everywhere, given it’s still a machine in a box. “There’s a natural ceiling because you really don’t have access to everything that a person experiences,” Gal-Or said. Ezra explained that the system’s accuracy also suffers when it misses important situational context, like if someone chooses to change their trading choices based on advice from a friend or a colleague.
Gal-Or said 42AI is working with 10 large brokerages, who are using it to manage liquidity, margin requirements, and hedging exposure. The company is onboarding more customers, he said, and is generating more than “seven figures” in revenue, though he wouldn’t specify a number.
Customers pay the startup a flat fee, or a percentage of the value the platform helps them gain. He claimed one brokerage generated approximately $30 million in additional value through dynamic hedging.
The $62.5 million seed funding came from the founders and Infinity Group over three years, and wasn’t entirely raised from external investors, Gal-Or said. Infinity was founded by his father, Amir Gal-Or, who is also 42AI’s chairman and a co-founder. 42AI now plans to raise more money.
Beyond finance, 42AI is now exploring applications for its model in gaming, fraud prevention, and cybersecurity, and one day wants to use it to help robots and AI agents understand people. But that may be a ways off yet: Ezra acknowledged that the model needs to be trained on rules and context for each new domain.
And like with all AI tools, there’s a potentially troubling side to 42AI’s technology: If a brokerage can anticipate what its customers may do, what’s to stop someone from using that knowledge to their own benefit? Ezra admitted that this was “a valid concern” when I raised it, and the founders pointed to regulations, monitoring, and an internal ethics committee, though they didn’t explain what, if anything, would stop their customers from using the technology to gain an advantage over the very people it’s profiling.
Amit Gal-Or, however, was confident that the technology could help address such risks. “We are very proud that actually we are part of the solution; we are the solution to this problem,” he said.

Elon Musk throttles Grok Bot limits due to ‘unsustainably high’ costs
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-02
16:23:21 UTC
01:23:21 KST

Over Halloween weekend, some Grok Bot users awoke to something especially spooky: Their weekly usage limits were unexpectedly maxed out, even if they hadn’t been using SpaceX’s agentic AI overnight. Some users reported on X that their usage jumped from as low as 50% to 100%.
SpaceX CEO Elon Musk confirmed in an X post on Sunday that this was not a glitch.
“The cost of serving Grok Bot was unsustainably high relative to revenue,” he wrote in a reply to a question from a user. He has not addressed the issue on his main feed but has continued boasting that Grok Bot can help you buy gifts for your family.
Grok Bot has been positioned as a success story for the newly public SpaceX, which said in a filing last month that AI now makes up 52% of company income. Its renamed “Super Intelligence” division actually turned a profit of $431 million, which is a rarity in the AI industry. Its profit would’ve been higher if not for $1.2 billion of one-time costs related to its acquisition of Cursor. Musk recently referred to Grok Bot as having “the fastest growth of a product that I’ve ever seen in my life.”
Musk has not yet shared how subscribers’ usage limits will be impacted moving forward.

Grokipedia offers lessons in how AI can help Wikipedia
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-02
16:15:26 UTC
01:15:26 KST

When I saw Grokipedia’s articles about me, the memories came rushing back: my 19 years in minor league baseball, including that impressive season with the Montgomery Rebels when I hit 20 home runs. I was lean back then — 6 feet tall and 168 pounds — and earned a career batting average just under .300. I became a minor league coach and managed the Chicago White Sox for a short stint in 1970, which Grokipedia said “highlighted [my] readiness for higher-level responsibilities.”
I then attended Arizona State University, switched to a career in journalism, created the fact-checking site PolitiFact, won a Pulitzer Prize, and now teach journalism at Duke University.
Ah, the memories!…except that I never played minor league baseball, never managed the White Sox, and can only dream about being 6 feet tall. (I’m 5’8″.) Grokipedia, the AI-written encyclopedia of Elon Musk, has confused me with Marion Danne “Bill” Adair, who died in 2002.
That article and another one about “Bill Adair (journalist)” — which really is me — illustrate the many flaws of Grokipedia, which launched a year ago. The articles are littered with mistakes and superfluous details. They are terribly written and ridiculously long. If Grok, the AI engine that wrote this turgid prose, were a student in one of my writing courses, I would give it a D.
Still, the articles provide valuable lessons for Wikipedia editors who are debating how and when to use AI. Grokipedia adds new content and context that Wikipedia often lacks. It shows that AI is actually pretty good at finding tidbits in the far corners of the internet and summarizing them in sweeping prose. It can also provide balance to articles that need it.
Grok can’t write worth a lick, but it shows how Wikipedia can take advantage of AI at a time when overworked editors are trying to keep the encyclopedia alive.
Musk launched Grokipedia as an alternative to Wikipedia, which he said was too liberal. (He called it “Wokepedia.”) Still, he trusted it enough to have Grok copy hundreds of thousands of its articles, which is permitted under Wikipedia’s Creative Commons license, and then add content from elsewhere on the internet.
With his usual bluster, Musk said his encyclopedia used a new version of his large language model that was good at “cogent analysis.” He said programmers told it to “cycle through the million most popular articles in Wikipedia and add, modify, and delete.”
When Grokipedia launched, Musk said he had big aspirations to overtake Wikipedia, which has 68 million articles in more than 300 languages. He said his encyclopedia would be more complete and more balanced and would make smarter use of images and video.
Maybe someday.
For now, Grokipedia is a massive collection of overly long articles that are often out of date. Its flaws have been well-documented: Articles are littered with mistakes, peddle conspiracy theories, rely on low-credibility sources, and sometimes skew to the right. And although Grokipedia shows edit requests from readers and whether they are approved, the site is not as transparent as Wikipedia, which reveals details about its editors and every change they make.
Last spring, Grokipedia mysteriously stopped updating, according to a report by researchers Renée DiResta and Ronald Robertson. It resumed in late September, still a hot mess, but with a more colorful homepage that always seems to feature its article about Musk.
Grokipedia has two articles about me: the conflated baseball player one and “Bill Adair (journalist),” the real one, which is incredibly boring, even to me. Both borrow heavily from Wikipedia’s articles about us and enrich them with details found on assorted websites.
Wikipedia’s article about me is nicely concise — just 700 words. It is written in the encyclopedia’s signature dry style and doesn’t contain any errors. The article recounts my career, some of the major news events I’ve covered, the two books I’ve written, and the awards I’ve won. But it highlights trivial events from my career, fails to emphasize larger ones, and lacks any criticism of my work. (I have not tried to improve the article because Wikipedia’s conflict of interest policy prohibits people from editing their own bios.)
By contrast, Grokipedia’s “Bill Adair (journalist)” is painfully long — 5,600 words. Its greatest value would be to help someone suffering from a sleep disorder.
Like a college student trying to meet a word count, Grok stuffed the article with needless details and redundant descriptions. The article tells you about my work with PolitiFact and then tells you again and again. It explains the site’s rating scale eight times and mentions my academic title four times.
Despite labels that say “fact-checked by Grok,” both articles had lots of errors. I spotted 32 in the journalist one and so many in the baseball mashup that I couldn’t count them all. Mistakes are not surprising in AI writing, but I discovered interesting patterns that reveal the weaknesses of large language models.
In the article about the real me, only seven of the errors were hallucinations in which Grok seemed to create a detail out of thin air. No, I did not win an award from Manhattan College, and no, PolitiFact did not begin by fact-checking the Tampa mayor’s race.
Five other errors showed Grok was a lazy researcher that did not look broadly enough to check a claim. Grok said I am the director of Duke’s journalism program, but I left that job five years ago. The sources cited in the article don’t explain Grok’s mistake, so it seems likely it remembered an old announcement and failed to see a newer one that named my successor.
The most prevalent errors were in a broad category I’d all cluelessness. These mistakes showed Grok didn’t fully understand what it read, like someone who skimmed an article too quickly. My favorite in this category was when I got credit for a big scoop in 2000 that George W. Bush had a DUI conviction, which the Grokipedia article said had an impact on “late-election discourse.”
I wish! Yes, I was among the many journalists who covered that story, but I had no scoop.
Together, the errors reveal that Grok is a knucklehead. It doesn’t know what’s important and what’s not. It is simply mashing together details from many web articles about me. Grok only knows what it found on the internet and it lacks experience and perspective to sort out what’s important.
The writing is dreadful. The articles read as if Grok was trained on too many online resumes and corporate memos. Grok uses words and phrases like “commenced” and “transitioned” and “multidisciplinary practicum” and “hypotheses on fact-check efficacy.” A typical sentence: “Adair’s approach in this position reflected a commitment to undiluted sourcing and causal analysis of policy impacts, influencing subsequent innovations in political journalism.”
Take a close look at that sentence and you’ll see it’s just a mush of buzzwords. I don’t know what “undiluted sourcing” is and have never used the phrase, nor would I refer to “causal analysis”…of anything. Grok is trying to impress us with an MBA vocabulary but is really just an epic bullshitter.
Bernadette Meehan, CEO of the Wikimedia Foundation, which provides money and technical infrastructure for the encyclopedia, has called AI “an existential threat” to Wikipedia.
The 25-year-old encyclopedia has long been a source and beneficiary of Google searches. Wikipedia provides the answers and in turn benefits from readers clicking through to read articles. But when people make a query to a chatbot, they often get the answer they need in an AI summary and don’t click through to read the encyclopedia article. That threatens donations because fewer readers see Wikipedia’s fundraising pitches.
Wikipedia has banned content generated by LLMs, but that hasn’t stopped many contributors — some well-intentioned, some not — from dumping an avalanche of AI-written content on the site. That has put a huge burden on the site’s “New pages patrol,” the editors who approve articles, because they must weed out the lousy content. (Wikipedia has a helpful guide of LLMs’ favorite words and phrases, such as “tapestry” and “meticulously” and sentences that start with “additionally.”)
Still, terrible as it is, Grokipedia offers lessons in how AI can help Wikipedia.
Grok’s voracious internet searches sometimes find things that human editors don’t. The long article about me includes a substantive discussion of my research about lying in politics and my work with fact-checkers around the world, neither of which are in the Wikipedia version.
Grokipedia also provides some welcome balance. When it offers context, Grokipedia can be a little heavy with hype (“His work has been lauded for innovating transparency in media…”) But it also included a “Criticisms and Controversies” section that details complaints that PolitiFact is biased against Republicans. That section has a healthy back and forth about the critiques and my response. That kind of balance could be helpful for Wikipedia, which has taken heat from Musk and others for being too lefty.
Musk’s encyclopedia also has a playful spirit that Wikipedia could learn from. Grokipedia’s homepage features random samples of recent edits that provide a glimpse at how the site is being improved. Another Grokipedia feature reads articles aloud in an AI voice you select: Eve, who sounds like a BBC anchor; Ara, a serious woman with crisp diction; Leo, who might have been a professor at Oxford; and Sal, whose name suggests a sports bar but whose voice is NPR.
My big takeaway from Grok is the importance of human judgment in curating knowledge. Grok is a bot trying to behave like a human editor. Its many mistakes and lousy writing show that AI is not ready to replace Wikipedia’s talented volunteers. Still, Grok shows that AI can be a tool that helps them research and write better articles.
After seeing an AI encyclopedia up close through my experiences with Grok, I come away with hope. Yes, AI can be a sloppy researcher and an awful writer. But Grok’s performance, shaky as it was, highlighted AI’s readiness for higher-level responsibilities.
Bill Adair, the Knight Professor of the Practice of Journalism and Public Policy at Duke University, is writing a book about how Wikipedia editors are adapting to the age of AI.

====================================================================================================

The video stays on your device: nothing is uploaded to a server.
Shoot video and photos as if on an old VHS camcorder, or give a video you already have the VHS look. Everything is processed on your device; nothing is uploaded to a server.
VHS Camera is a free VHS filter for your camera: shoot video and photos that look as if they came off an old camcorder tape, with colour bleed, tape noise and a date stamp. It runs in the browser, so there is no app to install.
Already have a clip? Tap UPLOAD A VIDEO on the start screen, or UPLOAD VIDEO in the camera, and pick a video from your phone or computer. It plays with the VHS effect right away, so you can try the tapes, the quality, the date stamp and the sound while you watch. Then save the whole video as an MP4 with its original sound. You don’t need a camera for this, and the video is processed on your device, never uploaded to a server. The sound gets the VHS treatment too: Tape sound, under the Sound button, adds a soft tape hiss, a slight wobble and muffled highs to uploaded videos and LIVE recordings, and one tap turns it off.
The VHS effect is calculated on your phone or computer, frame by frame. Your video and photos are never uploaded to a server.
Yes. There is no sign-up and nothing to install; it works in the browser.
Yes, in a current mobile browser such as Safari or Chrome with camera access. On slower phones, the settings let you trade some detail for a smoother frame rate.

Moving our warehouse off DuckDB
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-02
16:02:26 UTC
01:02:26 KST

Why we're moving our warehouse off DuckDB
Contents
Five months ago, we rebuilt our warehouse on DuckDB instead of ClickHouse. We did it because a shared ClickHouse cluster couldn't handle hours-long modeling queries, and it lacked a cost-based query optimizer. DuckDB gave every customer their own instance, and tools like dbt could connect over Postgres. We're still fans.
But we underestimated how big and complex our customers' workloads would get. We didn't want them to have just a good experience, we wanted the best experience you can get when you pay someone else to run your warehouse. It should work at any scale, at a fair price.
So we spent the last three months re-architecting it, this time without DuckDB.
DuckDB is brilliant on one machine
We love DuckDB. It's probably the best single-node tool for analyzing heaps of data, and that node can be your laptop. It's robust and reliable. It almost always finishes your query, and if it runs out of memory, it spills to disk.
The same features that are great locally gave us problems in the cloud. Customers ran more complex queries than we expected, so we had to add huge instances. Adding disks was an option, but queries would have been too slow and expensive to be viable.
To run a single-node engine safely in the cloud, we have to size the worker before the query arrives. The ergonomics here are wonky. We couldn't find a good way to tell customers what resources their queries would need. Predicting CPU and memory for a specific query was even harder.
Users shouldn't have to worry about this. They should be able to run whatever they need to get their work done. Our job is to handle the rest and make it as predictable as possible.
We hit other bottlenecks too, like fetching lots of data from S3 without async fetching. DuckDB only added that recently.
DuckLake is built for compatibility, not scale
To use DuckDB as a fully-fledged data warehouse, we installed the DuckLake extension by default. It is a new lakehouse format from the same creators and follows the same principles as DuckDB: keep things simple and run on almost any setup. That works well up to a point, but at PostHog's scale, we needed something designed for distributed scale from the start.
DuckLake's catalog schema has to stay portable across many SQL databases, which limits engine-specific optimizations. At our scale, a single-table write could take more than three minutes because every commit re-read the statistics for the entire catalog.
We tuned DuckLake with table indexes and similar tweaks. But to keep offering DuckLake, we had to stay 100% compatible with its existing, "official" schema. This left us with fewer optimization options.
Another problem is that over 99% of the catalog's statistics rows belonged to tables that no longer existed. Cleaning them up made writes 30 to 50 times faster, but the rows kept growing back.
We also spent a lot of time on the number of connections DuckLake opens to metadata databases. That hurts on shared Postgres instances, where every connection has a real cost.
1 trillion rows was a challenge
DuckLake at PostHog didn't start small and ramp up. We fully committed and put all of PostHog in a DuckLake to dogfood as the first customer.
When we moved off it, it held about 2 PB: roughly 1 trillion rows and 15.3 million snapshots. It broke in lots of ways that were hard to fix within DuckLake's scope.
We'd rather have contributed to the existing community and helped it grow. But we couldn't land changes as fast as we needed, so we ended up carrying patches in a fork which defeats one of the main benefits of using DuckLake: the community.
Speaking Postgres is harder than it sounds
Offering direct Postgres connections over port 5432 sounds like a great idea. In practice, it was much harder to scale than HTTP.
Missing reconnects and managing TTLs and idle connections at scale is hard, and long-running connections are expensive. There's also very little tooling for load balancing pgwire compared with HTTP. Compare the ergonomics of an AWS NLB with an ALB and you'll see what we mean. HTTP headers also make routing in Kubernetes much more flexible.
There's also an endless list of Postgres clients, and each expects its own niche Postgres behavior. Customers kept asking us to fix their client or library. We spent a lot of time shipping many of those fixes, but coverage never got good enough. We'd rather spend our time building an excellent warehouse than chasing the long tail of Postgres compatibility.
We're rebuilding on Trino with HogLake
We've learned not to hype the tech behind a warehouse so much. What counts is the experience it gives you.
Still, we're happy to share what we're cooking next. We rebuilt everything around our own flavor of Trino and created our own open table format, HogLake. And yes, we're testing it at PostHog's petabyte scale first. We made it solid before handing it to design partners.
If your current warehouse is slow or expensive, let's talk, especially if you have a lot of data to query. We're always looking for design partners.
PostHog is your product's context layer. With a full suite of developer tools – AI observability, product analytics, session replay, feature flags, experiments, error tracking, logs, and more – PostHog ingests and stores all the data agents need to diagnose problems, uncover opportunities, and ship fixes. A data warehouse and CDP tie it all together, unifying that context into one source agents can read across. You can steer it all from Slack, the web app, the desktop (PostHog Desktop), or your own editor via the MCP.

Supermarket Freezers Across Europe Hit By Second Suspected Cyberattack
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-02
16:00:00 UTC
01:00:00 KST

Supermarket Freezers Across Europe Hit By Second Suspected Cyberattack (theguardian.com) 81
			
		 	
				An anonymous reader quotes a report from The Guardian: Supermarket chains in Sweden, Belgium and Luxembourg have said the temperatures of their refrigerators and freezers in dozens of stores rose overnight, prompting fears of a cyber-attack similar to one that caused millions of euros of food losses in France. Sweden's Coop chain told the daily Aftonbladet on Monday that six of its stores had been affected, while ICA said it was "aware that a limited number of stores were affected by an incident overnight which impacted their refrigeration and freezer systems".
The Dutch retailer Ahold Delhaize, which last month reported a cybersecurity incident to police in the Netherlands, said an "IT incident" had hit fridges and freezers in dozens of its supermarkets in Belgium and Luxembourg. A company spokesperson, Sarah De Meester, said the supermarket chain had established that temperatures in the cooling systems had increased, adding that the incident was being investigated. Belgium's Le Soir newspaper reported that dozens of stores and supermarkets were affected, including five Spar outlets, multiple Shop & Go convenience stores attached to Q8 service stations, and a number of smaller Proxy Delhaize minimarkets. "We started mobilizing technicians at 1am," Stephan Moelans, the chief executive of Sabcobel, which specialises in refrigeration system installations, told the paper. "We spent all night and morning disconnecting the affected installations from the grid."

Technofeudalism
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-02
15:53:30 UTC
00:53:30 KST

Technofeudalism
Technofeudalism is a modern economic system where big technology companies have power similar to feudal lords in the past. Instead of land, these companies control digital platforms, data, and online markets. People and smaller businesses rely on these platforms, just like peasants once relied on feudal lords for land and protection.
Origins of the idea
[edit]
As a subject technofeudalism sits at the intersection of two subject areas, Information technology and Economics, and the concept has cropped up independently within both disciplines.
In Information Technology area Bruce Schneier wrote an article in November 2012 entitled "When It Comes to Security, We're Back to Feudalism"[1], which was published in Wired Magazine, another in December 2012 entitled "Feudal Security"[2], and another "You Have No Control Over Security on the Feudal Internet"[3] for Harvard Business Review in June 2013. Since then the term has entered the vocabulary of sites such as Slashdot.
Within Economics the concept of technofeudalism was described in an article[4] by Marina Gorbis of the Institute for the Future in 2010 and has been developed by several thinkers who argue that digital capitalism is shifting toward a new kind of economic control. While the term "technofeudalism" was later popularised by Durand (2020) and Varoufakis (2023), its conceptual roots can be traced to Kostakis and Bauwens, whose Network Society and Future Scenarios for a Collaborative Economy (2014) described "netarchical capitalism" as a "new-feudal form of cognitive capitalism"[5] in which centralised platforms enable user cooperation but appropriate all monetised exchange value, leaving the actual creators of value unrewarded. One of the most well-known voices on this topic is Yanis Varoufakis, an economist and former Greek finance minister. He argues that companies like Google, Amazon, Facebook, and Apple do not simply compete in markets—they own and control them, just like feudal lords controlled land in the Middle Ages.
French philosopher Cédric Durand also explores this idea in his book Technoféodalisme: Critique de l’économie numérique (2020), where he describes how digital platforms extract wealth from users, workers, and businesses without true competition. He claims that today’s economy is no longer based on traditional capitalism, where businesses compete, but on a system where a few tech giants rule over digital space.
These thinkers believe that rather than creating a free and open Internet, digital platforms have built a system where power is concentrated in the hands of a few, making it harder for smaller players to succeed.
Key features
[edit]
Technofeudalism is so called because users of the term perceive similarities between present-day technological systems and medieval feudal systems:
- Control over platforms – Large companies own and manage platforms like social media, e-commerce, and cloud computing, deciding who can participate.[6]
- Dependence on Big Tech – Businesses and workers must follow platform rules to earn money, similar to how peasants depended on lords.[7]
- Data ownership – Tech companies collect and use personal data, which gives them more power over users.[8]
- Limited competition – A few companies dominate entire industries, making it hard for smaller businesses to compete.[9]
See also
[edit]
References
[edit]
- ↑ Schneier, Bruce (2012-09-26). "When It Comes to Security, We're Back to Feudalism". Schneier on Security.
- ↑ Schneier, Bruce (2012-12-03). "Feudal Security". Schneier on Security.
- ↑ Schneier, Bruce (2013-06-06). "You Have No Control Over Security on the Feudal Internet". Schneier on Security.
- ↑ Gorbin, Marina (2010-09-01). "Ain't Gonna Work on Arianna's Farm No More". Institute for the Future.
- ↑ Kostakis, Vasilis; Bauwens, Michel (2014). Network Society and Future Scenarios for a Collaborative Economy. London: Palgrave Pivot. p. 23. doi:10.1057/9781137406897. OL 26384680M.
- ↑ Taghizade, Elgun; Ahmadov, Elchin (2025). "Techno Feudalism and the New Global Power Struggle: Echoes of a Digital Cold War". International Journal of Research and Innovation in Social Science. 9 (2): 1150–1151. doi:10.47772/IJRISS.2025.9020093. ISSN 2454-6186. Today, instead of land, technology companies like Amazon, Google, Facebook, and others hold control over vast digital infrastructures, data, and online platforms, positioning themselves as the new 'lords' of the digital realm. ...Under techno-feudalism, cloud capital functions much the same way as digital 'land' in creating virtual domains or 'digital fiefdoms,' such as Amazon.com. In those fiefdoms, platform owners not only extract value from the consumers but also the producers by imposing fees, which are similar to the ground rent of feudal times.
- ↑ Taghizade & Ahmadov (2025): 1151. "These platforms become so essential to modern life—much like utilities such as electricity or telecoms—that societies become dependent on them, making it nearly impossible to function without their services. This dependence gives tech companies immense power, much like feudal lords once held over land and resources."
- ↑ Taghizade & Ahmadov (2025): 1151. "French economist Cédric Durand, in his 2020 book Technoféodalisme: Critique de l’Économie Numérique, argues that Big Tech companies have created monopolies by using advanced methods to extract and control data."
- ↑ Taghizade & Ahmadov (2025): 1151. "Varoufakis believes this is the end of capitalism and the beginning of a new system using technology to extract cloud rent, not profit, as the main source of wealth accumulation."

- Varoufakis, Yanis (2023). Technofeudalism: What Killed Capitalism. New York City: Vintage Books. OL 34024904W.
