Three Dots
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-02
15:13:36 UTC
00:13:36 KST

Last Friday, Reddit had its worst day since its IPO, with a drop of almost 30%1. It didn’t matter that they beat their third-quarter estimates; what mattered most was a weak Q4 forecast and the quote from Reddit CEO Steve Huffman, “Our search referral traffic was down meaningfully in Q3, with the decline accelerating later in the quarter.” He also wrote, “During October, we have seen that search traffic has dropped by more than 1 million referrals on average per week.”
There’s no baseline to understand whether 1 million referrals is a lot or a little, but there is no doubt a stock dropping 30% in a day is a lot.
Reddit, which I am certain looks at the data daily, has seen this coming for years; hence their other announcements that shape a larger strategy:
- Testing a logged-in-only experience outside the U.S.
- Shutting down RSS feeds
- Shutting down the public API
- Moving data access to its Developer Platform, which makes all Reddit data access paid only.
The Three Dots Are Pretty Easy to Connect
Dot #1: Traditional Google search: you search for X, Google searches for X and provides a list of high-quality X links, which — if the links are high quality — means you click on X, which is a Reddit post about X.
Google would greatly prefer if you didn’t click on X because the moment you do, you’ve left Google and they’ve mostly lost their ability to present you with advertising. You read that right: the reason you go to Google is to find other high-quality stuff that they don’t want you to actually see. My favorite attempt was AMP, Accelerated Mobile Pages, where publishers built lightweight copies of their content, and Google served them from their own servers. Yes, you were promised a traffic advantage, but you lose control over design, ads, and analytics.
Google dropped AMP’s search perks in 2021 in the face of continued criticism, but all they really had to do was wait a few years because a vastly better solution would present itself.
Dot #2: I’m guessing sometime in 2022, all the AI players started sucking down all the data in order to train ever-improving models. The more data? The better the models. It’s vastly more complicated than that, but alarms at Reddit apparently started going off in April of 2023, when they moved to charge for API access. The CEO quote: “… [w]e don’t need to give all of that value to some of the largest companies in the world for free.”
While content producers perhaps saw a massive theft of intellectual property, content consumers did not. What they eventually saw was the eerily accurate answer to the dumb throwaway question they typed into their first chatbot. They thought, “Golly, that’s cool.”2 Sub-dot thought: Like Google, AI players have paradoxical motivations: the goal is to get you to use the services so they can help, but helping doesn’t pay the bills. Using more tokens is the business, which means that, like Google, the gig is to keep helping whether you want the help or not.
Dot #3: Present day. Google has a feature called AI Overviews, which you’ve already seen. It’s a human-sounding summary that attempts to answer your questions. There is no way to turn it off — it exists to keep you on Google. The research shows that when an AI Overview shows, clicks on the first result drop 58%. More than half the folks who would have clicked that top result are no longer leaving, which means more sweet, sweet ad revenue.
That’s Not the Dot
I’ve spent the past decade watching the blogging community vanish. There are the stalwarts, but the vast majority of bloggers either stopped or left for lighter-weight walled gardens. Sure, blogging was a fad. Writing, however, is not. I get it — it’s easier to write a pithy one-line Reddit post than a fully fleshed-out thought. Guilty as charged.
I giggle when folks mention Google is in trouble because of declining search usage. First, decades of search habits will take time to disassemble and reassemble. Second, if you are using Google Search less, I’m guessing you’re still using Gmail and similar Google services. If you’re like me and have wired the robots into Gmail, you’re using Gmail significantly more. Yes, I’ve turned off every switch that says “don’t train on me,” but go throw those switches yourself and report on how confident you are that you flipped them all successfully.
I started this piece with Reddit because the company provides a service that creates tremendous value, and I’m not talking about the monetary value; I’m talking about the social value. Early on in my robot journeys, I asked Claude to source any fact it shared with me. I skimmed the sources and was surprised to see how often a random Reddit post was the source of the robot’s thoughts.
Yes, the robot sucked down all the books (including all of mine). Yes, they did the same with countless images, as well. I feel we haven’t yet seen the full set of legal consequences, but that’s not the dot: it’s the value of humans talking with other humans, building things for each other — often simple, frequently hilarious, and doing the work to document why they did what they did… because they want to help.
This was supposed to be a brief introduction to a new feature I’m launching on Rands on Tuesday, but it turned into a whole bunch of dots explaining the disproportionate value of what happens when humans hang with other humans.
See you Tuesday.

Vivo’s first Pro Max phone proves its Ultra is still in a league of its own
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-02
15:13:26 UTC
00:13:26 KST

When I declared this spring that Vivo’s X300 Ultra had the best cameras in any phone, two things made it stand out in my mind: its excellent main camera with a narrow 35mm focal length and what remains the most capable ultrawide in any phone worldwide.
Vivo’s first Pro Max phone proves its Ultra is still in a league of its own
The X500 Pro Max is great, but it’s a step back from my favorite phone camera.
You won’t find either in the new X500 Pro Max. It has an impressive camera system with world-leading dynamic range and the same great telephoto lens found in the Ultra. But it’s lost a little something of what made its predecessor quite so special.
Vivo X500 Pro Max
The Good
- Fantastic telephoto camera
- 17 stops of dynamic range on the main camera
- Big 7,800mAh battery
- 1440p, 144Hz display
The Bad
- Ultrawide camera is a weak link
- Drops the X300 Ultra’s 35mm main camera
- Expensive
The X500 Pro Max is the latest in a trend that’s seen China’s major Android players roll out Pro Max models, including Xiaomi, Oppo, Honor, and now Vivo. The new models close the gap between their previous Pro flagships and camera-centric Ultra models — or, if you prefer to be cynical about it, are an excuse to slap “Max” on the box to better mask RAM-induced price hikes. A little of column A, a little of column B, I suspect.
The Pro Max adopts plenty of flagship features found in the older Ultra. It’s the same size, for one, and has a matching 1440p, 144Hz OLED display. It’s got an even bigger battery — 7,800mAh — which breezes through two days of use, with similar 90W wired and 40W wireless charging. Unlike the Ultra it doesn’t use the latest Snapdragon chipset, but its MediaTek Dimensity 9600 Pro delivers roughly comparable performance. It even features the exact same periscope telephoto camera, one of the best in any phone. Then there’s the price: €1,799 (about $2,000) puts this squarely in the ultra-premium bracket.
But despite all that, this isn’t a true successor to the X300 Ultra, which is still rumored to see a direct sequel sometime next year. The X500 Pro Max still sits half a step down in Vivo’s hierarchy, and so it would be a little unfair to expect it to match or beat the older Ultra. The problem isn’t that this camera is worse than that phone’s, though, but that it’s much less unique.
As you’d expect from a step-down model, the 50-megapixel, 1/1.28-inch-type main camera sensor is both smaller and lower resolution than the Ultra’s, impacting its quality. But the newer phone offsets that a little thanks to one edge on the Ultra: It uses Sony’s new Lytia L910 image sensor, which features LOFIC technology that enables a wider 17-stop dynamic range, with the enhancements primarily in the highlights.
But the X300 Ultra’s camera wasn’t only notable for technical quality. I loved the fact that it adopted a 35mm focal length, and am disappointed that the Pro Max returns to the same 24mm focal length found on iPhones and countless others.
This, like most phones’ main cameras, is a wide-angle in truth. It’s designed to take in as much of a scene as possible, reserving the ultrawide for truly large landscape shots. It’s part of why I’ve taken to stepping back and using the telephoto by default on many phones, enjoying the tighter framing and better bokeh, but that often brings other quality compromises, especially in lower light.
I’m stubborn about shooting with my cameras’ full focal lengths whenever I can, and you should be too.
A 35mm main camera, by contrast, feels closer to many of the kit and prime lenses you might grab for a regular camera, narrow enough to use for portraits and purposeful framing. It sits more evenly in between the 14mm ultrawide and 85mm telephoto, giving you natural options across the range. You could of course just use the X500 Pro Max’s 24mm main camera and zoom in a little, but then you’re wasting some of the sensor and relying on digital zoom tricks, rather than making the most of the lens’s optics. I’m stubborn about shooting with my cameras’ full focal lengths whenever I can, and you should be too.
If there’s a saving grace to the decision to return to a wider main camera, it’s that it helps make up for the X500 Pro Max’s other shortcoming: a much smaller ultrawide camera sensor.
The ethos of the X300 Ultra was that all three lenses should deliver comparable quality. As such, it treated its ultrawide like another main camera, with a large sensor and even optical image stabilization, a rarity on wide lenses. In fact, its sensor is the same size as the X500 Pro Max’s main camera, so a repeat of that feat was never especially likely, but the 1/2.75-inch-type ultrawide in the X500 Pro Max is still a notable step down.
Taking the phone to a nighttime Champions League game, there was a clear gap in quality moving from the ultrawide to the other two lenses. Under the bright stadium lights, the ultrawide blew out the white details on the pitch and player kits and left the crowd in comparative darkness. The main and ultrawide were able to bring more detail out of the dimly lit spectators and better handle the highlights on the pitch, without entirely giving up on the contrast that gave the scene its character.
It’s important to put these gripes in context: This is a truly excellent camera system for a phone, and still more capable than any you can officially buy in the US. The returning telephoto in particular is exceptional; every lens benefits from Vivo’s naturalistic color-tuning, developed together with Zeiss, and video capabilities include Log recording and 4K at 240fps slow motion. It supports Vivo’s teleconverter lenses too, including a seriously chunky 400mm option. It’s just a shame that the most interesting elements of the Ultra’s camera haven’t trickled down to its first Pro Max too.
Strangely, another phone I’ve tested this month feels like it picked up where the X300 Ultra left off: Oppo’s Find X10 Pro Max, a pretty direct rival to this phone. Its triple 200-megapixel camera system still uses a wide-angle main camera, but at least includes a substantially improved ultrawide camera to create that same feeling that each of its three rear lenses is of comparable quality.
All I hope now is that when the X500 Ultra rolls around come spring, it builds on the success of the older Ultra, and perhaps finds new ways to make its cameras stand out. After all, if the Pro Max costs €1,799, the Ultra could cost substantially more — and will have its work cut out to prove it’s worth it.

Could data center backlash flip Florida?
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-02
15:03:31 UTC
00:03:31 KST

Patti Zirhut, a lifelong Republican, has been studying her ballot for weeks. Zirhut eagerly voted for President Donald Trump in 2016, 2020, and 2024, but she isn’t toeing the party line this year. “My ballot is all mixed up,” she says. She’s voting for some Republicans, some independents, and even a few Democrats. Zirhut remains undecided on a few races, but of one thing she is absolutely certain: “I will not vote for Byron Donalds,” she tells me. “No way will I ever vote for him.”
Could data center backlash flip Florida?
Bipartisan outrage has been growing for months — but Republicans’ many advantages could still help them win.
Could data center backlash flip Florida?
Bipartisan outrage has been growing for months — but Republicans’ many advantages could still help them win.
Donalds, the Republican nominee in Florida’s gubernatorial election, has Trump’s endorsement, the party’s backing, and $148 million in campaign funds, much of it from the tech industry. It’s this latest point that particularly rankles Zirhut, an organizer with a group called No Data Center Hernando County, whose purpose is summed up in its name. Donalds has been a vocal supporter of the controversial developments — a fact that may now cost him votes, even among members of his own party.
I first met Zirhut in August at a small protest in the rural community of Spring Hill, Florida. Back then, Donalds was the front-runner in a crowded Republican primary, and Zirhut told me she wouldn’t vote for him if he won. Now Donalds is the nominee in a race that is much closer than anyone anticipated, and Zirhut is sticking to her word. “I’m disgusted with all these dirty politics,” she tells me. She’s thinking about voting for Donalds’ Democratic opponent, former Rep. David Jolly.
Jolly, a former Republican who left the party in 2018, is now hoping to win over newly disgruntled conservatives like Zirhut — and his stance on data centers may help him do it. In Florida, the opposition to data centers “is more intense among Republicans than it is among Democrats,” Jolly tells The Verge, “because it is a reflection of the outsized influence and control of Big Tech and Big Data in a world where people are worried about surveillance, whether it’s AI or development encroaching on their land and their freedom.”
When it comes to data centers, the differences between Donalds and Jolly couldn’t be more stark. Jolly has traveled to Virginia’s Loudoun County, also known as Data Center Alley, to “see what massive hyperscale data centers really do to a community.” Donalds has said he wants to “make Florida the financial capital of the world.” Jolly has promised to implement a one-year moratorium on data center developments. Donalds has said he wants to welcome the tech industry — including data center developers — to the Sunshine State. “There ain’t no bans coming,” Donalds said in August. “But it sounds good on TV, or it sounds good on social media.” Donalds has since moderated his stance on data centers, trying to appeal to the 68 percent of Floridians who say they oppose the developments in their communities.
“This is an opportunity you rarely see in politics for candidates to both lead and follow,” Jolly says. “If 70 percent of the state is saying ‘Don’t do it,’ the job is to listen to voters, not to tell voters they’re wrong. But there are policy reasons, as well, to lead in this moment.”
Jolly rattled off a litany of reasons to slow down, if not altogether halt, data center development in Florida: air and noise pollution; the public health effects on people who live near data centers’ on-site power plants; and environmental concerns including water use, an issue he acknowledged is partly mitigated by the move to closed-loop air cooling. He also pointed to the rapid development of AI technology, which is advancing so quickly that facilities built today “may well be functionally obsolete in the next 10 years.”
When it comes to data centers, the differences between Donalds and Jolly couldn’t be more stark.
Donalds is quick to point out that his running mate, state Sen. Bryan Ávila, sponsored a bill regulating new data center projects that requires them to pay their fair share for water and electricity. And he’s emphasized that these developments ultimately aren’t the governor’s purview. “When it comes to data centers and local control, local governments are going to have the final say,” Donalds recently told CBS News. “Governors don’t issue building permits; local governments do. So if a local community does not want a data center, then no data centers.” But Jolly has saddled his opponent with a nickname that sums up their differences on the issue: “Data Center Donalds.” Donalds’ campaign did not respond to a request for comment.
Raul Alfonso, the chair of Watchdogs of Fort Meade, a community group fighting a $2.6 billion hyperscale data center development in deep-red Polk County, said his group has reached out to candidates in the lead-up to the election. Earlier this year, Watchdogs of Fort Meade received what Alfonso describes as a “form letter” from both Donalds and from Ron DeSantis, Florida’s current Republican governor. “It was basically, ‘We have to trust that your local city commissioners and your local city representatives will do the right thing,’” he says. The group hasn’t heard back from Jolly — which doesn’t bother Alfonso much, given Jolly’s promised moratorium. James Fishback, the controversial Republican contender who ended up with 10 percent of the primary vote, was the first to respond. Bobby Williams, a Republican candidate who garnered just 4.1 percent of the vote, has attended several events. Angie Nixon, the Democrat running to unseat Sen. Ashley Moody, hosted a town hall in Fort Meade to discuss residents’ opposition to the data centers. For Alfonso, the difference comes down to a willingness to hear locals’ concerns. Nixon, a Democratic Socialist, has little in common with the far-right Fishback, but both have engaged with the locals fighting the Fort Meade data center in a way Donalds hasn’t.
“There are a lot of people that were deep-red individuals who voted party line all the way, who are looking at flopping over to somebody who will actually take the concerns of the citizens more seriously,” Alfonso says. Alfonso’s voting history skews conservative — he voted for Trump in 2016, 2020, and 2024 — but he was a registered independent until this year, when he switched his affiliation to Republican to vote for Williams in the primary.
Williams, a pastor who had not previously run for office, has since endorsed Jolly. “The Republican Party, they kind of selected their nominee. They had their guy from the get-go: Donalds,” Williams tells me. “Big corporations are obviously buying a lot of these seats. They’re buying the governor’s role.” For Williams, data center developers are the latest in a long list of outsiders that want to have their way with Florida.
If he wins, Jolly will be the first Democratic governor Florida has elected in 28 years. The polls are close. One recent survey conducted by the University of North Florida put Jolly just two points behind Donalds, within the 2.8 percent margin of error. Donalds has several advantages over Jolly, not least of which is money. Jolly has raised $22 million to Donalds’ $148 million. There’s also the fact that Florida, once a swing state, now has nearly 2 million more registered Republicans than Democrats, in part because of DeSantis’ response to the covid-19 pandemic. The fact that Republicans currently lead Democrats in early voting may suggest that the state is unlikely to flip. But some of those Republicans, including Zirhut, are wary of Donald’s support for — and from — the tech industry, and are voting accordingly. Donalds has received $5 million from Leading the Future, a pro-AI super PAC whose funders include executives of OpenAI, Palantir, and Andreessen Horowitz.
As vocal as data center opponents may be, these developments aren’t Florida voters’ main issue. Poll after poll has shown that in Florida and elsewhere, the cost of living is top of mind for voters of all political orientations. But Jolly says the mass opposition to data centers is a manifestation of voters’ broader concerns.
“It’s almost a physical metaphor for anxieties around, ‘What is the future of work? What’s the future of wages? What does this mean for security and privacy for myself and for my kids?’” Jolly says. The huge sums of money spent on the governor’s race, he adds, are a testament to economic inequality in a state with an ever-rising cost of living. “In a state like Florida with unlimited money, where people feel like they’ve lost their political and economic power and voice, the deepest-pocketed special interests — be it tech, data, utilities — are able to capture our politicians and decision-making.”

Omen AI adds liquid know-how in $16 million Aquatic Labs acquisition
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-02
15:00:00 UTC
00:00:00 KST

U.S. data centers use 17 to 19 billion gallons of water to cool their hot-running chips each year — and that number is expected to triple in the next five years, according to The Water Foundation.
That’s an expensive and politically tricky proposition for investors throwing billions of dollars at the challenge of scaling up AI. One way to use less water is to do a better job monitoring the cooling fluids, a task that currently involves sending samples away to a laboratory for tests in a multi-day process.
Omen AI was founded in 2024 to solve that problem. The company is building a real-time sensor to determine whether valves were failing, or if bacteria growth in the system threatened to choke off water flow. After raising a $31 million Series A in June, Omen is now acquiring competitor Aquatic Labs in a $16 million cash-and-stock deal.
The deal came together after Omen founder Zach Laberge was introduced to Aquatic Labs’ CEO Allan Adams by an investor in the filtration space. Both startups were building real-time water sensors with complementary technology: Omen’s sensors looked for elements in the water, while the Aquatic Labs sensor tests its alkalinity.
Putting those two technologies in the same sensor creates a “much richer picture of the water and the fluid,” Adams told TechCrunch, while uniting their vision of automating what had previously been a slow, manual process.
The combination unites two classic species of founder: Laberge dropped out of high school to pursue his first company, which did similar fluid analysis for large construction machines that depend on hydraulics. Adams was an academic, trained as a physicist, who spent much of his career developing advanced ocean sensing technology as an MIT professor and Woods Hole Oceanographic Institute researcher before starting his company.
Now, with Adams as the company’s chief science officer, the combined teams are working to integrate both technologies into one sensor, which they expect to have on the market by early next year. Omen’s initial sensors will be monitoring chips pulling in 250 to 300 MW of power by the end of the year, worth about $5 to $7 billion, Laberge told TechCrunch, with the hope of being installed on the equivalent of 1 GW of chips by the end of 2027.
The added value is in uptime: If the fluid system gets out of whack, data center operators typically choose to dump the water rather than risk damage to their expensive equipment. That means shutting down operations until the fluid system is restored, which costs a lot of money. Monitoring lets data center operators use the same fluid for longer, and minimize the costs of a clean up.
Adams hopes to see their technologies have an impact outside of data centers as well, telling TechCrunch that “water goes into data centers, and then it comes out and gets dumped into municipal water, and you want that whole process to happen in a way that’s as safe and responsible as possible — and that means you got to measure stuff.”
AI companies once estimated as much as $4 trillion would be spent on AI infrastructure by 2030, but some investors have grown skeptical of the buildout, sending hyperscaler stocks plummeting in recent days. Omen’s team isn’t worried about shifting sentiment though, since their work comes after a data center has been built.
“We’re working customers on stuff they’re building today or have already built, and so it doesn’t really affect us because all of those projects were financed 12 months ago,” Laberge said. “Our view is, if there’s tightness, this is still going to be the biggest market in human history, even if you sliced it in half.”

C64 Basic Dungeon Crawler: Stairs, Levels and Levelling Up (Part 11)
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-02
14:56:21 UTC
23:56:21 KST

The feeling of progress is a huge part of a game’s enjoyability. I am not 100% sure that is a word, but you know what I mean!
This is the difference between a “pick up and play mini game” versus “wow, look at the time, I just spent my entire work day on this!”. Hypothetically.
As of now, our C64 BASIC dungeon has no levelling up, no character development, just one map. Don’t get me wrong, lots of fun games have one screen and no character development, but they do usually increase difficulty as your session continues.
So next we need to add progress, difficulty ramping, and character stats buffing.
You can play and examine the finished code listing by loading part11a.bas in the RGC online IDE. If you missed it, check out the previous part too (part ten).
Progress so far
While we only have one map, it is a playable one now. When you find the chalice the game ends with “YOU WIN” and a play again prompt.
Selecting to play again rolls a new character and generates a new map. All your progress is wiped, it is essentially the same as hitting ‘N’ for new game.
You can now follow the tutorials and edit the code right in your web browser with the Online Retro IDE
– No downloads, configuration, etc necessary, and it is free!
Defining what a level is now
In this version we move to have five levels. Each one generates a new map using the same generator as part ten, but with more rules around object placement and creation.
Rather than one chalice, now to leave a level you need to grab every chalice in the dungeon, only then can you walk onto the stairs. The number of chalices increases as the level goes up, and just to make it interesting it is not a simple +1:
LV is the level number, CL is chalices left. INT throws away the fraction, so levels 1 and 2 give 1, levels 3 and 4 give 2, level 5 gives 3.
CL=INT((LV+1)/2)
Your character now persists while they are still alive, keeping your hit points, stats, gold, keys and magic.
For now, the stairs on level 5 gets you out of the dungeon and wins you the game.
Win or lose, we now also show the level you reached as well as the gold acquired.
One routine builds a level
In part ten, I set the player up and drew the map in two places, starting a game, and the N key for a new game.
That code is now split in two. INITPLAYER is for everything about the character and only runs once per game. BUILDLEVEL is now everything about each level and runs once per level.
BUILDLEVEL:
PX=10 : PY=11 : OX=PX : OY=PY
WN=0 : LA=0
CL=INT((LV+1)/2)
POKE 53280,0 : POKE 53281,0
GOSUB MAPBLANK
GOSUB MAPROLL
GOSUB DISPLAY
GOSUB POPULATE
GOSUB DRAWPLAYER
RETURN
PX, PY put the player back at the start cell. WN is the “leave this level” flag. LA is the number of lines in the message log, zero because the screen has just been cleared.
A new game is GOSUB INITPLAYER then GOSUB BUILDLEVEL. Going down the stairs is GOSUB BUILDLEVEL on its own.
That is the difference between resetting the game and resetting the level, and is what lets the character survive the map generation intact.
Using the stairs
The stairs are placed in one of the four corner rooms, picked at random. Your player starts in the centre room, so the stairs can never be in the room you start in.
EX=4*INT(RND(1)*2) : EY=4*INT(RND(1)*2)
INT(RND(1)*2) is 0 or 1. Times 4 makes it 0 or 4, the first or last room across, and the same again for down.
Part ten’s map rule already makes every room reachable from every other, so any selected room should be a safe choice. I don’t check that the stairs can be reached, because by that logic they always can be.
I do force the stairs room to the open room type, and the stairs are placed in its centre cell.
IF RX=EX AND RY=EY THEN MT=1
The reason is stairs block movement until the chalice(s) are collected. We don’t want to block movement by placing the stairs in a bad spot.
Getting the position of that centre cell comes from the room numbers. A room is 4 cells wide including its left wall, and the map starts on screen row 2.
ROW=EY*4+4 : COL=EX*4+2
Gathering chalices and activating the stairs
Each chalice is still worth 10 gold and counts CL down. When it reaches zero the log tells you to “FIND THE STAIRS”.
POPULATE places as many chalices as the level needs, then sets CL to the number that were placed. PLACEONE gives up after 200 failed tries at finding empty floor, so on a crowded map a chalice could actually fail to appear. Counting only the ones that were added means the level can still be finished.
Q=CL : CL=0
IT$="{PURPLE}(" : FOR N=1 TO Q : GOSUB PLACEONE : CL=CL+PF : NEXT N
PF is PLACEONE‘s “it worked” flag, 1 or 0, so adding it up counts the successes.
Walking into the stairs with chalices still unpicked bounces the player back with “CHALICES LEFT” and the number.
With none left, the stairs set WN=1 and return. WN started life as ‘won’ in part 10, and now it means the level is won, not the entire game!
The main loop sees the flag and jumps to NEXTLEVEL. It’s the same flag-then-GOTO approach we used for winning in part ten. The stairs are found inside a GOSUB, and building a new level from in there would leave that GOSUB unfinished on BASIC’s stack every time.
Levelling up
For now, each new level rewards your character hit points equal to half your constitution, rounded down. We also add +1 to strength on even levels, and +1 dexterity on odd ones.
This is mainly to show how it can be done at this point, we can give more thought to what would be actually beneficial later in a polishing phase.
HL=INT(STATS(2)/2) : HP=HP+HL
S=LV AND 1 : STATS(S)=STATS(S)+1
AC=10+INT((STATS(1)-10)/2)
STATS(0) is strength, STATS(1) is dexterity, STATS(2) is constitution.
LV AND 1 is 1 for an odd number and 0 for an even one. AND compares the two numbers bit by bit, and the lowest bit of any odd number is 1. So the answer is also the array index of the stat to raise.
Armour class is worked out again afterwards, because it is derived from your dexterity.
Strength helps your to-hit roll and the damage you deal via its modifier. A modifier only changes on every second point, so one point of strength does not always immediately make much difference. Again, balancing character abilities versus good fortune is a balancing thing once you have code working.
The log reveals to you the new level, the stat that went up and the hit points gained.
Potions finally become magic
Collecting a * potion now adds 50 magic, up to a ceiling of 100.
IF CH=42 THEN MG=MG+50:MSG$="MAGIC UP 50":GOSUB TAKEITEM
IF MG>100 THEN MG=100
Nothing spends magic yet, but it is counted and shown, ready for the ranged attack in part twelve.
The numbers match the C version of this game, where the same pickup gives 50 and a spell costs 5 plus 1 for each cell it travels.
The status line
This means we need two more counters, your magic, and the current level. As we take up more room, the gaps between the counters went from two spaces to one. Our status line is now 31 characters out of the 40 available.
It is built with two PRINT statements. The first ends in a semicolon, which keeps the cursor on the same line for the second part.
Still on the to-do list
First, we added some progression but we really need to ramp up difficulty. Every level still has the same 3 to 6 goblins, for example.
Second, magic can be collected but not be used yet.
Lastly, we still have some RAM available, so can we fit into memory more pickups and gameplay variety?
Some questions for you:
- Should stairs work in both directions? Would revisiting a level respawn the enemies?
- Collected keys carry over, so should we encourage holding a spare key from one level so you can open a door on the next?
- Levelling up your character is automatic, should we offer the player a choice of which stat to raise?
- How many levels should there be before game over?

====================================================================================================

WTF Ubuntu?
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-02
14:52:00 UTC
23:52:00 KST

WTF Ubuntu?
2026-11-02
What the fuck, Ubuntu?
In case you missed it: CVE-2026-98432 is a critical (CVSS 9.8) bug in the Linux kernel's TCP code. It's been there since 2016, so virtually every Linux server on the planet has it, and it can be hit remotely. Anthropic says it built a working exploit that gets root on a server. A total takeover.
Debian patched it on October 16. Ubuntu still doesn't have a patch more than two weeks later. Every supported release, LTS included, is listed as "Vulnerable".
Ubuntu's website promises "timely patches for critical vulnerabilities." Ubuntu hasn't shipped the patch nine days after it was rated 9.8 and six days after a working root exploit. Very fucking timely indeed.
Software is full of this blabber where words mean nothing. Ubuntu uses words like these: "Timely." "Enterprise-grade." "Production grade." "Enterprise resilience. Developer velocity. Build with confidence on Ubuntu."
Somebody, or perhaps worse, some special team at Canonical is dedicated to writing these........
So I'm asking again... What the fuck, Ubuntu?
I'm moving all my servers off Ubuntu. I've completely lost trust in it. On the desktop I always hated it anyway. On servers I mostly used it because it's the default on most VPS providers, and I considered it the solid, no-drama LTS distro. Fuck that now.
I can't express how crazy this is. I didn't have time to migrate my services and data when I learned about this, so I had to take all my servers that run Ubuntu offline.
Oh and btw, distros run by volunteers (Debian, Arch, Alpine...) hve shipped the fix. Meanwhile Ubuntu, made by a company that sells security subscriptions, hasn't. This is true as of 2026-11-02 14:48 UTC.

We halved DNS build delay and cut memory use by 85%
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-02
14:47:28 UTC
23:47:28 KST

How we halved DNS build delay and cut memory use by 85%
When I last wrote about our DNS propagation pipeline in 2022, the Zone Builder received about 250 DNS record changes per second. Today it receives about 700, nearly three times as many, plus about 100 other events, such as zone setting changes. However, the number of events was never the real problem. The problem was the kind of work some of those events needed, and how that work grows with the size of a zone. Some zones now hold more than 20 million records, and we expect them to keep growing toward hundreds of millions.
Each event starts a clock that measures how long it takes us to turn that change into data our authoritative DNS servers can use. We call that work a build. A build reads the current database state, converts it into the format used at the edge, and writes any changes to Quicksilver.
In 2022, per-record builds were 150 times faster than full builds on average. On a test zone with one million records, per-record builds were 4,250 times faster. We ended that post with the obvious next step: do the same thing for zone settings.
Why doesn't the Zone Builder just rebuild the setting that changed?
It should, and that is where we ended up. However, proving when it was safe took most of the work. A record event identifies what changed. A zone event can come from many Cloudflare products, may not say why the zone changed, and can alter data shared by several zones with the same name. Choosing too much work wastes capacity. Choosing too little can leave stale or incorrect DNS data at the edge.
We built a targeted build planner that chooses the smallest build we can prove is safe, and a new scheduler that combines work for the same zone. Zone setting changes no longer rebuild zones with tens of millions of records. Together, they cut peak memory by 85%, and the extra headroom let us safely shorten waits the pipeline has always needed for resilience, halving the time from a DNS change to its data being written to Quicksilver.
How DNS changes reach the edge
A DNS zone is the collection of records and settings for a domain. Cloudflare uses a multi-stage pipeline to turn a change to that zone into data available across our global network.
- A customer changes a DNS record through the DNS Records API or dashboard, or another Cloudflare service changes a zone setting.
- The change is stored in a database.
- The database change or another system produces a Kafka event.
- The DNS Kafka Aggregator combines events from several Kafka topics and sends them to the Zone Builder.
- The Zone Builder reads the required zone data and converts it into keys understood by our authoritative DNS servers.
- The Zone Builder compares those keys with Quicksilver, our distributed key-value store. It writes only what changed, and Quicksilver propagates those changes across Cloudflare's network.
This is a simplified view. The Zone Builder does not copy database rows directly to the edge. It combines records with zone settings, applies DNS serving rules, and organizes the result so our authoritative DNS servers can read it quickly.
Why full builds stopped scaling
The original Zone Builder had one useful property: it did not need to know why a zone changed. It rebuilt the desired state from the current database state.
- Read the current zone and its settings.
- Read every DNS record in the zone.
- Convert those records into Quicksilver keys.
- Compare the new keys with those already stored.
- Remove stale keys, write changed keys, and update zone metadata.
The final write contains only changed keys. However, a full build still reads, encodes, and compares every record, and holds the whole zone in memory while it does so. That is cheap for a small zone. For our largest zones, with tens of millions of records, the zone does not fit in memory at all. I explain how we handle that below.
Zone settings made targeting harder. Universal SSL is one example. Enabling or disabling it updates metadata and can change Certification Authority Authorization (CAA) records at the zone apex, the bare domain, such as example.com. CAA records tell certificate authorities whether they may issue a certificate for the domain.
A full build only needed to know that the zone changed. A targeted build must answer three questions:
- Which DNS names can change?
- Can shared metadata or the list of published zones change?
- What previous state must be removed from Quicksilver?
Answering those questions safely meant solving five problems, which the rest of this post covers in order:
- Events had to say what changed.
- Each change had to map to the smallest build that is still correct.
- Builds had to account for data shared by every zone with the same name.
- The database could change between the event and the build.
- Combining work could not lose a Kafka message.
Making events say what changed
We changed the event producer to include changed zone fields and settings, with previous and new values when available. We then traced each setting through the code that generates DNS output and classified what it could affect.
Not every event was that specific. Some database functions only update a timestamp to say, in effect, "something about this zone changed." Sometimes that is harmless bookkeeping. Sometimes it means data changed elsewhere and must be read again.
Those cases look identical in the event. Until we can identify the caller and its purpose, a timestamp-only update still causes a full build. The same rule applies to missing details, unknown events, or any change that may affect records anywhere in the zone. The planner never turns uncertainty into less work.
Choosing the smallest safe build
Quicksilver output has two parts that matter here. Record keys contain the DNS answers for one zone and name. A smaller shared metadata key tells our authoritative DNS servers how to use that zone, including where to find special records such as nameservers and wildcards. Some events change only record keys; others must update both.
With that distinction, the planner can choose one of five build types:
- Resources only: Rebuild the records at specific DNS names, which we call resources, without changing zone metadata. Ordinary A, AAAA, TXT, and CNAME changes usually take this path.
- Resources and metadata: Rebuild specific names and shared metadata. Nameserver and wildcard changes use this path.
- Apex and metadata: Rebuild the zone apex and metadata. Settings such as Universal SSL can use this path.
- Purge: Remove a zone that should no longer be published.
- Full zone: Rebuild every record when smaller work cannot be proven safe.
The rule is simple: every key a targeted build writes must be byte-for-byte identical to what a full build would write. If we cannot prove that, we do a full build.
Removing old state works as it did for per-record builds in 2022. Record events include deleted records and the previous version of updated ones. The Zone Builder then scans Quicksilver for the existing keys under each name it rebuilds, removing any that the new build did not write.
Full builds remain necessary when a change can affect records anywhere in the zone or when a zone may need to be published for the first time.
There is one deliberate exception. A full build holds every record from the database and every existing Quicksilver key for the zone in memory at once, and our largest zones do not fit. Above a fixed record-count threshold, the Zone Builder never escalates to a full build. It rebuilds the apex, the metadata, and every name identified by the queued events instead. We accept this tradeoff because a full build of those zones cannot finish, and, while it retries, it holds back every other change on its Kafka partition. Many of the cases that would otherwise need a full build involve several zones sharing one name, and zone holds reduce that risk by stopping the same name from being added to another account. For the rare cases that remain, such as enabling a zone on another network, we can manually rebuild individual names in the zone.
When one name means more than one zone
Quicksilver stores each zone's record keys separately. It also stores one metadata key shared by every Cloudflare zone with that domain name. This shared key acts like a directory: it tells our authoritative DNS servers which internal zone IDs they may use when answering a query.
Why can one name have several zone IDs? During customer onboarding, offboarding, and other lifecycle changes, multiple Cloudflare zone objects can briefly exist for the same public domain. We use a priority policy to choose a small, bounded set to publish.
Consider four internal zones for example.com: Zone 101, Zone 202, Zone 303, and Zone 404. The metadata key currently lists Zones 101, 202, and 303. Zone 404 exists, but it is not selected. The numbers identify separate Cloudflare zone objects; customers still see one domain name.
A TXT record change at www.example.com in Zone 202 affects only Zone 202's record keys, so the metadata key does not change. Nameserver and wildcard changes are different because the metadata key records information about them. Those builds must update both the zone's records and the metadata key.
At execution time, the Zone Builder reads the current list again. If it writes the metadata key, it rebuilds the whole key from that fresh view rather than patching an older copy. It writes the triggering zone's records before the metadata that refers to them.
Planning from the event, verifying before writing
An event describes what happened in the past. A build may wait while more events arrive and the database changes, so planning happens twice. The event first creates a conservative work list. Immediately before execution, the Zone Builder reads a fresh publication snapshot: the current zone settings, whether this Zone Builder should publish the zone to its network, and which same-name zone IDs belong in the metadata key. Here, a network is a separate, isolated copy of Quicksilver for one environment. Each Zone Builder deployment writes to one network, and a zone can be enabled on some networks but not others. For example, a zone can be served from Cloudflare's global network and also from the China Network.
Fresh state can change the result in four ways:
- A targeted build can become a full build if the zone now has a feature that requires complete-zone work.
- A build can become a purge if the zone should no longer be published to this network.
- A build can be skipped if the triggering zone no longer belongs in the metadata key.
- A metadata build can reconstruct the shared key from the current candidate set.
The fresh read comes from read-only copies of the database, called replicas, so builds do not compete with customer writes. Kafka delivery and database replication move independently: an event can arrive before its database change is visible on a replica. The DNS Kafka Aggregator delays events long enough to cover normal lag, and a final database check rejects the read if that replica is still behind.
Combining changes in one scheduler
Record work and full-zone work previously used different schedulers. Each could combine its own work, but not work from the other scheduler. We replaced them with one unified scheduler. The planner decides what must be built, and the scheduler decides when it can run.
When several events for one zone arrive before a build starts, the scheduler combines their work:
- An A record changes at www.example.com, requiring that name.
- A wildcard changes at *.example.com, requiring that name and shared metadata.
- The scheduler combines both names and the metadata update into one build.
Merging only adds work. If any event requires the complete zone, the combined build becomes a full build. A purge overrides every rebuild because its desired result is that the zone is no longer published.
The scheduler tracks two things separately: the DNS work that must be performed and the Kafka messages waiting to be acknowledged. A retry preserves both. Even when a full build or purge no longer needs the individual record names, the messages stay attached until the result is safely written.
Keeping Kafka moving
One slow build can delay unrelated customers even when workers are available. Kafka divides messages into ordered partitions. For each partition, it stores one committed offset: the point before which every message is known to be complete. Moving that offset past unfinished work could lose a customer change after a restart.
We use soft committing, which I previously described in my Secondary DNS deep dive. A build can finish out of order and be marked complete in memory, but Kafka's committed position advances only after every earlier message has also finished.
In the diagram, message 2 is still running, so messages 3 through n cannot be committed. If the Zone Builder restarts, those in-memory completions disappear and Kafka replays messages 2 through n. We deliberately choose repeated work over losing a change.
Targeted plans reduce how long the oldest message stays unfinished. The scheduler also prevents one busy zone from taking every worker. After a zone builds, its next build waits for a per-zone rebuild interval, which differs by Cloudflare plan. Changes that arrive in the meantime are merged into that next build. The scheduler normally enforces this interval, but lets old queued work run before it blocks a Kafka partition for too long. Zone locks and retry delays still apply, so a failing build cannot run in a tight loop.
Releasing safely
A smaller build is useful only if it produces the same DNS data. We added equivalence tests for nameserver, wildcard, and apex paths. Each test runs a full build and a targeted build from the same database state, pins time-dependent values, and compares every Quicksilver key owned by the targeted plan byte for byte. Planner and scheduler tests cover classification, same-name zones, merging, purges, and escalation.
During rollout, we also ran a full build next to every targeted build and compared the output. It was expensive by design, reading the complete zone every time, so we used it only until the mirror comparison below gave us the same confidence.
Tests cannot reproduce every customer combination, so every release also runs through a mirror Zone Builder. It consumes the production Kafka stream and reads production database state, but writes to a separate Quicksilver environment that serves no customer traffic.
A comparer checks a 20% sample of zones for missing keys, extra keys, and different values. The two environments do not always build a zone at the same instant, so a temporary mismatch is not automatically a regression. We look for new types of differences that remain after both pipelines catch up. That gives us confidence that targeted builds produce the same output as production.
We built the DNS Mirror Dashboard to group those differences and keep examples for investigation. Naturally, it runs on the Cloudflare Developer Platform: a Worker receives results, Durable Objects maintain state, and Cloudflare Access protects it. The critical groups in the screenshot below were each checked and confirmed safe before release. The diff rate is the share of compared zones where production and mirror disagreed on at least one key at the moment of comparison. Most of those are timing differences that disappear once both pipelines catch up, so we watch two things: that the rate stays low, and that no new kind of difference appears.
After the mirror results remained healthy, we rolled the scheduler out by a hash of the zone name. Hashing the name rather than the zone ID kept every zone sharing a name on the same scheduler, so two schedulers never wrote the same metadata key. We increased the percentage for free zones, then repeated the process for paid zones.
Results
Over 24 hours, we measured 86.81% of builds as resources only, 2.54% as resources and metadata, 2.30% as apex and metadata, and 1.30% as purges. Together, 92.95% of builds avoided rebuilding the complete zone. Full builds were the remaining 7.04%.
The two metadata build types were full builds before, even for large zones. Together, they account for 4.84% of builds. Combined with the 7.04% that are still full builds, that is 11.88%, so full builds fell by about 40%. Their share of the count also understates the work avoided, because they include the largest zones. The full builds that remain average 0.35 seconds, because the events that still trigger them, such as a zone being published for the first time, mostly affect new zones with few records.
In 2022, we also planned a batching system. The unified scheduler makes it unnecessary: it does not wait to form batches, and it merges events only when work is already queued for the same zone. Before, long full builds held back the committed offset for their partition. With fewer of them, we now consume Kafka at the rate events arrive, instead of falling behind and catching up in bursts of up to 1,550 messages per second.
We also measured build completion delay: the time from a DNS record or zone setting change until the Zone Builder finished writing to Quicksilver. It does not include Quicksilver's distribution across our network, which varies by location and is unaffected by this work. Production ran the old schedulers, and the mirror ran the unified scheduler on the same events over the same 24 hours. The two environments are identical apart from the code: they consume the same Kafka events, read from the same database, and write equivalent data to Quicksilver. That makes this a controlled, side-by-side comparison. The figures combine every build type and customer plan, so they are not a latency guarantee for an individual zone. A large share of this time is the rebuild interval described earlier, not build work. At each measurement point, p99 is the time within which 99% of events finished. Typical delays barely changed here, because most builds were already small. The difference is in the tail: the largest p99 spike fell from 14.6 minutes to 1.21 minutes, a 12-fold reduction. The chart uses a logarithmic scale.
Targeted builds also load complete large zones far less often. Aggregate working-set memory, the memory actively used by the deployment, fell from an average of 49 GiB to 7 GiB, an 86% reduction. Peaks fell from 79 GiB to 12 GiB, an 85% reduction.
Those were the immediate results of the new scheduler. They also let us revisit two safeguards. The first is the rebuild interval, which stops one busy zone from rebuilding constantly and taking capacity from everyone else. With builds now cheaper, and far fewer of them reading complete zones, we could safely shorten that interval on most plans. The second is how long the DNS Kafka Aggregator holds each event, to make sure database replicas have caught up before we read from them. Once our databases began rejecting reads from replicas that had fallen behind, that buffer could safely be smaller. Together, these halved the median build completion delay, from about 7.7 seconds to about 3.3 seconds. In the chart below, the first step down is the DNS Kafka Aggregator change. The red band marks the Zone Builder release with shorter rebuild intervals. That release rolled out gradually across our servers, so the delay falls in two further steps rather than all at once.
The p90 and p99 lines in that chart are less precise than they look. The histogram had buckets at 1, 5, 10, and 30 seconds, and a percentile that falls between two edges is estimated as if delays were spread evenly across that range. Most delays sit near the bottom of their range, so those estimates come out high: p99 reads close to 30 seconds, and p90 above 7 seconds. The exact average, which does not depend on buckets, confirms the halving. We have since switched to a native histogram, which chooses fine-grained buckets automatically. It puts p90 at about 5.5 seconds and p99 at about 12 seconds.
Why full builds still exist
In 2022, we said we planned to eliminate full builds entirely. We now know that is neither realistic nor useful. A zone being published for the first time needs every record written. A change that can affect records anywhere in the zone needs every record checked. An event that does not say what changed still gets a full build, because the planner never turns uncertainty into less work. For a small zone, a full build is also cheap.
As we learn what more events change, we will keep moving them to targeted builds. However, the biggest gain has already landed: the events that used to rebuild our largest zones no longer do, and the full builds that remain are mostly triggered by events, such as first publication, that affect new, small zones.
Want to know what happens after the Zone Builder writes a change? Read how Quicksilver distributes configuration to every Cloudflare data center in seconds.

Together, they cut peak memory by 85%, and the extra headroom let us safely shorten waits the pipeline has always needed for resilience, halving the time from a DNS change to its data being written to Quicksilver.
How DNS changes reach the edge
A DNS zone is the collection of records and settings for a domain. Cloudflare uses a multi-stage pipeline to turn a change to that zone into data available across our global network.
- A customer changes a DNS record through the DNS Records API or dashboard, or another Cloudflare service changes a zone setting.
- The change is stored in a database.
- The database change or another system produces a Kafka event.
- The DNS Kafka Aggregator combines events from several Kafka topics and sends them to the Zone Builder.
- The Zone Builder reads the required zone data and converts it into keys understood by our authoritative DNS servers.
- The Zone Builder compares those keys with Quicksilver, our distributed key-value store. It writes only what changed, and Quicksilver propagates those changes across Cloudflare's network.
This is a simplified view. The Zone Builder does not copy database rows directly to the edge. It combines records with zone settings, applies DNS serving rules, and organizes the result so our authoritative DNS servers can read it quickly.
Why full builds stopped scaling
The original Zone Builder had one useful property: it did not need to know why a zone changed. It rebuilt the desired state from the current database state.
- Read the current zone and its settings.
- Read every DNS record in the zone.
- Convert those records into Quicksilver keys.
- Compare the new keys with those already stored.
- Remove stale keys, write changed keys, and update zone metadata.
The final write contains only changed keys. However, a full build still reads, encodes, and compares every record, and holds the whole zone in memory while it does so. That is cheap for a small zone. For our largest zones, with tens of millions of records, the zone does not fit in memory at all. I explain how we handle that below.
Zone settings made targeting harder. Universal SSL is one example. Enabling or disabling it updates metadata and can change Certification Authority Authorization (CAA) records at the zone apex, the bare domain, such as example.com. CAA records tell certificate authorities whether they may issue a certificate for the domain.
A full build only needed to know that the zone changed. A targeted build must answer three questions:
- Which DNS names can change?
- Can shared metadata or the list of published zones change?
- What previous state must be removed from Quicksilver?
Answering those questions safely meant solving five problems, which the rest of this post covers in order:
- Events had to say what changed.
- Each change had to map to the smallest build that is still correct.
- Builds had to account for data shared by every zone with the same name.
- The database could change between the event and the build.
- Combining work could not lose a Kafka message.
Making events say what changed
We changed the event producer to include changed zone fields and settings, with previous and new values when available. We then traced each setting through the code that generates DNS output and classified what it could affect.
Not every event was that specific. Some database functions only update a timestamp to say, in effect, "something about this zone changed." Sometimes that is harmless bookkeeping. Sometimes it means data changed elsewhere and must be read again.
Those cases look identical in the event. Until we can identify the caller and its purpose, a timestamp-only update still causes a full build. The same rule applies to missing details, unknown events, or any change that may affect records anywhere in the zone. The planner never turns uncertainty into less work.
Choosing the smallest safe build
Quicksilver output has two parts that matter here. Record keys contain the DNS answers for one zone and name. A smaller shared metadata key tells our authoritative DNS servers how to use that zone, including where to find special records such as nameservers and wildcards. Some events change only record keys; others must update both.
With that distinction, the planner can choose one of five build types:
- Resources only: Rebuild the records at specific DNS names, which we call resources, without changing zone metadata. Ordinary A, AAAA, TXT, and CNAME changes usually take this path.
- Resources and metadata: Rebuild specific names and shared metadata. Nameserver and wildcard changes use this path.
- Apex and metadata: Rebuild the zone apex and metadata. Settings such as Universal SSL can use this path.
- Purge: Remove a zone that should no longer be published.
- Full zone: Rebuild every record when smaller work cannot be proven safe.
The rule is simple: every key a targeted build writes must be byte-for-byte identical to what a full build would write. If we cannot prove that, we do a full build.
Removing old state works as it did for per-record builds in 2022. Record events include deleted records and the previous version of updated ones. The Zone Builder then scans Quicksilver for the existing keys under each name it rebuilds, removing any that the new build did not write.
Full builds remain necessary when a change can affect records anywhere in the zone or when a zone may need to be published for the first time.
There is one deliberate exception. A full build holds every record from the database and every existing Quicksilver key for the zone in memory at once, and our largest zones do not fit. Above a fixed record-count threshold, the Zone Builder never escalates to a full build. It rebuilds the apex, the metadata, and every name identified by the queued events instead. We accept this tradeoff because a full build of those zones cannot finish, and, while it retries, it holds back every other change on its Kafka partition. Many of the cases that would otherwise need a full build involve several zones sharing one name, and zone holds reduce that risk by stopping the same name from being added to another account. For the rare cases that remain, such as enabling a zone on another network, we can manually rebuild individual names in the zone.
When one name means more than one zone
Quicksilver stores each zone's record keys separately. It also stores one metadata key shared by every Cloudflare zone with that domain name. This shared key acts like a directory: it tells our authoritative DNS servers which internal zone IDs they may use when answering a query.
Why can one name have several zone IDs? During customer onboarding, offboarding, and other lifecycle changes, multiple Cloudflare zone objects can briefly exist for the same public domain. We use a priority policy to choose a small, bounded set to publish.
Consider four internal zones for example.com: Zone 101, Zone 202, Zone 303, and Zone 404. The metadata key currently lists Zones 101, 202, and 303. Zone 404 exists, but it is not selected. The numbers identify separate Cloudflare zone objects; customers still see one domain name.
A TXT record change at www.example.com in Zone 202 affects only Zone 202's record keys, so the metadata key does not change. Nameserver and wildcard changes are different because the metadata key records information about them. Those builds must update both the zone's records and the metadata key.
At execution time, the Zone Builder reads the current list again. If it writes the metadata key, it rebuilds the whole key from that fresh view rather than patching an older copy. It writes the triggering zone's records before the metadata that refers to them.
Planning from the event, verifying before writing
An event describes what happened in the past. A build may wait while more events arrive and the database changes, so planning happens twice. The event first creates a conservative work list. Immediately before execution, the Zone Builder reads a fresh publication snapshot: the current zone settings, whether this Zone Builder should publish the zone to its network, and which same-name zone IDs belong in the metadata key. Here, a network is a separate, isolated copy of Quicksilver for one environment. Each Zone Builder deployment writes to one network, and a zone can be enabled on some networks but not others. For example, a zone can be served from Cloudflare's global network and also from the China Network.
Fresh state can change the result in four ways:
- A targeted build can become a full build if the zone now has a feature that requires complete-zone work.
- A build can become a purge if the zone should no longer be published to this network.
- A build can be skipped if the triggering zone no longer belongs in the metadata key.
- A metadata build can reconstruct the shared key from the current candidate set.
The fresh read comes from read-only copies of the database, called replicas, so builds do not compete with customer writes. Kafka delivery and database replication move independently: an event can arrive before its database change is visible on a replica. The DNS Kafka Aggregator delays events long enough to cover normal lag, and a final database check rejects the read if that replica is still behind.
Combining changes in one scheduler
Record work and full-zone work previously used different schedulers. Each could combine its own work, but not work from the other scheduler. We replaced them with one unified scheduler. The planner decides what must be built, and the scheduler decides when it can run.
When several events for one zone arrive before a build starts, the scheduler combines their work:
- An A record changes at www.example.com, requiring that name.
- A wildcard changes at *.example.com, requiring that name and shared metadata.
- The scheduler combines both names and the metadata update into one build.
Merging only adds work. If any event requires the complete zone, the combined build becomes a full build. A purge overrides every rebuild because its desired result is that the zone is no longer published.
The scheduler tracks two things separately: the DNS work that must be performed and the Kafka messages waiting to be acknowledged. A retry preserves both. Even when a full build or purge no longer needs the individual record names, the messages stay attached until the result is safely written.
Keeping Kafka moving
One slow build can delay unrelated customers even when workers are available. Kafka divides messages into ordered partitions. For each partition, it stores one committed offset: the point before which every message is known to be complete. Moving that offset past unfinished work could lose a customer change after a restart.
We use soft committing, which I previously described in my Secondary DNS deep dive. A build can finish out of order and be marked complete in memory, but Kafka's committed position advances only after every earlier message has also finished.
In the diagram, message 2 is still running, so messages 3 through n cannot be committed. If the Zone Builder restarts, those in-memory completions disappear and Kafka replays messages 2 through n. We deliberately choose repeated work over losing a change.
Targeted plans reduce how long the oldest message stays unfinished. The scheduler also prevents one busy zone from taking every worker. After a zone builds, its next build waits for a per-zone rebuild interval, which differs by Cloudflare plan. Changes that arrive in the meantime are merged into that next build. The scheduler normally enforces this interval, but lets old queued work run before it blocks a Kafka partition for too long. Zone locks and retry delays still apply, so a failing build cannot run in a tight loop.
Releasing safely
A smaller build is useful only if it produces the same DNS data. We added equivalence tests for nameserver, wildcard, and apex paths. Each test runs a full build and a targeted build from the same database state, pins time-dependent values, and compares every Quicksilver key owned by the targeted plan byte for byte. Planner and scheduler tests cover classification, same-name zones, merging, purges, and escalation.
During rollout, we also ran a full build next to every targeted build and compared the output. It was expensive by design, reading the complete zone every time, so we used it only until the mirror comparison below gave us the same confidence.
Tests cannot reproduce every customer combination, so every release also runs through a mirror Zone Builder. It consumes the production Kafka stream and reads production database state, but writes to a separate Quicksilver environment that serves no customer traffic.
A comparer checks a 20% sample of zones for missing keys, extra keys, and different values. The two environments do not always build a zone at the same instant, so a temporary mismatch is not automatically a regression. We look for new types of differences that remain after both pipelines catch up. That gives us confidence that targeted builds produce the same output as production.
We built the DNS Mirror Dashboard to group those differences and keep examples for investigation. Naturally, it runs on the Cloudflare Developer Platform: a Worker receives results, Durable Objects maintain state, and Cloudflare Access protects it. The critical groups in the screenshot below were each checked and confirmed safe before release. The diff rate is the share of compared zones where production and mirror disagreed on at least one key at the moment of comparison. Most of those are timing differences that disappear once both pipelines catch up, so we watch two things: that the rate stays low, and that no new kind of difference appears.
After the mirror results remained healthy, we rolled the scheduler out by a hash of the zone name. Hashing the name rather than the zone ID kept every zone sharing a name on the same scheduler, so two schedulers never wrote the same metadata key. We increased the percentage for free zones, then repeated the process for paid zones.
Results
Over 24 hours, we measured 86.81% of builds as resources only, 2.54% as resources and metadata, 2.30% as apex and metadata, and 1.30% as purges. Together, 92.95% of builds avoided rebuilding the complete zone. Full builds were the remaining 7.04%.
The two metadata build types were full builds before, even for large zones. Together, they account for 4.84% of builds. Combined with the 7.04% that are still full builds, that is 11.88%, so full builds fell by about 40%. Their share of the count also understates the work avoided, because they include the largest zones. The full builds that remain average 0.35 seconds, because the events that still trigger them, such as a zone being published for the first time, mostly affect new zones with few records.
In 2022, we also planned a batching system. The unified scheduler makes it unnecessary: it does not wait to form batches, and it merges events only when work is already queued for the same zone. Before, long full builds held back the committed offset for their partition. With fewer of them, we now consume Kafka at the rate events arrive, instead of falling behind and catching up in bursts of up to 1,550 messages per second.
We also measured build completion delay: the time from a DNS record or zone setting change until the Zone Builder finished writing to Quicksilver. It does not include Quicksilver's distribution across our network, which varies by location and is unaffected by this work. Production ran the old schedulers, and the mirror ran the unified scheduler on the same events over the same 24 hours. The two environments are identical apart from the code: they consume the same Kafka events, read from the same database, and write equivalent data to Quicksilver. That makes this a controlled, side-by-side comparison. The figures combine every build type and customer plan, so they are not a latency guarantee for an individual zone. A large share of this time is the rebuild interval described earlier, not build work. At each measurement point, p99 is the time within which 99% of events finished. Typical delays barely changed here, because most builds were already small. The difference is in the tail: the largest p99 spike fell from 14.6 minutes to 1.21 minutes, a 12-fold reduction. The chart uses a logarithmic scale.
Targeted builds also load complete large zones far less often. Aggregate working-set memory, the memory actively used by the deployment, fell from an average of 49 GiB to 7 GiB, an 86% reduction. Peaks fell from 79 GiB to 12 GiB, an 85% reduction.
Those were the immediate results of the new scheduler. They also let us revisit two safeguards. The first is the rebuild interval, which stops one busy zone from rebuilding constantly and taking capacity from everyone else. With builds now cheaper, and far fewer of them reading complete zones, we could safely shorten that interval on most plans. The second is how long the DNS Kafka Aggregator holds each event, to make sure database replicas have caught up before we read from them. Once our databases began rejecting reads from replicas that had fallen behind, that buffer could safely be smaller. Together, these halved the median build completion delay, from about 7.7 seconds to about 3.3 seconds. In the chart below, the first step down is the DNS Kafka Aggregator change. The red band marks the Zone Builder release with shorter rebuild intervals. That release rolled out gradually across our servers, so the delay falls in two further steps rather than all at once.
The p90 and p99 lines in that chart are less precise than they look. The histogram had buckets at 1, 5, 10, and 30 seconds, and a percentile that falls between two edges is estimated as if delays were spread evenly across that range. Most delays sit near the bottom of their range, so those estimates come out high: p99 reads close to 30 seconds, and p90 above 7 seconds. The exact average, which does not depend on buckets, confirms the halving. We have since switched to a native histogram, which chooses fine-grained buckets automatically. It puts p90 at about 5.5 seconds and p99 at about 12 seconds.
Why full builds still exist
In 2022, we said we planned to eliminate full builds entirely. We now know that is neither realistic nor useful. A zone being published for the first time needs every record written. A change that can affect records anywhere in the zone needs every record checked. An event that does not say what changed still gets a full build, because the planner never turns uncertainty into less work. For a small zone, a full build is also cheap.
As we learn what more events change, we will keep moving them to targeted builds. However, the biggest gain has already landed: the events that used to rebuild our largest zones no longer do, and the full builds that remain are mostly triggered by events, such as first publication, that affect new, small zones.
Want to know what happens after the Zone Builder writes a change? Read how Quicksilver distributes configuration to every Cloudflare data center in seconds.

GPT-Rosalind-6 System Card Addendum
