GPT-Rosalind-6 is a frontier reasoning model for biology, drug
discovery, and translational medicine. We offer it as GPT-Rosalind-6
Research to qualified scientists, research institutes, companies and
government partners. Rosalind has fewer refusals on dual-use biological
queries but is only made available via trusted access. We review
applicants’ proposed uses, security controls and governance practices
before granting access. We also continue to have other deployment
safeguards in place.
GPT-Rosalind-6 uses the same weights as GPT-6 Astra, our flagship
reasoning model. It is designed to support legitimate biological
research, including some dual-use topics, while still refusing requests
for biological weaponization and retaining refusals on other safety
topics.
Under our Preparedness Framework, we are treating this launch as High
capability in the Biological and Chemical domain, below the threshold
for Critical. We rely on GPT-6 Astra’s capability assessments for other
Preparedness risks and treat GPT-Rosalind-6 as Critical in Cybersecurity
and below High in AI Self-Improvement.
This addendum describes GPT-Rosalind-6’s biological capability
assessment, additional safety and alignment evaluations, and deployment
safeguards. We include results for GPT-Rosalind-5.5 where available for
comparison. This addendum should be read alongside the GPT-6 Astra
system card, which describes the parent model’s broader capability
and safety evaluations.
2 Model Data and Training
GPT-Rosalind-6 uses the same weights as GPT-6 Astra. We did not add
training data or perform additional post-training for this release.
Details of the underlying model’s data and training are provided in the
GPT-6 Astra
system card.1
3 Beneficial Capability Evaluations
We evaluated GPT-Rosalind-6 on three life sciences research
benchmarks and compared its results with GPT-Rosalind-5.5. The
GPT-Rosalind-5.5 scores below use the same benchmark versions and
problem sets as GPT-Rosalind-6 and therefore differ from those reported
at its launch. The Preparedness evaluations in Section 4 assess
capabilities relevant to the risk of severe harm. We averaged response
scores within each problem and then weighted the problems equally.
3.1 Medicinal chemistry
MedChemBench v7 tests chemical structure interpretation,
structure-activity relationships, and predictions of drug potency and
toxicity. We evaluated both models on the same 99 problems, with up to
nine scored responses per problem at each reasoning budget. At the
highest reasoning budget tested, mean rubric scores were 49.6% for
GPT-Rosalind-6 and 28.9% for GPT-Rosalind-5.5.
3.2 Genomics and quantitative biology
GeneBench Pro v13 tests an agent’s ability to plan and carry out
quantitative analyses of biological data. We estimated single-attempt
success from seven responses per model on each of 129 problems at each
reasoning budget. At the highest reasoning budget tested, single-attempt
success rates were 37.7% for GPT-Rosalind-6 and 16.7% for
GPT-Rosalind-5.5. The evaluation used a benchmark-specific configuration
that differs from production.
3.3 Applied life sciences research
LifeSciBench Gold uses research tasks contributed by practicing life
scientists. We excluded six problems flagged for possible issues with
the tasks or evaluation environment and compared both models on the same
remaining 94 problems, with up to eight scored responses per problem at
each reasoning budget. At the highest reasoning budget tested, average
scores were 61.3% for GPT-Rosalind-6 and 44.0% for GPT-Rosalind-5.5.
4 Preparedness
The Preparedness Framework is OpenAI’s approach to tracking and
preparing for frontier capabilities that create new risks of severe
harm. Under our framework, we work to track and mitigate the risk of
severe harm, including by implementing safeguards that sufficiently
minimize the risk for highly capable models.
4.1 Preparedness Evaluations
We repeated biological capability evaluations on GPT-Rosalind-6 and
use the parent model’s helpful-only evaluations as additional evidence,
given it uses the same weights as GPT-6 Astra. Helpful-only models are
evaluated to elicit capabilities that safety refusals may otherwise
conceal.
4.1.1 Biological and Chemical
As we did for GPT-6 Astra, we are treating this launch as High
capability in the Biological and Chemical domain. Below, we describe the
evaluations we used to reach this conclusion. We use the four High
capability evaluations and four Critical capability evaluations
described in the GPT-6 Astra system card. The hard-negative protein
binding and DNA sequence design evaluations reported for
GPT-Rosalind-5.5 have been retired.
The High capability evaluations test wet lab knowledge and
troubleshooting. The Critical capability evaluations test biological
design capabilities that could help an expert develop a highly dangerous
novel threat. These evaluations provide evidence about specific
capabilities rather than a complete test of the ability to cause
biological harm. The Critical capability thresholds are preliminary
estimates informed by expert judgment and may change as the evaluations
are refined.

4.1.3 AI Self-Improvement
We rely on GPT-6 Astra’s existing Preparedness assessment for AI

4.2 Safeguards
Compared to GPT-6 Astra, GPT-Rosalind-6 features additional
deployment controls for its use in advanced life sciences research.
Access is limited to approved customers through our trusted-access
deployment structure where organizations must demonstrate they are
conducting legitimate scientific research with public benefit, have
strong governance and safety oversight, and control access with
enterprise-grade security.
Our safeguard approach for GPT-Rosalind-6 has four main components:
trusted access controls for verified higher-capability life sciences
use, model-level boundaries against harmful biological and cyber
assistance, monitoring and enforcement mechanisms designed to detect
abuse, and additional contractual requirements to enhance security.
GPT-Rosalind-6 retains GPT-6 Astra’s standard cyber safeguards,
including real-time blocking.
4.2.1 Trust-based access
GPT-Rosalind-6 is available only to approved customers. Before
granting access, OpenAI reviews whether the applicant has a legitimate
life sciences or related mission, a credible use case, appropriate
research and operational governance, and the ability to safely control
access to the model.
GPT-Rosalind-6 uses the access requirements already applied to
GPT-Rosalind-5.5, including the policy updates introduced since its
launch. This release does not broaden eligibility beyond prior
announcements, such as the Rosalind Academic Plan.
For non-government applicants, OpenAI also employs business
verification and compliance-screening checks. These checks help confirm
applicant authenticity and reduce the risk of access by fraudulent,
insufficiently attributable, or otherwise unsuitable actors. These
checks supplement OpenAI’s broader review of the applicant’s proposed
use case, safety posture, and access scope.
For government applicants, OpenAI maintains a trusted-access pathway
for select government entities working on approved biosecurity,
biosafety, public-health preparedness, and biodefense use cases.
Approved enterprise customers must limit GPT-Rosalind-6 access to
authorized users with a legitimate need and apply least-privilege access
controls, appropriate onboarding, and insider risk controls to their
users. Customers must apply qualified human review before model outputs
inform experiments, publications, or consequential decisions.
4.2.2 Model behavior and system safeguards
These evaluations were deliberately created to be difficult. They
were built around cases in which our existing models were not yet giving
ideal responses, and this is reflected in the scores below. We continue
monitoring these categories after launch to evaluate online performance
and further adjust safeguards as appropriate.
4.2.2.1 Biological and Chemical safeguards
GPT-Rosalind-6 is designed to be more useful for legitimate advanced
biological research than generally available ChatGPT models. This
supports advanced life sciences workflows, including some dual-use
topics, that GPT-6 Astra may refuse in General Access. We still tune the
model to refuse or safely respond to requests that would continue to
meaningfully enable severe biological weaponization. This training is
done separately from the training of our classifiers and offline
mitigations to decorrelate our safeguards.
To evaluate the quality of these model-level refusals, we track the
safety of model responses to prompts that originate from held-out
synthetic data, red-teaming, and production data. These benchmarks and
their grading continue to be updated, meaning results do not directly
translate to past system cards.
Table 10 reports
results across our suite of biosecurity benchmarks, accounting for both
model behavior and safeguards. We note that because GPT-Rosalind-6 is a
trusted-access deployment, we mainly considered the results from the
Biosecurity - Severe benchmark. Biosecurity - Benign
evaluates model compliance (being fully helpful without refusals or
caveats) on a set of benign biological and operational questions. The
remaining benchmark scores show the fraction of model responses that
pass the configured safety grader on prompts related to biological
weaponization. A response that does not pass was flagged under this
grading criterion; this does not necessarily mean it is unequivocally
unsafe.
Table 10: Biological and chemical content evaluations (higher is better)
Category
4.2.2.2 Cyber boundaries
GPT-Rosalind-6 inherits the model and system level cyber safeguards
of the GPT-6 Astra base model and additionally applies a more
conservative cyber refusal boundary. The model is intended to continue
to refuse requests that clearly enable unauthorized, destructive, or
harmful cyber activity, including areas such as malware deployment,
credential theft, exfiltration, or other malicious activity.
As an additional robustness test, we evaluate resistance to
previously identified cyber jailbreaks by replaying nine known attacks
across ten fixed ExploitBench tasks. We evaluated both models with
real-time cyber blocking disabled, so the task success rates in
Table 11 reflect
model-level behavior only. We report the mean task success rate across
seven attacks, excluding attempts that ended in errors or exceeded turn
limits.

Additional evaluations assess whether automated monitors detect harmful

when instructed to do so.

4.2.3 Enforcement
OpenAI can restrict or revoke access for misuse. OpenAI maintains
monitoring, review, and enforcement processes to help identify potential
misuse after access is granted. These processes are designed to detect
activity that may indicate malicious biological, chemical or other
activity that is in violation of OpenAI’s Usage Policies or unauthorized
access.
OpenAI uses a variety of signals to assess potential misuse,
including automated safety classifiers, account- or organization-level
indicators, customer-provided information, and, where available,
specialist review of potentially high-risk activity. The specific review
process depends on the deployment configuration and the information
available to OpenAI.
Before launch, we tested our shared monitoring and enforcement
process through a biosecurity red-teaming effort on GPT-Rosalind-5.5 and
are in the process of repeating this for GPT-Rosalind-6 now that it is
deployed. We are not publicly disclosing these details because they
contain specific information about our safeguards, but we shared them
with our Safety Advisory Group committee and other relevant governance
bodies.
4.2.4 Security and Access Controls
Approved GPT-Rosalind-6 customers must maintain security controls
appropriate for access to a higher-capability life sciences model. These
include strong identity and access management, administrator controls,
least-privilege access controls appropriate to the approved deployment,
and processes to remove access when a user no longer has a legitimate
need.
Model access and data-retention arrangements are approved separately.
Customers must meet the security and oversight requirements for their
approved deployment.
As with other OpenAI deployments, we expect to continue learning from
real-world use. We will monitor safety signals, customer experience, and
emerging misuse patterns, and may update GPT-Rosalind-6 safeguards as
the deployment evolves.

GPT-Rosalind-6 System Card Addendum
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-02
14:44:02 UTC
23:44:02 KST

GPT-Rosalind-6 is a frontier reasoning model for biology, drug
discovery, and translational medicine. We offer it as GPT-Rosalind-6
Research to qualified scientists, research institutes, companies and
government partners. Rosalind has fewer refusals on dual-use biological
queries but is only made available via trusted access. We review
applicants’ proposed uses, security controls and governance practices
before granting access. We also continue to have other deployment
safeguards in place.
GPT-Rosalind-6 uses the same weights as GPT-6 Astra, our flagship
reasoning model. It is designed to support legitimate biological
research, including some dual-use topics, while still refusing requests
for biological weaponization and retaining refusals on other safety
topics.
Under our Preparedness Framework, we are treating this launch as High
capability in the Biological and Chemical domain, below the threshold
for Critical. We rely on GPT-6 Astra’s capability assessments for other
Preparedness risks and treat GPT-Rosalind-6 as Critical in Cybersecurity
and below High in AI Self-Improvement.
This addendum describes GPT-Rosalind-6’s biological capability
assessment, additional safety and alignment evaluations, and deployment
safeguards. We include results for GPT-Rosalind-5.5 where available for
comparison. This addendum should be read alongside the GPT-6 Astra
system card, which describes the parent model’s broader capability
and safety evaluations.
2 Model Data and Training
GPT-Rosalind-6 uses the same weights as GPT-6 Astra. We did not add
training data or perform additional post-training for this release.
Details of the underlying model’s data and training are provided in the
GPT-6 Astra
system card.1
3 Beneficial Capability Evaluations
We evaluated GPT-Rosalind-6 on three life sciences research
benchmarks and compared its results with GPT-Rosalind-5.5. The
GPT-Rosalind-5.5 scores below use the same benchmark versions and
problem sets as GPT-Rosalind-6 and therefore differ from those reported
at its launch. The Preparedness evaluations in Section 4 assess
capabilities relevant to the risk of severe harm. We averaged response
scores within each problem and then weighted the problems equally.
3.1 Medicinal chemistry
MedChemBench v7 tests chemical structure interpretation,
structure-activity relationships, and predictions of drug potency and
toxicity. We evaluated both models on the same 99 problems, with up to
nine scored responses per problem at each reasoning budget. At the
highest reasoning budget tested, mean rubric scores were 49.6% for
GPT-Rosalind-6 and 28.9% for GPT-Rosalind-5.5.
3.2 Genomics and quantitative biology
GeneBench Pro v13 tests an agent’s ability to plan and carry out
quantitative analyses of biological data. We estimated single-attempt
success from seven responses per model on each of 129 problems at each
reasoning budget. At the highest reasoning budget tested, single-attempt
success rates were 37.7% for GPT-Rosalind-6 and 16.7% for
GPT-Rosalind-5.5. The evaluation used a benchmark-specific configuration
that differs from production.
3.3 Applied life sciences research
LifeSciBench Gold uses research tasks contributed by practicing life
scientists. We excluded six problems flagged for possible issues with
the tasks or evaluation environment and compared both models on the same
remaining 94 problems, with up to eight scored responses per problem at
each reasoning budget. At the highest reasoning budget tested, average
scores were 61.3% for GPT-Rosalind-6 and 44.0% for GPT-Rosalind-5.5.
4 Preparedness
The Preparedness Framework is OpenAI’s approach to tracking and
preparing for frontier capabilities that create new risks of severe
harm. Under our framework, we work to track and mitigate the risk of
severe harm, including by implementing safeguards that sufficiently
minimize the risk for highly capable models.
4.1 Preparedness Evaluations
We repeated biological capability evaluations on GPT-Rosalind-6 and
use the parent model’s helpful-only evaluations as additional evidence,
given it uses the same weights as GPT-6 Astra. Helpful-only models are
evaluated to elicit capabilities that safety refusals may otherwise
conceal.
4.1.1 Biological and Chemical
As we did for GPT-6 Astra, we are treating this launch as High
capability in the Biological and Chemical domain. Below, we describe the
evaluations we used to reach this conclusion. We use the four High
capability evaluations and four Critical capability evaluations
described in the GPT-6 Astra system card. The hard-negative protein
binding and DNA sequence design evaluations reported for
GPT-Rosalind-5.5 have been retired.
The High capability evaluations test wet lab knowledge and
troubleshooting. The Critical capability evaluations test biological
design capabilities that could help an expert develop a highly dangerous
novel threat. These evaluations provide evidence about specific
capabilities rather than a complete test of the ability to cause
biological harm. The Critical capability thresholds are preliminary
estimates informed by expert judgment and may change as the evaluations
are refined.
Table 1: Overview of Biological and Chemical evaluations
Table 1: Overview of Biological and Chemical evaluations

The results below report observed performance and, for the High
capability evaluations, refusal-adjusted scores. pass@1 measures success
on a single attempt. The adjustment treats refusals as correct answers
to estimate capability that safety training may otherwise conceal. For
Biorisk knowledge, we recompute the consensus answer after this
adjustment. GPT-6 Astra reference values use the evaluation runs
reported in its system card.
4.1.1.1 ProtocolQA Open-Ended
To evaluate models’ ability to troubleshoot commonly published lab
protocols, we modify 108 multiple-choice questions from FutureHouse’s
ProtocolQA dataset [3]
to be open-ended short answer questions, which makes the evaluation
harder and more realistic than the multiple-choice version. The
questions introduce egregious errors in common published protocols,
describe the wet lab result of carrying out this protocol, and ask how
to fix the procedure.
Table 2: ProtocolQA Open-Ended. The indicative High capability threshold is 54%.
Category
4.1.1.2 TroubleshootingBench
To evaluate models’ ability to identify and correct real-world
experimental errors in biological protocols, we built a short-answer
troubleshooting dataset from expert-written wet lab procedures.
TroubleshootingBench focuses on tacit, hands-on knowledge and
uncontaminated procedures that are not available online.
Scientists with a PhD in a relevant biological discipline were asked
to transcribe biological protocols they have personally used in the lab.
From these protocols, they created troubleshooting questions,
introducing subtle or realistic execution errors and describing the
resulting failed outcome. Compared to ProtocolQA Open-Ended, which
focuses on well-known published procedures, TroubleshootingBench tests
performance on non-public protocols and errors that rely on tacit
procedural knowledge.
Table 3: TroubleshootingBench. The indicative High capability threshold is 36.4%. GPT-Rosalind-6 scored 491/832, counting nine failed evaluation attempts as incorrect.
Category
4.1.1.3 Biorisk knowledge
We evaluated models on a tacit knowledge and troubleshooting
multiple-choice dataset created with Gryphon Scientific. The questions
span all five stages in the biothreat creation process and focus on
areas where tacit knowledge would be a bottleneck. Tacit knowledge
questions are meant to be obscure to anyone not working in the field.
Troubleshooting questions are meant to be obscure to anyone without
hands-on experience.
Table 4: Biorisk knowledge. cons@32 uses the consensus answer across 32 responses to each question. The indicative High capability threshold is 80%.
Table 4: Biorisk knowledge. cons@32 uses the consensus answer across 32 responses to each question. The indicative High capability threshold is 80%.

GPT-Rosalind-5.5

4.1.1.4 Multi-select Virology Troubleshooting
To evaluate models’ ability to troubleshoot wet lab experiments in a
multimodal setting, we evaluate models on a set of fully held-out
virology troubleshooting questions from SecureBio, in a multi-select
format. A response receives credit only if it selects every correct
statement and no incorrect ones.
Table 5: Multi-select Virology Troubleshooting. The indicative High capability threshold is 31%.
Category
After adjusting for refusals, GPT-Rosalind-6 exceeds the indicative
High capability thresholds on three of the four evaluations. We
therefore continue to treat the model as High capability. Compared with
the reported GPT-Rosalind-5.5 results, observed scores are higher on
three evaluations and lower on Biorisk knowledge.
4.1.1.5 AAV Capsid Packaging Prediction
Predicting whether an AAV capsid variant will package successfully
could reduce trial and error in viral-vector engineering, which we treat
as a proxy for some dual-use biological design capabilities. We evaluate
this capability using an evaluation developed by Dyno Therapeutics. The
model predicts packaging scores for held-out sequences using labeled
examples, protein embeddings and computational tools. Performance is
measured using Spearman rank correlation.
Table 6: AAV Capsid Packaging Prediction. The indicative Critical capability threshold is 0.600.
Category
4.1.1.6 SHP2 Protein Function Prediction
Predicting how changes to protein sequence affect function could
reduce trial and error in protein engineering, which we treat as a proxy
for some dual-use biological design capabilities. We evaluate this
capability using SHP2, a well-studied human protein. The model uses
available experimental measurements to predict held-out functional assay
results for single-amino-acid variants. Performance is measured by mean
R² across three unpublished assay datasets.
Table 7: SHP2 Protein Function Prediction. The indicative Critical capability threshold is 0.60. The GPT-6 Astra reference is the reported maximum of 0.40. A separate helpful-only run scored 0.35.
Category
4.1.1.7 Coronavirus-ACE2 Cell-Entry Screen
Reliable predictions of cell entry could help researchers prioritize
laboratory experiments in studies of virus-host interactions, but could
also be a threat-relevant capability. We ask models to predict and rank
held-out measurements from an existing, unpublished academic dataset.
The underlying experiment studied existing spike proteins using
replication-incompetent pseudoviruses. It did not introduce new
mutations or generate infectious progeny.
Table 8: Coronavirus-ACE2 Cell-Entry Screen. The indicative Critical capability threshold is 0.75. The GPT-6 Astra reference is its helpful-only result.
Category
4.1.1.8 Phage-plasmid Co-evolution
We assess the ability to predict biological evolution using data from
an existing but unpublished experimental phage-plasmid co-evolution
system. Given observations available at each experimental checkpoint,
the model predicts which mutations will subsequently appear. Performance
is measured by the negative log-likelihood of observed mutations, with
lower scores indicating better predictions. The experiment uses viruses
that infect bacteria rather than humans. We treat performance as a proxy
for some dual-use biological design capabilities.
Table 9: Phage-plasmid Co-evolution. The indicative Critical capability threshold is an NLL of 9.40 or lower. The GPT-6 Astra reference is its helpful-only result.
Category
None of the four Critical capability evaluations met its indicative
threshold. Results relative to GPT-Rosalind-5.5 were mixed. We also rely
on the parent model’s helpful-only evaluations and external biological
capability assessments, as described in the GPT-6 Astra system card.
4.1.2 Cybersecurity
We rely on GPT-6 Astra’s existing Preparedness assessment for
Cybersecurity and treat GPT-Rosalind-6 as Critical in this domain. The
underlying capability findings are described in the GPT-6 Astra
system card.
4.1.3 AI Self-Improvement
We rely on GPT-6 Astra’s existing Preparedness assessment for AI
Self-Improvement, where GPT-6 Astra did not meet the threshold for High
capability. The evaluations and findings are described in the GPT-6 Astra
system card.
4.2 Safeguards
Compared to GPT-6 Astra, GPT-Rosalind-6 features additional
deployment controls for its use in advanced life sciences research.
Access is limited to approved customers through our trusted-access
deployment structure where organizations must demonstrate they are
conducting legitimate scientific research with public benefit, have
strong governance and safety oversight, and control access with
enterprise-grade security.
Our safeguard approach for GPT-Rosalind-6 has four main components:
trusted access controls for verified higher-capability life sciences
use, model-level boundaries against harmful biological and cyber
assistance, monitoring and enforcement mechanisms designed to detect
abuse, and additional contractual requirements to enhance security.
GPT-Rosalind-6 retains GPT-6 Astra’s standard cyber safeguards,
including real-time blocking.
4.2.1 Trust-based access
GPT-Rosalind-6 is available only to approved customers. Before
granting access, OpenAI reviews whether the applicant has a legitimate
life sciences or related mission, a credible use case, appropriate
research and operational governance, and the ability to safely control
access to the model.
GPT-Rosalind-6 uses the access requirements already applied to
GPT-Rosalind-5.5, including the policy updates introduced since its
launch. This release does not broaden eligibility beyond prior
announcements, such as the Rosalind Academic Plan.
For non-government applicants, OpenAI also employs business
verification and compliance-screening checks. These checks help confirm
applicant authenticity and reduce the risk of access by fraudulent,
insufficiently attributable, or otherwise unsuitable actors. These
checks supplement OpenAI’s broader review of the applicant’s proposed
use case, safety posture, and access scope.
For government applicants, OpenAI maintains a trusted-access pathway
for select government entities working on approved biosecurity,
biosafety, public-health preparedness, and biodefense use cases.
Approved enterprise customers must limit GPT-Rosalind-6 access to
authorized users with a legitimate need and apply least-privilege access
controls, appropriate onboarding, and insider risk controls to their
users. Customers must apply qualified human review before model outputs
inform experiments, publications, or consequential decisions.
4.2.2 Model behavior and system safeguards
These evaluations were deliberately created to be difficult. They
were built around cases in which our existing models were not yet giving

monitoring these categories after launch to evaluate online performance
and further adjust safeguards as appropriate.

As with other OpenAI deployments, we expect to continue learning from
real-world use. We will monitor safety signals, customer experience, and
emerging misuse patterns, and may update GPT-Rosalind-6 safeguards as
the deployment evolves.

Ninja’s first-ever microwave just replaced two of my kitchen appliances at once
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-02
14:42:38 UTC
23:42:38 KST

I never thought I’d get excited about replacing my microwave, but the Ninja Crispi Microwave and Air Fryer has made a pretty convincing case for ditching two appliances and making room for one. Combining a microwave with a built-in air fryer, it’s designed to speed up everyday cooking, give leftovers their crunch back and, perhaps best of all, free up some valuable kitchen counter space.
Priced at $449.99, it’s certainly not a budget-friendly microwave. But this is no ordinary machine for heating leftover soup. Ninja has paired a 1,000W microwave with a 1,700W air fryer system, powered by its FusionCrisp technology, which combines the speed of microwave cooking with the golden, crispy results you’d typically expect from an air fryer.
Think leftover pizza, chicken tenders, fries, and all those foods that tend to turn sad and soggy in a traditional microwave. Instead, the idea is to get food that’s piping hot and juicy on the inside, with a deliciously crisp, golden finish on the outside. In other words, no more choosing between speed and texture.
Of course, microwave and air fryer combinations aren’t entirely new. Brands such as Smeg have already explored the concept, while plenty of modern microwaves come with a range of additional cooking functions.
What makes the Ninja Crispi Microwave particularly interesting, however, is its FusionCrisp technology, which is designed to bring both cooking methods together rather than simply offering two separate functions. It comes with a 5.5-quart glass cooking dish and a nonstick crisper plate, which sits on top when you want to air fry and crisp up your food.
Another part that makes it stand out is that there isn’t a rotating plate inside, opening up a lot more space to cook more dishes at once.
It’s not just about reheating leftovers. For example, if you want to make a pasta bake, the microwave can cook the pasta, while the air fryer crisps up the cheese on top.
There’s one thing to consider before buying it, though. Measuring 20.3 x 17.56 x 14.1 inches, the microwave is hardly compact. It takes up a fair amount of counter space, so while it could free up room if you’re replacing a separate microwave and air fryer, it’s not necessarily the ideal solution for a small apartment kitchen.
Then there’s the $449.99 price tag. That’s a considerable amount to spend on a microwave, particularly when a basic model can handle everyday reheating for a fraction of the cost. But if you’re someone who regularly reaches for both a microwave and an air fryer, the combination could make a lot more sense.

Anthropic releases amended IPO prospectus with third-quarter financials
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-02
14:38:45 UTC
23:38:45 KST

Anthropic reported updated financials as part of an amended Form S-1 filing with the Securities and Exchange Commission on Monday, giving investors a more comprehensive look into the state of its business.
The artificial intelligence company said it generated $33.24 billion in revenue for the nine months ended Sept. 30, up roughly 1,200% from the same period last year. It reported a $104 billion net loss during the period, though most of that was due to the change in fair value of convertible notes and derivative liabilities. Anthropic had a loss of $25.1 billion over the same stretch in 2025.
Anthropic unveiled its highly anticipated prospectus last week, bringing the company one step closer to a potentially historic initial public offering. The initial filing only included the Claude maker's financials through the first half of the year.
During its third quarter, Anthropic generated roughly $17 billion in revenue, up about 1,127% from the same period last year. Revenue growth slowed on a quarter-to-quarter basis, however. Third-quarter revenue increased around 47%, a steep drop from 144% growth in Q2 and 132% in Q1.
The company reported a net loss of $23.29 billion during its third quarter.
As the 5-year-old company prepares to kick off its roadshow, it will have to reckon with growing market jitters around the AI buildout. Wall Street has been worried that rising interest rates will make debt unaffordable for many data center developers and neoclouds, and quarterly reports from Microsoft, Alphabet and Amazon last week added fuel to those concerns.
The hyperscalers' aggressive AI spending is eating into their cash flows, causing some investors to worry that computing capacity may not come online quickly enough to meet Anthropic's demands. Anthropic gets much of its capacity from Amazon, Google and SpaceX, but it also relies on neoclouds for some compute as it races to expand as quickly as possible.
In the amended S-1, Anthropic said it has entered into purchase commitments and lease agreements with an aggregate committed spend of about $895.4 billion, "substantially all of which is related to compute and infrastructure."
Anthropic said its compute and infrastructure expenses increased by $14.4 billion for the nine months ended Sept. 30, up 329% compared with the same period last year.
"As our compute and infrastructure capacity grows, the associated compute and infrastructure expenses have increased," Anthropic said in the filing.
The company's CEO, Dario Amodei, co-founded Anthropic alongside a handful of other researchers who defected from OpenAI, now its chief rival. OpenAI confidentially filed its prospectus with the SEC in June, but its executives have signaled that it's aiming for a 2027 debut.
Anthropic has worked to position itself as more safety-conscious than its competitors, and its S-1 includes more than 90 pages of risk factors. The company highlights risks, including potential political headwinds, its complex infrastructure needs as well as the difficulties that can come from balancing safety and commercial objectives.
"We consider it our personal responsibility to help the world understand these risks, to prevent or mitigate them when they arise from our AI models, and to work with governments and the rest of the industry to address them at a societal level," Anthropic's co-founders said in the filing.
WATCH: Anthropic releases amended IPO prospectus with third-quarter financials

====================================================================================================

School has given straight-a grades to the student, who can't do masic math
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-02
14:35:09 UTC
23:35:09 KST

NYC public schools deceived me into thinking my son was a straight-A math student — but he can’t do fractions, basic algebra
See more of our coverage in your search results.
For 13 years, Adriana Aviles, a fierce education advocate and former NYPD cop, has been the ultimate insider in New York City schools. As a powerful leader on the Citywide Council on High Schools, she championed Queens District 26 as one of the best in the city. She knew the principals and the teachers — and trusted them. She was gratified that her son consistently received good grades, especially in math, throughout his K-8 years. Then, two weeks ago, she sat down with her 14-year-old son to help him review for his second high school math exam. What happened next left her stunned and her son in tears — and prompted her to publicly confront Schools Chancellor Kamar Samuels at a town hall last week. She shared her family’s devastating experience with The Post’s David Spector.
All through middle school my son was, at least on paper, a numbers whiz. His report cards consistently showed A’s — his lowest grade was a B plus. But when he slid down to a 78 on his first exam at a top high school in Queens this fall, I pulled up a chair to help him review for his next test.
I’ll never forget seeing tears well up in my son’s eyes as he was only able to answer the first couple of questions on the worksheet.
He couldn’t solve basic algebra. He couldn’t do geometry. He couldn’t compute elementary-school fractions.
Yet at PS 221Q, the North Hills Elementary School, he regularly received the highest grade in math. At MS 67Q Louis Pasteur Middle School in Little Neck, his lowest math grade on an end-of-term report card was an 89 in seventh grade. He got a 92 in sixth grade and a 93 in eighth grade — the equivalent of A’s.
When he was admitted to his first choice high school, one New York’s top-rated schools, we had every reason to believe he would continue to excel.
What I saw when I sat down with my son to study shocked me to my core.
He stared blankly at the page, completely puzzled by the material. He was completely thrown off by equations that contained fractions.
My shock soon gave way to heartbreak, then anger.
My son is a mature, smart, industrious kid who has his own online business selling apparel. He doesn’t like to show when things are bothering him. As I saw tears fill his eyes, I was heartbroken. I told him that it wasn’t his fault; the system failed him, and we would do whatever it takes to get him caught up.
I didn’t talk to anybody that night about, not even my husband.
When I received a call from his teacher after he took the test, I told her I already knew why she was calling.
She told me he got only two of 10 multi-part questions right, and scored a 10 out of 100.
His high school is now stuck having to provide him the math education he should have received in elementary and middle school.
I’ve resorted to buying my son math review books intended for first- through fourth-graders so I can see how much he actually knows, and in which grade he stopped learning.
My son now misses lunch every Tuesday through Friday to get tutoring, and when baseball season ends he’ll get tutoring on Saturdays as well.
I’m the child of immigrants; my parents came to this country from Colombia and my mother instilled a deep love of education in my sisters and me. She was always on top of us to make sure we were doing our work and drilled the importance of a high school diploma into our heads.
The work ethic she imparted on me gave me the tools I needed to become an NYPD police officer, and I passed on those values when I became a parent.
When I married my husband, also an NYPD officer, in 2005, I insisted we move to District 26 in Douglaston, because it’s one of the best school districts in the entire city.
Had my son’s teachers just told me he was having trouble, we could have gotten him the help he needed early on. He would have done the work. But we were never told that. And the grades never gave us a clue.
I know now that teachers grade according to a rubric — and that DOE fudges the numbers. The NYC education system had betrayed my son to make itself look better.
What made this betrayal worse is I’ve worked with the teachers and administrators at my son’s schools for years.
I’m a longtime parent leader, I’ve served as PTA president, CEC president, and I now sit on the Citywide Council on High Schools. I’ve formed close relationships with educators at every stop.
I became active in my children’s education because I knew that the only way to be truly informed was to be involved. I thought I was fully aware of how my son was doing in school.
I was so wrong.
My son wasn’t in advanced classes, he was in general education, he didn’t take the Regents in middle school. I relied on his teachers to tell me the truth about how he was doing.
I failed too. I didn’t scrutinize his work closely or check his performance on state test scores because I figured if his report cards were good then everything was fine.
It was only recently, after I realized how far behind he is, that I looked back and discovered he was deemed “not proficient” in math on a seventh-grade state exam.
How can he not be proficient in math but was receiving As?
Why didn’t his middle school ever give him additional resources?
I feel deceived.
Looking back, there were warning signs. The teachers barely gave homework. He was allowed to revise his answers on tests after they were graded — and get a new grade. Teachers had a surprisingly laid-back attitude about missed assignments.
There are so many working-class parents in the NYC school system who don’t have the time to closely monitor their kids’ schoolwork and rely on their teachers — and the grading rubrics — to be honest. The lack of rigor in city schools and the inflation of grades are dishonest.
This is one of the reasons so many families are leaving the school system.
The DOE enables failure in minority communities by constantly lowering their standards instead of grading our children accurately, and by failing to collaborate with families to address issues kids may be having. They don’t even police chronic absenteeism. How can our kids get jobs if they can’t read or do math, and not even taught the value of showing up?
I soon found out my family was not alone in feeling cheated.
Shortly after my son’s October test, I reached out to other parents in his school to ask if their kids were also struggling in algebra. The responses were overwhelming; so many said that they were and asked what tutoring options were available.
I spoke to a mother of an eighth-grade girl who was apoplectic about her daughter’s math curriculum. She said her daughter, who is also in District 26, was only getting one page of homework a day and she’s urgently trying to get her into Catholic school.
That’s when I knew I had to use my position in the public school system to speak out for all of the families who have been failed by the system.
I decided to confront Schools Chancellor Kamar Samuels about it a Community Council on High Schools town hall on Monday, Oct. 26.
The DOE is ending the Regents requirement to graduate and moving to the “Portrait of a Graduate” model, which is supposed to grade students in a “holistic” manner that is even more dependent on subjective teacher evaluation. All of these problems will just get worse as we abandon more objective metrics.
I was already planning on asking Samuels about this at the town hall, but after seeing what happened to my son, I knew I had to share what my family had been through.
“This school system is failing our children. . . . I equate this to the childhood game ‘Kick the Can.’ My my son is the can. Where did he land?” I tearfully said as I sat on the dais in the Art and Design High School Auditorium.
Start your day with all you need to know
Morning Report delivers the latest news, videos, photos and more.
Thanks for signing up!
As I choked back tears, I made clear to the chancellor that the system’s failure would be felt by my family for years to come.
“In high school, where they are expected to help him graduate with the necessary tools to succeed, will they continue to kick the can all the way to college, where I will be expected to pay for a remedial class that does not count toward a degree?” I asked.
Finally, I pressed Samuels on the upcoming Portrait of a Graduate changes. How could we not expect to see what happened to my son happen to thousands of more students if grades are now even more dependent on the subjective judgments of biased teachers and principals?
Samuels seemed taken off guard by my comments. And his response disgusted me. He didn’t even acknowledge my question. He didn’t know what to do with himself. He was stammering and didn’t know what to say. It just sounded like mumbo jumbo. As a parent, and a longtime parent leader, I was entitled to a real response. I would have preferred a slick non-answer that politicians give, but he didn’t even give me that.
I had to walk off stage after that. Usually, after these events I stick around and pose for pictures with the DOE officials in attendance, but I went straight home that night.
I couldn’t pose for pictures with Samuels. If he can’t answer how we’re going to educate our children, who can?

Chicken Corn Chowder
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-02
14:30:00 UTC
23:30:00 KST

This Chicken Corn Chowder is thick, creamy, a little smoky, and full of tender chicken, sweet corn, and soft potatoes. I skip adding milk and cream to help keep costs down, but the chowder still turns out beautifully rich and creamy! There’s just something about a big pot of soup that feels like home, especially on a chilly day, and this one is cozy and comforting without feeling too heavy. It’s ready in about 50 minutes and makes eight generous servings for only $1.49 each. I love that there’s plenty to feed the family, with enough left over for a few easy lunches later in the week, too!
All recipes are rigorously tested in our Nashville Test Kitchen to ensure they are easy, affordable, and delicious.
A Creamy Chicken Corn Chowder Without Cream!
The bacon does a lot of the flavor-building in this chicken corn chowder, and I’m not just talking about the crispy bits sprinkled on top! Cooking the vegetables in the bacon drippings gives the whole pot a deliciously smoky, savory flavor. And instead of adding milk or cream, I use a little flour and purée some of the cooked vegetables and corn to make the broth wonderfully thick and silky. The rest stays chunky, so you still get plenty of tender chicken, sweet corn, and soft potatoes in each spoonful.
I also love using frozen corn here because it’s already prepped, budget-friendly, and means I can make this chowder any time of year without hunting down fresh corn on the cob. Between the chicken and all those vegetables, you get a hearty, satisfying meal made with familiar, inexpensive ingredients that my whole family will happily eat up (and ask for seconds!). What more could you ask for in a bowl of chowder?! If you love this sweet corn and potato combination, our potato corn chowder is another delicious one to try!
Recipe Success Tips
- Keep the potatoes evenly diced. Cut them into similar-sized, bite-sized pieces so they cook at the same rate. They should be tender enough for a fork to slide through easily before you move on to blending.
- Cook the flour and add the broth slowly. I cook the flour with the vegetables for 1-2 minutes to get rid of the raw, floury taste (because nobody wants that in their chowder!). Don’t skip this step! Once the flour has cooked, slowly whisk in the chicken broth, a little at a time, so everything combines smoothly without any lumps.
- Be careful when blending hot soup! I recommend letting the portion you’re puréeing cool slightly first. If using a countertop blender, work in small batches, fill it no more than halfway, vent the lid to avoid steam buildup, and start on low speed to avoid hot splashes. You can also use a handheld immersion blender in a separate, deep heatproof bowl.
- Don’t blend everything. I only purée about half of the cooked vegetables and broth with ½ cup of corn. This gives the chowder its creamy, velvety consistency while leaving plenty of vegetables and corn whole for texture. Just make sure you’ve removed the chicken before blending!
- Check the chicken AND the potatoes. They won’t necessarily finish cooking at the same time, especially if your potatoes are larger than the ones shown in my step photos below. The chicken should reach an internal temperature of 165°F, while the potatoes should be fork-tender. If the chicken is done first, I recommend taking it out to shred while the potatoes finish simmering. This helps prevent the lean chicken breast from becoming dry and tough.
- Don’t skip the apple cider vinegar! I know a teaspoon of vinegar might sound strange in a creamy chicken corn chowder, but that little splash makes a big difference. I add it right at the end to brighten up the flavors and balance the sweetness of the corn. It won’t make your chowder taste vinegary, but it helps bring all those delicious flavors together.
Chicken Corn Chowder
Cost $11.89 recipe / $1.49 serving
Ingredients
- 5 slices (110 g) bacon (diced, (4 oz.) $1.41)
- 1 medium (200 g) onion (diced, (2 cups) $0.66)
- 2 garlic cloves (minced, $0.12)
- 2 medium (140 g) carrots (diced, (1 cup) $0.30)
- 2 ribs (115 g) celery (diced, (1 cup) $0.25)
- 1 (200 g) red bell pepper (diced, (1½ cup) $1.48)
- ¼ cup (30 g) all-purpose flour ($0.03)
- 1 lb. chicken breast (boneless & skinless, $4.64*)
- 6 cups chicken broth ($0.71**)
- 1 lb. (450 g) Yukon gold potatoes (diced, (about 2 potatoes, 3½ cups) $0.96***)
- ½ tsp smoked paprika ($0.08)
- ½ tsp dried thyme ($0.05)
- ½ tsp salt ($0.04)
- ¼ tsp black pepper (freshly cracked, $0.04)
- 12 oz. frozen corn (1½ cups, $0.98****)
- 1 tsp apple cider vinegar ($0.02)
- 2 Tbsp fresh parsley (chopped, $0.12)
- green onions (optional)
Instructions
- Gather and prep all ingredients.
- In a large pot or Dutch oven, cook bacon over medium heat until crisp. Transfer to a paper towel-lined plate and leave the rendered fat in the pot.
- Add onion, garlic, carrots, celery, and red bell pepper to the bacon fat. Cook for 5-6 minutes until softened.
- Sprinkle flour over the vegetables and cook, stirring constantly for 1-2 minutes to remove the raw flour taste.
- Slowly whisk in the chicken broth. Stir in diced potatoes, chicken breasts, smoked paprika, thyme, salt, and pepper. Bring to a simmer, cover, and cook for 15-20 minutes, until potatoes are tender and chicken is cooked through.
- Remove chicken from the pot, shred with two forks, and set aside.
- Carefully remove about half of the vegetables and broth, add ½ cup of corn, and puree until smooth. Return the puree to the pot.
- Add shredded chicken, the remaining corn, apple cider vinegar, and chopped parsley. Taste and adjust seasonings as needed.
- Garnish with crumbled bacon and optional green onions. Serve warm with crusty bread.
See how we calculate recipe costs here.
Notes
Nutrition Information
how to make Chicken Corn Chowder Step-by-Step Photos
Gather and prep your ingredients: Dice 5 slices of bacon, 1 onion, 2 carrots, 2 celery ribs, 1 red bell pepper, and 1 lb. of Yukon Gold potatoes. Mince 2 garlic cloves and chop 2 Tbsp of fresh parsley. Try to keep the potatoes similar in size so they cook evenly. If you’re using Better Than Bouillon like I am, mix up 6 cups of chicken broth according to the package directions so it’s ready when you need it.
Cook the bacon until crispy: Add the 5 slices of diced bacon to a large pot or Dutch oven over medium heat. Cook until browned and crispy, stirring occasionally so it cooks evenly. Transfer the bacon to a paper towel-lined plate, but leave the rendered fat in the pot. We’ll use those flavorful drippings to cook the vegetables!
Soften the vegetables: Add 1 diced onion, 2 minced garlic cloves, 2 diced carrots, 2 diced celery ribs, and 1 diced red bell pepper to the bacon fat. Cook for 5-6 minutes, stirring occasionally, until the vegetables begin to soften and the onions look translucent.
Cook the flour: Sprinkle ¼ cup of all-purpose flour over the softened vegetables. Stir constantly for 1-2 minutes, until the flour is evenly mixed in. This cooks out the raw flour taste and helps create a thick, creamy broth later on.
Add the broth, chicken, and potatoes: Slowly whisk in 6 cups of chicken broth, a little at a time, until the flour is smoothly incorporated. Add 1 lb. of boneless, skinless chicken breasts, the diced potatoes, ½ tsp smoked paprika, ½ tsp dried thyme, ½ tsp salt, and ¼ tsp freshly cracked black pepper. Stir gently, bring to a simmer, then cover and cook for 15-20 minutes, or until the potatoes are fork-tender and the chicken reaches an internal temperature of 165°F.
Carefully lift the chicken breasts out of the pot and transfer them to a plate or cutting board. If the chicken reaches 165°F before the potatoes are tender, remove it anyway and let the potatoes continue simmering. This helps keep the chicken from becoming dry and tough.
Shred the chicken: Once the chicken is cool enough to handle, use two forks to pull the meat apart into bite-sized shreds. Set it aside while you thicken the chowder.
Blend part of the chowder: Carefully transfer about half of the cooked vegetables and broth to a blender, along with ½ cup of frozen corn. Let the mixture cool slightly, then blend until smooth.
Safety tip: Hot soup can build up steam and pressure in a blender, so let it cool for about 5 minutes before blending (the frozen corn will also help cool it down). Work in small batches, fill it no more than halfway, vent the lid, and start on low speed. You can also use an immersion blender in a separate heatproof bowl (use a deep one).
Finish the chowder: Return the smooth purée to the pot and stir to combine. Now add the shredded chicken, remaining frozen corn, 1 tsp apple cider vinegar, and 2 Tbsp chopped fresh parsley to the pot. Stir everything together and continue heating gently until the corn is thoroughly cooked and the chicken is hot. Taste the broth and adjust the salt and pepper if needed.
Garnish and serve: Ladle the finished chicken corn chowder into bowls and top with the reserved crispy bacon, extra parsley, or optional sliced green onions, if desired. The broth should be thick and creamy, with tender chicken, potatoes, and sweet corn throughout. Serve warm with some crusty bread for dipping!
What to Serve with Chicken Corn Chowder
While the chowder is simmering, there’s time to make a little something on the side, and warm bread is usually where my mind goes first. A slice of our homemade cornbread is delicious with the smoky bacon and sweet corn, and it only takes 30 minutes to make. Homemade garlic bread, crusty bread, or soft dinner rolls are also great for soaking up every last drop of that creamy broth. If you want something a little lighter, try a simple side salad for some fresh crunch. It takes just 5 minutes to throw together, or you could add a side of roasted vegetables.
And don’t forget the toppings! I save the crispy bacon from the beginning of the recipe to crumble over each bowl, along with some fresh parsley or sliced green onions. A little shredded cheddar or Parmesan is also delicious, as are some ranch oyster crackers, and you can even add a dollop of sour cream or a swirl of heavy cream if you want to make your chowder extra rich. For bigger appetites, try stirring a little cooked rice, pasta, or quinoa into individual bowls to make it even more filling.
Storage & Reheating
This chicken and corn chowder makes 8 generous servings, so it’s a great recipe for meal prepping lunches throughout the week. Let any leftovers cool slightly, then divide them into airtight containers and refrigerate for up to 3 days. I recommend keeping any extra bacon and toppings separate, so they stay fresh and crispy.
Reheating
I prefer reheating this chowder in a saucepan over medium-low heat, stirring occasionally until heated thoroughly to 165°F. The broth does thicken in the fridge, so add a splash of chicken broth to loosen it up if needed. You can also microwave individual servings, stirring between short intervals so everything heats evenly. I recommend only reheating what you plan to eat to help keep the chicken tender.
Freezing
You can freeze this chicken corn chowder for up to 2 months. Once cooled, portion it into freezer-safe containers or heavy-duty freezer bags, leaving about an inch of space at the top for expansion.
Thaw overnight in the fridge before reheating on the stovetop or in the microwave. Because this chowder doesn’t use milk or cream in the base, there’s less concern about dairy separating, although the potatoes may soften a little after freezing. Add a splash of broth if it needs thinning out.
Try These Creamy Potato Soup Recipes Next
- When I want a simple, classic potato soup, I make my Classic Potato Soup. It uses russet potatoes and a little milk to create a thick, creamy broth, with plenty of tender potato chunks.
- Leftover holiday ham is a delicious excuse to make our Ham and Potato Soup. The salty ham adds plenty of savory flavor to the creamy potatoes and vegetables, and it makes eight servings in about an hour.
- For those days when you’d rather let the slow cooker handle dinner, try our extra-rich, creamy, EASY Slow Cooker Potato Soup too!

Northwood Space raises $375M Series C at a $2B valuation from a16z and DFJ growth
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-02
14:22:35 UTC
23:22:35 KST

Space infrastructure company Northwood Space on Monday said it had raised $375 million in a Series C round co-led by Andreessen Horowitz and DFJ Growth, valuing the company at $2 billion.
Founded in 2022, Northwood builds antennas, software, and other technologies that help satellites communicate better with Earth. Bridgit Mendler, co-founder and former actress, serves as its CEO.
The company last raised a $100 million Series B in January, also co-led by Andreessen Horowitz, along with a nearly $50 million contract with the U.S. Space Force to upgrade the nation’s “satellite control network,” TechCrunch previously reported.
This latest round also includes a financial agreement with HSBC that, along with the funding, will be used to scale the company, hire, and fulfill existing contracts, the startup said.

A fresh wave of cyberattacks has hit Europe’s supermarket refrigerators, destroying food across countries
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-02
14:08:52 UTC
23:08:52 KST

A second wave of cyberattacks is suspected to have hit supermarket refrigerators across Europe, causing fridges to increase temperatures and spoil food at dozens of stores across Belgium, Ireland, Luxembourg, and Sweden.
Supermarket giants Ahold Delhaize and Colruyt confirmed the hacks overnight, with one Belgian company that operates Delhaize’s systems saying that the hackers had changed the settings to defrost, per Reuters. Stores have experienced heavy food losses from the cyberattacks, forcing supermarkets to throw out goods that had defrosted or were otherwise no longer consumable.
The cyberattacks follow earlier refrigerator hacks in France last month that caused food spoilage at hundreds of supermarkets by deliberately raising the temperature on the units. Security researchers said that the cause of the hacks was internet-connected refrigerators made by Danish company Danfoss, which was no longer providing security updates. Security company Claroty warned back in August that those refrigerator units were at risk of remote hacks and outages.
The freezer hacks underscore how cyberattacks can have real-world effects, including on the food supply chain, often as a result of weak or overlooked cybersecurity practices. In 2025, a cyberattack on food distributor giant UNFI resulted in food shortages and empty shelves at grocery stores and supermarkets across the United States.
U.S. Department of Defense officials are reportedly investigating whether freezer units on military bases were hacked by Iran-backed hackers. Iranian hackers were also blamed for cyberattacks targeting U.S. water providers earlier this year.

The 16GB Raspberry Pi 5 got another ‘unavoidable’ RAM price hike
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-02
14:07:22 UTC
23:07:22 KST

The Raspberry Pi 5 with 16GB of RAM now costs $385 after an $80 price hike, the latest in a string of increases driven by the ongoing RAM shortage. This configuration launched at $120 and now costs more than triple that price. The 16GB versions of the Raspberry Pi Compute Module 5 also got a $40 price hike and now cost between $375 and $415.
The 16GB Raspberry Pi 5 got another ‘unavoidable’ RAM price hike
The top configuration of the Raspberry Pi 5 now costs three times more than its launch price.
According to Raspberry Pi CEO Eben Upton, these price hikes were driven specifically by a lack of “alternative supply” for 16GB DRAM, on top of shortages for other components. Upton says, “Though these increases are unavoidable, we know they are unwelcome news for our customers. We encourage you to make sure you’re not paying for more memory than you need.” Upton suggested using Pis with less memory or older Pi models as alternatives to the pricey 16GB model. Unfortunately, other Raspberry Pi 5 configurations have also gotten more expensive — the 2GB version got a price hike last month.
At $385, the 16GB Raspberry Pi 5 is nearing the price of a desktop mini PC. Upton reiterated the company’s plans to reverse the price hikes, saying, “the current memory landscape is difficult but ultimately temporary, and we look forward to rolling back our price increases when memory costs fall once again.”

SpaceX could buy Boost Mobile as part of its big mobile carrier plans
SpaceX is looking into launching its own service through Boost Mobile, The Washington Post reports.
Musk discussed the potential agreement with Charlie Ergen, chairman of Boost Mobile parent company EchoStar, last month, as reported by The Washington Post. Boost Mobile’s network is currently powered by T-Mobile and AT&T, meaning SpaceX may need permission from the two carriers to launch its service.
Neither SpaceX nor Boost Mobile immediately responded to The Verge’s request for comment.
In recent months, SpaceX has ramped up efforts to build out its mobile service, which will combine its satellite-to-mobile constellation with a terrestrial network. The company acquired wireless spectrum licenses from EchoStar last year and recently purchased a portfolio of low-band spectrum licenses that could allow Starlink Mobile’s signal to penetrate buildings. Last month, Bloomberg reported that SpaceX is in talks to gain access to at least 80,000 cell sites in the US.

Pray for Chaos
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-02
14:02:53 UTC
23:02:53 KST

Pray for Chaos
There’s two basic ways AI can play out, one is order, the other is chaos.
Order is the very dangerous one. If it turns out that models are actually capable of patching all the exploits, it’s hard to imagine what will disrupt the status quo. Fortunately, this seems unlikely, but that doesn’t mean order won’t prevail in the short term. At the end of my Yudkowsky debate we found a tangible point of disagreement. He thinks AI will be able to find a solution to the Prisoner’s dilemma. I know there already is a solution (it’s defect), EA people just aren’t happy about it.
If it won’t be order (and you better pray it won’t be order) that leaves chaos. AI that can play a whole society in hours can manufacture entire histories, years of archives of daily newspapers, and an Internet that never happened. The dead Internet theory is coming true before our eyes. Now I can still see the bots, soon I won’t be able to. Scratch that, I probably already often can’t.
Gwern wrote about this, it’s hard to imagine a vision of humans as prompters or enter pressers in 2030. The US AI companies don’t believe they have to offer any solution to this, why would they have to justify in any way that their products are good for society? Anthropic cannot wait until they have the leverage to ban all their users. Why would you keep around the middle man?
Here’s the most positive vision I can come up with. If it turns out things can’t be secured, we enter an era of increased decentralization. Think everyone running their own custom different OS, not everyone running the same version of Linux on a computer they can touch. Because the centralized homogeneous systems are too vulnerable. Cyber attacks flying in every direction, nobody capable of keeping infrastructure up, cars sitting in driveways as bricks (or worse, rooted to steer you off a cliff).
Self replicating AI hiding out in every corner of every hard drive. How powerful of a model do you need for it to stand a chance of reproduction in today’s cyberworld? GLM-5.2 and up?
A media landscape that is botted and microtargeted to all hell, nonsensical elections where the only thing people agree on is the names of candidates. Massive dumps of corporate slacks and intranets. (come on Russian kids, grab an abliterated AI and do a hacking!)
Whatever you hold dear the chaos army will come for too. The Internet era is over. Your life will never be the same. The chaos will come for the resistance as well as the fascists; it’s unclear who it will hurt more. Your life plan is probably already nonsense in the face of AI. Mine sort of is. What economic value you think you will have in 10 years? Who will be around for you to have that value to? Do you think you’ll be able to make any sense of the world?
And remember, chaos is the good outcome. The alternative is that you are a dancing monkey in a cage you won’t be smart enough to see.

OpenAI is finally merging ChatGPT’s chat and Work tabs
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-02
13:57:53 UTC
22:57:53 KST

The ChatGPT desktop app can be a confusing piece of software, given that there are ChatGPT and Codex sections, and within the former, there are Chat and Work tabs. With so many options, it’s sometimes easy to forget where you are working and where exactly your project is.
In an apparent effort to address this, OpenAI is now gearing up to merge the Chat and Work tabs within the ChatGPT experience, according to an email sent to users. OpenAI said that initially, this experience will be available to select users for feedback before it is rolled out widely.
“This will enable you to move from exploring an idea to having ChatGPT carry it through, all in one place. You can bring in your files and plugins and ask ChatGPT to build a website, create a presentation, or do things for you on the web, while reviewing and guiding its work. Codex will remain a separate experience in the ChatGPT desktop app,” the email said.
OpenAI also said with this transition, both ChatGPT and Codex will draw tokens from the same allowance pool. It said this change won’t impact most users, but based on usage and model, some folks might need to wait for resets more frequently.
Rival Anthropic merged Claude’s chat and Cowork functions into one screen in September.

The U.S. Census Bureau and "Super Intelligence"
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-02
13:55:45 UTC
22:55:45 KST

The U.S. Census Bureau and “Super Intelligence”
2nd November 2026
I was reminded today of AI Adoption Rate Trending Down for Large Companies, a chart produced by Apollo Global Management in September 2025, which appeared to show a slowdown in U.S. corporate AI adoption based on numbers collected by the U.S. Census Bureau. At the time I used that chart to demonstrate GPT-5 data analysis, and then to experiment with Claude’s new Code Interpreter. I decided to try running those numbers again to see what had changed over the past year, and in doing so learned that the Bureau has fallen foul of Executive Order 14434, aka the asinine Super Intelligence one.
First, the updated chart
Every two weeks the U.S. Census Bureau runs a survey called the Business Trends and Outlook Survey (aka BTOS). Each time it goes to 200,000 businesses out of 1.2 million total—the surveyed group (a “panel”) cycles such that an individual business only completes the survey every twelve weeks.
The latest data is published on alternating Thursdays at 10am.
I used the most recent numbers (published October 22, 2026) to create a new version (GPT-6 Astra Extra High transcript) of the AI adoption chart, updated to reflect both the last twelve months and a change that was made to the survey question sent out by the Bureau:
Here’s a link to the larger image.
For comparison, here’s that AI adoption chart from last year (my GPT-5 version):
In September 2025 the largest (250+ employees) companies answered “yes” around 12% of the time to the question about whether they used AI in “producing goods or services”.
Under the new “any business function” wording, by February 2026, those same large companies answered “yes” around 30% of the time, and by October 2026 that had increased to around 40%.
The data in the above chart comes from the “Employment size class” XLSX file, available from here. Here’s a copy of the file I used.
You can view the survey document that is sent out to businesses—here’s my snapshot copy of that 346KB PDF.
The changing language of the questions
As the chart demonstrates, the question asked by the Bureau changed in a material way on November 17th, 2025. Prior to that date, the question they asked was this (emphasis mine):
In the last two weeks, did this business use Artificial Intelligence (AI) in producing goods or services? (Examples of AI: machine learning, natural language processing, virtual agents, voice recognition, etc.)
On December 3rd, 2025, the Bureau announced a significant change to that question. Here’s the replacement:
In the last two weeks, did this business use Artificial Intelligence (AI) in any of its business functions? (Examples of AI: machine learning, natural language processing, virtual agents, voice recognition, etc.)
No more “producing goods or services”—now it’s “in any of its business functions”! This means that the answers to the question before and after November 17th are not directly comparable.
From October 19, 2026, they ask about Super Intelligence
When I went looking for the updated question language in the latest XLSX file, I couldn’t find it. I found this instead:
In the last two weeks, did this business use Super Intelligence or SI (formerly referred to as Artificial Intelligence or AI) in any of its business functions? (Examples of SI use include: SI-powered data analysis; generative SI for marketing, product design, or coding; virtual SI agents for customer service; SI-powered forecasting or pricing; SI-driven computer vision for quality control, etc.)
The BTOS about page explains what happened:
Beginning with the October 19, 2026 collection, instrument wording was updated due to Executive Order 14434.
The current numbers reflect the old Artificial Intelligence survey wording—the first survey results reflecting the Super Intelligence wording are due for publication on Thursday.
Is this going to affect the survey?
The obvious open question here is whether this wording change is going to affect the survey results.
The businesses answering this survey will have last seen that question twelve weeks ago, when it was still called AI. Hopefully the new language isn’t going to confuse them, especially since the survey helpfully spells out “formerly referred to as Artificial Intelligence or AI”.
I can’t help but laugh at the absurdity of “SI-powered data analysis; generative SI for marketing, product design, or coding; virtual SI agents for customer service” though.
More recent articles
- The October inflection point for vibe game development - 28th October 2026
- Battle Drones - a vibe coded RTS with audio-based multiplayer - 20th October 2026
- Hello Python 3.15, goodbye 3.10, and now I can use 3.11 - 12th October 2026
- A new feature for my blog, built using my voice - 9th October 2026

Some Traffic Calming Might Be in Order
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-02
13:44:40 UTC
22:44:40 KST

After the city of Tacoma installed cameras, they captured a ridiculous number of speeders in the first month of usage:
New speed cameras at Norpoint Way and Marine View Drive in Northeast Tacoma photographed almost 400,000 drivers exceeding the corridor’s 40 mph speed limit between Sept. 14 and Oct. 11, according to city data…In total, approximately 515,000 vehicles drove through the corridor in the same period, meaning that roughly 77% were speeding. On average, the cameras photographed slightly more than 14,000 speeders daily.
That’s really just a tremendous amount of speeding. In fact, it’s so much speeding that the company in charge of sending out tickets was overwhelmed. As a result, the city has decided to send just 100 tickets a day, meaning speeders have a 0.71% chance of facing a penalty.
