If you love a website, back it up
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-02
13:25:35 UTC
22:25:35 KST

This story starts with a little tragedy. See, I’ve got these old webpages I love to go back and look on every now and then. There’s websites that have been online unchanged for years and years, or decades, and there’s something a little cozy about checking in on them every now and then to see an old friend is still doing okay. Sometimes, of course, tragedy strikes.
There was this fansite called teapuppies.com for an old anime series and and toy line called Ochaken. It’s about these cute cartoon dog characters who have tea leaves for ears, each of them named after a different kind of tea.1 The site had been online since 2004 and had basically not changed since 2005, but it was nice just having it around. Except when I went to visit it a few weeks ago, and discovered that not only was the site gone but the domain is now being used to serve online gambling ads.
I work in web archiving, by the way. Which is to say that I should have known the site would go offline someday, and that I have the tools to have done something about it. I could have archived that site while it was still around, but I didn’t. This post is here to beg you to do what I didn’t think to, and to teach you how.
You see, when I talk to a lot of people about web archiving, they always seem to believe it’s something they couldn’t do. It’s something a big organization like the Internet Archive does, and if you want a webpage to be saved you just have to wait and hope it gets done. But that’s not true! What I really want everyone to know is that you have control over the world around you, you can take action to preserve things and make things better. And not just can, but should! Don’t just wait for someone else to act - get out there and and make it happen! Got a favourite site? Back it up! Got some information you don’t want to get lost? Back it up! Favourite post somewhere? All together now. Back! It! Up!
All of the tools I’ll be showing off in this post use standard web archiving formats like WARC2. You don’t have to worry about any of the technical details3, but I’m telling you that because it means there are a lot of really great tools to do whatever you want with them. Here, just as an example, is an entire archived website embedded right in this blog post:
You can even click through all the links, look at all of the images, the works. It’s an entire website right in a post! (And if you don’t trust me to keep this up, and you want the web archive to host somewhere else? You can download it for yourself right from there. Just click the kebab menu and choose “download archive”, and you can do whatever you like with it. In fact, please do!)
By the way, for disclosure: the tools I’m going to mention in this blog post are made by the company I work for, but that’s not because they’ll pay my salary (most of them are free!). I just happen to think these are the best tools there are for personal web archiving.
Option 1 - ArchiveWeb.page browser extension
By far the easiest personal web archiving tool is the ArchiveWeb.page browser extension for Chrome. This doesn’t take much in the way of setup or technical skills, and it works for anything: a single page, an entire website, a social media post or profile. So long as you can see it in a web browser, you can save it. All you really need to do is:
- Install the browser extension.
- Visit the first page you want to archive.
- Click the ArchiveWeb.page icon in the toolbar, then click “Start Archiving”.
- If your page has any interactive elements, click on those to make sure all their data loads.
- Visit any other pages you want captured in the same collection and let the extension grab them.
- Click the ArchiveWeb.page icon in the toolbar, then click “Stop Archiving”.
You’re done! You just archived a web page! Now that it’s all saved, you can preview it and see how it went by clicking the extension’s icon and then the home icon to open the extension dashboard. From there, you can click on your archiving session to preview the pages you archived, load them in browser, and see if they work. (I’ll go into more details on how you can look at web archives and share them with others later in this post.)
Option 2 - archiveweb.page app
If you don’t want to install a browser extension, or you don’t have Chrome installed, you can also grab the standalone archiveweb.page app. This is basically the same thing as the browser extension above, it just has its own web browser built in. It also opens straight to the menu that lets you browse your web archives, so it’s a handy way to look at your web archives locally. Pretty much everything else I described above applies.
Option 3 - Browsertrix
The above options are great for single pages or sites that are small enough for you to browse manually. But if you’ve got a webpage that’s big enough that it’s going to be annoying visiting all of the pages by hand, you probably want to look at a web crawler tool for web archiving. There are a bunch of options out there, but the easiest to use as an individual is probably Browsertrix. A web crawler is basically an automation tool built around a web browser; you provide instructions on what page to start from and what pages it should visit, and it will go ahead and click links to automatically find pages so that it can archive the page as completely as possible. The web archive I embedded above was done using Browsertrix on pretty close to its default settings: I just told it I wanted to do a site crawl for the entire domain https://teapuppies.mistys-internet.website, and it worked it out from there:
But, depending, on what you’re looking for, it has options which can be customized to narrow down what content it archives. For example, if you decided you only want pages for the episodes, you could tell it to only capture anything at the /episodes/ section of the website:
Or even just a single page and anything that page links to:
There are a lot more options than I can go into in a quick overview - it’s a comprehensive tool with a lot of options. But if you’re looking to archive a whole website, or a complete social media profile, I’d recommend giving it a try.
Unlike the other tools, which you mainly run on your own computer, Browsertrix is primarily a web app. It’s also the only paid tool in this post - there’s a seven day trial, but after that you do have to pay per use. Since it’s open source, though, technically inclined users can run their own copies on their computer or deploy a copy somewhere if they like.
Viewing your archived webpage
Once you’ve actually backed the site up, you can replay it to use it in your browser just like a live webpage. It’s kind of like having your own private version of the wayback machine. You can test it out yourself for your own private use using the interface built into the version of archiveweb.page you used, but if you want to share it with someone else you have a few options. If you want to share it with someone privately, you can send them the WARC/WACZ file and let them load it into the ArchiveWeb.page app or the ReplayWeb.page website. If you want to share it with the world, though, you can set up a website with your web archive on it.
This post is using the ReplayWeb.page embedded viewer, which is open source and can be embedded inside any other web page you want. You don’t have run any kind of app on your server to do this; as long as you have the ability to upload the archive and a little bit of JavaScript, you can easily add a copy of the replay interface into any web page or blog post.
Preserving the data once you have it
Since everything you backed up is bundled inside a single WACZ file, it’s really easy to keep safe - or share with someone else. Lots of copies keeps stuff safe, after all; if you think you have friends who’d like to hold on to a copy of whatever site you’ve backed it up, pass it along and share the duty of preserving it!
If any of this sounds interesting to you, I’d encourage you to at least give it a try. I’d love for the world to be filled with thousands upon thousands of little web archives, with people and community groups archiving and sharing the things that matter to them for themselves. If you’re finding any of this overwhelming, and you’re not sure where to get started, please reach out to me - I’m very happy to give you a hand.
- The appeal to me is, I hope, obvious. ↩
- Most of the tools I’m mentioning also use the WACZ format, which packages up WARC files along with extra metadata. ↩
- For those who want a little extra technical detail, though: one of the things that makes WARC special is that it records complete information about requests and responses, including all headers. This gives WARC a much more complete view of the whole process of visiting a website, and makes it possible to archive even complicated sites with client-side JavaScript that make a ton of requests. ↩

22:25:35 KST

This story starts with a little tragedy. See, I’ve got these old webpages I love to go back and look on every now and then. There’s websites that have been online unchanged for years and years, or decades, and there’s something a little cozy about checking in on them every now and then to see an old friend is still doing okay. Sometimes, of course, tragedy strikes.
There was this fansite called teapuppies.com for an old anime series and and toy line called Ochaken. It’s about these cute cartoon dog characters who have tea leaves for ears, each of them named after a different kind of tea.1 The site had been online since 2004 and had basically not changed since 2005, but it was nice just having it around. Except when I went to visit it a few weeks ago, and discovered that not only was the site gone but the domain is now being used to serve online gambling ads.
I work in web archiving, by the way. Which is to say that I should have known the site would go offline someday, and that I have the tools to have done something about it. I could have archived that site while it was still around, but I didn’t. This post is here to beg you to do what I didn’t think to, and to teach you how.
You see, when I talk to a lot of people about web archiving, they always seem to believe it’s something they couldn’t do. It’s something a big organization like the Internet Archive does, and if you want a webpage to be saved you just have to wait and hope it gets done. But that’s not true! What I really want everyone to know is that you have control over the world around you, you can take action to preserve things and make things better. And not just can, but should! Don’t just wait for someone else to act - get out there and and make it happen! Got a favourite site? Back it up! Got some information you don’t want to get lost? Back it up! Favourite post somewhere? All together now. Back! It! Up!
All of the tools I’ll be showing off in this post use standard web archiving formats like WARC2. You don’t have to worry about any of the technical details3, but I’m telling you that because it means there are a lot of really great tools to do whatever you want with them. Here, just as an example, is an entire archived website embedded right in this blog post:
You can even click through all the links, look at all of the images, the works. It’s an entire website right in a post! (And if you don’t trust me to keep this up, and you want the web archive to host somewhere else? You can download it for yourself right from there. Just click the kebab menu and choose “download archive”, and you can do whatever you like with it. In fact, please do!)
By the way, for disclosure: the tools I’m going to mention in this blog post are made by the company I work for, but that’s not because they’ll pay my salary (most of them are free!). I just happen to think these are the best tools there are for personal web archiving.
Option 1 - ArchiveWeb.page browser extension
By far the easiest personal web archiving tool is the ArchiveWeb.page browser extension for Chrome. This doesn’t take much in the way of setup or technical skills, and it works for anything: a single page, an entire website, a social media post or profile. So long as you can see it in a web browser, you can save it. All you really need to do is:
- Install the browser extension.
- Visit the first page you want to archive.
- Click the ArchiveWeb.page icon in the toolbar, then click “Start Archiving”.
- If your page has any interactive elements, click on those to make sure all their data loads.
- Visit any other pages you want captured in the same collection and let the extension grab them.
- Click the ArchiveWeb.page icon in the toolbar, then click “Stop Archiving”.
You’re done! You just archived a web page! Now that it’s all saved, you can preview it and see how it went by clicking the extension’s icon and then the home icon to open the extension dashboard. From there, you can click on your archiving session to preview the pages you archived, load them in browser, and see if they work. (I’ll go into more details on how you can look at web archives and share them with others later in this post.)
Option 2 - archiveweb.page app
If you don’t want to install a browser extension, or you don’t have Chrome installed, you can also grab the standalone archiveweb.page app. This is basically the same thing as the browser extension above, it just has its own web browser built in. It also opens straight to the menu that lets you browse your web archives, so it’s a handy way to look at your web archives locally. Pretty much everything else I described above applies.
Option 3 - Browsertrix
The above options are great for single pages or sites that are small enough for you to browse manually. But if you’ve got a webpage that’s big enough that it’s going to be annoying visiting all of the pages by hand, you probably want to look at a web crawler tool for web archiving. There are a bunch of options out there, but the easiest to use as an individual is probably Browsertrix. A web crawler is basically an automation tool built around a web browser; you provide instructions on what page to start from and what pages it should visit, and it will go ahead and click links to automatically find pages so that it can archive the page as completely as possible. The web archive I embedded above was done using Browsertrix on pretty close to its default settings: I just told it I wanted to do a site crawl for the entire domain https://teapuppies.mistys-internet.website, and it worked it out from there:
But, depending, on what you’re looking for, it has options which can be customized to narrow down what content it archives. For example, if you decided you only want pages for the episodes, you could tell it to only capture anything at the /episodes/ section of the website:
Or even just a single page and anything that page links to:
There are a lot more options than I can go into in a quick overview - it’s a comprehensive tool with a lot of options. But if you’re looking to archive a whole website, or a complete social media profile, I’d recommend giving it a try.
Unlike the other tools, which you mainly run on your own computer, Browsertrix is primarily a web app. It’s also the only paid tool in this post - there’s a seven day trial, but after that you do have to pay per use. Since it’s open source, though, technically inclined users can run their own copies on their computer or deploy a copy somewhere if they like.
Viewing your archived webpage
Once you’ve actually backed the site up, you can replay it to use it in your browser just like a live webpage. It’s kind of like having your own private version of the wayback machine. You can test it out yourself for your own private use using the interface built into the version of archiveweb.page you used, but if you want to share it with someone else you have a few options. If you want to share it with someone privately, you can send them the WARC/WACZ file and let them load it into the ArchiveWeb.page app or the ReplayWeb.page website. If you want to share it with the world, though, you can set up a website with your web archive on it.
This post is using the ReplayWeb.page embedded viewer, which is open source and can be embedded inside any other web page you want. You don’t have run any kind of app on your server to do this; as long as you have the ability to upload the archive and a little bit of JavaScript, you can easily add a copy of the replay interface into any web page or blog post.
Preserving the data once you have it
Since everything you backed up is bundled inside a single WACZ file, it’s really easy to keep safe - or share with someone else. Lots of copies keeps stuff safe, after all; if you think you have friends who’d like to hold on to a copy of whatever site you’ve backed it up, pass it along and share the duty of preserving it!
If any of this sounds interesting to you, I’d encourage you to at least give it a try. I’d love for the world to be filled with thousands upon thousands of little web archives, with people and community groups archiving and sharing the things that matter to them for themselves. If you’re finding any of this overwhelming, and you’re not sure where to get started, please reach out to me - I’m very happy to give you a hand.
- The appeal to me is, I hope, obvious. ↩
- Most of the tools I’m mentioning also use the WACZ format, which packages up WARC files along with extra metadata. ↩
- For those who want a little extra technical detail, though: one of the things that makes WARC special is that it records complete information about requests and responses, including all headers. This gives WARC a much more complete view of the whole process of visiting a website, and makes it possible to archive even complicated sites with client-side JavaScript that make a ton of requests. ↩

More European supermarkets are getting their freezers hacked
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-02
13:00:35 UTC
22:00:35 KST

Grocery stores across Europe have been hit by another wave of suspected cyberattacks, reporting sudden changes in the temperatures of their fridges and freezers. Major supermarket chains across Belgium, Sweden, Luxembourg, and Estonia were affected, according to local reports shared by Bloomberg and Reuters.
More European supermarkets are getting their freezers hacked
Stores in Belgium, Sweden, Luxembourg, and Estonia have had their cooling systems tampered with.
One Ica Kvantum store in Sweden said on its Facebook page that its fridges and freezers had been hacked overnight, with temperatures remotely raised to 50 degrees Celsius (122 Fahrenheit). In a statement to Business Insider, a spokesperson at Swedish supermarket chain Coop also confirmed that “unauthorized actors were able to access and tamper with the cooling systems at a small fraction of our stores.” Dutch retailer Ahold Delhaize said an “IT incident” was responsible for adjusting the temperature of refrigeration units in dozens of its supermarkets in Belgium and Luxembourg. The full scale of the incident has yet to be confirmed.
Many modern supermarket fridges and freezers are connected to the internet to support remote maintenance and system failure alerts, but the increasing number of these suspected hacks is raising concerns around how security flaws can impact food supply chains. This latest incident follows an apparent cyberattack that targeted hundreds of French supermarket refrigerators last month, with the resulting food spoilage estimated to be worth millions of euros, along with a string of US military base freezer malfunctions also suspected to be the result of a cyberattack.

It picked the drug. It picked the brain region. It d… / X
Gabriel Ocana-Santero on X: "Last night an AI agent performed brain surgery on a mouse.
We gave it 19 gene therapy vectors in a spreadsheet, a mouse model of epilepsy, access to a motorize surgery rig with cameras and one goal: reduce seizures.
It picked the drug. It picked the brain region. It derived the coordinates from three papers. Then it drove the stereotaxic arms into the brain and injected 250 nanolitres while watching the entry site through the rig camera for bleeding.
A licensed vet gated every irreversible step and held a physical kill switch to stop the AI agent.
Thread on what it decided, what it saw, and the moment it refused to let me override it.
[Writing this on the spot out of excitement, but I've been 18hrs in front of my laptop coding and coordinating this, so keeping it brief and descriptive, more cohesive thoughts on this soon...]"

Itch Is Being Flooded By AI-Generated Garbage
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-02
12:34:00 UTC
21:34:00 KST

Itch Is Being Flooded By AI-Generated Garbage (aftermath.site) 56
			
		 	
				The number of game pages being created per-day on Itch has doubled, the indie games site announced Saturday on Bluesky.  When asked why, they responded:  "take a guess."
As the gaming news site Aftermath puts it, "the surge has been almost entirely down to AI-created garbage racing onto the platform as the popularity of vibe-coded theft, copycats and machine-built slop explodes."
Back in 2024, when the spectre of AI-'created' video games was still in its infancy, indie platform itch.io decided that the best it could do to combat this growing threat was, like Valve has done with Steam, institute a voluntary 'AI' filter when adding new projects to the site. By November 2026, as itch buckles under the weight of new AI-generated 'games' pouring onto the site, that filter is proving about as robust as a wet paper bag....  Shopfronts are being deluged by this shit, and it's making it a terrible user experience for customers.
And while in Valve's case there's a compelling case for stronger human moderation of the store, I'm not sure that's a viable option for a relatively tiny, community-run platform like itch...  [A]t time of writing I visited the latest releases section and there was still a huge number of clearly AI-generated titles (or titles making extensive use of AI in their visual assets) which made no use of itch's voluntary tag system. 
The issue here isn't that these games are making money--itch themselves say in a reply that while the number of projects listed has doubled, "the number of downloads and purchases has not"--it's that an inability to combat this surge makes the site worse for every single person using it. It gets harder to find real games, for example, and it also makes the shopfront look like a clearance rack.

Affinity’s creative studio is now available on iPad for free
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-02
12:10:16 UTC
21:10:16 KST

Canva is giving digital creatives a new way to design, illustrate, and edit photos on their iPads, and it doesn’t have to cost them a thing. After relaunching the Affinity creative suite as an all-in-one desktop app last year, Canva has now introduced Affinity for iPad, which provides the same pro-grade tools for vector graphics, photo editing, illustration, and page layouts in a format optimized for Apple Pencil and touchscreens.
Affinity’s creative studio is now available on iPad for free
Affinity for iPad has a new Sketch Studio that’s designed for freehand drawing and simplifying sketch-to-vector workflows, including optimized tools for masking and automatically converting rough sketches into cleaner geometric shapes. The app also supports Apple Pencil Pro capabilities like natural gestures, pressure sensitivity, tilt, barrel roll, squeeze, and hover, allowing creatives more control over tools and shortcuts.
“Affinity for iPad delivers the full power of Affinity, together with the versatility and portability of iPad,” Affinity product design head Neil Ladkin said in the announcement. “Every development decision was made by people who create on iPad themselves, from how the Apple Pencil responds to where the tools sit. If you’re the sort of person who rotates the canvas 40 times to get one line right, Affinity for iPad was made by people who’ve done exactly that.”
Sketch, vector design, photo editing, and layout projects can all be saved to the same .af file to limit tasks needing to be exported, converted, or remade, but Affinity for iPad is also compatible with all major design file types. Canva has added its AI Studio to the app, which includes features like Generative Expand for extending backgrounds, automatic motion blur and noise reduction, and image resolution scaling.
When Canva acquired Affinity-developer Serif in 2024, some users were concerned that Affinity would move from a one-time license to a subscription model and adopt Canva’s generative AI tech, eradicating a large part of the creative suite’s appeal compared to rival design software provider Adobe. Canva and Serif pledged not to force users into subscribing to the service, and so far that’s still the case.
Affinity for iPad’s core sketch, vector, photo editing, and layout tools are completely free for individuals, though they require a free Canva account. The suite of AI tools is optional and requires a Canva Pro subscription to access, which starts at $18 per month.

Never Complain, Never Explain
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-02
11:55:48 UTC
20:55:48 KST

Never complain, never explain
"Never complain, never explain" is a public relations strategy that has become particularly associated with the British royal family.
Origins
[edit]
The phrase is believed to have originated with the prime minister of the United Kingdom, Benjamin Disraeli.[1] It was attributed to Disraeli by John Morley in 1903, as quoted in Morley's Life of William Ewart Gladstone with the saying originating from "Maxims for a Statesman" by Benjamin Jowett, the Master of Balliol College, Oxford, written between 1873 and 1876.[1][2] One of Jowett's 11 maxims was "never quarrel, never explain, never hate, never fret, never fail".[1] The surgeon and writer Robert Tuttle Morris wrote in his 1915 book Doctors vs. Folks that "It is well to follow the rule to 'Never complain, never explain'. A man is judged by his character as a whole – not by individual acts."[2]
The writer Walter Bagehot in his 1867 work The English Constitution wrote of the British royal family that "Above all things our royalty is to be reverenced, and if you begin to poke about it you cannot reverence it... Its mystery is its life. We must not let in daylight upon magic".[1] Bagehot's views have been seen as a precursor of "Never complain, never explain", and Queen Victoria and her descendants followed his advice.[1] Lady Elizabeth Bowes-Lyon (the future Queen Elizabeth The Queen Mother) gave a newspaper interview after her engagement to Prince Albert, Duke of York (the future King George VI) which reportedly enraged his father, King George V.[1] She never subsequently spoke or commented on royal issues.[1]
The motto became particularly associated with Queen Elizabeth II and was perceived as crucial to the success of her long reign.[3]
With a less deferential and increasingly aggressive media landscape, the strategy of "Never complain, never explain" was abandoned by Prince Charles (the future King Charles III) and his wife Diana, Princess of Wales, in the 1990s. Charles was the subject of Jonathan Dimbleby's authorised biography The Prince of Wales: A Biography and a subsequent television interview, Charles: The Private Man, the Public Role broadcast that year. Diana collaborated with Andrew Morton for his 1992 book Diana: Her True Story and Martin Bashir in a 1995 television interview.[1] It[clarification needed] was seen as damaging to the royal family in the 1980s as it left them unable to correct erroneous media stories about themselves.[1]
Prince Harry, Duke of Sussex, the younger son of Charles III, referenced his family's adherence to the motto in a 2023 interview to promote his forthcoming memoir, Spare.[4] He told Anderson Cooper on 60 Minutes that "The family motto is 'never complain, never explain', but it's just a motto ... [Buckingham Palace] will feed or have a conversation with a correspondent, and that correspondent will literally be spoon-fed information and write the story, and at the bottom of it, they will say they have reached out to Buckingham Palace for comment. But the whole story is Buckingham Palace commenting."[4]
Camilla, Duchess of Cornwall (later Queen Camilla), said in a 2017 interview that she was told as a child "Never complain and never explain – just get on with it".[5] The motto has also been described as a favourite of such personalities as Zsa Zsa Gabor, Simon Le Bon, Kate Moss, and Raine Spencer, Countess Spencer.[6][7][8]
References
[edit]
- 1 2 3 4 5 6 7 8 9 James Crawford-Smith (29 March 2022). "Royal Family 'Never Complain Never Explain' Motto—Who Said it First?". Newsweek. Retrieved 3 January 2022.

====================================================================================================

Never Complain, Never Explain
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-02
11:55:48 UTC
20:55:48 KST

Never complain, never explain
"Never complain, never explain" is a public relations strategy that has become particularly associated with the British royal family.
Origins
[edit]
The phrase is believed to have originated with the prime minister of the United Kingdom, Benjamin Disraeli.[1] It was attributed to Disraeli by John Morley in 1903, as quoted in Morley's Life of William Ewart Gladstone with the saying originating from "Maxims for a Statesman" by Benjamin Jowett, the Master of Balliol College, Oxford, written between 1873 and 1876.[1][2] One of Jowett's 11 maxims was "never quarrel, never explain, never hate, never fret, never fail".[1] The surgeon and writer Robert Tuttle Morris wrote in his 1915 book Doctors vs. Folks that "It is well to follow the rule to 'Never complain, never explain'. A man is judged by his character as a whole – not by individual acts."[2]
The writer Walter Bagehot in his 1867 work The English Constitution wrote of the British royal family that "Above all things our royalty is to be reverenced, and if you begin to poke about it you cannot reverence it... Its mystery is its life. We must not let in daylight upon magic".[1] Bagehot's views have been seen as a precursor of "Never complain, never explain", and Queen Victoria and her descendants followed his advice.[1] Lady Elizabeth Bowes-Lyon (the future Queen Elizabeth The Queen Mother) gave a newspaper interview after her engagement to Prince Albert, Duke of York (the future King George VI) which reportedly enraged his father, King George V.[1] She never subsequently spoke or commented on royal issues.[1]
The motto became particularly associated with Queen Elizabeth II and was perceived as crucial to the success of her long reign.[3]
With a less deferential and increasingly aggressive media landscape, the strategy of "Never complain, never explain" was abandoned by Prince Charles (the future King Charles III) and his wife Diana, Princess of Wales, in the 1990s. Charles was the subject of Jonathan Dimbleby's authorised biography The Prince of Wales: A Biography and a subsequent television interview, Charles: The Private Man, the Public Role broadcast that year. Diana collaborated with Andrew Morton for his 1992 book Diana: Her True Story and Martin Bashir in a 1995 television interview.[1] It[clarification needed] was seen as damaging to the royal family in the 1980s as it left them unable to correct erroneous media stories about themselves.[1]
Prince Harry, Duke of Sussex, the younger son of Charles III, referenced his family's adherence to the motto in a 2023 interview to promote his forthcoming memoir, Spare.[4] He told Anderson Cooper on 60 Minutes that "The family motto is 'never complain, never explain', but it's just a motto ... [Buckingham Palace] will feed or have a conversation with a correspondent, and that correspondent will literally be spoon-fed information and write the story, and at the bottom of it, they will say they have reached out to Buckingham Palace for comment. But the whole story is Buckingham Palace commenting."[4]
Camilla, Duchess of Cornwall (later Queen Camilla), said in a 2017 interview that she was told as a child "Never complain and never explain – just get on with it".[5] The motto has also been described as a favourite of such personalities as Zsa Zsa Gabor, Simon Le Bon, Kate Moss, and Raine Spencer, Countess Spencer.[6][7][8]
References
[edit]

Supermarket freezers across Europe hit by second suspected cyber-attack
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-02
11:50:13 UTC
20:50:13 KST

Supermarket chains in at least seven European countries have said the temperatures of refrigerators and freezers in some of their shops soared overnight to as high as 50C (122F), echoing a cyber-attack last month that caused millions of euros of damage in France.
Retailers in Belgium, Estonia, Ireland, Finland, Luxembourg, the Netherlands and Sweden reported problems with their refrigeration systems on Monday, and several companies confirmed they had been hacked.
The EU food and agriculture commissioner, Christophe Hansen, said the repeated cyber-attacks were deeply concerning, calling them “a deliberate attempt to disrupt our food supply chain and undermine consumers’ confidence in it”.
The incidents follow an attack in France last month in which hackers seized control of cooling units in as many as 700 supermarkets, raising their temperatures as high as 50C (122F) and causing fires and food losses estimated in the tens of millions of euros.
A growing range of devices, including refrigeration systems, can now be monitored and controlled remotely, leading authorities to particularly fear government-sponsored hybrid attacks in after Russia’s 2022 invasion of Ukraine.
Sweden’s Coop chain told the daily Aftonbladet that six of its shops had been hit, while ICA said it was “aware that a limited number of stores were affected by an incident overnight which impacted their refrigeration and freezer systems”.
An ICA shop at Sjöbo in southern Sweden posted that its fridges and freezers had been hacked and their temperature set at 50C. Another in Stockholm said its freezers were broken “due to a 2am cyberattack” and that “everything was destroyed”.
The head of Sweden’s national cybersecurity centre, John Billow, said it was working on incidents around the country. It had established the country had “quite a lot of exposed operational technology devices” that needed updating or patching, he told Reuters.
The Dutch retailer Ahold Delhaize, which last month reported a similar incident to police in the Netherlands, said fridges and freezers in dozens of its supermarkets in Belgium and Luxembourg had been hit.
It did not specify the cause, but reporters visiting a shop north-east of Brussels saw refrigerators and freezers empty and a sign reading: “Due to cyberattacks, there will be no products on the refrigerated or freezer shelves. We offer our sincere apologies.”
A company spokesperson, Sarah De Meester, said the range of fresh and frozen products was “temporarily somewhat limited” in affected shops, but that distribution centres were operating normally and there was no disruption to deliveries.
Belgium’s Le Soir newspaper reported that dozens of shops and supermarkets had been affected, including five Spar outlets, Shop & Go convenience shops attached to Q8 service stations and some smaller Proxy Delhaize mini-markets.
“We started mobilising technicians at 1am,” Stephan Moelans, the chief executive of Sabcobel, which operates Delhaize’s refrigeration systems, told the paper. “We spent all night and morning disconnecting the affected installations.”
He told the Belga news agency the incident had been a cyber-attack and that the units’ evaporators were switched to defrost mode.
The Colruyt and Intermarché supermarket chains also said they had been hit by technical problems affecting fridges in five and 15 of their shops respectively, and Carrefour Belgium reported disruption in about 15 of its 700 shops.
“It’s a cyber-attack, there is no other reason,” said Mich Vergauwen, a Carrefour spokesperson. An Ikea Belgium spokesperson likewise told Agence France-Presse that a cyber-attack had caused the temperature of freezers in its Mons shop to rise suddenly.
Ikea, famous for its in-store Swedish meatballs, said it had had to remove some food products from refrigeration units as a precaution and expected some food waste as a result.
In Finland, the Helsingin Sanomat newspaper said shops operated by the Kesko supermarket chain were investigating similar problems, while the Dutch public radio NOS reported that the systems of at least 17 mainly small local shops had been affected.
At one Estonian supermarket chain, attackers accessed a computer controlling the refrigeration system and turned up the temperature to 50C, the refrigeration supply company Kitman Coldtech told the ERR broadcaster.
“This is very fresh, it happened this morning,” its chief executive, Jann Mutso, said, adding that the attack was responded to within minutes and there had been no significant damage. The Selver supermarket chain said one of its outlets had been affected.
Ireland’s third-largest supermarket chain, SuperValu, said refrigeration systems limited to a specific model in 13 of its 223 shops had been affected by a cyber-incident, and that chilled and frozen products had been removed from sale.
Many cyber-attacks involve extortion or are politically motivated and are potentially carried out by state-level actors, said Tom Hegel of the cybersecurity firm SentinelOne. But the supermarket attacks, with no financial demands, point to sabotage, he said.
“Retail is an easy target because retail chains run enormous estates of refrigeration controllers, building management systems, door access panels and point-of-sale gear,” Hegel told Reuters.
Patrick Lamplmair of the cybersecurity firm Tributech said cyber-attackers would keep going “until it stops working” because the method was “cheap and the damage is immediate”. Many refrigeration systems had known and as yet unfixed flaws, he said.
“Thousands of these controllers were still reachable from the internet after the French attack,” he added. “A few commands to a temperature controller can destroy stock overnight and close stores.”
In the French attack, the hackers pirated controllers mostly supplied by the Danish company Danfoss, which said subsequently that it had identified some security flaws last year and had “long recommended” customers install updates.
The hackers remotely overrode the settings on thousands of freezers, fridges and chiller cabinets at chains including Intermarché, Coopérative U, Carrefour, Leclerc and Monoprix, pushing them from as low as -27C to well over 50C.
The shops had to discard vast quantities of fresh meat, dairy and frozen products. Individual supermarkets reportedly suffered losses ranging from tens of thousands of euros to more than €130,000 (£109,000).
The Paris prosecutor’s office has opened a formal investigation into the French attack and the prime minister, Sébastien Lecornu, instructed France’s national cybersecurity agency, Ansii, to investigate.
The agency subsequently reported that supermarkets using controllers from two other companies had also been targeted in the attack, but authorities have not indicated who could have been behind the incidents. There have been no claims of responsibility.

Supermarket freezers in Belgium and Sweden hit by suspected cyber-attack
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-02
11:48:02 UTC
20:48:02 KST

Shop freezers in seven European countries hit by cyber-attack
Dozens of supermarkets across seven European countries have been hit by a cyber-attack affecting their refrigerators and freezers.
Around 40 Delhaize supermarkets in Belgium and Luxembourg were affected, while a smaller number of shops were hit in Sweden, Ireland, the Netherlands, Estonia and Finland.
Sabcobel, a Belgian company that manufactures some of the affected refrigerators, said the units' evaporators were switched to defrost mode, causing them to warm up, in what is believed to be the same method used in last month's incident that hit hundreds of French supermarkets.
Millions of euros worth of food had to be thrown away in October, local media reported at the time.
Stephan Moelans, the CEO of Sabcobel, which operates Delhaize's systems, on Monday told the Belga news agency that the incident was a cyber-attack and that the company had been "working all night and morning to disconnect the affected units from the network".
He said since the French attack in early October, the company had been working to "protect our customers fridges against this vulnerability" but that the units needed physical modifications, not just software updates.
Most supermarkets across Europe are linked to the internet, and maintenance and troubleshooting can be done remotely.
"The simplest solution is to manage the fridges over a secure internet connection. Most of our customers switched to that after the attack in France," Moelans added.
The temperature of freezers in several shops across Europe reportedly rose to as high as 50C (122F).
It is unclear who is behind the latest apparent attack.
In the US, authorities are investigating what caused refrigeration malfunctions at a number of American military bases in August and working together with French officials to examine whether the incidents could be linked.
Both Belgium and Sweden's national cyber-security agencies said they were monitoring the incident.
The UK's National Cyber Security Centre said it was working with international partners and industry to "fully understand details of an incident". No incidents have been reported in the UK.
In Flanders, northern Belgium, a Delhaize branch in Hoeilaart told news website Het Laatste Nieuws it had lost €100,000 (£83,500) worth of produce.
The manager said that estimate did not even take into account further losses that would result from closing the shop.
Carrefour, a French-owned supermarket, said the incident had only affected 15 of its Belgian stores, adding that measures had been taken immediately to ensure all shops remained open.
A spokesperson for Colruyt Group, which operates Spar stores in Belgium under a franchise system, told Reuters news agency five shops had their fridge systems attacked.
Sweden's budget furniture retailer Ikea said refrigeration systems of its store in the western Belgian city of Mons were affected, with a spokesman quoted by Reuters as saying "we cannot rule out that additional locations may be affected".
In Sweden, several ICA and Coop shops were hit.
A spokesperson for ICA confirmed the incident had affected a "small number of ICA stores operating outside ICA's central network".
"ICA is currently supporting the affected stores in managing the situation," they said.
One of the ICA branches located in Sjöbo, southern Sweden, wrote on social media: "Our refrigerators and freezers have been hacked overnight and they have set the temperature to 50 degrees."
The store was closed earlier but has now reopened to customers.
Seven out of the 800 Coop stores in Sweden were also affected but the "situation is currently contained and under control", the company told the BBC.
In neighbouring Finland, refrigerator malfunctions were detected in some K-stores.
The owner of the supermarket chain Kesko was quoted by Finland's Helsingin Sanomat newspaper as saying its security experts were investigating what caused the problem.
The attack is also affecting shops in Ireland, the Netherlands and Estonia.
In Ireland, it impacted 13 SuperValu supermarkets. A spokesman for the owner of the chain, Musgrave Group, confirmed to The Irish Times that a "cyber incident" affected its refrigeration systems, but added that the impact was limited to a "specific model" of refrigerator.
A spokesperson for Danish manufacturer Danfoss told Dutch public radio channel NOS that the freezing and cooling systems of at least 17 companies in the Netherlands were attacked overnight, affecting butcher shops and small neighbourhood stores.
A Selver supermarket in Estonia was also affected and there are reports of other affected companies in the country, according to ERR.
Additional reporting by Shiona McCallum
Have you been affected by the issues raised in this story? Click here or use the form below.

====================================================================================================

Ford launches a ‘privacy promise’ and explains how and why it uses customer data
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-02
11:00:00 UTC
20:00:00 KST

As Ford prepares to launch a new generation of connected cars, the automaker is making a public promise about how it will handle that information.
The company unveiled on Monday what it calls a “privacy promise” — an “explicit commitment” to keep customers’ vehicle data safe and give them control over what they share. Ford also launched a website designed to educate customers about how, where, and why the automaker uses their data, and to show them how to change those settings.
While those controls have existed for years now, Ford wants to communicate more clearly with its customers about them, especially ahead of the launch of new, more connected vehicles like the ones being built on its upcoming “universal electric vehicle” platform.
“I personally think that it’s super important for us to be making it very clear to our customers what our data practices are, and not only that, but making a commitment to them about it,” Ford’s chief privacy officer Kristin Jones said in an interview with TechCrunch. “I also see this as a great opportunity to educate our customers about how they share data.”
This new privacy push comes as automakers are making their cars increasingly connected. While some polling shows U.S. customers want the kinds of features enabled by this connectivity, such as remote start, navigation, and driver-assistance software, only 14% said they fully understand what data their vehicle collects.
That has left room for some companies to quietly sell that data to third parties — a practice that got General Motors in trouble with the Federal Trade Commission, and also resulted in a $220 million settlement with 36 states last week.
This lack of consumer awareness around data collection and sharing has also opened the door for other companies to market themselves as better stewards of customer data. Slate Auto, the EV startup backed by Jeff Bezos, has made that pitch ahead of the expected launch of its minimalist pickup truck later this year.
“We did a survey of 1,000 consumers, and 91% of them said that data privacy was a key consideration for them. Realistically, it’s not something they engage with every day, but they want to know that they’re, again, in control, and that they have access to the information they need there,” Jones said. “I wouldn’t comment on what our competitors are doing, or anything that’s sort of been in the news there, but certainly it’s something that people don’t trust companies to take care with their data, and we really want to make sure we’re building that trust relationship with them.”
Jones, who joined Ford in 2024 after nearly two decades of working on privacy in the legal, financial, and healthcare fields, said the effort to improve how the automaker communicates with customers about data sharing has been in the works for a while.
“It’s been a journey for, frankly, most of the time I’ve been at Ford,” she said.
Ford’s new website will feature a mix of content that explains what data the company collects, how it uses that information, and how customers can opt in or out.
Opting out of sharing some data will naturally make some modern features inaccessible, Jones said. For example, turning off “connected services” in a Ford vehicle will disable features like real-time navigation and the built-in access to roadside assistance.
But one of the goals of this “privacy promise” push is to better justify why Ford is asking for access to this data and make it clear that the information won’t be used for purposes such as targeted advertising.
Giving Ford access to data about a vehicle’s health, for instance, will provide an owner with better real-time information about whether their car needs maintenance. But it will also “support research and development for future technologies,” Jones said.
“We also do not share information about how our customers are driving their vehicles to data brokers,” Jones said. “I just want to kind of reiterate that we’re not in it to try and monetize data in some way. We’re trying to offer products and services that customers derive value from.”

====================================================================================================

Show HN: I've updated my Linux System Call website and API
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-02
09:29:16 UTC
18:29:16 KST

Linux syscall tables
Architectures
Every architecture's table, with the full history of when each syscall appeared and when it was dropped, is also a plain JSON file at /<arch>/syscalls.json, for example /x64/syscalls.json.
Calling conventions
Number in NR, arguments in ARG0 to ARG5, then the instruction. The result comes back in RETURN.
API
curl https://api.syscall.sh/v2/syscalls/x64/read
Any kernel release, diffs between releases and more in the API docs.
Syscall tables across kernel versions
Entries get added and, rarely, removed between releases. Each architecture page has a kernel selector, and its JSON download records when each entry first appeared (since) and when it was dropped (removed_in).
Removed entries stay visible: pick "all, with removed" in the kernel selector and each dropped syscall is marked with the release that dropped it.
Where the data comes from
Everything on this site is read from the kernel source itself. For each architecture, the generator walks the syscall table and the SYSCALL_DEFINE macros of every mainline release it scans, so these are the numbers the kernel actually dispatches on.
It also records each architecture's calling convention, the instruction that enters the kernel, so every syscall table shows exactly where the number and the arguments go.
A syscall whose since equals the first release scanned for its architecture was already there back then, and may be much older.
Descriptions and full history
The short descriptions come from the Linux man pages project, and the io_uring ones from liburing. Whenever the data changes, the site is rebuilt from the syscall API.

====================================================================================================

Show HN: I've updated my Linux System Call website and API
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-02
09:29:16 UTC
18:29:16 KST

Linux syscall tables
Architectures
Every architecture's table, with the full history of when each syscall appeared and when it was dropped, is also a plain JSON file at /<arch>/syscalls.json, for example /x64/syscalls.json.
Calling conventions
Number in NR, arguments in ARG0 to ARG5, then the instruction. The result comes back in RETURN.
API
curl https://api.syscall.sh/v2/syscalls/x64/read
Any kernel release, diffs between releases and more in the API docs.
Syscall tables across kernel versions
Entries get added and, rarely, removed between releases. Each architecture page has a kernel selector, and its JSON download records when each entry first appeared (since) and when it was dropped (removed_in).
Removed entries stay visible: pick "all, with removed" in the kernel selector and each dropped syscall is marked with the release that dropped it.
Where the data comes from
Everything on this site is read from the kernel source itself. For each architecture, the generator walks the syscall table and the SYSCALL_DEFINE macros of every mainline release it scans, so these are the numbers the kernel actually dispatches on.
It also records each architecture's calling convention, the instruction that enters the kernel, so every syscall table shows exactly where the number and the arguments go.
A syscall whose since equals the first release scanned for its architecture was already there back then, and may be much older.

Bad Flock Info Leads Police To Tow Wrong Car, Then Towing Company Totals It
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-02
08:34:00 UTC
17:34:00 KST

Bad Flock Info Leads Police To Tow Wrong Car, Then Towing Company Totals It (jalopnik.com) 106
			
		 	
				"Police continue to blindly trust Flock cameras above and beyond the evidence in front of their own eyes," reports Jalopnik.com.
"After incorrectly connecting Rosalyn Crump's car to a shooting that had occurred the previous day, police had her car towed and impounded as evidence, reports Moneywise..."
According to WWBT 12 On Your Side, Richmond, Virginia, police didn't have a license plate number for the suspect's car, making it impossible to search Flock's records for that plate. Flock cameras collect more than just license plate information, so police searched for a make, model, color, and approximate model year.    Based on this extremely vague description, police zeroed in on Crump's 2012 Hyundai Elantra. Around 2 a.m. they started looking through the windows with flashlights to see what was in plain sight, which isn't technically a "search" — at least, not one that would require a warrant...   [Crump was asleep, but left her home to speak to the police officers to tell them they had the wrong car. But they didn't believe her.]
Other body cam footage shows an officer expressing doubt that Crump's car is the right one, since child seats and other items inside seem inconsistent with a shooting. Another officer replies, "Don't be surprised if they would have set that up like that." Instead of believing the evidence, or Crump, or interviewing witnesses who could have corroborated her story, police called Seibert's Towing to impound the car...
[Later] Police identified a similar-looking car that seemed to be actually connected to the shooting, and released Crump's Elantra. Nobody actually told Crump this until four days later. Seibert's told Moneywise that police didn't actually release the car until then. Either way, they didn't tell her that Seibert's had ripped the front bumper off during the towing process — leaving the car undrivable — until she arrived to pick it up. Seibert's took responsibility for causing the cosmetic damage, and was willing to cover the $2,212.30 bill to paint and replace the bumper cover, fog lights, and wheel liners. Then Crump asked for a full mechanical inspection, which revealed damaged tie rods and ball joints, jacking the bill beyond $6,000. Seibert's says it didn't cause this damage. Regardless of how it occurred, Seibert's insurer and Crump agree that her Hyundai is now a total loss due to the cost of the repairs it needs. It had been drivable, if mechanically worn, before police ordered it towed, damaging it for nothing more than a case of mistaken identity. 
The president of Seibert's towing told Moneywise that while they'd originally offered $2,200 to repair the bumper damage, Crump must now negotiate with their insurance company.   Crump has now resorted to a GoFundMe campaign to attempt to raise $17,100 for rental car expenses, a new car, and the expenses of moving to a different neighborhood.  130 donors have already contributed $7,431.
Reached for comment, the Richmond Police Department told local newscasters they "understand that Ms. Crump did not ask to become part of this investigation," and that they recognize the "frustration of having a vehicle towed and subsequently damaged."  But they also said they were following a "credible" lead, which is their responsibility.  The department "appreciates Ms. Crump's cooperation" (and "sincerely hopes the remaining concerns about her vehicle can be resolved.")
The city of Richmond has "already started backing away from Flock before Crump's story aired," reports Moneywise.  "Mayor Danny Avula has decided not to renew Richmond's license plate reader deal with Flock, which ends June 30, 2027. As of September, the city had 99 Flock readers on its streets."
They note that the Institute for Justice, a public interest law firm in Arlington, Virginia, "has tallied at least 27 cases since 2018" of similar police errors. "In each case, a license plate reader mistake led police to stop, jail or point guns at drivers who'd done nothing wrong. More than half came in 2023 or later."

BioWare co-founder: game execs "have never made a game" and don't get it
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-02
08:32:30 UTC
17:32:30 KST

'None of these people have ever made a game,' BioWare co-founder Greg Zeschuk says of game industry executives: 'They just don't know how it works'
In a rare interview, Zeschuk said the closure of BioWare is sad, but his greater concern is for the people put out of work.
It's been almost 15 years since BioWare co-founders Greg Zeschuk and Ray Muzyka announced their retirements from the videogame industry, and they've been resolute about staying out of it ever since: Muzyka continues to head up the ThresholdImpact investment firm he founded in 2012 and holds multiple roles at the University of Alberta, while Zeschuk owns Blind Enthusiasm Brewing Company, also based in Alberta, where he works with a small handful of other BioWare veterans.
But Zeschuk stepped back into the videogame spotlight, briefly, following last week's closure of BioWare by parent company Electronic Arts, telling 80lv that he got the news hours after it happened because he was on a fishing trip, and by the time he flew home a couple days later, he'd "already shelved it away."
"I wasn't there to share in the moment, which in some ways was nice because I was able to kind of just take my time with it," Zeschuk said. "I’m not saying it doesn't hurt and doesn't suck. And I think it's kind of sad. It's actually, in some ways, saddest for the city of Edmonton, where we're from, because we were kind of this beacon of hope and excitement and possibility. We showed you could do something really special in a place that was not known for doing things like that."
Zeschuk said he had his own "emotional moment" about BioWare years ago, when he left the company; his real concern now is for the people at the studio who now find themselves out of work: "Anyone getting put into the job hunt is a sort of terrifying endeavor and super sad."
He also said he has no regrets about the EA acquisition, saying it offered "stability" as the studio continued to grow and that "we saw there'd be value in being part of something bigger rather than going our own way and taking all the risks." At the same time, he had a feeling when EA was acquired by Saudi Arabia's PIF that it probably wouldn't go well for BioWare, because it doesn't fit into the guns-and-sports model: "BioWare makes these kind of unusual games that take a long time and they're complicated and so it's easier just to pump out sports games every year and that's a better business model in some ways."
Zeschuk was also critical of the idea, seen in the recent wave of studio mergers and AI-powered cutbacks, "that you can just kind of recreate great games with random people and AI. It doesn't work," Zeschuk said.
"The other harsh reality of almost all these companies, the people that go down the business track never really come from the development track. Because none of these people have ever made a game, like, never really sat and made one, like, from start to finish, even a small thing, they just don't know how it works."
From the creators of Edge: A free weekly videogame industry newsletter, featuring in-depth news, expert analysis and exclusive interviews
Zeschuk does know how it works, and he was clear that he's not interested in going back. "For me it was time to go, and I kind of walked out of the games business without any intention of ever returning. I'm happy being a consumer and playing games," he said. "I have my opinions on how to make stuff, but as enticing as it would be, it's just not [worth] the personal struggle and cost and effort required to make a great game. Having gone through the crucible of game development, I'm not going back in there."

Andy has been gaming on PCs from the very beginning, starting as a youngster with text adventures and primitive action games on a cassette-based TRS80. From there he graduated to the glory days of Sierra Online adventures and Microprose sims, ran a local BBS, learned how to build PCs, and developed a longstanding love of RPGs, immersive sims, and shooters. He began writing videogame news in 2007 for The Escapist and somehow managed to avoid getting fired until 2014, when he joined the storied ranks of PC Gamer. He covers all aspects of the industry, from new game announcements and patch notes to legal disputes, Twitch beefs, esports, and Henry Cavill. Lots of Henry Cavill.
